I The Book of His Luck

### I / 一
===Original===

The house of Saïd the fisherman nestled among the sandhills of the seashore at a long stone’s throw from the town, in whose shadow it lay at sunset. Within, it was a single room, very dirty, the abode of many aged smells; without, a squat cube with walls of stone and roof of mud sunbaked and rolled to a seemly flatness. Hard by was a fig-tree, the nearest to the sea in all that coast. Here, in a crotch of the branches, Saïd would place his mattress in the stifling summer nights and snore two deep bass notes in peace and coolness, while his wife trumpeted a treble from her couch upon the housetop. Here, when the day’s work was done, he would squat in the shade, drawing leisurely at his narghileh, with the sound of bubbling water to cool him at every puff.
He was not a great fisherman, such as is to be found in Europe, with a sailing-boat of his own, who will go far out to sea with his nets. If there were any such in all the coasts of Arabistan, Saïd had never heard of them. Sometimes he would row out in a friend’s boat to a little distance from the shore and drop his nets, a great circle of bobbing cork and driftwood to mark their whereabouts. But mostly he would go to some river-mouth or promontory where flat-topped rocks stretched far into the sea, promising safe foothold. And there, mother-naked, save for a huge turban, he would paddle and flounder all day long with his cast-net, sometimes alone, sometimes with several comrades.
At times, when the catch had been good, he would go into the city with a crate of fish and take his stand in the marketplace, in a corner which from long use he had come to call his own. There he would cry in a loud voice, beseeching Allah to put a craving for fish into the hearts of the passersby. And Allah often lent a kindly ear to his prayer, for he seldom went home but with an empty basket.
It was one evening as he was wending homeward, dragging his empty basket with him across the sand, that the first gust of misfortune struck him.
The sun drew near to his setting, though as yet the sky was innocent of red. Shadows lengthened eastwards across the sand, of the colour of a periwinkle flower. A number of dogs were lying replete about the body of a dead donkey at the edge of the ripples, panting drowsily with their tongues out. They blinked at him as he passed, and their bellies heaved uneasily. They were too full to snarl.

===Chinese===

渔夫萨义德的屋子，藏身在海岸的沙丘间，离城有一长箭之地；日落时分，它便卧在城的影子里。屋内是一间房，很脏，是许多陈年气味的居所；屋外是个矮墩墩的立方体，石砌的墙，泥抹的顶，让太阳晒得滚平熈帖。近旁有一株无花果树，是那一段海岸上离海最近的树。闷热的夏夜，萨义德就把他的褥子搁在树杈间，在清凉里安安稳稳打出两声低沉的鼾；他的妻子则在屋顶的铺上吹出高音与之相和。一天活计做完，他就蹲在树荫下，慢悠悠抽他的水烟壶，每一口都有咕嘟咕嘟的水声给他降温。
他不是欧洲那种了不起的渔夫——有自己的帆船、肯带着网远出大海的那种。整个阿拉比斯坦的海岸上有没有那样的人，萨义德没听说过。有时他划着朋友的船离岸一小程，撒下网去，一圈漂浮的软木和浮木标出它们的位置。但多半时候，他去某个河口或海岬，那里平顶的岩石远远伸进海里，许人一个站稳的落脚处。在那里，他赤条条一丝不挂——只除了一顶巨大的缠头巾——拿着撒网扑腾终日，有时独自，有时同几个伙伴。
赶上渔获好的日子，他就提一筐鱼进城，在市场里站定一个角落——长久站下来，那角落他已当作自个儿的了。他就在那里高声叫卖，祈求安拉把想吃鱼的馋念放进过路人的心里。安拉也常常垂听他的祷告，因为他极少不是提着空筐回家的。
正是在这样一个傍晚，他拖着空筐往家走，穿过沙地，第一阵厄运的风迎头扑来。
太阳快落了，天色却还未染红。影子向东拉长，横过沙地，是长春花的那种颜色。几条狗吃撑了，围着一头死驴卧在浪痕边上，吐着舌头困倦地喘。他经过时它们眨眨眼，肚皮不安地起伏。它们饱得连龇牙都懒得。

===Original===

A sense of well-being was upon him. He stopped to draw forth a little bag from the girdle of his robe. It contained the gains of the day. He let go the empty basket and squatted down upon the sand, telling out the money piece by piece into his lap. His eyes gloated over the pile.
He held the fingers of his left hand wide apart and touched them one by one with the forefinger of his right. His brows puckered with the effort to reckon how much he could afford to lay by in that hole in the floor of his house which held his savings.
So far as he could count, it needed but one more day like this to make up the price of the coffeehouse he had it in his mind to buy. Then he would leave the fishing business to Abdullah, his friend and partner, and customers would know him thenceforth as Saïd Effendi. That was but the first step in the path of his ambition. Presently he would be a Bey﻿—an Emìr, perhaps. He would lie all day upon a cushioned couch, smoking from a narghileh of rare workmanship. And when Abdullah came to beg him to buy fish, he would seize him by both ears and spit in his face.
Of a sudden the sound of loud shouting broke upon his reverie.
“*Oäh! Oäh!* Look to thyself, son of a dog!”
He was aware of two horsemen galloping madly down upon him from a gap in the sandhills﻿—Turkish officers of the garrison by their uniform. They were close upon him. He leapt to his feet and sprang aside just in time to save himself from being knocked down and trampled under their horses’ hoofs. He heard them laugh aloud and curse him as they sped by, blinding him for the moment in a cloud of sand.
“May their house be destroyed!” he snarled, looking after them and showing his fangs like a dog that is angry. Then he remembered the money which had been in his lap when their shouts startled him, and there was no longer any room for anger in his heart.
A wild light of hope and fear in his eyes, he flung himself full length upon the ground and fell to groping and sifting with trembling hands. But the wild rush of the horses had played the whirlwind with the sand, scattering it hither and thither and dinting it deep with hoof-prints. After many minutes of burrowing and seeking he had found only two small copper coins; and already the sun was sinking behind the city and its headland, whose shadow was within a hand’s-breadth of him.

===Chinese===

他浑身是一片安乐。他停下来，从袍子的腰带上摸出一只小钱袋，里头是一天的进项。他摞下空筐，蹲在沙地上，把钱一枚一枚数到大腿上。他的眼睛对那一小堆财货放出光来。
他左手五指张开，右手食指一根一根点过去。眉头皱起来，盘算着还能往屋里地面上那个存钱的窟窿里添多少。
照他数得出的，只消再有这么一天，就凑够了他心里盘算要买的那家咖啡店的价钱。到那时，他就把打鱼的营生丢给阿卜杜拉——他的朋友兼伙伴——主顾们从此就要称他萨义德·埃芬迪了。那不过是他青云路上的头一步。过不了多久他就是贝伊——兴许还是埃米尔。他可以成天躺在软垫榻上，抽一只做工精奇的水烟壶。等阿卜杜拉来求他买鱼的时候，他就揪住他两只耳朵，啐他一脸。
忽然一声大喊打断了他的好梦。
「哇赫！哇赫！找死啊，狗崽子！」
两骑人马正从沙丘的豁口里朝他狂奔而来——看军装是土耳其驻军的军官。眼看就要撞上。他跳起来往旁边一蹿，恰好躲过，没叫他们撞翻、踏在马蹄底下。他听见他们一边飞驰而过一边放声大笑，骂他，扬起的沙土一时糊了他一头一脸。
「叫他们家宅败落！」他冲着他们的背影飏出牙来低吼，像一条发怒的狗。随即他想起叫喊惊着他时还摊在腿上的那些钱，心里便再也容不下怒气了。
他眼里燃起又狂喜又惶恐的光，整个人扑倒在地，用发抖的手扒呀、筛呀。可是马群那一阵横冲直撞，早把沙地搅成了旋风过后的模样，刮得四散，蹄印压得深深的。他扒了半天、找了半天，只寻回两枚小铜子；太阳已经在城和城岬后面沉落，那影子离他只剩一掌宽了。

===Original===

A long train of camels passed him going towards the gate, the drivers cheerful at sight of their journey’s end.
“What seekest thou, young man?” cried one of them as he passed the fisherman.
Saïd raised himself to a kneeling posture and spread his hands over his eyes.
“Away, scoffer!” he cried sternly. “Who art thou that thou shouldst question a pious man at his prayers?” Then, after an interval of meditation, he prostrated himself so that his forehead touched the sand and forthwith resumed his search, earnestly beseeching Allah to guide his fingers aright and to keep all prying strangers at a distance.
The shadow was now upon him. All the west was a blaze of red gold, so that every roof, every dome, every palm-tree upon the skyline stood outlined clear and black. It was time to give over this frantic groping and clutching which gave such meagre results. He sat up and, squatting on his heels, began a more orderly and less haphazard search, taking one handful of sand at a time, sifting it between his fingers and laying it on one side upon a heap. After more than an hour’s experience of this process he had recovered some twenty small coins, amounting perhaps to a fifth part of the sum he had lost.
Night fell: the stars shone out, blackening the bulk of the dead ass, a few paces distant, which the dogs, reinforced by stray comrades from the city, were beginning to worry anew. The ripples, breaking in luminous foam upon the beach, murmured sadly in his ears. Hunger began to get hold of him. Hasneh would be wondering what had happened, and that savoury mess of lentils and oil would be baked to a cinder. Why should he not go home, eat and drink, and return to his search later on? It was not likely that the sand would be again disturbed that night. He could come back early in the morning and collect the rest of his scattered fortune. His basket would mark the exact spot.
So thinking, he rose and went homewards. A faint light streamed from the door and window of his dwelling. Hasneh was in there with the lentils. His heart warmed at the thought, making the neighbouring void colder and more empty by contrast. As he drew near to the house a sound of wailing grew in his ears﻿—such wailing as he had heard at funerals of the rich, where mourners were well paid for it.
His first thought was of the lentils, that they were spoilt.

===Chinese===

一长列骆驼从他身边经过，往城门去，赶驼人望见旅途将尽，个个欢畅。
「你找什么呢，年轻人？」一个赶驼人路过渔夫身边时喊道。
萨义德直起半身跪着，双手遮在眼上。
「去，嘲笑人的！」他厉声道，「你是什么人，敢盘问一个正在祷告的虔诚人？」默想了一阵，他伏下身去，额头触到沙土，随即接着寻他的钱，一面恳切地求安拉指引他的手指，又叫一切好事打探的生人远远走开。
影子这时罩到他了。整个西天烧成一片赤金，天际线上每一片屋顶、每一个穹顶、每一株棕榈都衬得清清楚楚，一片墨黑。这种胡抓乱摸、收获寒磘的勾当，该收场了。他坐起来，蹲踞在脚跟上，换了个有条不紊、不靠运气的找法：一次攂一把沙，从指缝里筛过，筛净了摞到一边堆起来。这套功夫做了一个多钟头，寻回来约莫二十枚小钱，凑起来约莫是失落的五分之一。
夜幕落了下来：星星亮出，把几步外那头死驴的轮廓衬得黑沉沉的；城里的野狗又汇合了几名散兵游勇，正重新撕扯它。细浪在海滩上碎成发亮的泡沫，在他耳边凄凄地响。饥饿开始抓住他。哈斯娜该在纳闷出了什么事，那一锅香喷喷的扁豆烧油也该烤成炭了。他何不先回家，吃饱喝足，回头再来接着找？这一夜沙地未必再遭践踏。他可以一清早回来，收拢他散落的其余家财。他的筐子会标出那个准地方。
这么想着，他起身往家走。他的住处从门窗里漏出一线微光。哈斯娜在里头守着扁豆。想到这个，他心头一暖，倒把四下的空旷反衬得更冷更空。走近屋子，一阵号哭的声音灌进他耳朵——那样的号哭，他只在富人的丧事上听过，哭丧人拿了丰厚的钱。
他的头一个念头是扁豆，怕它们糟了。

===Original===

His next, not without relief, that someone was dead within the house. But there was no one to die except Hasneh herself, and she it was who was wailing, as he had sometimes heard her scold, in a shrill cadence. His desire to learn the truth lent wings to his feet. In a few long strides he gained the threshold.
His woman lay stretched upon the floor within﻿—a heap of clothes from which those ghastly moans and howls proceeded, mingled with curses on some unknown being of the male sex. For a moment Saïd stood frozen in the doorway. Then the sight of something black and shrivelled in a pan upon the brazier sent angry blood coursing through every vein in his body. That something had once been a savoury mess of lentils baked in oil, the lust of which had drawn him from his search among the sand. He sprang to a corner of the room, seized a great staff which leaned against the wall, and fell to belabouring the woman with all the strength of his arm. Her droning wail changed all at once to a lively shriek. She leapt to her feet and closed with him, trying vainly to wrest the stick from his hand.
“May Allah cut short thy life!” she cried. “What have I done to deserve this of thee?”
“The lentils are spoilt!” retorted Saïd, furiously, wrenching his arm free of her and bringing the stick down heavily on her back. “May thy house be destroyed!”
“Madman!” she screamed. “Thou speakest of lentils when an enemy has robbed thee, ruined thee! Look!”
She pointed to a hole in the floor which had been hidden by her body when Saïd entered. Little mounds of fresh sand on the brink of it showed that hands had lately been at work there.
As Saïd’s eyes followed the line of her forefinger his jaw fell and the anger died out of his face. His stick clattered on the ground. Some thief had found out the place where his treasure was hidden, had come in his absence and unearthed the savings of ten long years.
He peered into the hole to assure himself that it was quite empty. Not a single para had been let fall or overlooked by the miscreant. His eyes became dull and filmy as those of a blind man. His face grew livid as the face of a corpse. He fell back against the wall of the room.
Supposing that the shock of her news had killed him, Hasneh began to wail anew, beating her breast and plucking at her robe to tear it. Her voice revived Saïd somewhat.
“Be silent,” he muttered﻿—“thou thief!

===Chinese===

第二个念头倒松了口气：屋里死了人。可屋里除了哈斯娜没有可死的人，而号哭的正是她本人，那调门的尖利，同她骂人时他偶尔听过的一样。要知道真相的欲望给他脚底安了翅膀。几大步，他就跨到了门槛上。
他的女人直挺挺躺在屋里的地上——一堆衣裳，那可怕的呜咽和嚎叫就从里头出来，夹着对某个不明身份的男人东西的咒骂。有一瞬，萨义德在门口僵住了。随后，火盆上那口锅里一坨焦黑干瘪的东西映入眼帘，愤怒的血登时冲遍他全身的血管。那东西原是一锅香喷喷的油焖扁豆，正是对它的馋念把他从沙地里唤了回来。他蹿到屋角，抄起靠在墙上的一根大棍，抡圆了胳膊朝那女人劈头打去。她哼哼唧唧的哀号一下子变成脆亮的尖叫。她跳起来扑上他，死命想夺那根棍，白费劲。
「叫安拉收了你的命！」她喊道，「俺做了什么，你这样待俺？」
「扁豆糟蹋了！」萨义德怒喝，猛地拧脱胳膊，一棍沉沉落在她背上。「叫你家宅败落！」
「疯子！」她尖叫，「仇家抢了你、毁了你，你还念念不忘扁豆！你看！」
她指着地面上一个窟窿——萨义德进来时，那窟窿叫她的身子挡着。窟窿边沿堆着几小丘新翻的沙，可见刚才有手在那里动过工。
萨义德的眼睛顺着她的食指望去，下巴垮了下来，脸上的怒气熄了。棍子当啷掉在地上。有贼摸到了他藏宝的地方，趁他不在，把十年长攒的家底挖走了。
他朝窟窿里张望，要亲眼确定它空空如也。那恶徒连一枚帕拉也没落下、没漏掉。他的眼睛变得浑浊无光，像盲人的眼睛。他的脸青得像尸首的脸。他向后一仰，靠住了墙。
哈斯娜只当这噩耗把他震死了，重新号哭起来，捶着胸口，撕扯袍子要撕开它。她的声音把萨义德多少唤回来一些。
「住口，」他咕哝道——「你这贼婆子！

===Original===

Thou alone wast in the secret of the hiding-place.”
“Thy life is my life; thy fortune, my fortune,” replied the woman, with indignation. “If thou prosperest, I prosper; and I have a part in thy loss. Listen now to the truth, nor judge me hastily unheard.
“Having prepared the lentils, I sat awaiting thy return, when my heart became sad within me. And I thought, if I uncover the hiding-place and fill my eyes with the sight of that which is good to see, there is no sin. So I took the piece of a broken vessel and scraped until the heap of coins was laid bare to mine eyes. So my heart had peace.
“And as I sat gazing upon my husband’s wealth which is mine, the voice of Abdullah called from without; ‘Behold the great fish, the giant of the deep, whose back is like Lebanon and his fins as the winnowing fans of Allah, with which he makes the winds to blow and stirs the sea to madness! It is Saïd who has brought it to land. It lies by the white stone where the nets of Saïd are spread out to dry. Run, O Hasneh, and thou shalt see that which no woman has ever seen.’
“At that I gathered up my raiment and ran out of the house, expecting to find Abdullah; but I found no man. I went all about the house, but I found not Abdullah nor any other. Then I trembled and fear came upon me. But the news of the great fish drew me onward, until I came to the white stone and found it lonely as ever and the sea-fowl undisturbed upon it. Then I knew that an evil spirit had cried in the voice of Abdullah to lead me astray. So I ran back with all speed along the shore. When I came to the house the hole was as thou seest it and all the money gone.”
Her last words were almost drowned in a flood of tears.
Saïd trembled and cold sweat stood in pearls upon his forehead.
“An evil spirit has done this,” he murmured hoarsely. “Oh, that my enemy had been a man!”
He fell to bemoaning his fate, cursing the day that he was born, and calling upon Allah to have mercy upon his faithful servant. The house that had been rifled by an evil spirit seemed dreadful and unfamiliar. The night which wrapped it about was filled with hideous faces, which glowered at him and mocked him through door and lattice. At length he exclaimed: “Abide here, Hasneh, and keep watch. If thou hearest a voice or seest any evil sight, cry aloud upon the name of Allah and thou shalt be safe.”

===Chinese===

藏钱的地方，知道底细的只有你一个。」
「你的命就是俺的命；你的财就是俺的财，」女人愤然答道，「你兴旺，俺就兴旺；你的损失里，也有俺的一份。如今听俺说实话，别不听分辩就断俺的罪。
「俺炖好了扁豆，坐着等你回来，俺心里忽然愁闷起来。俺想，俺把藏钱的地方揭开，让俺的眼睛看饱那看了叫人痛快的东西，这不算是罪。俺就拿起一片破瓦，刮呀刮，直到那一堆钱露到俺眼前。俺的心这才安生了。
「俺正坐着端详俺男人的家财——也就是俺的家财——外头忽然有阿卜杜拉的声音喊：『瞧那大鱼，深海里的巨怪！它的背像黎巴嫩山，它的鳍像安拉簸扬谷物的扇，扇起风来，把大海扇成疯癫！是萨义德把它弄上岸的。它就躺在白石那儿，萨义德的网正摊在白石上晾着。快跑呀，哈斯娜，你要见到女人从没见过的东西了。』
「俺一听，掖起袍子就往外跑，指望见到阿卜杜拉，却一个人影也没有。俺绕着屋子转了一圈，既没有阿卜杜拉，也没有别人。俺就发抖，害怕上了俺的心。可那大鱼的消息牵着俺往前走，一直走到白石跟前，它还是跟往常一样孤零零，海鸟在上头安然没受惊动。俺这才明白，是恶鬼学了阿卜杜拉的嗓音来引俺上钩。俺就顺着海岸拼命往回跑。等俺跑到家，窟窿就是你见的这样，钱全没了。」
她末了几个字几乎淹没在泪水里。
萨义德发着抖，冷汗在额头上结成一粒粒珠子。
「这是恶鬼干的，」他声音沙哑地喃喃，「唉，俺的仇家要是个男人就好了！」
他捶胸顿足地哀叹起自己的命来，咒自己出生的那一天，又求安拉怜悯他这个忠心的仆人。这间叫恶鬼洗劫过的屋子，显得狰狞而陌生。裹住房子的黑夜里塞满了狰狞的脸，从门口、从窗棂朝他瞪着、嘲弄他。末了他喊道：「你待在这儿，哈斯娜，守着。你听见什么声音、看见什么凶象，就高声念安拉的名字，保你平安。」

===Original===

With that he stepped out into the night, and, girding up his robe, sped across the sand to the city, black on the starlight, where a few scattered lights shone faintly.

===Chinese===

说罢他跨进夜色里，掖起袍角，穿过沙地直奔那座城——星光下黑黢黢一团，几盏零落的灯火幽幽地亮着。

### II / 二
===Original===

Close to the gate which is called the sea-gate, by which one goes down to the shore, there was a house, or rather hovel, built against the wall. This was the dwelling-place of Abdullah, Saïd’s bosom friend and partner. Abdullah himself was sitting in the doorway, smoking his narghileh, when Saïd came upon him. He was a fat man, with small bright eyes which were seldom at rest. Within the house a wick, floating in a saucer of grease, threw a fitful light upon the four walls, upon a couch whereon his wife lay huddled, a baby at her breast, upon the disorderly litter of the floor. At sight of his friend Abdullah started to his feet. His eyes were shifty to right and left, as though seeking some way of escape.
“May thy night be happy,” he faltered.
“May thy night be happy and blessed,” replied Saïd, keeping the rule which bids every man return a compliment with interest. Then with a frantic gesture, “I am ruined! An evil spirit is my ill-wisher. My money﻿—all that I had saved these many years﻿—has been stolen. Oh, that a man had been the thief!”
Saïd’s hands clutched murderously at the air and clenched, showing how he would have dealt with a mortal foe.
Abdullah’s composure returned to him at these words. His face was almost cheerful as he exclaimed, “Merciful Allah!”
“Listen, Abdullah,” pursued the other. “In my way homeward from the market I sat down to count over the price of the fish I had sold, when﻿—whizz!﻿—came two horsemen out of the air, and would have ridden over me had not Allah put it into my mind to jump aside. They laughed as they galloped by. They had the faces of jin﻿—you know them!﻿—eyes set slantwise, ears long and leaf-shaped like the ears of a pig. Then I found that all the money I had been counting was scattered in the sand. After long seeking I recovered but a few coins of small value. It grew dark. A train of camels came along the shore. Each camel was as big as a house, with a hump like the dome of a mosque. One of the drivers looked at me and asked me what I did. His eyes were two flames. They seemed to burn through to my heart. But I prayed to Allah and he vanished, the camels with him. I went home, hungry and thirsty, to supper; but I found my wife cast down upon the floor, weeping, and the lentils quite spoilt.
“Then she told me what had happened.

===Chinese===

离那道唤作海门——由它下到岸边——的城门不远，贴着城墙有一座屋子，与其说是屋，不如说是棚。这是阿卜杜拉的住处——赛义德的知交兼合伙人。赛义德撞见他时，阿卜杜拉正坐在门口抽他的水烟壶。他是个胖子，一双小眼睛贼亮，难得有片刻安分。屋里一截灯芯浮在一碟油上，忽明忽暗地照着四壁，照着一张卧榻——他妻子缩在榻上，奶着怀里的婴孩——照着满地狼藉。一见朋友，阿卜杜拉腾地站起。他的眼睛左右乱转，像在寻一条脱身的路。
「愿你今夜安好，」他结结巴巴地说。
「愿你今夜安好而且蒙福，」赛义德答道，谨守着人须加码回敬吉言的规矩。随即他狂乱地打着手势：「我完蛋了！有个恶灵同我作对。我的钱——我这些年攒下的全部——被人偷了。唉，贼要是个真人就好了！」
赛义德的双手朝空中凶狠地一抓一攋，表演出他若是遇上血肉之敌要怎样发落。
这几句话一出口，阿卜杜拉的镇定回来了。「仁慈的安拉啊！」他喊道，脸上几乎是喜气洋洋。
「你听我说，阿卜杜拉，」对方接着讲。「从集市回家的路上，我坐下来数卖鱼的钱，忽然——嗖！——两个骑手凭空而来，若不是安拉叫我心里一动、往旁边一跳，早从我身上踏过去了。他们驰过时大笑。那两张脸是精灵的脸——你认得的！——眼睛长得斜插着，耳朵又长又阔，像猪耳朵一样成叶片状。我再一找，方才数的钱全散在沙里了。寻了半天，只拾回几个不值钱的小钱。天黑了。一列骆驼沿滩走来。每一峰都大得像一座房子，驼峰赛过清真寺的圆顶。有个赶驼的望着我，问我在做什么。他的眼睛是两团火，像要一直烧穿我的心。可我向安拉祷告，他就没了，骆驼也随他没了。我又饿又渴地回家吃晚饭；却见我老婆扑倒在地上哭，扁豆全糟蹋了。
「她于是告诉我出了什么事。

===Original===

As she sat in the house a voice cried to her, for there was a great fish like a mountain lying on the shore by the white stone. She stepped out, but saw no man. She went to the stone, but there was no fish great or small. When she returned to the house she found a hole in the floor at the place where my treasure was hidden. All the money was clean gone. Oh, that my enemy had been a man!”
“Said she aught of the voice which tempted her?” asked Abdullah, with a hint of anxiety. His form was outlined in shadow upon the faint light which streamed from the doorway, so that Saïd could not see his face.
“Yes﻿—a strange thing﻿—she says that the voice was as thy voice, O father of Azìz.”
“There is no doubt that some devil has robbed thee,” said Abdullah, quickly. “Allah be my witness, I have not left my house since noon by reason of a pain in my belly. Is it not true, Nesibeh?”
The woman thus appealed to rose from her couch and came shuffling to the door. “Yes, it is true, by Allah,” she averred. “He has been very ill, I feared he was at the gate of death. But, praise to Allah, the pain has left his belly and he is now in health again. An afreet has robbed thee and has beguiled thy woman with the voice of Abdullah.”
“I am ruined! What can I do?” Saïd cried in a frenzy of despair. “Thou, O Abdullah, art known in all the city for a wise man. Counsel me, I entreat thee!”
Abdullah’s face assumed the stolid expression supposed by the muleteers and camel-drivers whose oracle he was, to betoken wisdom. His eyes became intent upon the inwards of a fish which adorned the ground near his feet. He sucked long and steadily at the mouthpiece of his narghileh, causing the water in the bowl to bubble convulsively and the charcoal in the cup above to give forth a lurid glow. Then he took the tube from his mouth, cleared his throat, spat solemnly, and said﻿—
“A devil has a spite against thee﻿—that is known. He has entered thy house once, he will enter it again. It is likely that he is of those who haunt the waste places of the shore, perhaps the very same who dwells in the ruined shrine among the sandhills. It were well for thee to take thy staff and thy woman and go into some far country﻿—into Masr or into the sunset-land which lies beyond. So thou shalt have peace, being far from the enemy.”
“What a mind!” exclaimed his wife, with hands raised in admiration.

===Chinese===

她坐在屋里，有个声音朝她喊，说白石旁边的滩上躺着一条山一样的大鱼。她跨出门去，却不见人影。她走到石头那里，大大小小的鱼一条也没有。等她回进屋，藏我钱财的那块地板上出了一个窟窿。钱干净利落地全没了。唉，我的冤家要是个真人就好了！」
「她提起那诱她出去的声音像谁没有？」阿卜杜拉问，带出一丝焦急。他身影的轮廓衬在门口漏出的微光上，赛义德看不见他的脸。
「提了——怪就怪在这里——她说，那声音同你的一模一样，阿齐兹的父亲啊。」
「不用说了，是哪个魔鬼偷了你，」阿卜杜拉赶忙说。「安拉给我作证，打中午起我就因为肚子疼没出过家门。是不是这样，内西贝？」
被点名的那女人从卧榻上起来，趿着步子挪到门口。「是，是真的，凭安拉起誓，」她一口咬定。「他病得不轻，我都怕他到了死门的关口。可是感谢安拉，疼痛离了他的肚子，他如今又康健了。是一个恶灵偷了你，还借用阿卜杜拉的嗓音哄了你的女人。」
「我完蛋了！可叫我怎么办？」赛义德绝望得发了狂似地喊。「你呀，阿卜杜拉，全城谁不知道你是个智者。求你指点我一条路！」
阿卜杜拉的脸摆出了那副呆板的庄严——那些尊他为神谕的赶骡人、赶驼人，就认定这模样是智慧的标志。他的眼睛凝神盯住他脚边地上点缀着的一条鱼的内脏。他衔住水烟壶的烟嘴，长长地、匀匀地吸，吸得烟壶里的水翻腾乱响，上头炭盅里的炭火放出幽幽的红光。然后他把烟管从嘴里拿下，清了清喉咙，庄重地碎了一口，说道——
「有个魔鬼同你结了仇——这是明摆着的。他进过你家的门一回，就会进第二回。多半他是流连海滩荒地的那一伙，兴许正是住在沙丘间那座废神龛里的那一个。你顶好带上你的行杖、带上你的女人，往远国去——去马斯尔，或是去更远的日落之邦。离了仇敌，你自然得安宁。」
「怎样的头脑啊！」他妻子赞叹道，双手都举了起来。

===Original===

“He speaks like a prophet. The mind of Abdullah is not as the mind of other men. He is a devil!”
“Tush, be silent, woman!” said the sage, indulgently.
Saïd squatted down at the threshold beside his friend. He put a hand to his forehead and remained thus thoughtful for some time. Then he said, “Thy advice is good. Tomorrow, at the rising of the sun, I shall depart. But thinkest thou in truth that the evil spirit will not follow me?”
“The jin have their homes like men,” replied Abdullah, sententiously. “They love to spend their lives in one place. In another city thou shalt surely live undisturbed.”
“But I have no money,” Saïd moaned, “without wealth I shall find no place in a strange land.”
Abdullah shook his head sadly.
“I am a poor man,” he said, “but all that I have is thine. Go, Nesibeh, see how much money there is in the house.”
The woman left the doorway and shuffled across the room to the couch where her baby slept. She felt under the coverings and drew forth a small box, which jingled as she shook it.
Raising the lid﻿—
“Alas!” she moaned. “It is a bad day with Abdullah. There are but a few *baras*.”
“It is a shame to ask my brother to accept so little!” exclaimed her husband.
“A little is much to one who has nothing,” whispered Saïd, eagerly. “Give me but the few *baras* that are there and may Allah increase thy wealth!”
Nesibeh turned the box upside down over the palm of her hand, and a number of small coins fell from it. Saïd’s brown fingers closed on them like an eagle’s claws. Then he rose to take leave.
“In thy grace, I depart,” he said. “May Allah prosper thee, O father of Azìz.”
“My peace go with thee,” said Abdullah, his voice broken with grief.
Saïd strode away, sad at heart, his mind busy with plans for the future. Hope was all but dead within him, for he had eaten nothing since sunrise. Alone once more and in the darkness, fear fell upon him with renewed strength. All the night was full of ghastly faces, of fiery eyes that glowered upon him. Strange shapes flitted among the sandhills. The sea burned with a pallid light. A fitful moaning was in the air. Pausing for a moment, he fancied the night an endless procession of weird forms﻿—a multitude which moved glidingly, silently, as one man. It filled him with a strange new horror, which yet seemed half familiar, as something remembered from a dream.

===Chinese===

「他说起话来像个先知。阿卜杜拉的头脑不是凡人的头脑。他简直是魔鬼！」
「去，闭嘴，女人！」智者宽厚地说。
赛义德在门槛上挨着朋友蹲下。他一只手支着额头，这样出神了好一阵。然后他说：「你的主意是好。明天太阳一出，我就动身。只是你当真以为，那恶灵不会跟着我么？」
「精灵同人一样也有各人的家，」阿卜杜拉拿腔拿调地答。「他们爱一辈子守在一个地方。到了别的城，你保管太太平平。」
「可我一个钱也没有了，」赛义德哀叹，「身无分文，异乡的地上哪有我的立足处。」
阿卜杜拉悲伤地摇头。
「我是个穷汉，」他说，「可我有的就是你的。去，内西贝，看看家里有多少钱。」
女人离开门口，趿着步子穿过屋子，到她婴孩睡着的卧榻那边。她在铺盖底下摸了一阵，掏出一只小匣子，一摇便叮当作响。
揭开盖子——
「哎呀！」她哀声叹气。「阿卜杜拉今日晦气。只剩几个*巴拉*了。」
「叫我兄弟收这么一点，臊也臊死了！」她丈夫喊道。
「有一点就等于有很多，对于一个一无所有的人，」赛义德热切地悄声说。「就把那几个*巴拉*给了我吧，愿安拉增长你的财富！」
内西贝把匣子倒扣在她摊开的手掌上，一小把铜钱落了下来。赛义德棕色的手指如鹰爪一般攋住了它们。随后他起身告辞。
「承你的恩，我走了，」他说。「愿安拉叫你兴旺，阿齐兹的父亲啊。」
「我的平安随你去，」阿卜杜拉说，嗓音让悲伤压得发颤。
赛义德大步走了，心里悲苦，脑子却忙着盘算前程。希望在他胸中几乎死绝了——打日出到此刻他还粒米未进。重新独自一人，又是在黑暗里，恐惧以加倍的力量搜住了他。整个黑夜布满狰狞的脸、瞪着他的火眼。怪异的形影在沙丘间掠过。海面燃着一层惨白的光。空气里一阵一阵地呜咽。他驻足片刻，只觉这黑夜是一列无尽头的怪形长队——一大群，滑行着、无声地，如一人般行进。这景象使他充满一种陌生的新恐怖，却又仿佛一半熟识，像梦里记得的东西。

===Original===

Well-known sounds, such as the hooting of an owl, the bark of a dog from the city, or the howl of a jackal from some landward garden, were separate terrors.
He had not made many steps from the door of his friend’s house ere the fear of the Unknown which lurks in darkness took hold of him. He girded up his loins and ran across the sand as fast as his brawny legs would carry him. He looked neither to the right nor left till he reached his house. On the threshold a savoury smell attacked his nostrils and hope suddenly revived. Hasneh stood with her back towards him, leaning over the brazier, from which light steam arose enveloping her and filling the house with that peculiarly hopeful smell. “Allah is just!” murmured Saïd, licking his lips.

===Chinese===

连熟悉的声音——猫头鹰的啼叫、城里传来的犬吠、或是内陆某座园子里豺的嘷——也各自成着一桩恐怖。
离了朋友的家门没有几步，那潜伏在黑暗中的「未知」之惧便攋住了他。他束紧腰布，凭着一双壮腿能跑多快跑多快，横越沙地。到得自家门前，他目不斜视。门槛上一股喷香直扑鼻孔，希望僚地复活了。哈斯娜背对着他站着，俯身在炭炉上；炉上腾起淡淡的蒸汽，裹住她的身子，也让满屋充满那一股格外叫人存着盼头的香味。「安拉是公道的！」赛义德舐着嘴唇喃喃道。

### III / 三
===Original===

It was the coolest hour of all the twenty-four when Saïd the fisherman climbed down from his nest in the fig-tree. In spite of the troubles and fears of the evening before he had slept soundly and was refreshed. The eastern sky was whitening to the dawn, and a wave-line of distant mountains was grey and cloudlike upon it. Darkness still lingered on sea and land, but it was a darkness of the earth rather than of the heavens.
From a jar within the threshold of the house he took a little water and went through the form of ablution. Then, facing south, he knelt and fell prostrate several times, thumbs fast behind his ears and hands spread across his eyes as an open book.
As he walked along the shore to the place where he had left his basket overnight, the cry of the first awakened seabird hailed the dawn. The little city with its dome and minarets grew white before him against a sky still dark and studded with stars. A man came down from the sea-gate riding upon an ass. Then came another man with two camels. The folk of the city were astir and going every man about his business.
The place was just as he left it, save that the carcase of the donkey had been dragged a few yards to landward by the hungry tearing of the dogs, and the backbone was now laid bare. He flung himself face downward on the sand and fell at once to his groping and sifting.
The stars shone dead in the west, then vanished altogether. Rosy light stole over land and sea, mantling on the white buildings of the city like the shame in a young girl’s cheek. Then the sun flashed forth above the distant hills and all things had colours of their own once more.
The rays struck warm on Saïd’s back as he lay prone beside his basket. Their touch cheered him like a friend’s hand. He set to work hopefully with the result that, in half an hour, he had recovered many coins, amounting to within a few paras of the sum lost.
By that time there were many people on the beach, some entering, some leaving the city. It was unsafe to prolong the search lest someone, guessing his task, should fall upon him and rob him. He got up, therefore, and walked homeward, trailing his basket along with him.
Hasneh stood in the doorway looking out for him. A donkey, burdened with two sacks, was tethered to a low-bending branch of the fig-tree. He smiled approval as he slipped off his shoes at the door.

===Chinese===

渔夫赛义德从无花果树上的窝里爬下来时，正是一昼夜中最凉快的时辰。尽管前一夜又烦又怕，他却睡得死沉，养足了精神。东天泛出黎明的鱼肚白，远山的一线峰峦灰蒙蒙地浮在上面，像一抹云。黑暗还留恋在海上陆上，可那已是尘世的暗，不是天上的暗了。
他从门槛里侧的水瓮中舀了点儿水，行过小净的仪节。随后他面朝南跪下，一次次叩首，两个拇指扣在耳后，双手拢在眼前，像摊开的一部书。
他沿着滩头走向昨夜存下鱼筐的地方，最先醒来的海鸟一声啼鸣，向黎明道了晨安。那座小城连同它的穹顶和宣礼塔，在依然缀满星斗的暗天衬托下，在他眼前渐渐显出白色。一个人骑着驴从海门下来。又来一个人，赶着两峰骆驼。城里的人已经起身，各自奔各自的营生。
那地方同他离开时一样，只是驴的尸身被饿狗们撕拽着拖向内陆挪了几码，脊梁骨如今露了出来。他一头扑在沙上，立刻摸呀筛呀地干了起来。
星星在西边发出死气的光，随即全然隐没。玫瑰色的光悄悄漫过陆地和海洋，拨在城中白屋上，如少女颊上的红晕。随后太阳在远山之上喷薄而出，万物又都有了各自的色彩。
赛义德伏在鱼筐旁，日光照得他背上暖洋洋的。那暖意像朋友的手，叫他精神一振。他怀着指望埋头苦干，结果不出半个钟头，拾回了不少钱，同丢失的数目只差几个帕拉。
这时滩上人已经多了，有进城的有出城的。再寻下去不安全，怕有人猜出他在干什么，扑上来抢他。于是他站起来，拖着鱼筐往家走。
哈斯娜站在门口张望他。一头驮着两条口袋的驴，拴在无花果树一枝低垂的枝桠上。他在门口脱鞋时，赞许地笑了。

===Original===

She had been stretched upon the roof when he set out and snoring loudly. He had been gone but a little while, yet here was the ass laden with all the house that was worth carrying, and the morning’s meal of bread and curds ready to be eaten.
His fast fairly broken, Saïd went out to the fig-tree to see that the girths were firm which held the sacks to the body of the ass. The sunlight danced on the little waves as they pushed shoreward, and made pearls of the dewdrops which yet hung in the shade of some feathery tamarisks behind the house. The sky was a great blue dome over sea and land. His heart turned sick with the thought of quitting the well-known scene, with its familiar voices, to sojourn among strangers in a strange country. Why need he go? The terrors of the night before had no weight with him now. They had faded with the darkness and the stars.
Doubtless his loss was great and hard to bear; but others had suffered worse things. The evil spirit which had robbed him might not return again; and if it did he had but to write the name of Allah upon the doorposts, then upon the shutters of the window, and his house would be safe. He stretched out his hand to loose the burden from the donkey’s back.
“May thy day be happy, O Saïd,” came a complacent voice from behind. Turning, he stood face to face with Abdullah, his partner. “Thou art to depart﻿—not so? I am coming to see if I can serve thee in the work of packing and lading.”
“My mind is changed. Perhaps I go not,” rejoined Saïd, moodily.
“What is this?” exclaimed the other, seeming horror-stricken. “Thou art mad to stay after all that has befallen thee here.”
“What matter! The like or worse may befall me in a strange land. I will stay in the place where I was born, wherein is my father’s grave.”
Once more Saïd put forth his hand to unload the ass, but Abdullah caught his arm.
“I advise thee to thy advantage,” he whispered angrily. “We spoke last night of devils. What are they? Their power is only in the night. There are those who have power to harm thee both by night and day.” He sank his voice as if fearing lest a bird of the air might carry his words to high places. “The Basha has heard of thy wealth which thou pretendest to have lost. Men have told him how thou dost grope in the sand.

===Chinese===

他出门时她还直挺挺躺在屋顶上，鼾声大作。他不过走了这一小会儿，驴已经驮上了这个家里值得一拿的全部家当，早饭的饼和凝乳也摆好待吃。
斋戒的这一顿着实开了，赛义德出到无花果树下，检查捆口袋的肚带在驴身上勒得紧不紧。阳光在推向岸边的小浪上跳舞，又把还挂在屋后几株羽毛似的柽柳荫里的露珠点成珍珠。天空像一座蓝色的大穹顶，罩着海和陆地。一想到要擜下这熟悉的地方、这些熟惯的声音，去异邦客居在陌生人中间，他的心就发苦。他何必要走呢？昨夜那些恐怖此刻于他已没了分量。它们已随黑暗和星光一同褪尽了。
他的损失固然惨重，难以承受；可比他更惨的也大有人在。抢了他的那个恶灵未必再来；就算再来，他只须把安拉的名写在门柱上，再写在窗板上，家宅便平安了。他伸手就要去卸驴背上的驮子。
「愿你今日安好，赛义德，」背后传来一个得意的嗓音。他转过身，同他的合伙人阿卜杜拉打了个照面。「你要动身了——是不是？我来看看收拾装载的活计里，能不能帮你一把。」
「我改了主意。兴许不走了，」赛义德闷闷地答。
「这是怎么说？」对方喊道，一副吓坏了的样子。「这里头出了这么多事，你还留下，你是疯了。」
「有什么要紧！异乡未必就不出这样的事，或者更坏的。我要留在我生身的地方，我父亲的坟就在这里。」
赛义德又一次伸手去卸驴，阿卜杜拉却一把攋住他的胳膊。
「我是为你好才劝你，」他气呼呼地低语。「昨夜咱们说到了魔鬼。魔鬼算什么？他们的本事只在夜里。可有人，白天黑夜都有本事害你。」他把嗓音压得更低，仿佛怕空中的飞鸟把他的话搜到高处去。「帕夏听说你了——你那笔你假称丢了的财。有人告诉他，你如何在沙里摸来摸去。

===Original===

Remember the fate of Ali ebn Mahmud, who was said to have a treasure hidden in his garden, how they beat and tortured him so that he died!”
Saïd’s jaw fell. “Is this true?” he faltered.
“True, by Allah!” replied the other, his face anxious, his little eyes keenly watchful of his friend’s countenance. “Am I a liar?”
A wild light of terror flamed in Saïd’s orbs. He strode to the door of the house and shouted to Hasneh to make ready for the start. Then he returned and, untying the rope which bound the ass to the tree, bestrode the already laden beast. At the same moment his woman appeared from the house, a great bundle upon her head.
“Allah be with thee!” he cried, striking the ass with his staff, so that it started forward at a shambling trot.
“But what of thy nets, of thy house, of thy fig-tree?” cried Abdullah, wringing his hands.
“Take them﻿—all that I have!” shouted Saïd, without looking back. He was sitting on the hindquarters of the donkey, flourishing a rope which served for bridle, his long brown legs stretched along the sacks, his feet erect beside the beast’s ears. His whole frame jolted with the trotting of his steed. The woman ran behind with one arm raised to keep her bundle from falling.
“Whither away?” shouted Abdullah.
“To Es-Shâm﻿—to Baghdad﻿—to India!﻿—far away! What matter, so that I be out of his reach. May all his race perish!”
Abdullah stood looking after the fugitives until they were lost to sight among the sandhills. Then he took a cigarette from somewhere in the depths of his trousers, lighted it and squatted down in the shade of the fig-tree now his own.

===Chinese===

记不记得阿里·伊本·马哈穆德的下场——人家说他园子里藏着财宝，他们把他打得百般折磨，活活弄死了！」
赛义德的下巴耷拉下来。「这是真的？」他嘨嚅道。
「真的，凭安拉起誓！」对方答道，一脸焦灼，那双小眼尖利地盯着朋友的脸色。「我是撒谎的人么？」
一阵狂乱的恐怖之火在赛义德眼里烧起来。他大步抢到屋门口，朝哈斯娜大喊，叫她收拾停当准备动身。然后他回来，解开拴驴的绳子，跨上那已驮满的牲口。同一刻，他的女人从屋里出来，头顶着个大包袱。
「安拉与你同在！」他喊着一杖打在驴身上，驴便一颠一颠小跑起来。
「那你的网呢，你的房子，你的无花果树呢？」阿卜杜拉捱着手喊。
「都归你——我所有的一切！」赛义德头也不回地喊。他坐在驴的屁股上，挥舞着充作笼头的一根绳，两条棕色长腿顺着口袋伸直，两只脚竖在牲口耳朵旁边。坐骑一路小跑，他全身跟着颠。那女人跑在后头，一只胳膊举着，扶住头顶的包袱不让它掉。
「往哪儿去呀？」阿卜杜拉喊。
「去沙姆——去巴格达——去印度！——去远远的地方！只要他够不着我，去哪儿都行。叫他们全家都灭了吧！」
阿卜杜拉站在原地望着那对逃难的人，直到他们的身影没进沙丘。随后他从裤子深处不知哪儿摸出一支烟，点上，在那棵如今归了他自己的无花果树的荫凉里蹲下了。

### IV / 四
===Original===

As for Saïd, he urged his steed across the sand as fast as the weight on its back and the looseness of the ground would allow. His arm rose and fell continually with a backward sweep, and the hindmost part of the donkey rang wooden to the thwack of his stick. A constant growl of curses rolled upwards from his throat. Hasneh, her bosom heaving, her breath coming and going in short pants, struggled to keep up with him.
As they proceeded the soil became firmer under foot; creeping branches of the wild vine, rank grasses and sundry big-leafed plants, bound the sand together. Soon they came into a road with a hedge of prickly pear on either side, fencing an orange garden. Through gaps in the hedge golden globes shone amid dark foliage with here and there a spray of white blossom. The air was cloyed with a fragrance from which the hum of bees seemed inseparable. A gate by the wayside stood open. Within were two men busy packing a great heap of oranges into square wooden boxes. Saïd shouted a salutation as he sped by, and in return they pelted him with the fruit﻿—a dozen at least﻿—which Hasneh stayed to gather up into the bosom of her robe. The scarlet flowers of a pomegranate tree flamed among the leafage on their right hand.
A little while and the gardens were left behind. The wide plain rolled in smooth waves before them, away to the foot of the mountains, with a shimmer of grey olives in the distance.
At the end of an hour, during the whole of which Saïd ceased not for a minute from beating his donkey, they drew near to a village which stood upon a hill, three fine palm-trees tapering skyward from among its squat dwellings of sunbaked mud. Here the fisherman proposed to rest awhile till the heat of the day should be passed. Hasneh praised Allah for the respite.
As they entered the narrow pathway, choked with offal, which ran between the hovels, a man’s voice called to them from a doorway﻿—
“Deign to enter, O Saïd! Honour my house with thy presence!”
The speaker came forth and bowed low, holding a hand to his forehead. He was a huge, loutish fellow, who had seen thirty summers and more. He had a bushy black beard, and big brown eyes of rare stupidity. His long garment and his turban had grown old upon him. He came sometimes to the market to sell the produce of his fields. Saïd had seen him there and spoken with him more than once.

===Chinese===

至于萨义德，他催着他的坐骑穿行沙地，快到驮的重物和酥松的地面所许可的极限。他的胳膊一起一落，向后抡个不停，驴的后半截身子挨一棍就发出木头的声响。一串咒骂从他喉咙里滚滚往上冒。哈斯娜胸口起伏，气一口一口地喘，挣扎着跟上他。
走着走着，脚下的土结实起来；野葡萄藤的爬蔓、茂草和各样阔叶的草，把沙土编结在一处。不久他们上了路，两边是仙人掌编的篱笆，圈着一座橙子园。篱笆的豁口里，金色的圆球在暗绿的叶子中间发亮，间或一簇白花。空气里灌满了浓得发腻的甜香，蜜蜂的嗡嗡声仿佛跟它分不开。路边一扇门敞着，里头两个汉子正忙着把一大堆橙子装进方木箱。萨义德飞驰而过时喊了一声问好，回敬他的是一顿果子雨——至少一打——哈斯娜留下来，一颗一颗捾进袍子的怀里。右手边，石榴树的红花在叶丛里烧成一片。
不多时，园子落在了身后。宽阔的平野像平滑的波浪在他们面前翻滚出去，一直翻到山脚下，远处是一片灰蒙蒙的橄榄树的微光。
一个钟头走到头——这一路萨义德没停过一分钟打他的驴——他们临近一座坐落在山冈上的村庄，三株挺秀的棕榈从晒土砌的矮屋中间直削向天。渔夫提议歇一歇，等正午的酷热过去。哈斯娜为这个喘息赞美了安拉。
他们走进那条塞满烂污的窄巷——巷子在破屋之间穿行——一个男人的声音从门洞里招呼他们：
「请进，萨义德！光临寒舍，蓬荜生辉！」
说话的人迎出来，一躬到底，手按在额上。他是个大块头、笨手笨脚的汉子，过了而立好几年。一把浓黑的大胡子，一双棕色的大眼睛，蠢得出奇。长袍和缠头巾在他身上都穿旧了。他有时进城赶集，卖他地里的出产。萨义德在那里见过他，跟他搭过不止一回话。

===Original===

He was called Muhammed abu Hassan, and bore the reputation of a good-tempered, lazy fellow.
The fisherman, nothing loth, alighted, and having touched the hand of his host in salutation, proceeded to tie up his ass to the doorpost. That done, he slipped off his shoes and allowed himself to be ushered into the house. Hasneh squatted down humbly at the threshold of the door.
Their host set to work to kindle some charcoal upon a stone in one corner of the room, grumbling all the while because his woman was not there to do it for him. She was at work in the tobacco-fields, it appeared, with others of the village.
Somehow﻿—it must have been by magic, or the laden ass tethered outside may have had something to do with it﻿—it soon became known in all the village that a stranger had arrived from the city and was the guest of Muhammed abu Hassan. Men dropped in, one by one, feigned surprise at sight of Saïd and of each other, and squatted down with their back to the wall.
“What news?” was the first question of a newcomer after the proper civilities had been exchanged.
To which Saïd replied, in every case, “There is nothing new today.”
“It is said that there will be war between the Turks and the Franks?” said an old man, reverend and very dirty, in a tone three parts of assertion, one part of inquiry.
“I have heard nothing of it,” Saïd answered, rolling a cigarette between thumb and forefinger.
“Allah grant that there be no war!” cried an aged sheykh, with face wrinkled as a withered olive, in a quavering voice. “I remember, when the last war was, they sent suddenly and seized every horse, mule, and donkey in our village for the soldiers to ride. Only a horse and two asses were restored to us when all was over. And after two days the horse died.”
There broke forth a chorus of guttural curses upon wars and soldiers.
At last the business of grinding and stewing the coffee was accomplished. Two small cups were passed round the circle from hand to hand, Muhammed filling and refilling them until all had partaken. Even Hasneh, sitting patient and submissive on the doorsill, was not forgotten in the end.
“Whither goest thou?” asked Muhammed of his guest, when at last he had leisure for conversation.
“To Damashc-ush-Shâm,” replied Saïd, and hesitated. He dared not tell the true reason of his leaving home, lest he should forfeit the esteem of his hearers.

===Chinese===

他叫穆罕默德·阿布·哈桑，名声是个好脾气、懒得动的家伙。
渔夫正巴不得，下了驴，跟主人碰手为礼，随手把驴拴上门柱。拴好了，他褪下鞋，由人往屋里让。哈斯娜谦卑地蹲在门槛上。
他们的主人动手在一角的一块石头上引炭火，嘴里嘟囔个不停，怪他的女人不来伺候。原来她跟村里旁的女人下烟田做工去了。
也不知怎么——想必是法术，要不就是外头拴着的那头驮驴多少出了力——消息很快传遍全村：城里来了个外乡人，是穆罕默德·阿布·哈桑的客。男人们一个接一个踱进来，见了萨义德、见了彼此，都装出一脸惊讶，然后背靠墙根蹲下。
「有什么新闻？」寒暄周全之后，每个新来的人头一句都是这个。
对此萨义德的回答回回一样：「今日无新闻。」
「说土耳其人要跟法兰克人开战？」一位可敬而极脏的老人说，口气七分是断言，三分是探问。
「俺没听说。」萨义德答，拇指和食指中间搓着一支烟卷。
「安拉保佑别打起来！」一位上了年纪的谢赫喊道，脸皱得像一颗干瘪的橄榄，声音发颤。「俺记得上一回打仗，他们突然派人来，把村里的马、骡、驴一头不剩地牵走给兵骑。等事情完了，只还了俺们一匹马、两头驴。过了两天，马也死了。」
一阵喉音浓重的咒骂腾起来，骂战争，骂兵。
末了，磨咖啡煮咖啡的活计总算办完。两只小杯在圈子里手手相传，穆罕默德斟了又斟，直到人人尝过。连蹲在门槛上耐心顺服的哈斯娜，末了也没被忘掉。
「你要上哪儿去？」等总算得了闲谈的空，穆罕默德问客人。
「上大马士革去。」萨义德答，却迟疑了。他不敢说出自家出逃的真实缘由，怕丢了听客们的敬重。

===Original===

A man who bewails his misfortunes before strangers is a fool and rightly despised; but he who exalts himself is sure of honour. He added﻿—
“I go to Es-Shâm, to the house of my brother, who is dead. He was a great man and rich. Moreover, his woman was barren. I go to claim the inheritance.”
The murmur of congratulation which this fiction called forth had scarcely died away when a clatter of hoofs rang through the village. Faint shouts and cries came from the distant field where the women were at work.
“The soldiers! The soldiers are upon us!” cried Hasneh from her post at the threshold.
Every man sprang to his feet and rushed to the door, Saïd with the rest. Five Turkish soldiers and a young officer rode at a foot’s pace up the narrow path between the hovels. Remembering the words which Abdullah had spoken that morning, Saïd’s teeth chattered. Doubtless the Pasha was informed of his flight and these men had been sent to take him.
“Where is the house of the sheykh of the village?” cried the officer as he rode by.
A score of turbaned heads were bowed, a score of brown hands saluted, and a score of voices proffered directions in divers tones of self-abasement. Saïd was reassured. Had the officer been looking for him he would not surely have asked for the house of the sheykh. The next moment his heart sank again and a cry of dismay broke from his lips. One of the troopers, in passing, bent down, and, severing the cord by which the donkey was tethered with one stroke of a knife, caught the end deftly as it fell, and rode on, leading with him all that remained of Saïd’s worldly goods. With a shriek of rage and despair, the wretched man broke through the crowd and sprang forth into the blinding sunlight. A few fierce bounds and he had overtaken the plunderer. He strove to wrest the rope from his grasp.
“Stay! Stay!” he cried. “Let me but take off the sacks! It is all that I have!”
For answer he received a blow on the wrist which forced him to quit hold.
“Pig!” cried the soldier, angrily. “The Sultàn has need of thy beast for his soldiers; and I that am his soldier have need of those sacks for myself. Dost understand? Let go, son of a dog!”
Saïd, baffled in his design upon the rope, was now struggling frantically to wrench the sacks from the donkey’s back.

===Chinese===

在生人面前哭自己倒霉的，是蠢人，活该叫人小看；抬举自己的，稳得敬重。他添上一句：
「俺上大马士革去，上俺兄弟家；他死了。他是个大人物，又有钱。再说，他的女人不生育。俺去承那份产业。」
这套鬼话引来的一片道贺还没落音，一阵蹄声嗒嗒地响遍了村庄。远处女人做工的田里传来隐约的喊叫。
「兵！兵来了！」哈斯娜在她门槛上的岗位上喊。
人人都跳起来冲到门口，萨义德也在其中。五个土耳其兵和一个年轻军官，踏着步子走上破屋间那条窄巷。想起阿卜杜拉那天早上说的话，萨义德的牙齿打起颤来。不用说，帕夏得了他出逃的信，这些人是来捉他的。
「村长谢赫的家在哪儿？」军官一路走一路喊。
二十缠头一齐低下去，二十只棕色的手一齐行礼，二十条嗓音以各种自贬的调门抢着指路。萨义德宽心了。军官要找的是他，断不至于问谢赫的家。可下一刻，他的心又沉下去，一声绝望的惊叫冲出他的嘴。一个骑兵经过时俯下身，一刀割断拴驴的绳，绳子一落，他手一探，灵巧地攥住绳头，继续往前骑——萨义德的家当，全部家当，就这么叫他牵走了。那倒霉的人又怒又绝望，一声尖啸从人堆里挤出去，蹿进晃眼的日头底下。几个狠跳，他追上了那个抢东西的。他死命要夺回那根绳。
「慢着！慢着！」他喊，「容俺把褡裢卸下来！这是俺的全部了！」
回答他的是手腕上一刀背，打得他松了手。
「猪！」那兵怒道，「素丹要用你这家伙给他的兵骑；俺是素丹的兵，俺要用你那些褡裢。明白吗？撒手，狗崽子！」
萨义德夺绳不成，又疯了一样去撕驴背上的褡裢。

===Original===

The cavalcade had come to a standstill before the house of the sheykh, and the other soldiers looked on good-humouredly, laughing now at their comrade, now at the fisherman, with perfect impartiality. Their laughter stung the plunderer to frenzy. He unslung the carbine from his back, and, leaning over the saddlebow, dealt a vicious blow at Saïd’s head with the butt of it. The daylight swam bloodred before the fisherman’s eyes. His head seemed to dilate and there was a singing in his ears. He fell forward, senseless, upon the ground.

===Chinese===

那一队人马在谢赫家门前停住，其余的兵兴高采烈地看着，一时笑他们的伙伴，一时笑那渔夫，一碗水端平。那笑声把抢东西的刺得性起。他从背上摘下马枪，隔着鞍桥探过身，用枪托朝萨义德的头狠狠砸了一记。渔夫眼前一片血红的日光晃荡。他的头仿佛胀大了，耳朵里响起一片尖啸。他脸朝前栽倒在地，不省人事。

### V / 五
===Original===

When Saïd again became conscious of his surroundings he was in the house of Muhammed abu Hassan, lying on a couch. Hasneh and another woman were bending over him. The latter drew her veil hastily across her face as his eyes blinked at her in bewilderment. Hasneh uttered a cry of delight.
Saïd looked about him wondering. Sullen, scowling faces filled the doorway, blotting out the sunlight. A sound of muttered oaths was in the room. Of a sudden he remembered all that had befallen him and staggered to his feet.
“I am ruined!” he cried. “They have taken my donkey﻿—all that I have. May Allah cut short their lives.”
Responsive curses came from the group in the doorway, and Muhammed replied﻿—
“We are sad for thee, effendi. The journey to Es-Shâm is long and wearisome for one that goes on foot. Yet art thou more happy than we. Thou wilt have the inheritance of thy brother who is dead. Thou wilt have wealth wherewith to buy horses and asses, as many as thou needest. But they have taken all that was ours. Curse their father! Of all our beasts there remain but a camel, and a mule which is on the point of dying.”
Saïd’s hand was pressed to his forehead. His face had the inward look of one reviewing things past. At length he asked eagerly, “What is the hour?”
“It is near the third hour since noon,” replied Muhammed after a brief glance at the shadow of his dwelling.
The fisherman turned to his woman. “Ready, O Hasneh?” he asked.
“Ready” was the meek rejoinder.
“But thou art yet weak from the blow which the soldier﻿—burn his house!﻿—gave thee,” Muhammed, as host, was bound to protest. “My house is thy house. Rest here till evening. The first hours of night are pleasant for travelling.”
But Saïd, remembering the words of Abdullah, was resolute. Pursuers might come upon him at any time. With profusion of thanks to Muhammed for his kindness he took up his staff and set out once more. Hasneh followed, her bundle poised upon her head.
They passed out from the village down a steep slope, where big red anemones shone amid ragged grass, across a stony wady with a trickle of water among the pebbles, and entered a grove of olive-trees. Here Saïd lay down in the shade. He was still dizzy from the stunning blow he had received, and the strength seemed to run out of his legs.

===Chinese===

赛义德再一回知觉到自己的境况时，他正躺在穆罕默德·阿布·哈桑家的一张卧榻上。哈斯娜和另一个女人俯在他身上。他茫然朝那女人眨眼，她慌忙把面纱拉上脸。哈斯娜欢喜地叫出声来。
赛义德诧异地四下张望。一张张阴沉愤怒的脸堵满门口，遮断了日光。屋里一片压低了的咒骂。他攜地记起了遭遇的一切，摇摇晃晃站起身。
「我完蛋了！」他喊。「他们牵走了我的驴——我的全部家当。愿安拉折他们的寿。」
门口那群人应声咒骂起来，穆罕默德答道——
「我们为你难过，埃芬迪。徒步走去沙姆，路又长又熬人。可你还比我们强。你还有亡兄的一笔遗产呢。你会有钱买马买驴，要多少买多少。我们的却给他们拿了个精光。咒他们的老子！牲口里就剩一峰骆驼，还有一头眼看要死的骡子。」
赛义德一只手按着额头，一脸内省的神气，像在回顾往事。末了他急切地问：「什么时辰了？」
「过午快三个钟头了，」穆罕默德朝自家的房影扫了一眼，答道。
渔夫转向他的女人。「好了么，哈斯娜？」他问。
「好了，」是那温顺的回答。
「可那士兵——烧了他的房子！——给你那一击，你还虚弱着呢，」穆罕默德身为东道，不得不挽留。「我的家就是你的家。歇到傍晚吧。夜里头几个钟头赶路最惬意。」
可赛义德记着阿卜杜拉的话，心意已决。追兵随时可能赶到。他对穆罕默德的种种好意千恩万谢，拉起行杖重新上路。哈斯娜顶着包袱跟在后头。
他们出了村子，走下一道陡坡——坡上凌乱的草丛间开着大片红艳艳的白头翁——又越过一条乱石的涸谷，卵石间淌着一线细流，便进了一片橄榄林。赛义德在荫凉里躺下。捾的那记闷棍还叫他头晕，力气仿佛正从两条腿里流走。

===Original===

He complained bitterly of thirst; whereupon Hasneh produced those oranges which had been thrown at them in the morning from the bosom of her robe. Having devoured two of them, Saïd wiped his dripping mouth upon his sleeve and felt refreshed. He was preparing to resume his way when the sound of a man’s voice close at hand stayed him.
“Praise be to Allah, who has placed such fools in the world! I asked for bread, and he gave me meat as well. And when I had finished eating he gave me money for my journey. A madman﻿—may Allah reward him!”
The sun through the leafage cast a chequer-work of golden light and blue shadow upon the ground. The speaker came towards them, walking slowly between the gnarled trunks, with eyes upturned. It was a hale old man of sixty years or more, tall and upright. His body was clad in a loose robe, whose colour had once been blue, reaching to a little below the knee. His bare feet and shins were grey with dust. Upon his head was a battered and tasselless fez, with a dirty rag wound round it by way of turban. Happening to let his eyes fall a minute from their heavenly contemplation, he became aware of the presence of fellow-creatures and his whole demeanour changed in a second. His form seemed to shrivel and grow less. His head sank down upon his breast, his eyes writhed upward so that only the whites of them were visible, and his whole body was distorted to a semblance of the last agony.
Stretching forth a trembling hand he besought the pity of his hearers for a poor old wretch who found himself alone and without money in a strange land.
“Allah will give to you!” he whined. “For the love of Allah, help me or I die!﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿… Allah will give to you!﻿ ﻿… By the Quran, I am at the gate of death!﻿ ﻿… Allah will give to you!﻿ ﻿… My sons were killed by the Bedawin; my daughters were ravished before my eyes!﻿ ﻿… Allah is bountiful!﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿… I myself have a hand that is withered!﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿… My house was destroyed by an earthquake; a thief came in the night and stole my mare from me!﻿ ﻿… Allah will give to you!﻿ ﻿… My children were slain before my eyes!﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿…”
It is likely that he would have gone on whining in this strain for an hour or more had not Saïd broken in﻿—
“Allah will give to thee! I am poor even as thou art. I, too, have been robbed and my house brought to ruin.

===Chinese===

他直喊口渴；哈斯娜便从怀里掏出早上人家扔给他们的那几只橙子。狼吞了两只，赛义德拿袖子一抹涪汁的嘴，觉得缓过来了。他正要重新赶路，近旁一个人的说话声留住了他。
「赞美安拉，祂竟在世上安放了这样的傻瓜！我讨的是饼，他连肉也给了我。我吃完了，他又给我盘缠。一个疯子——愿安拉报答他！」
日头透过叶隙，在地上铺下一格金光一格蓝影。说话的人朝他们走来，在虬曲的树干间慢慢踱着，眼睛仰望着天。是个六十开外的硬朗老头，高而挺直。身上一件宽松的袍子，颜色原本是蓝的，长及膝下。赤脚和小腿蒙着灰土。头上一顶瘪塌塌、没了缨子的菲斯帽，缠一条脏布权充头巾。他的眼睛偶然从上天的冥想里垂下片刻，发觉了同类在场，整个作派霎时变了。他的身形仿佛枯缩变小，头垂到胸前，两眼向上翻扭，只剩眼白露着，周身扭作一团，活脱是临终的惨状。
他伸出一只颤抖的手，恳请听者怜悯一个可怜的老东西——孤身流落异乡、身无分文。
「安拉必赏报你们！」他哀号。「看在安拉的分上，救救我，不然我就死了！……主啊！……安拉必赏报你们！……凭古兰经起誓，我已经到了死门关口！……安拉必赏报你们！……我的儿子们叫贝都因人杀了；我的女儿们当着我的眼被人糟蹋了！……安拉是大能的！……主啊！……我自己有一只手是枯的！……主啊！……我的房子叫地震震塌了；夜里来了贼，偷走了我的母马！……安拉必赏报你们！……我的孩子们当着我的眼被宰了！……主啊！……」
要不是赛义德打断，他这一路哀下去，怕要哀上一个钟头还不止——
「安拉必赏报你！我同你一样穷。我也遭了抢，家也毁了。

===Original===

I, too, was once a rich man, having flocks and herds, houses and vineyards, ay, and the half of a city belonging to me. And now there is no difference between me and thee. Allah will give to thee; I have nothing.”
In a twinkling the old beggar resumed his natural shape. His head rose, his body straightened, the pupils of his eyes came again into sight.
“Is it true?” he said in a friendly tone, squatting down in the shade beside the fisherman. “Then I tell thee thou art happy. All to gain; nothing to lose. There is no trade like ours. All the day long we cringe, we flatter, we weep, and none can resist us. And afterwards, when the evening is come, we laugh and are merry, with eating and drinking, with music and women. Behold, I love thee, for thy likeness to my son, Mansûr, who forsook me. I feel as a father toward thee. Is it a long time that ruin is upon thee?”
“But a few hours, O my uncle,” replied Saïd, bitterly.
The old rascal threw up his hands and cast his sly eyes skyward.
“Ah, it is sad at the first, and thou art downhearted﻿—it is natural. But after a few days﻿—a week﻿—a month, thou wilt not envy the greatest in the land.”
Saïd was not pleased to have his misfortunes thus lightly treated as part of the common lot of mankind. He made haste to explain.
“With another man it would have been a small thing. He would have lost a camel, or perhaps a house. But as for me, I was a great man﻿—the greatest in all the city. Men ran to kiss my robe as I walked abroad. I had camels and horses, asses and mules, more than a man can count in an hour. It is no common loss that makes me sad.”
“I suffer with thee,” said the beggar, with a reminiscent shake of his head. “I also was lord of great wealth. In those days men knew me by the name of Mustafa Bek. Now I am only Mustafa, the old beggar. Allah is greatest!”
But Saïd was not to be outdone.
“But yesterday men kissed the ground between my feet,” he said, with a shake of the head the counterpart of the other’s. “I was called the Emìr Saïd, and none dared come near me save with forehead to the earth. Allah is greatest!”
“I had twenty men whose only pleasure was to do my bidding,” said the beggar in his turn, “and the beauty of my three wives made the fair ones of Paradise jealous.”

===Chinese===

我也曾是个富人，牛羊成群，房屋连片，还有葡萄园，是啊，半座城都归过我。如今我同你没有两样。安拉必赏报你；我一无所有。」
一眨眼，老叫花子恢复了原形。头抬起来了，身子直起来了，瞳仁也回到了眼窝里。
「是真的么？」他用一种友好的口气说，在荫凉里挨着渔夫蹲下。「那我告诉你，你是幸福的。全是挣头，没有赔头。天底下没有比咱们这行更好的营生。一整天我们卑躬、谄媚、淌眼泪，谁也招架不住。到了晚上呢，我们又笑又乐，有吃有喝，有音乐有女人。看哪，我爱你，因为你像我那丢下我跑了的儿子曼苏尔。我拿你当儿子待。你败落多久了？」
「不过几个钟头，我的伯伯，」赛义德辛酸地答。
老无赖双手一扬，狡黠的眼睛朝天一翻。
「啊，起头总是难受的，你也灰心——人之常情。可过上几天——一礼拜——一个月，全地面顶大的人物你也不眼馋了。」
自己的不幸被人这样轻飘飘地归入人类的通例，赛义德不乐意了。他赶紧申明。
「换了别人，这算小事。丢一峰骆驼，顶多一座房子。可我呢，我是个人物——全城头一份的人物。我在街上走，人人抢着来吻我的袍角。骆驼、马、驴、骡，我一个钟头都数不过来。叫我不痛快的，不是寻常的损失。」
「我同你一同难受，」叫花子说着，怀旧地摇了摇头。「我也曾是一大注财富的主人。那些日子，人人知道我叫穆斯塔法贝克。如今我只是老叫花子穆斯塔法。安拉至大！」
可赛义德不肯服输。
「就在昨天，人们还吻我两脚之间的地，」他说，摇头的架势同方才那位一模一样。「人称我埃米尔赛义德，谁也不敢近我的身，除非额头贴地。安拉至大！」
「我有二十个汉子，唯一的乐事就是听我差遣，」这回轮到叫花子，「我三个老婆的美貌，叫天园里的仙女都眼红。」

===Original===

“All the men of the city were as slaves before me,” said Saïd; “and if I had a desire towards any girl, I had but to command her father and she was given to me.”
“And how wast thou deprived of all this?” asked his rival, curiously. “Such things do not fade away like stars at the sun’s rising. By Allah, they do not go out like a lamp for a puff of wind.”
“My city was by the seashore,” faltered Saïd, after a moment’s hesitation. “Last evening, at the hour of sunset, the waters rose and swallowed up all that was mine. I and this woman alone remain alive of all that were in the city.”
The beggar rose to his feet with a laugh.
“Thou hast yet much to learn, O Emìr,” he said scornfully, yet with a certain indulgence. “The sea rises not once in a hundred years, and then all the world knows of it. Yesterday, at the hour of sunset, I stood by the shore and beheld the sea calm and undisturbed as usual. Thou hast much to learn, my son.”
“May thy house be destroyed!” muttered Saïd, grinding his teeth with mortification. “How far is it to the next village, old man?”
“Perhaps an hour﻿—maybe an hour and a half﻿—Allah knows!﻿—perhaps two hours.”
“Who was that of whom thou wast speaking at the first?” asked Saïd with some eagerness. “He gave thee meat, thou wast saying, and money for thy journey. Doubtless it is some great one whose house is open to poor wayfarers?”
“I spoke but of a Frank who passed me in the way,” said the old man, with a chuckle at the recollection. “He was dressed all in black, and rode upon a fine horse. I knew him for one of those who preach to the Christians and would have all men believe in three gods. I saw him a long way off and, when he drew near, I flung myself down in the way, swearing horribly, and crying out that Allah had forsaken me. Thereat he got down from his horse and tried to comfort me with soft speaking and hard words from the book of his religion. But I cursed the louder and let him know that I was very hungry; whereupon he drew out a paper from his saddlebags, wherein was bread and meat, which he gave to me.
“When I had made an end of eating I began to weep and told him a grievous tale of how my house had been burned and all my children killed by Turkish soldiers. This I said knowing that a Frank loves always to hear evil of the Turks. He wept with me as he listened.

===Chinese===

「全城的男人在我跟前都同奴隶一样，」赛义德说；「我看上哪个姑娘，只消吩咐她父亲一声，人就归了我。」
「那这一切是怎么丢的呢？」他的对手好奇地问。「这种事不会像星星碰上日头那样说没就没。凭安拉起誓，它们也不像灯，一口风就吹灭了。」
「我的城在海边，」赛义德迟疑了片刻，支吾道。「昨天傍晚，日落时分，海水涨起来，把我的一切都吞了。满城的人，活下来的只剩我和这个女人。」
叫花子笑着站起身。
「你要学的还多着呢，埃米尔，」他不无鄙夷地说，却又带着几分宽容。「海一百年也涨不了一回，涨了也是天下皆知。昨天日落时分，我就站在海边，亲眼见海同往常一样平静无波。你要学的还多着呢，我的儿。」
「叫你的房子塌了才好！」赛义德嘨哝着，羞恼得咬牙。「到下一个村子还有多远，老头？」
「兴许一个钟头——兴许一个半——安拉知道！——兴许两个钟头。」
「你起先说的是谁？」赛义德带着几分热切问。「照你说，他给了你肉，又给了你盘缠。想必是位大户，宅门专向穷苦的行路人开着？」
「我说的是一个弗兰克人，路上打我跟前过，」老头说，想起那一幕嘿嘿一笑。「他一身黑衣，骑一匹好马。我一认便知，是那等向基督徒布道、要人人信三位神的家伙。我老远望见他，等他近了，就一头扑倒在路当中，满口毒咒，哭喊安拉丢弃了我。他听了便下马，拿好话安慰我，还拿他那教门经书里的硬话劝我。我却骂得愈发响，又叫他知道我饿得慌；于是他从鞍袋里抽出一个纸包，里头是饼和肉，给了我。
「我吃完了，便哭起来，给他编了一段惨事：房子如何烧了，孩子如何全叫土耳其兵杀了。我这么说，是因为知道弗兰克人顶爱听土耳其人的坏话。他听着，陪着我落泪。

===Original===

He gave me money﻿—as much as a man could earn by the labour of a week. Then he mounted and rode away, his face sad from the tale which I had told him. May Allah reward the unbelieving fool!”
“Y’Allah! Let us depart at once,” cried Saïd, eagerly. “Perhaps we may overtake him before the night.”
“Did I not tell thee that he rides upon a horse, and that a fine one?” said the beggar. “Thou canst never hope to overtake him. He told me that he was going two days journey on the way to Es-Shâm, to the place where he dwells. Whither goest thou?”
“To Es-Shâm,” cried Saïd, gleefully. “I will visit him and tell the tale of my great loss. Allah be with thee!”
Saïd set forward through the olive grove at a great pace, Hasneh shuffled after him with her usual docility﻿—the good beast of burden, ready to stand or go on at her master’s word. As for the beggar, he stood looking after them until they were lost to sight among the tree trunks. He chuckled often as he went his way, repeating the word “Emìr” with scornful emphasis.
Sunset fires were blazing high in the west when Saïd and Hasneh drew near to the village of which the beggar had told them. It was a small place, built of stone, crowning the utmost slope of the mountain seaward. To reach it they had to climb a pebbly road, which wound upwards serpent-wise among terraces of fig and olive-trees. At the entering in of the village grew a giant sycamore, about whose trunk the elders of the place were squatting in solemn conclave, smoking. Saïd saluted them politely as they drew near.
“What news?” asked a reverend sheykh, who seemed the head man of the place.
“There is war,” replied Saïd, with a low obeisance. “Soldiers scour the country for horses and mules. I know it well, alas! for they have taken my mare﻿—curse their fathers!﻿—a thoroughbred worth fifty Turkish pounds, by Allah!﻿—and I am forced to pursue my journey on foot.”
“Allah restore her to thee,” rejoined the sheykh, fervently. “We guessed that all was not well in the land, for this afternoon, as my son was ploughing on the hillside yonder, he beheld a company of soldiers ride across the plain, and many beasts of burden with them. Thanks be to Allah, we are warned in time. Ere the rising of the sun all our cattle shall be in a safe place among the hills, save a few that are sick, which they can take if it please them.”

===Chinese===

「他给了我钱——抵得上一礼拜的苦工。随后他上马走了，脸上还挂着我那段故事添的愁。愿安拉报答那个不信教的傻瓜！」
「呀，安拉！咱们这就走，」赛义德急切地喊。「兴许天黑前还追得上他。」
「我没告诉你他骑的是马，还是匹好马么？」叫花子说。「你休想追得上。他说他往沙姆方向还有两天路程，到他家去。你往哪儿去？」
「去沙姆，」赛义德喜洋洋地喊。「我要去拜访他，讲讲我这笔大损失。安拉与你同在！」
赛义德迈开大步穿过橄榄林，哈斯娜照旧温顺地趿拉着跟在后面——一头好驮兽，主人叫站就站，叫走就走。至于那叫花子，他站在原地望着他们，直到身影没进树干之间。他一路走一路频频窃笑，把「埃米尔」两个字翻来覆去地念，念得轻蔑至极。
赛义德和哈斯娜走近叫花子说的那个村子时，落日的火在西天烧得正高。那是个小地方，石头砌的，蹲在海那面山坡的顶上。要上去，得爬一条碎石路，路在无花果和橄榄的一层层梯田间蛇一般盘旋。村口长着一棵大西克莫无花果树，村里的长老们正围树干蹲成一圈，肃然议事，抽着烟。走近时赛义德客气地行了礼。
「有什么消息？」一位苍髯可敬的谢赫问，看样子是村里的头人。
「打仗了，」赛义德深深一躬，答道。「兵们在四乡搜马骡。我深知得很，唉！他们就牵走了我的母马——咒他们的老子！——凭安拉起誓，值五十土耳其镑的纯种！——害我只能步行赶路。」
「愿安拉把马还给你，」谢赫热诚地应道。「我们也猜到地面不太平：今天下午，我儿子在那边山坡上犁地，瞧见一队骑兵过了平原，带着好些驮畜。感谢安拉，我们得着信儿还来得及。太阳升起之前，我们的牲口都要转移到山里妥当的地方去，只留下几头病着的——他们乐意就牵走好了。」

===Original===

Saïd, seeking tidings of the missionary, was told that he had ridden through the place about the third hour after noon, and must be sleeping at Beyt Ammeh, a mountain village four hours distant.
“Is there a guest-chamber in this village where I and my woman may pass the night?” asked Saïd, in some anxiety.
“Thy news is timely and thou art welcome,” replied the sheykh. “My house is thy house. Deign to follow me.”
With that he rose and led the way to a house which was larger by a room than other houses of the village. This room was built on the roof and had the appearance of a tower when seen from a distance. Within, it was a small chamber, softly carpeted, with a cushioned divan running round the walls, destined for the lodging of guests of distinction. Saïd would never have been admitted to its precincts but for that fabulous mare of his worth fifty Turkish pounds.
Here, having partaken of a feast such as he had seldom enjoyed, he spent the night, a pale sky flaked with stars watching his slumbers through open door and lattice.

===Chinese===

赛义德探问那传教士的消息，得到的回答是：过午三个钟头前后他骑马穿村而过，此刻必定宿在拜特阿迈，一个四个钟头山路外的山村。
「这村里可有客舍，容我和我的女人过夜？」赛义德略带焦急地问。
「你的消息来得及时，欢迎你，」谢赫答。「我的家就是你的家。请随我来。」
说着他起身引路，到一座屋子去——比村里别家多出一间房。那间房盖在屋顶上，远远望去像一座小塔。房内是个小间，铺着软毯，四壁绕一圈垫褥的长榻，是预备安顿贵客的。若不是凭着他那匹值五十土耳其镑的神话般的母马，赛义德是断断进不了这重境地的。
在这里他受用了一顿平生少有的盛宴，过了夜；一片缀满星斗的淡色天空，透过敞开的门和窗棂，守着他的酣睡。

### VI / 六
===Original===

In the morning Saïd rose early, and having breakfasted and taken leave of his host, set forth with Hasneh in the cool twilight and started to climb the steep path which twisted among olive-trees up from the village. At the top he paused for a last look at the plain he was leaving. Away to the southwest a little promontory jutted into the sea. White buildings, a dome and two slender minarets were just discernible upon it in the pale light which comes before the sun. That was the city of his birth, and there, somewhere on the yellow rim of the bay, was his own little house with the fig-tree beside it from which he had seen the sun rise morning after morning, year after year. From where he now stood he could trace his whole course of the previous day. There, midway in the plain, on the crest of a wave of green, was the village where his donkey had been taken from him, where he had been stunned by that blow of the soldier’s carbine of which the very memory brought pain. He knew it from the other villages dotting the landscape by the three tall palm-trees tapering above its hovels, like rich plumes in a ragamuffin’s cap. There was the olive grove where he had spoken with the old beggar. And here, two hundred feet below, at the foot of a terraced slope so steep that it seemed easy to throw a stone down on to the roof of the sheykh’s house, was the village he had just left. His eyes ranged over the prospect, to return always to that white town upon the headland which was his birthplace. The sun rose upon the sea and the skirts of the plain, though the shadow of the mountains still darkened the near villages. Standing at the doorway of his home he would have been in the sunlight now. The thought gripped him by the throat. A sob from Hasneh told that her mind was straying in the same direction. Saïd’s voice was hoarse as he set forward once more, bidding her follow him.
The path dipped rapidly to the brink of a rocky gorge, and naked hills closed in upon them as they descended. To Saïd it seemed as if a door had slammed behind him, shutting off the past. His heart sickened for a while.
But the fresh air of the spring morning would not brook despair. In spite of himself hope came uppermost as he made his way along the rugged mountain side. The beggar’s words kept ringing in his ears: All to gain, nothing to lose! He could rob a man now without fear of reprisal.

===Chinese===

早晨萨义德起个大早，吃了早饭，辞别主人，趁凉爽的晨昏带着哈斯娜上路，开始爬那条从村里盘上来的陡路——路在橄榄树间绕来绕去。到顶上，他停下来，最后望一眼就要抛下的平野。往西南远处，一个小小的海岬伸进海里。白色的屋宇、一座穹顶、两支细细的宣礼塔，在日出前淡淡的光里刚刚能辨出。那是他生身的城；那儿，在海湾黄色边沿的什么地方，有他自己的小屋和屋旁的无花果树——在树下，他一年又一年、一个早晨又一个早晨看着太阳升起来。从他此刻站的地方，他能把昨天的全部路线描出来。那儿，平野中腰，一道绿浪的浪尖上，是那个他丢了驴的村庄，是那个他被兵的枪托砸晕的地方——那一下的记性至今还带着疼。四下点缀的村庄里他认得出它来，靠的是破屋之上三株高高削上去的棕榈，像破草帽上插的三根阔翎子。那边是橄榄树林，他跟老叫花子搭话的地方。而这里，两百尺底下，在一片陡得仿佛一抬手就能把石头丢上谢赫家房顶的坡田脚下，是他方才离开的村庄。他的目光扫过这片景致，总是又回到海岬上那座白城——他的生身之地。太阳升上海面和平野的边上，山的影子却还罩着近处的村庄。此刻要是站在自家门口，他已经晒着太阳了。这个念头扼住他的喉咙。哈斯娜一声抽噎，说明她的心思也正往那边跑。萨义德再开步时嗓音沙哑，喎一声叫她跟上。
路陡陡地落向一道岩石峡谷的边上，他们往下走，赤裸的山冈四面围拢来。在萨义德觉来，就像一扇门在身后砰地关上，把过去关在了外头。他的心空了一阵。
可是春天清晨的鲜气容不得绝望。他顺着崎岖的山腰一路走，希望不由自主地占了上风。叫花子那句话老在耳边响：全都是赚头，没什么可赔！如今他抢人，也不怕人报复了。

===Original===

He had all the world before him, and bright, keen wits, undulled by the least rust of conscience, for a sword against his fellow-man. He had nothing to lose, unless﻿—
A thought, which was almost a wish, flitted through his brain. He turned his head and let his eyes rest for a minute upon the form of Hasneh plodding patiently beneath her burden.
The shadows dwindled with every minute. The dew on the ground rose in steam wherever the sun’s rays touched it. For long they trudged on in a land of mountains barren and rocky. Overhead the deep blue sky paled about a blinding sun. Not a tree was to be seen. The distance swam before them in streams of heat. The sound of Hasneh’s breathing was like the panting of a dog at his heels. In the shade of a great rock they sat down to rest. All around them, between the boulders, anemones held out scarlet cups to the sun. Small pink flowers filled the crannies of the rocks. Here and there, from its clump of dark-green leaves, a tall spear of asphodel stood up, bristling with buds. Saïd eyed the scene with disgust as he mopped his forehead with one hand.
“By the Quran, it is hot today,” he muttered. “And there is no water until we come to Beyt Ammeh.”
Hasneh thrust a hand into her bosom and drew forth the few oranges which were left. Saïd seized one and devoured it greedily. A second went the same way. By the time his thirst was slaked but one remained, which Hasneh, despite the craving of her dry lips and throat, put back within her robe.
They set forward once more and had not made many steps before a man met them, asleep on the hump of a camel. Saïd called to him to know the way; whereat he awoke with a start, lost balance, and fell heavily on the stones by the wayside. He staggered to his feet, blood streaming from a wound in his forehead. Cursing bitterly, he caught up a big stone and hurled it at Saïd, who dodged it narrowly and, without waiting for further provocation, rushed on his assailant and closed with him. Hasneh shrieked loudly for help, wakening vain echoes. The camel, nose in air, chewed the cud placidly, as a wise man smokes his pipe, with a downward, supercilious glance at the fighters.
Victory did not hang long in the balance. Saïd was a tall man, lean and wiry, while his opponent was short and hampered with fat. The fisherman forced him backward until he tripped on a boulder and fell.

===Chinese===

整个世界摊在他面前，还有一副又亮又快的脑筋——良心的锈一星也没沾——好拿来当对付同类的刀。他没什么可赔的，除非——
一个念头，几乎算个心愿，从脑子里掠过。他回过头，把眼睛在那具人形上停了一分钟：哈斯娜驮着包袱，一步一步耐着性子挪。
影子一分一分缩小。太阳照到的地上，露水腾起白汽。他们久久的跋涉在一片荒山石海里。头顶上，深蓝的天绕着一轮晃眼的太阳淡下去。一棵树也望不见。远处热流在眼前游动。哈斯娜的呼吸声像一条狗喘在他脚后跟。在一块巨石的荫凉里，他们坐下歇脚。四下里，巨石与巨石之间，银莲花朝太阳举着猩红的杯子。粉色的小花塞满岩缝。这里那里，一杆高高的日影草从墨绿的叶丛里挺出来，顶着一穗毛刺刺的花苞。萨义德一只手抹着额头，一脸厌恶地打量这光景。
「凭古兰经起誓，今天真热，」他咕哝，「到贝特阿姆才有水。」
哈斯娜往怀里一探，捾出剩下的几个橙子。萨义德抓过一个，狼吞虎咽。第二个同样下场。等他解了渴，只剩一个了；哈斯娜嘴唇喉咙干得冒火，还是把它放回了怀里。
他们重新上路，没走几步，迎面来个人，正睡在骆驼的驼峰上。萨义德喊他问路；那人一惊醒来，失了平衡，重重摔在路边的石头上。他摇摇晃晃爬起来，血从额头的伤口淌下来。他狠狠地咒骂，抓起一块大石头朝萨义德掷去；萨义德险险躲过，不等再挨第二下，就扑上去跟袭击他的扭作一团。哈斯娜高声尖叫呼救，唤回的只是空谷回音。那骆驼鼻子朝天，安安静静倒嚼，像智者抽烟斗，眼睛朝下藐视着这两个打架的。
胜负没悬多久。萨义德是瘦长精悍的个子，对手又矮又拖累着一身肥膘。渔夫逼得他步步后退，绊在一块圆石上，摔倒了。

===Original===

Then he set foot on the belly of the fallen one and raised his staff to strike at the face of his enemy. Fury blazed in his eyes.
“Stay! may thy religion be destroyed!” panted the camel-driver in a rapture of fear. “What am I to thee that thou shouldest slay me? Thou art a devil to cause me to fall and then to destroy me! May thy father perish! Strike not; I am no enemy of thine! I never beheld thee till this hour!”
Saïd lowered his stick, but his brow was still clouded and his posture threatening.
“Take away thy foot!” gasped the other. “What have I done that thou dost so ill-treat me? All that I have is thine, only spare my life!”
Saïd did not budge.
“A man’s life is worth much,” he said thoughtfully. “How much wilt thou give me?”
“May thy whole race perish! I will give thee all that I have﻿—ten piastres.”
“Not enough.” Saïd’s foot pressed more heavily upon the mound of flesh.
“Twenty﻿—thirty piastres!” shrieked the man.
“Not enough.”
“A Turkish pound!﻿ ﻿… By Allah, it is all that I have. And it is my master’s money, not my own. Alas for me, I am ruined!”
Saïd withdrew his foot.
“Rise not until thou hast paid the ransom or I will slay thee,” he said savagely.
The man loosened his garment, showing a linen bag which hung by a string from his neck. Slipping the cord over his head he flung the bag to Saïd with a curse. The fisherman examined the contents in a kind of dotage, then nodded to the hostage.
“It is well,” he said. “Go in peace. And another time, when thou fallest by chance from thy camel, throw no stones at those who stand by lest a worse thing befall thee.”
Calling to Hasneh, he strode on his way with a light heart, leaving the camel-driver to digest the gall of his loss as best he might. They had gone some twenty paces when a noise of mighty cursing filled the air behind them. At the same moment a great stone came whizzing within a foot of Saïd’s head. Another struck Hasneh on the back, causing her to stagger and fall forward. Saïd girded up his loins and ran until he was beyond the utmost range of any missile. Then he got upon a rock and began to revile his assailant in a loud voice, using his hand as a trumpet. He watched the wretched man climb upon his camel again and heard the scream of rage and hate with which he turned to shake a fist at his plunderer.

===Chinese===

萨义德一脚踏上那堆倒地的肉，举起棍要朝仇人的脸砸下去。狂怒在他眼里烧。
「手下留情！叫你的教门败落吧！」赶驼人喘着气，怕到极点反带出一种兴奋。「俺于你算个什么，你要俺的命？你是个魔鬼——先把俺摔下来，再来害俺！叫你爹完蛋！别打；俺不是你的仇人！俺到这个时辰才头一回见你！」
萨义德放下棍，眉头还锁着，架势还吓人。
「把你的脚拿开！」对方喘着说，「俺做了什么，你这样糟践俺？俺的一切都归你，只饶俺一条命！」
萨义德不动。
「一条人命值大钱，」他若有所思地说，「你给俺多少？」
「叫你全家死绝！俺把俺所有的都给你——十个皮阿斯特。」
「不够。」萨义德的脚在那堆肉上压得更沉。
「二十——三十个皮阿斯特！」那人尖声叫。
「不够。」
「一个土耳其里拉！……凭安拉起誓，这是俺的全部了。这还是俺东家的钱，不是俺的。俺完了，俺毁了！」
萨义德收回脚。
「交清赎金再起来，不然俺宰了你。」他凶狠地说。
那人解开衣裳，露出一只麻布钱袋，用绳挂在脖子上。他把绳套褪下头，咒骂着把钱袋朝萨义德一摔。渔夫如痴如醉地清点里头的东西，然后朝那个人质点了点头。
「好，」他说，「走好。下回你从骆驼上摔下来，别朝旁边站着的人扔石头，省得遭更凶的报应。」
他喊上哈斯娜，心头轻快地大步去了，留那赶驼人自己咽那口损失的苦胆。他们走出去约莫二十步，背后忽然腾起一片震天的咒骂。同一瞬间，一块大石头嘕地贴着萨义德的头飞过，差不到一尺。另一块打中哈斯娜的背，打得她一个趔趄，脸朝前栽倒。萨义德勒紧腰身撒腿就跑，直跑到任何投掷物够不着的地方，才登上一块岩石，拿手圈在嘴边当喇叭，扯开嗓门大骂那袭击他的。他望着那倒霉人重新爬上骆驼，听见他回身朝抢劫他的人挥拳时那一声恨极怒极的尖叫。

===Original===

The fisherman laughed aloud and ceased not from insulting his enemy until a shoulder of the mountain hid camel and rider from sight.
Hasneh had struggled to her feet by this time and was making her way towards him, stumbling, one arm hugging her bundle, the other outstretched, like one walking in the dark. He cried to her to know if she were hurt. Her answer was in the negative, but faintly and without conviction. Saïd waited until she was within a few yards of him and then pursued his way, chuckling over his own cleverness in turning what had once seemed a misadventure to good account. The linen bag nestled lovingly to his chest, seeming to recognise a worthier owner.
All to gain, nothing to lose.﻿ ﻿…
He could no longer apply the words strictly to himself. Nevertheless, they rang hopefully in his ears, seeming to tell him that the sum he had just acquired was but an earnest of the wealth in store for him.
The sun was almost at the zenith when they came in sight of the village of Beyt Ammeh; for the great heat oppressed them and they walked slowly, taking frequent rests. The squat, flat-roofed houses were hardly to be made out at a distance, so little did they differ in form and colour from the surrounding rocks. Only a few ragged fig-trees and a thankless striving after cultivation in the immediate neighbourhood told of a dwelling-place of man.
On the outskirts of the village, just below the ringed threshing-floors, a spring gushed out beneath a ruinous arch by the wayside. Flat-topped stones had been placed in the shadow to serve as seats to wayfarers. Here Saïd stopped, and after a long, refreshing drink proceeded to bathe his head, hands and feet. Hasneh sank down upon a stone with hand pressed at her side, waiting patiently until her lord should have done with the water. Then she rose, took one step forward, staggered, and, with hands outstretched to the fountain, fell heavily upon her face.
For full three minutes Saïd stared down at her blankly. Such behaviour was quite beyond the cycle of his experience. At last he bethought him of the cold water and began to dash it over her wildly with both hands.
Then, as she did not move, he concluded her dead and sat down to try and get used to the notion. He was engaged thus, staring at the lifeless form of the woman at his feet, when a shadow darkened the ground before him.

===Chinese===

渔夫放声大笑，骂不绝口，直骂到一处山肩把骆驼和骑手都挡在背后看不见。
这时哈斯娜已经挣扎着爬起来，一步一挪地朝他走，跌跌撞撞，一条胳膊抱着包袱，一条胳膊朝前伸着，像黑地里走路的人。他喊问她伤了没有。回答是没有，声音微弱，没什么底气。萨义德等她挪到离自己几码远，就接着赶路，一边为自己的精明得意地笑——一场本来看着倒霉的遭遇，叫他做成了好买卖。那只麻布钱袋亲亲热热地贴在他胸口，像是认了个更配的主子。
全都是赚头，没什么可赔。……
这话如今用不到他自个儿头上了。可它还在他耳边响得欢，像告诉他，刚到手的那笔钱不过是囤在他前头的财富的一笔定钱。
他们望见贝特阿姆村的时候，太阳快到头顶了；酷热压得他们受不住，走得慢，歇得勤。那些矮墩墩的平顶房子，远看几乎认不出来，形状颜色同四下的岩石差不了多少。只有几株蓬乱的无花果树和紧邻处一片不见报答的垦植，说明那里有人烟。
村外，一圈打谷场底下一点，一股泉水从路边一道破拱底下涌出来。几块平顶的石头摆在荫凉里，给过路人当座。萨义德在这里停下，痛痛快快喝了个够，接着洗头、洗手、洗脚。哈斯娜跌坐在一块石头上，一只手抵着腰侧，耐心等她主人把水用完。随后她站起来，往前挪了一步，一个趔趄，两手朝泉眼伸着，脸朝下重重栽倒。
足足三分钟，萨义德木木地低头望着她。这样的行止完全超出了他经验的轮回。末了他想起凉水，开始双手狂乱地朝她身上泼。
见她还是不动，他断定她死了，便坐下来试着习惯这个概念。他正这么办着——盯着脚边那具没有生气的女人——一块影子暗了他面前的地。

===Original===

At the same moment a quavering voice asked to know what was the matter. Lost in reflection, Saïd had not heard the patter of feet drawing near.
Alarmed by the suddenness of the apparition, he leapt up with a curse. An old woman stood before him, bent almost double beneath a heavy burden. Her head nodded, her limbs quaked with palsy. Her jaw working like a camel’s, she repeated the question in a shriller tone as Saïd stared at her with wide-open eyes.
“It is my woman who is dead,” said the fisherman, ruefully, pointing to the ground.
“How dost thou know that she is dead?” asked the old hag, in scorn. “As I came out from the village I saw her fall, and would have run to help her but that I am very old and feeble. But I watched thee. Thou hast done nothing more than throw a little water upon her clothes. Turn her over, madman, so that she lies upon her back.”
Something in the manner of the old woman daunted Saïd and made him ashamed. He had not done much to revive Hasneh, it was true; but then, he had supposed her dead, and none but a fool would wantonly waste his time in trying to bring a dead woman back to life. He had now little doubt that she lived, thanks to the old woman’s scornful suggestions. In his heart he cursed the crone for breaking in upon him just when he had brought his mind to a peaceful contemplation of his wife’s dead body. Yet he obeyed her, and, lifting Hasneh in his arms, laid her down again, face uppermost.
“Now sprinkle water upon her lips!”
Saïd obeyed a second time, with the result that after a little while Hasneh opened her eyes.
“Take her up and bear her to the village! Thou hast no more mind than a donkey!” piped the hag, in shrillest scorn, seeing him stand purposeless.
The shame Saïd felt at having his actions ordered by a woman found vent in a hearty curse on her, her religion and all her belongings. Nevertheless, he did as he was bidden, and taking Hasneh in his arms entered the village, grumbling at every step.
At the threshold of one of the hovels, on the edge of the sunlight, sat a woman grinding at a small handmill. Saïd called to her that his wife had fallen sick and needed rest. She rose at once from her business to bid him enter and welcome. The darkness of the room within was refreshing after the scorching glare of noon. A man rose from a squalid couch against the wall and greeted Saïd in a sleepy voice.

===Chinese===

同一刻，一个发颤的声音问出了什么事。萨义德想得出神，没听见走近来的脚步。
这个突然出现的东西吓了他一跳，他咒骂着跳起来。面前站着个老太婆，差不多叫背上的重负压成对折。脑袋一点一点，四肢带着麻痹直抖。下巴像骆驼那样磨动着，见萨义德睁大眼瞪她，她又用更尖的嗓子问了一遍。
「是俺的女人死了。」渔夫指着地上，哭丧着脸说。
「你怎么知道她死了？」老虔婆不屑地问。「俺从村里出来，看见她栽倒，本想跑过去帮她，怎奈俺又老又衰。俺可是一直看着你。你不过往她衣裳上泼了点儿水。把她翻过来，疯子，叫她仰面躺着。」
老太婆的气派镇住了萨义德，也叫他臊得慌。要说救哈斯娜，他确实没出多少力；可他方才只当她死了，只有傻子才肯把工夫白糟践在救活一个死女人上。多亏老虔婆的讥点，他如今不大疑心她还活着了。他心里咒那老婆子——偏在他心平气和看着老婆尸首的当口来搅他。可他依了她，把哈斯娜抱起来，重新放下，脸朝上。
「往她嘴唇上洒水！」
萨义德第二次照办，过不多时，哈斯娜果然睁开了眼。
「抱她起来，抱进村里去！你这点心思还不如一头驴！」老婆子见他杵着不动，用最尖刻的鄙夷嚷。
被一个女人支来支去，萨义德臊出来的火气，一股脑发作成对她、对她的教门、对她全部家当的一通痛骂。可骂归骂，他还是照办了，抱起哈斯娜进了村，一步一步嘟囔着。
一座破屋的门槛上、日头的边沿里，坐着个推小手磨的女人。萨义德喊她说他老婆病倒了，要歇歇。她立刻摺下活计，让进屋里，说欢迎。里头的黑，在正午白晃晃的灼光之后，叫人一进去就爽。一个男人从墙边一张腌臜的榻上起来，睡意朦胧地跟萨义德见了礼。

===Original===

He waved a hand to the dirty mattress he had quitted, and then to the woman in the fisherman’s arms.
“May Allah increase thy wealth!” murmured Saïd, laying down his burden upon the bed.
“Leave a woman to the care of a woman,” said the man of the house, beckoning him to the doorway. “This woman of mine will tend her and, after a little, we will drink some coffee.”
Saïd squatted down beside his host, just within the shadow of the room. The outlook was of stony hills whitening under the burning noonday sky, and in the foreground the low mud roofs of the village in broken terraces.
“Whence comest thou?” asked the lord of the house, after a silence spent in the rolling and lighting of cigarettes. Saïd told him the name of the village where he had passed the night.
“Didst thou meet any man by the way?” he asked with sudden interest. “My brother﻿—his name is Farûn﻿—set out this morning on the road to the plain. He is a short man and very fat. He rides upon a camel laden with stone. Hast seen him?”
“Yes, I saw him,” replied Saïd, thoughtfully, as one recalling a picture to his mind. “He was sitting by the wayside and blood streamed from a wound in his head. His camel strayed browsing at a little distance. He told me that robbers had fallen suddenly upon him in the way. They had taken all that he had of money. They had beaten him with a stick and stoned him. I helped him to bind up his wound and gave him of my money﻿—all that I could spare. Then I saw him mount upon his camel and ride away. He bade me tell his brother what had befallen him when I should reach this village. The sickness of my woman had ousted it from my mind till now.”
“Now, may Allah requite thee, for thou art a good man and bountiful!” said the other, with eyes and hands upraised. “I hold thee as my near kinsman for this kindness done to my brother. My house is thy house. Rest here tonight, I pray thee. Tomorrow, about the third hour, my brother will return. Abide with us till then that he may thank thee once again. By Allah, I think he would slay me were I to suffer thee to go thy way unfeasted. Stay at least till the evening. Seeing the mishap which has befallen him it may well be he will return ere night. By the Quran, it is lucky that the robbers did not take his camel also!”
“I cannot stay,” said Saïd hurriedly. “My brother is dead in Damashc-esh-Shâm and I go to claim the inheritance.

===Chinese===

他摆摆手，指指自己刚离开的脏褥子，又指指渔夫怀里的女人。
「叫安拉增添你的财富！」萨义德喃喃着，把怀里的负担放上榻。
「女人交给女人照应。」屋主说着，招手叫他到门口去。「俺这个会伺候她；过一会儿，俺们喝咖啡。」
萨义德在主人身边、屋影的里口蹲下。望出去是正午烈天下发白的石山，近前是村里一层层破断的泥屋顶。
「你从哪儿来？」屋主搓烟、点烟的一阵沉默之后问。萨义德说了他过夜的那个村子的名字。
「路上遇见人没有？」他突然起了兴味。「俺兄弟——他叫法伦——今儿一早上了去平野的路。矮个子，一身肥膘，骑一头驮石头的骆驼。见着没有？」
「见着了。」萨义德答，若有所思，像往心里唤一幅画。「他坐在路边，血从头上一个口子往外淌。他的骆驼在不远处溜达吃草。他说半路上强人突然扑了他，把他身上的钱搜了个精光，还拿棍打他、拿石头砸他。俺帮他裹了伤，又送了他钱——尽俺所能。后来俺看他骑上骆驼走了。他嘱咐俺到了这个村，把他的遭遇告诉他兄弟。俺女人的病把它从脑子里挤了出去，直到这会儿。」
「哎呀，叫安拉报答你，你真是个好人、慷慨的人！」对方眼睛双手一齐举起。「冲你待俺兄弟这份好，俺拿你当近亲。俺的家就是你的家。求你今晚在这里歇。明儿，第三个小时上下，俺兄弟就回来了。住到那时候，好叫他再谢你一回。凭安拉说，俺要是叫你不受款待就上路，他能宰了俺。至少待到晚上。瞧他遭的那场祸，说不定天黑前就回来了。凭古兰经说，强人没把他的骆驼也牵走，真是万幸！」
「俺不能待，」萨义德慌忙说，「俺兄弟死在大马士革，俺去承产业。

===Original===

I must hasten on my way.”
“If not for thine own sake, for the sake of thy woman abide here till evening,” urged the host.
Saïd appeared wrapt in thought for some minutes. His face was moody with knitted brows. Of a sudden it brightened.
“For myself, I cannot stay,” he said. “But it were well for my woman that she should rest a while till the sickness leave her.﻿ ﻿…”
His eyes looked eager inquiry at the other.
“She is welcome and more than welcome!” cried the host, without hesitation.
“May Allah increase thy wealth!” murmured Saïd, fervently, making a low salaam. “When I come to the city I will send to fetch her, and thy reward shall be very great. Think not because thou seest me poorly clad that thou art showing kindness to a beggar. My brother was rich and I go to claim the inheritance.”
He glanced furtively towards the couch, in fear lest Hasneh should have heard anything of his speech.
