But her eyes were closed, and her bosom’s rise and fall was of one in a peaceful sleep, gentle and even. Her robe hung open at the neck showing something round and yellow nestling in the soft brown hollow between her breasts. It was the orange which she had forborne to eat that morning. The sight of it in the bosom of the sleeping woman warmed Saïd’s heart to something like pity. It was an appeal to his good nature, the stronger for being voiceless. For a moment his purpose was shaken.
“All to gain: nothing to lose!”
His heart hardened as he recalled the words of the old beggar. There was a glint of steel in his eyes as he turned them once more upon his host.
“It is past noon,” he said. “In thy grace I depart. Take care of the woman belonging to me and thy reward shall be great. May thy wealth increase!”
“My peace with thee!” said the man, staring at him with amazement. “But stay at least until thou hast drunk coffee with us. See! it is almost ready.”
Saïd dared not break the law of hospitality. He waited, fidgety, and ill at ease like one sitting upon a red-hot iron. He shifted his seat continually, and his eyes kept veering round to where Hasneh lay asleep, yet never looked at her. When at length a tiny cup of coffee was put into his hand he flung his head back and swallowed the whole contents at a gulp. Then he pressed both hands to his chest and his whole body writhed. He had forgotten in his haste to drink and be gone that the stuff was scalding hot.

===Chinese===

俺得赶路。」
「就算不为你自个儿，为你女人，待到晚上吧。」主人劝。
萨义德装出好几分钟的沉吟。他眉头锁着，一脸阴沉。忽然，脸亮了。
「俺自个儿是待不得，」他说，「可俺的女人呢，歇一歇、等病气散了，倒是大好。……」
他拿眼睛急切地探问对方。
「欢迎！加倍欢迎！」主人毫不迟疑地喊。
「叫安拉增添你的财富！」萨义德热烈地喃喃，深深行了个礼。「等俺到了城里，就打发人来接她；你的报答小不了。别瞧俺穿得寒磘，就把俺当叫花子待。俺兄弟有的是钱，俺是去承产业的。」
他偷偷朝榻那边瞟了一眼，生怕哈斯娜听见他哪句话。可她眼睛闭着，胸口一起一伏，是安睡的人的节奏，匀净温和。袍子在领口敞着，露出一点圆圆的黄的东西，卧在两乳之间柔软的棕色凹窝里。那是她今早忍着没吃的橙子。望着它卧在熟睡女人的怀里，萨义德的心头暖出了一点近乎怜惜的东西。那是一桩对他善性的无声陈情，正因为无声，力量更大。有一瞬，他的主意晃了晃。
「全都是赚头，没什么可赔！」
想起老叫花子这句话，他的心又硬了。他把眼睛再转向主人时，里面闪着一星铁光。
「晌午过了，」他说，「叨光，俺上路了。照应好俺的女人，你的报答小不了。叫你的财富增长！」
「俺送你平安！」那人瞪着他，一脸惊愕。「可至少喝了咖啡再走。瞧！这就好了。」
萨义德不敢破待客的规矩。他等着，坐立不安，像坐在一块烧红的铁上。他不停地挪座位，眼睛一遍遍转向哈斯娜睡着的地方，却从不看她。到末了，一小杯咖啡递到手里，他仰脖一口倒了个干净。随即两手死死按住胸口，浑身拧着疼——走得心急，忘了那东西是滚烫的。

===Original===

Tears streamed from his eyes, sweat stood in great beads on his forehead as he set down the empty cup and rose to take his leave.
“Thou art a fire-eater, by Allah!” cried the lord of the house, staring aghast at him, cup in hand. “Why art thou in so great a hurry? A minute or two will not rob thee of thy inheritance, and the heat of the day is not yet past.”
But Saïd was more eager than ever to be off. Glancing fearfully in the direction of the bed he had seen Hasneh open her eyes and stare vacantly about her.
“Take all care of her, and may Allah prosper thee!” he muttered hurriedly, crossing the threshold and dodging behind the doorpost. “After a week I shall send to thee. Allah requite thee, O father of kindness!”
He set off at a great pace, spurred by the thought that Hasneh might discover the trick played on her and come running after him.

===Chinese===

他眼泪直流，汗珠大颗大颗顶在额头上，放下空杯，起身告辞。
「凭安拉说，你真是个火性汉子！」屋主手拿杯子，瞠目结舌地喊。「你急的哪一门？一分钟能偷了你的产业去？白天的暑气也还没过去呢。」
可萨义德比先前更急了。他心惊胆战地朝床那边一瞟——他看见哈斯娜睁开了眼，正茫茫然四处张望。
「万般照应她，叫安拉成全你！」他慌慌张张咕哝着跨出门槛，闪到门柱后头。「过一礼拜俺打发人来。安拉报答你，大善人！」
他撒开大步走了，那个念头在后头抽着他：哈斯娜兴许看穿了耍她的把戏，会追着撵来。

### VII / 七
===Original===

At the village where he passed the night, a village halfway down a mountain side, terraced and fledged with olive-trees, which looked over a wide stretch of flat country, Saïd gleaned tidings of the missionary of whom he hoped so much. The man in black had ridden through the place before noon and was gone to his house in the plain, an hour’s journey beyond. His heart was light when he set out in the morning. Far away across the plain, mountains﻿—the hugest he had ever seen﻿—were dreamy in the mists of early dawn. A white gleam of snow among their summits was new to him, and would have held his eyes but for the nearer charms of a red-roofed house in the plain below, where a blessed fool dwelt and a man could have money for the asking. Thanks to the hospitality of the villagers, the Turkish pound was still untouched in the linen bag upon his chest. With what he hoped to obtain from the preacher he would enter the great city in triumph instead of beggary.
The sun was already hot upon the plain when he reached the house of the Frank. A tall negro, clad in a flowing robe of yellow and white, finely striped, with a clean white turban, bound about his scarlet fez, was sweeping the doorstep with a broom. Saïd wished him a happy day, and sitting down upon his heels﻿—for the ground was dewy﻿—disposed himself for a chat. But the negro was gruff. All Saïd’s compliments were returned as curtly as the barest politeness would allow, and his leading questions answered by an “Allah knows!” and a shrug of the shoulders far from satisfying.
Finding that there was nothing to be gained by flattering the surly doorkeeper, the fisherman changed his tone. Rising to his feet, he cried, in a loud voice, meant to sound like thunder, “Go, tell thy master that I wish to speak with him!”
The negro paused in his sweeping to look at him and laughed, showing two rows of dazzingly white teeth.
“My master sleeps,” he said. “Thou knowest little of the ways of a Frank if thou thoughtest to speak with him at this hour.”
“At what hour will he awake?” asked Saïd in the same lofty tone.
“Allah knows!” replied the negro, with a shrug, going on with his sweeping.
Saïd squatted down once more upon his heels.
“I wait here till he is ready!”
The negro grinned angrily and indicated the vastness of the horizon by a flourish of his broom.
“Walk!” he said grimly.
Saïd seemed not to understand.
“Walk!”

===Chinese===

他过夜的那个村子，坐落在半山腰，层层梯田，遍植橄榄，俯瞰着一大片平川。就在那里，赛义德打听到了他寄予厚望的那位传教士的消息。那黑衣人午前骑马穿村而过，回他平原上的家去了，再走一个钟头就到。
清晨动身时他心里轻快。平原尽头远远地立着群山——他生平仅见的庞然大物——在黎明的雾霭里如梦似幻。峰顶间一抹雪光是他没见过的，本要勾住他的眼睛，只是下面平原上一座红顶房子有更近的诱感——那里住着一个有福的傻子，肯白给人钱。多亏村民们的款待，他胸口亚麻袋里那枚土耳其镑还没有动。加上指望从传教士那儿到手的，他进那座大城时便可以凯旋，而不是去讨饭。
他到达那弗兰克人的宅子时，平原上的太阳已经很毒了。一个高个黑奴，穿一件黄白细条纹的宽袍，猩红的菲斯帽上缠着雪白的头巾，正拿扫帚扫台阶。赛义德向他道了日安，随即蹲坐到脚后跟上——地上还带着露水——摆开了闲聊的架势。可那黑奴言语生硬。赛义德的恭维话，句句只换来最低限度礼貌之内的简短回敬；他套话的问句，得到的不过是「安拉知道！」加一耸肩膀，远不能解渴。
见奉承这阴门的看门人无利可图，渔夫换了一副腔调。他站起身，放出一副雷鸣似的高声喝道：「去，告诉你家主人，我有话同他讲！」
黑奴停下扫帚瞧他，笑了，露出一口白得晃眼的牙。
「我家主人睡着呢，」他说。「你要是以为这个时辰能同他说话，可见你不懂弗兰克人的规矩。」
「他什么时辰醒？」赛义德用同样倨傲的腔调问。
「安拉知道！」黑奴耸耸肩答道，继续扫他的地。
赛义德又蹲坐到脚后跟上。
「我就在这儿等他起来！」
黑奴愠怒地一咧嘴，扫帚一挥，指了指天地的辽阔。
「走！」他冷冷地说。
赛义德像没听懂。
「走！」

===Original===

repeated the negro, fiercely, rushing upon him with broom upraised.
With a scared curse Saïd scrambled to his feet and bounded away, swift as a gazelle in fear of the hunter. The negro stood looking after him, his bosom still threatening, until the flutter of a blue robe and the twinkle of brown legs were lost to sight among the knotted trunks of an olive grove.
As soon as he thought himself safe Saïd flung himself upon the ground, panting for breath. A pair of doves fluttered somewhere among the branches, cooing sadly over a lost paradise. The sunlight made its way here and there through the leafage in bars of golden haze. A sound, made up of the barking of a dog, the cries of children and the musical clink of a hammer on iron, told him that there was a village somewhere in the depths of the wood. The grating song of the cicadas, that waxed and waned in his throbbing ears, seemed the live spirits of the sunlight stirring in the shade. Warm breaths, the sweet steam from dew-drenched plants and moistened earth, rustled the leaves and silvered them faintly.
“May his father perish!” muttered Saïd between his clenched teeth﻿—a sign that his breath was returning.
A little later, when he had ceased panting, he crept to the edge of the sunshine. Keeping his body hid behind the widespread trunk of an ancient olive he peeped forth.
At a stone’s throw the house of the missionary rose sheer amid a waste of rank grass and thistles traversed by a bridle-path. Beyond rose the mountain side, filmy in a bluish heat-mist. Halfway up Saïd descried the place where he had slept, a cluster of low buildings of the same hue as the neighbouring rocks, seeming as natural a growth as they.
The negro had left the doorway ere this, and was gone out of sight to some other place where was need of his broom. But Saïd dared not yet step forth into the open, an impression of the black man’s strength of limb and the broom’s menace being fresh upon him. He watched and waited.
Soon there were signs of a stirring to life within the house. The shutters of an upper window were closed against the sun by an arm thrust out for the purpose. At the same time a man’s head was seen for a moment. Then a little boy with thin brown legs came out of the olive wood, passing close to Saïd but without seeing him.

===Chinese===

黑奴又喝一声，凶猛地高举扫帚扑过来。
赛义德骂了一声丧气的咒，爬起来蹿了出去，快得像怕猎人的瞪羚。黑奴站在原地望着他跑远，胸膅还在起伏，直到一袭蓝袍的翻飞和两条棕腿的闪动没进橄榄林虬结的树干之间。
自觉到了安全地带，赛义德扑倒在地上，直喘。一对斑鸠在枝头某处扑腾，咕咕地哀悼着失去的乐园。阳光这里那里透进叶隙，化成一杠一杠金色的薄雾。一阵声响——狗吠、孩子的叫喊、铁锤打铁的叮当有如乐音——告诉他林子深处还有个村庄。蝉聒噪的长吟在他嗡嗡作响的耳朵里涨了又落，仿佛是阳光的精灵活在了荫凉里。一股股暖息，从饱蘸露水的草木和润湿的泥土里蒸出的甜香，簌簌地掀动树叶，又给叶面淡淡镀上一层银。
「叫他老子完蛋！」赛义德咬着牙嘨哝——这说明他的气缓过来了。
又过了一会儿，喘定了，他爬到阳光的边上。把身子藏在一棵老橄榄张开的主干后面，探头张望。
一掷石之遥，传教士的宅子拔地而起，立在一片疯长的荒草和蒠丛中间，一条骑马小径横贯而过。再往上是山坡，笼在蓝色的暑霭里，朦朦胧胧。半山腰上，赛义德认出了他过夜的地方——一片低矮的屋舍，同四近的岩石一个颜色，仿佛也是从那里长出来的。
黑奴这时已离开门口，到别的什么用得着他扫帚的地方去了，不见踪影。可赛义德还不敢走到明处——黑人的膂力和扫帚的威吓在他印象里还新鲜着。他望着，等着。
很快，屋内有了活泛起来的迹象。一扇楼窗里伸出一条胳膊，把窗板拉上挡太阳，同时一颗男人的头露了一露。随后一个细腿细胞脚的棕色小男孩从橄榄林里出来，打赛义德身边经过，却没有看见他。

===Original===

He must have come from the village near at hand for he carried a big pitcher of milk easily and without fatigue. He passed round a corner of the house, and shortly returned swinging the empty pitcher. Windows were opened. A shrill Arab chant in a woman’s voice came from some lower room. How many servants had this accursed unbeliever? Saïd wondered.
Presently, just as he was thinking of trying his luck once more, the negro being nowhere to be seen, a tall Frank, clad all in black save his arms, which were in white sleeves, appeared in the gloom of the doorway and shouted, “Cassim!”
Saïd had taken a step forward, with intent to rush across the intervening space and fling himself at the blessed madman’s feet, when the reappearance of his enemy made him shrink back. The man in black seemed to be giving an order, to which the negro bowed assent. Then Saïd saw the Frank reenter the house, while the servant ran round to the back of the building.
The coast was clear once more. But the second coming of the negro to thwart him had made Saïd cautious. Choosing what he deemed the wise man’s part, he watched still and waited. But after a few minutes the negro returned, leading a handsome grey stallion by the bridle, when Saïd had the vexation of seeing the missionary mount and ride away. His parting charge to the black servant, shouted as the restive horse broke into a canter, reached Saïd’s ears distinctly through the still, sounding air.
“I return at sundown, O Cassim! Tell the people there will be no school today!”
The negro stood awhile looking after the horseman. Then he turned and, going about his business, passed once more out of sight.
Saïd flung himself down in the deep shadow behind his tree trunk, calling down every ill he could think of upon the Frank and all his race. The tall negro also was not forgotten in that all-embracing curse, nor his father, nor his grandfather; not so much as an aunt or a cousin was left out. Then, feeling better, he began to sound the depths of his disappointment.
From the time of his meeting with the old beggar he had looked to the bounty of the Frankish missionary as a traveller in the waste looks forward to the place of waters. He snarled as he thought that he might have gained his end and gone rejoicing on his way but for the selfish devil that kept the door, who guarded the well for his own use.

===Chinese===

他准是从近旁的村子来的，捧着一大罐奶，轻松省力。他绕过屋角去了，不多时晃着空罐回来。窗子开了一扇。一个女人的尖亮嗓音在唱阿拉伯调子，出自楼下一间屋。这遭瘟的不信教者到底用着多少仆人？赛义德纳闷。
正在这时——他正想着趁黑奴不见再碰一回运气——一个高个弗兰克人出现在门口的暗处，一身的黑，只有两条胳膊罩着白袖子，喊道：「卡西姆！」
赛义德本已迈出一步，打算冲过那片空地、扑倒在那有福的疯子脚下，冤家的再度露头又把他吓得缩了回来。黑衣人像是在吩咐什么，黑奴躬身领命。随后赛义德见那弗兰克人回屋去了，仆人绕到楼后。
道上又空了。可黑奴二度出来揽局，叫赛义德学乖了。他捾了自以为智者的路数，仍旧观望等待。可没几分钟黑奴回来了，牵着一匹神骏的灰牡马；赛义德眼睁睁懊恼地看着传教士上马离去。那匹烈马撒开小跑时，他朝黑仆回头喊的临别嘱咐，穿过静止清朗的空气，字字清晰送进赛义德耳朵。
「日落我就回来，卡西姆！告诉众人，今天不开学堂！」
黑奴站了一阵，目送骑马的人。然后他一转身，忙他的活计去了，重又没了踪影。
赛义德扑倒在树干后浓深的荫影里，把想得出的一切灾祸都咒到那弗兰克人和他全族头上。这场无所不包的咒骂里，那高个黑奴也没有被忘掉，连带他的父亲、他的祖父；连一位姑母、一个表亲也没有漏网。骂罢，觉得痛快了些，他开始掂量自己失望的深浅。
打遇见老叫花子那时起，他就把指望放在这弗兰克传教士的慷慨上，像荒漠里的旅人盼着水泽。他想到本可以到手了心愿、欢欢喜喜上路，全坏在那个守门的自私魔鬼手里——那口井他自己霸着用——不由得咬牙切齿。

===Original===

Now he must leave the place as he had come, with only a single Turkish pound in the linen bag against his chest. It was nothing beside what he had hoped to get from the mad preacher of unbelief. He had no mind to stay there till nightfall on the slender chance of eluding the watchfulness of the negro and winning the ear of his master. The city called him with a siren’s voice. There, in the vast bustling hive, were wondrous chances for a young man and a strong who had nothing to lose. There were women fairer and sweeter than Hasneh﻿—young girls, perhaps, pure as lily buds, who would tremble and wax faint at a kiss. He licked his lips softly.
A sound of footsteps close at hand startled him out of a languorous dream. It was the negro, who, unobserved of Saïd, had crossed the open space of sunlight and was threading his way among the gnarled trunks of the grove, a large basket on his arm. He passed within twelve paces of the fisherman, but without perceiving him, so still he lay.
Then a thought came to Saïd. Now that the enemy was gone what was to hinder him from entering the house and viewing for himself the splendour which must assuredly reign within? From all he had seen and heard during his long watch it was unlikely that the unbeliever had more than one manservant. There would be none but women in the house; and if one of them should surprise him and ask what he did there, he had only to tell her of his wish to speak with the Frank, her master. He stole from his lair and stepped out into the sunlight.
The silence of the place, with all those windows gazing so fixedly at him, was a little daunting at first, so that he advanced warily. It seemed as if a shout must come from the open door, which looked so like a mouth. But when he had made a few paces unchallenged courage returned to him. The Arab chant he had before heard came faintly from some room at the back. But for that, and a great cat blinking to sleep on a windowsill, the place seemed desolate.
Slipping off his shoes on the doorstep he passed swiftly into the cool gloom within. There was a sort of hall, wide and lofty, having two windows, one on either side of the entrance. Upon a table in the midst of it lay the remains of a feast﻿—broken bread and meat, a plate of oranges and a bowl half empty of curds, besides a great cup and saucer and two white jugs of an outlandish fashion.

===Chinese===

如今他只能像来时那样离开，胸口亚麻袋里仍旧只有孤零零一枚土耳其镑。比起指望从那不信教的疯传道人手里得到的东西，这算得了什么。他无意守到天黑，单为一线渺茫的机会——躲过黑奴的耳目、巴上他主人的耳朵。那座城正用海妖的嗓音唤他。在那里，在那座熙熙攘攘的巨大蜂巢里，对一个一无所有、输得起的好汉青年，有的是奇妙的机缘。那里有比哈斯娜更白更甜的女人——兴许还有少女，纯得像百合的蓓蕾，一吻便要发颤、便要晕去。他轻轻地舐了舐嘴唇。
近旁一阵脚步声把他从酥软的迷梦里惊醒。是那黑奴，趁赛义德不留神，已穿过明处的阳光，胳膊上捥着个大篮子，正穿行在林中虬曲的树干间。他打离渔夫十二步远的地方过，却没发觉他——他躺得纹丝不动。
随后赛义德起了一个念头。冤家既然走了，还有什么拦着他进屋去，亲眼看看里面必定摆着的那份富丽？从长久的守望中见闻的一切看来，那不信教者未必雇着一个以上的男仆。屋里剩下的该只有女人；倘有哪个撞见他、问他在此做什么，他只须说想同她主人弗兰克人说话便是。他溜出藏身之地，跨进了阳光。
这地方的岑寂，连同那一扇扇窗定定瞪着他的样子，起初叫他有点发怵，走得小心翼翼。那扇敞开的门活像一张嘴，仿佛随时要喊出声来。可走了几步没人喝止，胆气又回来了。先前听见的阿拉伯调子从后面哪间屋里隐隐传来。除了它，和一只在窗台上眨着眼睡去的大猫，这地方像荒了似的。
他在台阶上蹭掉鞋，一闪身进了屋里清凉的幽暗。有一间厅堂模样的屋子，宽而高，开着两扇窗，进口两侧各一。当中一张桌上摆着一席残宴——掰碎的饼和肉，一盘橙子，一只凝乳吃剩半碗的碗，另有一副大杯碟和两把异邦样式的白水壶。

===Original===

Facing him, beyond the table, were two doors, both shut, from behind one of which the sounds of chanting seemed to proceed. He stole past the table, his bare feet making no noise on the stone pavement or the matting which was over part of it. There was a stairway in a recess to the right. He mounted swiftly and stealthily.
At the top an open door attracted him. It showed a room with a bed in it and soft rugs upon the floor. Saïd went straight to the bed and fell to examining its framework, sitting on his heels and exclaiming, “Mashallah!” under his breath. It was almost like a table standing on six iron legs; but four of the legs reached above it as well as below, and each of the four was crowned with a little knob, like an orange, of some burnished yellow metal he took for gold. A wonderful thing! It was long ere he could tear himself away from the marvel.
The room was cool and pleasant, shaded from the sun, which beat on that side of the house, by the shutters of the window, which were closed. Upon a small table there was a mirror. He saw his counterpart for a minute without recognition. Then he grinned, and scanned the face in the glass with complacency. From a peg beside the door hung a long garment of brown stuff, soft as wool, yet thick and strong as if it had been of camel’s hair. It was braided with red at the collar and on the sleeves, and a red cord dangled from a loop in the middle, ending in two red tassels. Above it, on a nail, was a scarlet fez, of the high shape worn by Turks and great ones.
Saïd took off his own cap and the encircling turban which old ties of dirt and perspiration had made of one piece with it. The back of his shaven head, thus laid bare, was reflected in the looking-glass, the ears standing out from it huge and grotesque as those of a jinni. He eyed his ancient headdress with disgust. The round tarbûsh, shaped like the half of a pomegranate, with its clumsy tassel which had once been blue, appeared a sorry thing indeed as he looked from it to the new scarlet of that other cap. His raiment, too, was old and stained, in need of a cloak to hide its shortcomings. Taking down the brown robe from the wall he turned it about and about, seeking the holes for the arms. Then he slipped into it and, setting the scarlet fez upon his head, went back to the mirror.
He noticed a fault.

===Chinese===

正对着他、在桌子那头，是两扇门，都关着，唱歌的声音像是出自其中一扇的后头。他蹑着脚绕过桌子，赤脚踩在石地上、踩在半屋的席垫上，没有一点声响。右手一个壁龛里有楼梯。他迅捷无声地上了楼。
楼梯顶上一扇敞开的门引住了他。门里露出一间屋子，摆着一张床，地上铺着软毯。赛义德径直奔到床前，蹲在脚后跟上研究起床架来，压着嗓子连连惊叹：「真主啊！」它简直像一张立在六条铁腿上的桌子；其中四条腿既往下立、又往上伸，四根柱头各顶着一颗小小的圆球，像橙子，是某种擦得锃亮的黄金属——他认定是金子。了不得的东西！好半天他才舍得从这奇观前挪开眼。
屋子凉爽宜人；日头正打宅子这一面晒着，窗板关着，把它挡在外头。一张小桌上有一面镜子。他一时没认出镜中那个是自己。随即他咧嘴笑了，得意地端详镜子里那张脸。
门边一个挂钩上垂着一件褐色的长袍，软得像羊毛，却又厚实结实，仿佛骆驼毛织的。领口和袖口镶着红绦，当中一个袢环垂下红绳，绳头两个红缨。它上头一颗钉子上，挂着一顶猩红的高筒菲斯帽——土耳其人和大人物戴的那种高样式。
赛义德摘下自己的帽子，连同缠在上面的头巾——经年的汗垢早把它们结成了一体。剃光的后脑勺这样露了出来，映在镜子里，两扇耳朵支棱着，又大又怪，活像精灵的耳朵。他嫌恶地打量自己那顶古旧的头衣。那顶圆塔布什，形状像半个石榴，缀着累赘的缨子——缨子原先是蓝的——再同旁边那顶猩红新帽一比，实在寒碜。他的衣裳也旧了、渍了斑，正需要一件外袍遮丑。他把褐袍从墙上取下，翻来覆去地找伸胳膊的洞。钻进去，再把猩红菲斯帽往头上一扣，回到镜子前。
他看出一处毛病。

===Original===

The fez, being used to cover a thick crop of hair, was too large for his shorn poll. His ears alone prevented it from putting out the light of his countenance. He cast about for a remedy. There was upon the table a small white cloth or kerchief of finest linen. This he made to serve his turn by twisting it tight round cap and forehead as a turban. That done, he grinned freely and examined other objects upon the table. Among them was a picture of a girl, clad indecently after the manner of the Franks. Saïd eyed it closely, wondering what purpose it could serve. Then he remembered that the Franks are but idolaters, who worship pictures and other forbidden things of their own making. “It is his god, by Allah!” he muttered, turning away with a gesture of disdain. Before leaving the room he cast his discarded headgear upon the bed with a parting curse on its religion.

===Chinese===

那顶菲斯帽原是扣一头浓发的，扣他这剃光的脑袋便太大，全靠两只耳朵才没把他的脸整个吞没。他寻思补救。桌上有块小白布，或是块细亚麻手绢。他把帽子和额头用它在头上紧紧缠成一道头巾，正好顶事。缠罢，他咧嘴尽笑，又打量起桌上别的物件。其中有一幅画，画个姑娘，照弗兰克人的路数穿得不成体统。赛义德凑近端详，纳闷这东西能顶什么用。随即他想起，弗兰克人本就是拜偶像的，供奉画像和自己造的种种禁物。「这是他的神，凭安拉起誓！」他嘨哝一声，鄙夷地一甩手转过身去。出屋之前，他把弃置的旧头巾丢在床上，还捎带咒了它的宗教一句。

### VIII / 八
===Original===

As Saïd was making his way downstairs, with less of caution than he had observed in his ascent (the joy of his new finery had elated him beyond all prudence), a door was opened in the hall below and a woman came out. Beholding him she drew her veil hastily across the lower part of her face. Her eyes were bright and her movements had the grace of youth.
“Who art thou? What dost thou here?” she cried shrilly. “The *khawaja* is on a journey and Cassim is gone to the village. I am alone in the house, the old woman, my mother, being ill. If perchance thou hast an errand to my master I can give word to him on his return.”
Of a sudden her voice rose to anger.
“Allah, pardon! Where gottest thou that cloak? Thief that thou art! It is the robe of my lord, which hangs always in his own chamber. O Cassim, there is a thief in the house! A thief! O Cassim, a thief!”
She ran screaming to the outer door and opened her mouth wide towards the olive grove, crying always, “O Cassim! O Cassim! A thief! a thief!”
Saïd rushed on her and pinioned her arms.
He tried to fling her to the ground, but she struggled like a mad thing, and at length, bending swiftly, with the yell of a wild beast, bit the fisherman’s hand so that he cried out with pain. Need to look at the wound made him loose his hold, whereupon she broke away and fled within the house, barring a door behind her.
Saïd frowned at the marks of her teeth in his flesh, from which the blood began to ooze. He put the place to his mouth and sucked it﻿—an act which prevented a storm of curses. And even as he was tending his wound in such a manner as Nature prompted, the screams of the woman broke out anew, as of one in a frenzy﻿—
“O Cassim! Help! a thief! O Cassim! O Cassim!”
This time there came an answering shout from the olive grove.
Turning, he beheld the negro running towards the house as fast as his long black legs could carry him. Saïd snatched up his slippers from the doorstep. With the spring of a hunted animal he leapt out into the sunlight, and gathering up his new robe, sped away from house and olive-trees, out into the wide plain, where hot air swam along the distance in liquid mist.
Once he turned to look back. The negro had set down his basket and was pursuing at a steady trot which meant business. Saïd fled on, but with slackened pace. He had need to husband his breath, for the race was like to be a long one.

===Chinese===

萨义德下楼梯时，比上来时少了那份小心（新行头的那份欢喜叫他得意得没了分寸），楼下的厅里一扇门开了，出来一个女人。她一眼看见他，慌忙把面纱掩上脸的下半截。她眼睛明亮，举止带着青春的风致。
「你是谁？在这儿干什么？」她尖声喊，「霍瓦贾（khawaja，老爷）出远门了，卡西姆又上村里去了。家里就俺一个人，俺娘那老婆子病着。你要是有什么话捎给俺主人，等他回来俺可以带到。」
忽然她的嗓门蹿成了怒骂。
「安拉饶恕！你那件斗篷哪儿来的？你这贼！那是俺老爷的袍子，一向挂在他自己房里的！卡西姆，家里有贼！有贼！卡西姆，有贼啊！」
她尖叫着奔向外门，朝橄榄林那边张大了嘴，一路喊：「卡西姆！卡西姆！有贼！有贼！」
萨义德扑上去，把她两条胳膊死死锁住。
他想把她掀翻在地，可她像疯了一样挣扎，末了猛一低头，野兽似的尖啸一声，咬了渔夫的手一口，痛得他大叫。要看伤口，他不由松了手；她趁势挣脱，逃进屋去，反手闩上了一扇门。
萨义德皱眉瞅着肉里那圈牙印，血开始渗出来。他把那处凑到嘴上吜——这一吜，堵住了一场咒骂的暴雨。他正照着天性的指点料理伤口，那女人的尖叫又炸开来，像发了狂——
「卡西姆！救命！有贼！卡西姆！卡西姆！」
这一回，橄榄林里有人应了一声。
他回头，望见那黑奴两条长长的黑腿能带多快就多快，直朝屋子奔来。萨义德从门槛上抄起拖鞋，像被追猎的牲口那样一纵，蹿进日光里，摺下新到手的袍子，撇下房屋和橄榄树，直奔宽阔的平野——那儿，热气像液体的薄雾顺着远处游动。
他回头望了一眼。黑奴摞下篮子，迈着说一不二的小跑追上来了。萨义德接着逃，却放缓了步子。他得省着点气，这场赛跑看样子短不了。

===Original===

Panting, sweating from every pore, he stumbled across a wady where a little freshet of water tinkled among boulders from pool to pool. Brushing through the belt of oleanders on the further bank, he ran on across the bare land, trampling rank grass, thistles and creeping plants.
But the negro had long legs. Saïd learnt, by the growing beat of footfalls in his ears, that he was losing ground. Soon he could hear also the hard breathing of his pursuer. He made a spirt, though his heart was near to breaking, it thumped so against his ribs.
“Allah is merciful!” He had almost fallen into a deep hole, overgrown with weeds at the mouth﻿—a disused cistern, it might be. He had lengthened his stride only just in time. A piteous shriek came from behind him. He turned to glance back, still running. The black was nowhere to be seen. He dropped to the ground, pressing his hand on his heart. “Praise to Allah!” he gasped, and then lay still, panting.
The sun beat hotly upon him there in the open plain. He longed for some patch of shade, were it but of a shrub, enough to shelter his head and face. Only a few paces distant a lonely carub-tree of great size spread its gnarled boughs and glossy dark foliage over a rough pavement﻿—a pious foundation for the repose of travellers. Saïd dragged himself thither and lay a great while with eyes closed.
“Praise be to Allah!” he exclaimed again, when breath had quite returned. Then he bethought him of the black man and that the hole might be of no great depth after all. He rose and went to the place.
While he was searching among weeds and dwarf shrubs for the mouth of the pit he saw a black hand come up out of the ground and clutch the stalk of a big blue thistle. Then he regretted bitterly that he had flung away his staff lest it should prove a hindrance in running. For want of it he took a jagged stone in his hand and beat viciously with it upon the bony parts of the fingers. The desired yell at once reached his ears, and the hand was nimble as a lizard to slip back into its hole. Then Saïd, lying flat upon his stomach, wriggled forward until he could look down into the prison. There was his enemy standing upright in a narrow place like a well, but dry to all appearance. By stretching down his arm he could almost have touched the negro’s white turban. Cassim glared up at him with white eyes of hate.

===Chinese===

他喘着，浑身每个汗毛孔都在淌水，踉跄着蹚过一道旱谷——谷里一线细流在一块块圆石之间从水洼叮咚到水洼。他钻过对岸那带夹竹桃，接着在裸地上跑，踩倒茂草、蓟草和爬蔓的藤。
可黑奴的腿长。脚步声在耳朵里越擂越近，萨义德明白自己吃了亏。不多久他连追赶者的粗气都听得见了。他又冲了一阵，心几乎要碎——在肋骨上撞得那么响。
「安拉是慈悲的！」他险些栽进一个深洞，洞口叫野草封着——兴许是口废了的蓄水井。他好在及时收大了步子。身后传来一声惨叫。他边跑边回头看。黑影不见了。他扑倒在地，一只手压着心口。「感谢安拉！」他喘着说，随即躺着不动，只剩喘。
旷野上，太阳火辣辣地烤他。他巴望有一片荫凉，哪怕一丛矮树，够遮住头脸也行。只几步外，一株孤零零的大角豆树张开虬枝和油亮的深叶，罩着一片粗石的场子——一处善人舍下、供旅人歇脚的功德。萨义德拖着自己过去，闭着眼躺了老半天。
「感赞安拉！」气匀净了，他又念了一遍。随后他想起那黑人，想起那洞兴许没有多深。他起来，走回那地方去。
他正在野草和矮树间摸洞口，只见一只黑手从地底下伸上来，一把攥住一株大蓝蓟的梗。他这才痛悔方才怕碍事甩掉了棍子。没奈何，他抄起一块带棱的石头，照那手指的骨节狠狠砸下去。期望中的嚎叫应声入耳，那只手蜥蜴一样麻利地缩回了洞。萨义德趴下肚子，匍匐着往前挪，挪到能往这口牢里张望。他的仇人就立在里头，一个窄得像井的地方，看样子倒是干的。胳膊伸下去，差一点儿就能摸到黑奴的白缠头。卡西姆瞪上来，两只白眼珠全是恨。

===Original===

Saïd could hear him grind his teeth for rage of helplessness.
He looked forth over the wide brown plain with faint blue mountains everywhere along the skyline, and back to where the house of the Frank at the foot of the hill was like a tiny white box shut tight with a high red lid.
Then peering again into the hole, he laughed aloud.
“Is it cool down there, O son of a pig?” he inquired. “By Allah, thou art well housed and I envy thee. Up here I am roasting in the noonday, whilst thou, within arm’s length of me, dost enjoy the cool of night. There is a road not far from thy dwelling, O foul scion of a race of swine; also a great tree where travellers may rest in the shade. But for all that, help is far from thee. Men will take fright at thy cries, coming from under the earth, and will fly swiftly as from a place of sin. I have it in my mind, thou dog, to drop earth down on thee and stones, and so bury thee. What sayest thou, ugly one? It would give me joy to defile thy grave!”
Of a sudden the negro made a great leap with hand upstretched. His nails grazed Saïd’s face, causing him to draw back in alarm.
“Curse thy father, son of a dog that thou art!” came a terrible voice from the pit. “May thy life be cut short! May all thy children rot, and thy woman betray thee to an enemy!”
“A wise man gives fair words to his master,” retorted Saïd, and his voice was like a leopard’s paw, so soft yet dangerous. “What art thou to me that I should delay to slay thee? At my elbow there is a nice stone which would break thy head as it were an egg. Speak smoothly to thy master, O Cassim, son of a pig!”
A fresh outbreak of cursing answered from the hole. Then Saïd reflected that he had wasted time enough in play by the wayside. The shadow of the carub-tree, lying like a blot of ink upon the whitening land, tempted him to rest there yet a little while. But two fears urged him onward. The negro might in the end get out of the hole, when Saïd could hope for no mercy if caught napping thereabouts; and the woman he had assailed, alarmed at Cassim’s nonappearance, would soon raise the hue-and-cry, if she had not already done so.
Saïd knew that his road lay towards those faint blue distant mountains with the whiteness among their crests, and there his knowledge ended.

===Chinese===

萨义德听得见他干咬牙——恨得没了办法。
他放眼望那片棕色的辽阔平野，天际线上处处一线淡蓝的山，又回头望山脚下法兰克人的宅子——像一只小小的白匣子，扣着一顶高高的红盖子。
再朝洞里一瞅，他放声大笑。
「底下凉快吗，猪崽子？」他问。「凭安拉说，你这住处真不赖，俺都眼热。上头俺在这儿叫晌午烤着，你呢，离俺一条胳膊远，享着夜里的凉。离你府上不远有一条道，猪种里下出来的脏东西；还有一棵大树，旅人好在上头歇荫凉。可是白搭，救兵离你远着呢。人们听见你从地底下嚎，只当撞了邪，跑得比躲罪孽还快。俺琢磿着，狗东西，干脆铲土丢石头下去，把你活埋了。怎么样，丑鬼？往你坟上撒泡尿，才叫痛快呢！」
忽然那黑奴猛地往上一蹿，手张到最高。指甲擦着萨义德的脸，吓得他赶紧缩头。
「咒你爹，你这狗崽子！」洞里上来一个可怕的声音。「叫你短命！叫你的一窝孩子全烂掉，叫你的女人把你卖给仇人！」
「聪明人对主人说话客气。」萨义德回敬，嗓音像豹子的爪子，软，却险。「你算俺的什么，值得俺磨蹭着不杀你？俺胳膊肘边上有块好石头，砸你脑袋跟砸鸡蛋一样。跟你主人说话客气点儿，卡西姆，猪崽子！」
洞里回敬他一阵新的咒骂。萨义德随即想，路边这套把戏，玩的工夫已经够本了。角豆树的影子像一摊墨渍躺在发白的地上，招他再歇一会儿。可两桩怕处催他上路。一是黑奴末了兴许爬得出洞，那时他要是在左近打盹，就别指望人家发善心；二是他动过的那个女人，等不来卡西姆，惊慌起来，早晚会嚷嚷出一场官缉——要是还没嚷嚷的话。
萨义德知道他的路在那淡蓝的远山一带，山顶间有一线白；他的知道，也就到这儿为止了。

===Original===

The plain stretched burning and treeless in that direction, but at a point far away a ripple of foliage broke the level. He could make out the shape of a palm-tree, seeming of no more substance than a blade of grass, so distant it was, and the quiver of hot air between. Palms do not grow solitary like weeds or carub-trees. A village was therefore near it, where he could inquire his road more perfectly. There remained only to take farewell of the prisoner.
He drew near once more to the mouth of the pit. With a look of concentration he leaned over and spat full in the upturned face of the negro. Then he rose lightly and went his way.

===Chinese===

平野朝那个方向铺出去，烧着，一棵树也没有；可在很远一个点上，一线叶影破了那道平。他辨出一株棕榈的轮廓，远得、隔着的热气颤得，它薄得像一根草叶。棕榈不长孤棵，不像野草和角豆树。那儿左近必有村庄，他可以好生问路。剩下的，就是跟囚犯道个别了。
他又凑近洞口，凝神屏气探过身去，一口唾沫，正正啐在那张仰着的黑脸上。然后他轻快地起身，赶他的路去了。

### IX / 九
===Original===

It was towards evening when Saïd left the place where, weary from long walking in the fierce eye of noon, he had sought shelter and refreshment. A crowd of men, women and children﻿—all who dwelt in that place﻿—went out with him from among the hovels as far as a tall palm-tree, which crowned a smooth hillock green with grass. In the midst of the obsequious rabble Saïd strutted a king, distinguished as he was by the missionary’s brown dressing-gown, braided conspicuously with red, and girt about the waist with a red and tasselled cord; not to speak of the new scarlet fez bound to his head by a turban of more than human cleanness.
Arrived at the palm-tree, all the villagers pressed forward to kiss his hand or, it might be, only the skirt of his wondrous robe. The glory of his raiment had enthralled them at his coming, and in the first rapture of greatness, in the joy of their cringing and flattery, he had promised to see that all their wrongs and grievances were presently redressed.
So he strode on his way with their blessings, turning ever and anon, with a gracious gesture, to look back at the squalid crowd of fellahìn, who stood grouped about the palm-tree, looking after him with hands shading their eyes. His brain was on fire with arrogance. Every herb on which he trod marked a new act of condescension. The whole earth fell down before him. The sun burned for him alone. Trees and shrubs cast their shadows like garments in his path.
But by-and-by, as the village shrank in distance, the vapours besetting his brain began to disperse. His legs were stiff from his race of the forenoon. He longed for a horse to carry him at ease, and the wish did much to sober him. A great one does not travel on foot, neither does he wander from home in the heat of the day without at least a sunshade in his hand, if not a servant to hold it over him. Sudden shame came upon him like an ague. The villagers would discuss his appearance now that he was gone, and remembering that he had neither horse nor servant, not so much as a parasol, would perceive their own folly and curse him for an impostor. At that he quickened his step so as to be far from a place where he must shortly be held in derision.
The violet mountains, which had seemed so far away in the morning, were now nearer to him than those others from whose base he had set out.

===Chinese===

快到傍晚，萨义德才离开那个地方——晌午毒日头底下走了长路，他乏了，在那儿讨了荫凉和吃喝。一群男人、女人、孩子——那地方住着的全部人口——跟着他出了破屋，一直送到一株高高的棕榈树下；树立在一座溜圆的青草小冈顶上。在这群胁肩谄笑的乌合之众中间，萨义德昂首阔步，俨然一介君王：他身上是传教士那件棕色晨衣，红绦子扎眼地镶着边，腰里束一根带穗的红绳；头上还有那顶簇新的猩红毡帽，用一条干净得不像人间物的缠头巾绑在头上——这些都不消说了。
到了棕榈树下，全村人都挤上来吻他的手，或者，兴许只吻他那件奇袍的下摆。他那身行头的荣光，在他来的时候就慑服了他们；而他在头一阵显贵的酿然里、在受人谄媚的欢喜里，许下愿要叫他们的一切冤屈都早早昭雪。
他就带着他们的祝福上了路，走几步就回头，做一个优容的手势，望一眼那群龌龊的费拉欣——他们围在棕榈树旁，手搭凉棚目送他。他的脑子烧着倨傲。他踩过的每一棵草，都是一桩新的屈尊。整个大地在他面前跪倒。太阳单为他一人燃烧。树木和灌丛把影子像衣袍一样铺在他的路上。
可是渐渐地，村庄在远处缩没了，围着他脑子的那团雾气开始散。他的两条腿叫上午那场赛跑弄僵了。他巴望有匹马驮着他舒舒服服走，这一愿大大醒了他的酒。大人物不徒步赶路；大人物也不在大天白日里离家乱走，手里至少要一把阳伞——再不然，也得有个仆人替他撑着。一阵羞耻突然上了身，像打摆子。村里人这会儿等他走远了，该议论他的排场了；想起他既没有马也没有仆人，连把遮阳伞都没有，就会悟出自己的糊涂，咒他一声骗子。想到这里他加快了脚步——趁还没到被人笑话的时候，赶紧离那地方远些。
那几座紫罗兰色的山，早晨还显得那么远，如今离他倒近了——比他动身的那座山的山脚还近。

===Original===

The sun, a disc of flame, was sinking down on the uttermost rim of the plain. Shadows were no longer dense and inky under every object, but stretched long and blue to eastward, growing with every minute. Far away across the flat Saïd was aware of a thin bright line, vague and dreamy beneath the setting sun. On that side was the sea. He grew sad as he recalled his little house among the sandhills. The cool breeze of evening was stirring the great leaves of his fig-tree even now.
As he pondered on things past a spirit awoke within him and showed him Abdullah in a new light. He stood still, as if gripped by a sudden twinge of pain. Stretching forth his hands to Heaven he bade Allah witness the trust he had ever placed in his friend and partner, and the consequent enormity of the fraud. In the first frenzy he thought to retrace his steps, to walk day and night without respite, until he had slain the treacherous liar. He even took a dreadful oath before Allah to that effect. But his mind soon changed. There was an evil report of him all along the way by which he had come. He felt ashamed because of Hasneh, and feared to see her face again. And the great city lay before him, where Allah alone knew what joys might be in store for him. Nevertheless, he made a vow: that, when he had achieved the greatness of his hopes, he would return to his native town riding upon a horse, with a company of horsemen, his servants, and would cause Abdullah to be whipped in his sight with a lash set thickly with sharp nails; and then, when his enemy lay bleeding and faint at his feet, he would recite the story of his crime aloud for all men to hear. And at last, to make vengeance complete, he would spurn his enemy with his foot and gallop off with his servants in a cloud of dust.
Twilight was closing swiftly into night when Saïd reached a place where was a well in the shade among some olive-trees, and hard by a low, flat-roofed house, from whose open door and window a faint red light flickered upon the trampled ground.
“Praise be to Allah﻿—a khan!” he murmured, espying the forms of two men smoking on stools before the door. Tethered to the nearest tree, a horse, which appeared black in the half light, was munching steadfastly in a wooden trough. The saddle was still on its back, though the girth was unfastened and dangling.

===Chinese===

太阳，一轮火焰的圆盘，正往平野最远的边上沉。影子不再是一团团浓黑的墨，而是长长的、蓝蓝的，向东铺去，一分一分地长。远远的平地那头，萨义德望见一线细细的亮，在落日底下虚虚的、梦似的。那一边是海。他想起沙丘间自己的小屋，心里愁起来。傍晚的凉风，这会儿怕正掀动他无花果树上那几片大叶子。
他想着旧事，心里醒起一个灵，把阿卜杜拉照了个新的亮。他站住了，像叫一阵突然的抽痛攦住。他双手向天，请安拉见证他一向寄在他朋友兼伙伴身上的信任，见证这桩欺诈因之何等滔天。头一阵狂怒里，他想顺着来路走回去，白天黑夜不歇地走，直到杀了那个奸诈的骗子。他甚至为此在安拉跟前发了一个可怕的誓。可他很快就改了主意。他一路来的那条道上，处处有他的恶名。他因哈斯娜害着臊，怕再见她的脸。何况那座大城就在他前头——那里囤着什么福乐，只有安拉知道。话虽如此，他立了一个愿：等他功成名就，他要骑一匹马、带一队骑马的仆人回他的本乡，叫人当着他的面，用一条密密钉着利钉的鞭子抽阿卜杜拉；等他的仇人流着血、半晕在他脚下，他要把那桩罪状高声宣讲给众人听；末了，叫复仇十全十美，他要用脚踢开仇人，带着仆人们扬起一蓬尘土飞驰而去。
萨义德走到一个地方，天光正飞快地合拢成夜：几株橄榄树的荫里有一口井，紧旁边一座低矮的平顶屋，敞着的门窗把一线暗红的光，闪在踩烂了的地上。
「感赞安拉——有客店（khan）！」他喃喃着，一眼望见门前凳上坐着两个抽烟的人影。拴在最近的树上的一匹马——半明半暗里瞧着是黑的——正埋头在木槽里一个劲儿地嚼。鞍子还在背上，只是肚带解开了，垂着。

===Original===

The two who sat smoking by the door rose courteously at the approach of a stranger. Saïd returned their salutation as though it had come from the dirt beneath his feet. He removed a stool to a seemly distance from them and sat down, calling impatiently for food and drink.
“My horse is fallen by the way,” he cried in a loud voice, for the enlightenment of all who might be in the house. “I bade my men stay to tend the beast, having yet hopes that he may recover. A good horse, by Allah, which I bought for fifty Turkish pounds, but I would not part with it for a hundred. In a little while they will be here, if they lose not their way in the darkness, which is very possible, their mind being little as the mind of a sheep.”
At the sound of that high speech the master of the khan appeared﻿—a tall, black shape on the glow of the doorway. Behind him other dark forms were discernible﻿—a cluster of heads, some turbanded, others draped in a shawl bound about the temples with a rope of camel’s hair.
Saïd was not pleased to find the khan so full of people. In such a crowd there might well be some great one who might expose him. The fear was vague but sickening. It was speedily laid to rest. A ray of firelight played on Saïd’s sleeve, showing the fine red braiding, when an awestricken murmur spread among the group at the door. It made him smile in his beard.
“What is thy will, effendi? All that I have is thine,” said the owner of the house, coming forward with a deep obeisance. “Deign but to enter the room. It is my shame that I have no meat to set before your Eminence. But condescend to wait a little and my woman shall slay a fowl.﻿ ﻿…”
“I have little hunger, I thank thee, and I prefer the open air,” broke in Saïd, loftily. “I do but await the men belonging to me, whom I left to tend my horse, which fell in the way hither. A good horse! Two hundred Turkish pounds would not requite me for his loss. Bring only a little fruit, some bread and some sherbet of roses. And forget not to prepare coffee and a narghileh for when I have done with eating.”
At that all was bustle and running to and fro. One ran to the well for water. Another undertook to pound the coffee. A third set a little stool before the fisherman and a lantern to shed light on his repast.

===Chinese===

坐在门口抽烟的两个，见生人走近，客气地站起来。萨义德还礼，那样子像是还给了脚底下的尘土。他把一张凳子挪到离他们不远不近的体面地方坐下，不耐烦地叫吃的喝的。
「俺的马半道倒了，」他放开嗓门，好叫屋里凡是有人的一概开窍，「俺叫俺的仆人们留下照看牲口——它兴许还救得回来。凭安拉说，一匹好马，俺买它花了五十个土耳其里拉，一百个也不卖。不大的工夫他们就到——只要别在黑地里迷了路；那可是大有可能，他们那点心思，跟羊差不多。」
这一番高谈阔论引出了客店的主人——门口的火光里一个高高的黑影。他背后还辨得出别的黑影——一攦脑袋，有的缠着巾，有的拿披肩裹头，鬓边勒一根驼毛绳。
萨义德见客店里人这么满，不大痛快。这样一群人里，保不齐有个大人物，当场戳穿他。这怕处朦朦胧胧，却叫人发呕。它很快就平息了：一线火光落在萨义德的袖子上，照出那精致的红绦子，门口那一攦人里顿时传开一片敬畏的低语。这叫他胡子里直发笑。
「您要什么，埃芬迪？俺的一切都是您的。」屋主深深一躬，抢上前来。「请赏光进屋。惭愧的是俺没有肉敬大人您。您屈尊稍候，俺的女人这就宰一只鸡。……」
「俺不怎么饿，谢了，俺也爱呆在外头透风。」萨义德打断他，居高临下。「俺不过是等俺的人——俺把他们留下照看俺那匹半道倒下的马了。一匹好马！两百个土耳其里拉也赔不了俺的损失。只管上一点儿水果、一点面包、一点玫瑰香露。别忘了预备咖啡和水烟壶，等俺吃完用。」
这一下满院子都是奔忙。一个跑去井边打水，一个揽下捣咖啡，第三个在渔夫面前摆了一张小几、一盏灯笼照他吃饭。

===Original===

A fourth prepared the weed for his narghileh by first plunging it into a jar of water, then wringing it out strongly with both hands. And those who could not be of active use raised their voices officiously in counsel and direction.
Only one held aloof. It was an aged man, one of those who sat smoking before the door. His bearing seemed superior to the rest. He alone remained seated, sucking lazily at his narghileh. Saïd divined a scornful smile on this man’s face as he looked on at the slavishness of his neighbours. Night, stealing out from under the olive-trees, had now completely hemmed in the house, so that, as they sat apart, Saïd could not see his countenance. But something told him the contempt was there, and it made him uneasy.
All that he required was presently brought and set upon the stool before him. There followed a hush, as the bystanders, having no more work to do, sat down on their heels at a discreet distance and watched his meal. They conversed together in whispers.
Saïd could hear the horse munching its chaff and barley under the trees hard by. There was now and then the stamp of a hoof, or a faint thud as it pushed against the wooden manger. He found it irksome to eat in state and apart. It came into his mind to call the host to him; but reflecting that true greatness brooks no fellow, he refrained. Instead, he pricked his ears to catch the gist of their whispering.
“Officer”﻿—“Soldiers”﻿—“War” were among the words which reached him. They fired a train of new ideas. Straightening his back, he stroked his moustache and beard with soldierly fierceness.
He was aware of a movement in the group. With the tail of his eye he saw the master of the khan draw near to that aged one who sat aloof and speak to him. Even in the darkness he knew that both their faces were turned in his direction.
“O Faris! Bring the coffee for his Excellency!﻿—and the narghileh also!” cried the host, whereat a man rose and ran quickly into the house. But the innkeeper himself did not budge. He remained whispering with the sheykh, and their eyes were fixed on Saïd.
Presently, when the great man seemed fully and happily occupied with his smoking, the sheykh rose with a show of carelessness, picked up a pair of saddlebags which lay by the wall, and went silently to where the horse was tethered.

===Chinese===

第四个伺候他的烟料：先把它按进一坛水里泡透，再两手死命拧干。那些插不上手的，扯起嗓子越俎代庖地出主意、做指点。
只有一个远远躲着。是位上了年纪的人，先前坐在门口抽烟的两个之一。他的气派瞧着高众人一等。只有他还坐着，懒懒地咂他的水烟壶。萨义德凭直觉辨出此人脸上挂着一丝讥诮的笑，看着邻人们的奴才相。夜从橄榄树底下渗出来，这会儿已经把屋子团团围拢；两人各坐一头，萨义德瞧不见他的脸。可有什么告诉他那份轻蔑在那儿，这叫他心头发毛。
他要的很快就齐了，摆在他面前的小几上。跟着是一阵肃静：闲人们没了营生，都退到得体的距离蹲在脚跟上，看他吃，彼此咬耳朵。
萨义德听得见那匹马在近旁树下嚼它的草料和大麦，间或一声蹄响，或是它拱木槽的一声闷响。一个人端着架子、孤零零地吃，是个受罪的事。他一度想叫店家过来陪话；转念一想，真显贵是不容人并肩的，忍住了。他竖起耳朵，去捉他们咬耳朵的要旨。
「军官」——「兵」——「打仗」——飘到他耳朵里的是这几个词。它们点着了一串新念头。他直起腰，拿出军人的剽悍捋他的八字胡和胡子。
他觉出那群人里有了动静。眼角里，他看见客店主人凑近那位独坐的老人，说了句什么。黑地里他也知道，两张脸都朝他这边转过来了。
「法里斯！给大人上咖啡！——还有水烟壶！」店主喊，于是有个人起来，快步进屋去了。可店主人自己没挪窝。他还跟那谢赫咬耳朵，两双眼睛都钉在萨义德身上。
过了一会儿，等那位大人物像是圆满地、幸福地沉进了他的烟里，谢赫装出一副漫不经心，拾起靠墙放着的一副鞍袋，悄悄走向拴马的地方。

===Original===

Saïd heard him thrust aside the portable manger, and knew, though he could not see, that he was busy strapping the girth. Then came the jingle of a bit.
The fisherman rose with an evil smile. He felt himself the object of all eyes, and in face of that quaking audience which believed in him was bold as a lion to act his part. Without a second’s delay he rushed upon the sheykh, and, seizing him by his clothing, swung him round and gripped his throat.
“I have thee, old fox,” he hissed, shaking his prisoner gently but with a deft suggestion of worse to come. “This horse is no longer thine. In the name of the Sultàn’s majesty﻿—may Allah preserve his life forever!﻿—I take him from thee. Thou knowest the law. After a little, when the war is over, he will be thine again﻿—if he die not in the meantime, which is very likely, for it is a sorry beast.”
With that he left hold of the old man, sending him reeling against the trunk of a tree, and, gathering up his grand robe, climbed into the saddle. All the men of the inn were now gathered to the spot. Their eyes were fierce upon Saïd, but fear sealed their lips. The sheykh, recovered from his stupor, grasped the bridle tightly.
“Yes, it is true, I know the law!” he screamed. “Thou mayst take my horse﻿—good, since there is war. But first thou must write me a paper of acknowledgment. I am no common man, I warn thee, to be robbed and no questions asked. I have friends in power. Give me, I tell thee, a writing of acknowledgment that I may claim my own when the evil time is past!”
Saïd hesitated, aghast. He had never dreamt of any more formality about the levying of a beast of burden for the army than had been observed in the taking of his own donkey. In any case, to give the paper was quite beyond his power, for he could scarcely write.
“What is this, son of a dog?” he exclaimed at last. “A paper, sayest thou?﻿—and the law? Am I one to take orders from a dog like thee? As soon as my men arrive with the other beasts thou shalt have thy paper, but not now. Dost hear﻿—eh, old dotard? Now stand aside or I ride over thee! I go to meet my followers.”
He urged the horse forward; but the old man still kept hold of the bridle, and the steed knew his master. His hesitation, and the misgiving which showed a little through his brave mask, had taken something from his prestige with the onlookers.

===Chinese===

萨义德听见他推开那活动的马槽，虽然看不见，也知道他在忙着系肚带。跟着是马嚼子一声脆响。
渔夫站起身，脸上挂着一个恶笑。他觉得自己是全场眼睛的靶子，面对这一群信了他的看客，他胆大如狮，要把他的角色演下去。他一秒也不耽搁，朝那谢赫猛扑过去，一把攦住衣裳，抡了半个圈，锁住他的喉咙。
「逮着你了，老狐狸，」他嘶嘶地说，轻轻摇着他的俘虏，手法灵巧地暗示着还有更凶的在后头。「这匹马不再是你的了。奉素丹陛下圣命——愿安拉永远保他的命！——俺从你手里征用了。你是懂法的。过些日子，等仗打完，它还是你的——只要它别在半道死了；那可是大有可能，这么一头孬牲口。」
说完他撒开老人，一送，送得他踉跄撞上树干；随即掖起他的大袍，跨进了鞍子。店里的人这时全围了上来。他们的眼睛凶凶地盯着萨义德，可恐惧封住了嘴。谢赫从懵懂里缓过来，死死攦住缰绳。
「是，俺懂法，这不假！」他尖叫。「你可以牵俺的马——好，谁叫打仗呢。可你先得给俺写张认领的字据。俺警告你，俺不是随便什么人，由你抢了就完了的。俺有做官的朋友。俺告诉你，给俺一张字据，等这劫难过去，俺好讨回俺自己的！」
萨义德迟疑了，吓了一大跳。他做梦也没想到，给大军征一头驮畜还有这么些手续——他自己那头驴叫人牵走时可没有。再说，写字据万万超出了他的本事：他几乎不会写。
「这算什么话，狗崽子？」他末了嚷起来。「字据，你说？——还法？俺是听你这种狗发号施令的人？等俺的人赶着牲口到了，字据自然给你，现在没有。听见没有——嗯，老糊涂？让开，不然俺从你身上骑过去！俺去接俺的部下。」
他催马向前；可老人还攦着缰绳不放，牲口认得它的主人。萨义德那一顿迟疑，加上他勇敢的面具底下隐隐漏出的心虚，把他在看客眼里的威光削了几分。

===Original===

They closed in upon him, clamouring for justice. It was a lonely place; in all the darkness there was no friend. He began to be afraid.
“At least the saddlebags are mine,” cried the sheykh, setting to work to free them.
“Fruit and bread and coffee are worth money, O my uncle! even without syrup of roses and the narghileh,” said the master of the khan in tones of blandest remonstrance. As he spoke his face was very near to Saïd’s, and its expression was terribly at variance with the suavity of his utterance.
All who stood by looked meaningly at one another. “By Allah, the right is with him!” they exclaimed, “All this is worth money. It is just that he be paid for it.”
Saïd moved uneasily in his seat.
“Take thy saddlebags, old madman!” he cried. “What are they to me? As for thee, dog, thou mayst count thyself happy if I send thee not to prison. I saw thee whisper to the sheykh here, and knew that thou wast warning him to be gone quickly with his horse. Thou art no true subject of the Sultàn. If I spare thy life it is payment enough.”
At that there was a great outcry from all the group. They beset him angrily with intent to drag him from the saddle. Saïd felt deadly sick. Only the thought that he was a high officer of the Sultàn’s army upheld him. Rough hands were already laid upon him, when he shouted “Praise be to Allah!” very fervently, with joy in his voice. They all drew back in surprise.
“Make haste, Ahmed!﻿—Mustafa!﻿—Muhammed! I, your leader, am assailed by robbers. Hassan and Ali, ride fast! Let Negìb, whose horse is lame, take charge of the captured beasts! I, Saïd Agha, am in peril of my life!”
Turning to the terrified innkeeper and his friends, he said shortly﻿—
“Dogs, count yourselves dead! Hear ye not the sound of hoof-beats?” And digging the sharp corners of the iron stirrups deep into the flanks of the horse he galloped away into the night. The last he saw of his assailants, they were standing huddled together, like silly sheep, half-dead with fright.

===Chinese===

他们围拢来，嚷着要个公道。这是个僻静地方；一片黑里没有一个朋友。他开始怕了。
「至少鞍袋是俺的！」谢赫喊，动手解那副鞍袋。
「水果、面包、咖啡都是钱哪，俺的叔！更别提玫瑰香露和水烟了。」客店主人的腔调是最温吞的规劝。他说着，脸凑得离萨义德极近，那副神色跟他话音的和气大相径庭，吓人得很。
旁边的人都心照不宣地面面相觑。「凭安拉说，他有理！」他们说，「这一摊都是钱。叫他付账才是公道。」
萨义德在鞍上不安地挪了挪。
「拿去你的鞍袋，老疯子！」他喊，「它算俺的什么？至于你，狗东西，俺不送你去坐牢，你就念你的好命吧。俺看见你跟这位谢赫咬耳朵了，知道你是催他快带马跑。你不是素丹的真臣民。俺饶你一条命，就算付清了。」
这话招来那一群的大哗。他们怒冲冲地围上来，要把他拖下鞍。萨义德恶心得要命。撑着他的只有一个念头：他是素丹大军的一名高官。粗暴的手已经按上他了，他却高喊一声「感赞安拉！」，喊得热烈无比，嗓音里全是欢喜。众人一惊，齐齐缩了回去。
「快点，艾哈迈德！——穆斯塔法！——穆罕默德！你们的长官遭了强盗！哈桑、阿里，快骑马来！叫瘸腿马的内吉布看管缴获的牲口！俺萨义德阿加有性命之危！」
他转向吓瘫了的店主和他的朋友们，短短地说：
「狗们，只当自己死了！没听见蹄声吗？」说着他把铁马镬的利角狠狠磕进马腹，冲进黑夜里跑远了。他最后瞥见的那伙袭击他的人，缩作一团站着，像一群蠢羊，吓了个半死。

### X / 十
===Original===

It was evening when Saïd at last came in sight of the great city. He reined in his horse on the brow of a steep hill, the last wave of the bare brown highlands through which his way had lain all day. Hard by was a little shrine, the crescent fiery above its dome. The sun was just setting among the dark peaks behind him, and the last gleam of day was warm upon the shrine and all the hilltop. Horse and man had a glory at their backs. But beneath, the city and its endless garden lay already in the lap of night. White domes and minarets, mosques and palaces, loomed wanly in the heart of a vast grove, which stretched, far as the eye could ascertain, to eastward towards a smooth horizon which was the desert. Gathering shades spread a thin veil over all the plain, like the bloom on a purple grape. An amethyst flush suffused the eastern sky﻿—a spirit flush, soft, yet living, wherein starlight and daylight seemed mingled. Saïd’s heart leapt as he beheld the mistress of his dreams, set in her gardens, seeming the fairer and more desirable for the grim, treeless mountains which were her girdle.
“It is paradise,” he murmured in ecstasy.
At the foot of the hill, on the utmost fringe of the gardens, he could see a little village of flat-roofed houses. A string of camels was drawing near to it along the base of the steep. The tinkle of their bells rippled the twilight cheerily. Of a sudden the noise of chanting arose﻿—a wild, delirious song of piercing shrillness. It came from the high platform of the only minaret of the village. Somewhat mellowed by the distance, it reached Saïd’s ears as heavenly music. The clangour of bells ceased of a sudden. The camels had halted. Their drivers, obedient to the muezzin’s call, were prostrate in prayer.
Saïd got down from his horse and went through the form of ablution with some dry dust he collected. Taking off his grand garment, a good deal the worse for his five days’ wearing of it, he stretched it on the ground for a mat. He turned his face carefully to the south and knelt down as near to the shrine as he conveniently might. He raised his thumbs to his ears and spread his hands over his eyes in the likeness of an open book. He rose, stooped, knelt again, prostrated himself and pressed his forehead to the earth.

===Chinese===

黄昏时分，赛义德终于望见了那座大城。他在一道陡岭的坡顶勒住马——那是光秃秃的褐色高地最后一道波澜，他一整天都在其间穿行。近旁有一座小神龛，新月当着圆顶烧得火红。太阳正在他背后黑黢黢的峰峦间沉落，白昼最后的光暖暖地照着神龛和整面坡顶。人马背上披着一身荣光。可下方，城和它无尽的园子已经躺在夜的膝上。白穹顶、宣礼塔，清真寺和宫阙，在一大片树海的腹心隐隐泛白；那树海往东铺去，直到目力穷尽处一道平直的地平线——那就是沙漠。渐浓的暮色给整个平原罩上一层薄纱，像紫葡萄皮上的果霜。一片紫水晶的晕染透了东天——那是精灵的晕，柔和却活着，星光和昼光仿佛交融在里面。赛义德望见他梦中的女主人端坐在她的园圃之中，那寸草不生的荒山是她的腰带，反衬得她愈发姣好、愈发可欲，他的心一跳。
「这是天园，」他心醉神迷地喃喃。
山脚下、园圃最外的边上，他望得见一个小村，尽是平顶的房子。一队骆驼正贴着陡坡的坡根向村子走近。驼铃的叮咚把暮色敲出一圈圈欢快的涟漪。蘶地一阵唱诵扬起——一支狂野迷醉的歌，尖利穿云。它出自村里唯一那座宣礼塔的高台。经了距离的柔化，传到赛义德耳中已是天上的音乐。铃声戛然而止。骆驼停了。赶驼的应着宣礼员的召唤，伏地祷告。
赛义德下了马，捾了些干土，权作净水行了小净的仪节。他脱下那件华服——五天穿下来已大为失色——铺在地上当拜毯。他把脸仔细对准南方，捾着离神龛最近又不碍事的地方跪下。拇指扣上耳朵，双手在眼前摊开如一部翻开的书。他起立，躬身，再跪，叩首，额头贴地。

===Original===

Then he sat awhile upon his heels with eyes closed, and then glanced to left and right, to exorcise any evil spirits who were thereabout.
At last he rose and resumed his cloak. The orange glow of sunset was fading fast, and the mountains he was leaving were black and grey upon it. He bestrode his horse once more and began to descend. It was night when he entered the city. The streets were almost deserted. The few men he met were wending homeward, some in a hurry, others with the leisure of importance. Light streamed from an arched doorway, making a yellow pool on the rough pavement. A red glow, sifted through the tracery of an upper lattice, made a delicate filigree upon the wall opposite. But for such chance alms the streets were pitchy dark. The strip of sky above, sprinkled thick with stars, was a brightness in comparison. At the clatter of a horse’s hoofs, dogs, seemingly without number, rose grudgingly and slunk snarling from the roadway. Every wayfarer had a lantern to light his steps, either in his own hand or in that of a servant who walked before.
Anon he came to a region where all the streets had roofs which shut out the sky, save a starry shred here and there where there was a rift in the black covering. Here was more life. A few merchants were yet busy stowing away their wares for the night, black shapes in flowing robes and turbans moving hither and thither about their lanterns. At a place where four of these covered ways met, seeming like corridors in a giant’s house, a sentry was standing in the door of his little hut talking to two muleteers.
The ride through the dim streets had humbled Saïd. He felt very lonely all at once. In all that wilderness of dwellings there was not one soul who knew him. He would have given much﻿—even his horse, or his brown cloak with the red braiding﻿—to have had Hasneh with him. Fearing he knew not what rebuff, he had been ashamed to accost any man hitherto. But now he reined in his horse before the sentry-box and, wishing the little group a happy evening, inquired after a khan. One of the muleteers knew a good one and offered to guide him thither. It was plain, by the fervour of their salutations, that they took him for a superior. He began to feel more at ease. It was not far to the hostelry. The muleteer talked glibly all the way, of travelling and of his own journeys in particular.

===Chinese===

然后他闭着眼在脚后跟上坐了一阵，又左右各望一眼，把附近可能潜伏的邪灵驱走。
末了他起身，重新披上外袍。落日的橙光正飞快地暗下去，他将要抛下的群山在那光里一团黑一团灰。他重新上马，开始下坡。进得城来，已是夜里。街上几乎空无一人。遇见的少数几个都在赶回家的路，有的步履匆匆，有的带着要人式的从容。
一道拱门里淌出灯光，在粗糙的路面上积成一汪黄。一点红光从楼上花窗棂里筛下来，在对面的墙上织成一幅细巧的镂花。除了这类偶然的施舍，街上黑得像漆。头顶那一线天，缀满星斗，两相比较之下竟算是亮的。马蹄一响，数也数不清的狗老大不情愿地起身，一路狺狺，从道中溜开。每个行路人都有灯笼照步，不是自己提着，就是由前头走的仆人提着。
不久他来到一带地方，条条街道都有顶盖，把天遮死，只有黑顶裂了缝的地方漏下几缕星光。这里人气旺些。几个商家还在忙着收拾货品过夜，一袭长袍缠头巾的黑影围着各自的灯笼移来移去。在四条带顶街道交汇的地方——恍如巨宅里的几条走廊——一个哨兵站在他那小屋门口，同两个赶骡人说笑。
一路昏暗街巷骑过来，赛义德的气焰矮了。他猛然觉得孤单。那一片屋宇的荒野里，认得他的一个魂灵也没有。他肯拿出许多来——连他的马，或是那件镶红绦的褐袍——只求哈斯娜在身边。怕着不知什么样的碰壁，他此前一直不好意思开口同人搭话。可这会儿他在哨亭前勒住马，祝这一小队人晚间安好，打听起客店来。一个赶骡人知道一家好的，自告奋勇引路。从他们行礼的热乎劲儿看，分明把他当成了上等人。他渐渐自在起来。客店不远。赶骡人一路嘴皮利索，谈行旅，尤其谈他自己的行程。

===Original===

His name it appeared was Selìm. He was but lately returned from Haleb the White, and before that he had been to Baghdad with a hundred camels. Whence had his honour come. From the South?﻿—from the seacoast. Ah, he had been there too, having journeyed with a caravan to Gaza, and back by El Khalìl and the holy city. It was a pleasant land, the lord of all for oranges; he had the taste of them yet in his mouth.
Saïd lent a gracious ear to his guide’s prattle, which relieved him of that feeling of loneliness which was weighing him down. Arrived at the khan, he bestowed a small coin upon the fellow, who blessed him and went his way.
A bare-legged lad belonging to the inn held his horse while he dismounted, and led it in through an archway. Saïd followed closely to be sure that the right measure of fodder was given and the beast properly cared for. He entered a huge vaulted chamber, its groined roof upheld by two rows of pillars. Couched upon the ground, big, ungainly camels were pompously chewing the cud, now and then rolling up a deep gurgling sound like a groan from some nether stomach. Horses were there, each fastened with a halter to a ring in the wall. One stallion, a newcomer, was screaming lustily and tugging at his rope. Patient asses with moving ears and swishing tails, and sullen mules whose eyes looked wicked in the lurid glow of the single lantern, were tethered here and there. There was a sound of stamping, of scrunching, and a pungent smell. A little donkey just within the gate lifted up his voice and brayed as Saïd entered.
Having seen his steed well placed and provided for, Saïd followed the serving-lad to a door in the wall, whence light streamed upon a camel’s hump. The noise of voices and a smell of cooking also issued from it, soothing two senses with the promise of cheer within. He found himself in a long room with cushions ranged along the wall, lighted by a number of wicks floating in a large saucer full of oil. A numerous company were seated, some smoking and chatting on the divan, others, on isolated cushions, eating ravenously with their hands out of dishes set upon brass trays before them. They all rose in acknowledgment of his salutation and a place of honour was offered to him, which, however, he declined to accept, choosing rather a lowly seat about midway in the room.

===Chinese===

原来他名叫塞利姆。刚从白色的阿勒颇回来不久；再往前，还赶着一百峰骆驼去过巴格达。老爷从哪儿来？打南边来？——打海边来。啊，那儿他也到过，随商队去过加沙，回程取道哈利勒和圣城。那是个好地方，产橙子的头一块地界；那滋味至今还留在他嘴里。
赛义德惠然听着向导的絮叨，那絮叨卸下了压着他的孤零。到了客店，他赏了那汉子一枚小钱；汉子为他祝了福，自去了。
店里一个光腿的小厮扶住马让他下来，牵着马从一道拱门进去。赛义德紧跟在后面，务必看着草料给足、牲口安置妥帖。他进了一间巨大的拱顶厅，肋拱的屋顶由两排柱子撑着。一峰峰高大笨拙的骆驼卧在地上，煞有介事地倒嚼，时不时从哪个底层胃里滚上来一声深深的咕噜，像呻吟。马也有，各各拿笼头拴在墙环上。一匹新到的牡马正引颈长嘶，死命拽绳。耐性的驴子扇着耳朵、甩着尾巴；阴沉的骡子，眼睛在孤灯昏红的光里透着凶，四处拴着。一片踏蹄声、咀嚼声，还有一股呛鼻的气味。赛义德进门时，门里一头小驴扬起嗓子叫了起来。
眼看坐骑安置停当、草料无缺，赛义德随着小厮走向墙上一道门，灯光从门里淌出来，正照着一峰骆驼的驼峰。人声和饭菜香也从那里涌出，用里面款待的许诺，安抚了两样感官。
他置身于一间长屋，沿墙排着坐垫，照明靠几截浮在满满一大碟油里的灯芯。屋里坐了满满一伙人：有的在长榻上抽烟闲谈，有的各踞一垫，手抓着铜盘里摆的菜肴大嚼。他一请安，众人全体起立还礼；上座让给了他，他却辞谢了，捾了屋子中腰一处低微的座位坐了。

===Original===

In an arched alcove or inner room a fire was glowing in a great brazier, whereon were many vessels steaming.
Saïd desired a portion of a savoury mess of pigeons and rice, which the bare-legged lad informed him was almost ready. The meal, though proper enough to his fine robe braided with red and the decent horse he rode, was scarcely in keeping with the sum of ready money in the linen bag upon his chest. But he had no longer any need of a horse. He would sell his steed on the morrow, and the price he hoped to get for it would keep him in comfort for many months.
When hunger was appeased, and a tiny cupful of the bitterest coffee had diffused a pleasant warmth within him, he began to take interest in the conversation around him. A big, sanguine fellow, who by his garb seemed a wealthy fellah﻿—the sheykh of some village, perhaps, or a small landowner﻿—was talking excitedly in a loud voice. His large brown eyes, of ox-like stupidity, were bright, but without a spark of cunning. His close-cut beard was reddish like his moustache.
“My cause is a just one. Also I have set aside much money to secure judgment. My enemy cannot bring forward a single witness in his favour, whereas I have my brother here and my servant who were present at the transaction. It is certain that I shall win.”
He took up the hem of his robe﻿—a rich one though somewhat soiled﻿—to wipe the amber mouthpiece of his narghileh.
“Truly thou art an honest man and a trusting,” said a bilious-looking person, short and swarthy, with a sneering smile. “It is well seen thou comest from a far village. As for witnesses, I tell thee thy adversary may have ten for thy two. Thou art rash, young man, to quarrel with one so powerful as the tithe-farmer. Thou hast wealth, it may be, but be sure he is richer than thee. Also he has the ear of the rulers, who profit by his exactions. The Mehkemeh is not a house of justice as thou thinkest, but an open market where judgment goes to the heaviest purse. Thou comest from afar; but I am of the city and speak from knowledge. Tomorrow, when thou goest to the court, thou wilt be beset at the gate by a crowd of rascals whose trade is to bear witness for money. Twenty piastres will buy thee a plausible fellow who will swear to aught that pleases thee.

===Chinese===

一座拱形凹室——算是里间——里，大火盆烧得正红，上面坐着好些冒气的锅子。
赛义德要了一份鸽子烑饭——光腿小厮告诉他这就快得了。这一顿饭，配他那件镶红绦的华服、他那匹体面的马，原也不算僭越；只是同胸口亚麻袋里那点现钱，就有些不般配了。可他如今用不着马了。明天他就把坐骑卖掉，指望卖得的价钱够他舒舒服服过上半年数月。
饥肠填平，一小盅最苦的咖啡又在体内烘起一团适意的暖，他开始留意起四下的谈话。一个红润的大块头，看衣着像个体面的庄稼汉——兴许是哪个村的谢赫，或者小田主——正高声激动地说着。他棕色的大眼睛带着牛一般的老实，亮是亮，却没有一丝狡黠。修剪得贴紧的胡子同髭一样泛红。
「我的官司是占理的。为买通判词，我也拨出了一注钱。我的仇家一个有利于他的证人也拿不出，我呢，兄弟就在这里，仆人也在，都是当场见事的。我必赢无疑。」
他撩起袍裾——一件富丽的袍子，只是有些脏了——擦了擦水烟壶的琥珀嘴。
「你真是个实心人，还容易信人，」一个面色灰黄、又矮又黑的汉子说，挂着讥诮的笑。「一看就知道你打远村来。说到证人，我告诉你，你的对头可以拿出十个对掉你两个。年轻人，你同包税人这样有权势的闹翻，是莽撞。你兴许有钱，可他保准比你富。况且官府还听得进他的话——他的盘剥，官府是有份的。教法庭不是你想象的那种公道衙门，是个公开的集市，判词归钱袋最沉的一方。你打远处来；我是城里人，说的是亲眼见识。明天你上法庭，门口就叫一帮无赖围上，他们的营生就是拿钱作证。二十个皮阿斯特，给你买来一个嘴皮像样的，你要他赌什么咒他就赌什么咒。

===Original===

The Qadi will count the witnesses on either side, and will give judgment for the greater number﻿—if he have not sold his verdict beforehand, which is most likely. Bakshìsh is lord of all. A wise man does not fall out with the rich. It is the same all the world over. They tell of countries where justice is for rich and poor alike; but that is all a lie!”
He looked round on the faces to mark the effect of his words. Then he leaned back and began to roll a cigarette.
The young man who had first spoken broke out in fierce invective at such a state of things. Yet he still believed that his own case would prove a big exception. He boasted wildly and a little foolishly of the revenge he would take if judgment were given against him. He even reviled those in authority, so that his listeners murmured, with fear in their eyes. It was ill to speak thus in a public place where none knew his company. The eyes of everyone sought a neighbour’s in concern. Saïd above all was singled out for suspicion. His brown cloak of outlandish make, and especially the red braid upon it, had a quasi-official look. It was a relief to all when a fat-faced man with roguish eyes, who sat in the lowest seat and seemed the poorest there, raised his voice in fantastical eulogy of riches. He stood up, and mimicking an advocate or other public speaker, talked nonsense glibly in a high poetic strain. It was rather brilliant nonsense, and it tickled his audience hugely. One and all rolled with laughter, holding their sides. By the time the wag sat down again he was dear as a brother to every man there. As an approved jester he might have taken the seat of honour without offence to the most arrogant.
After that the talk became less general. Men yawned one after another. Those nearest to Saïd made overtures of friendship. They asked questions: whence he came, what his name was, whether he had a son, what might be his business in the city, and so forth﻿—questions Saïd was often puzzled to answer. To escape from their inquisitiveness he declared himself with a yawn to be very weary, and asked to be shown to the place of sleep. One or two of the company had already set the example. He salaamed to the room in general as he went out.

===Chinese===

「卡迪数数两边的证人，哪边多判哪边赢——倘若他没有先把判词卖出去的话，而十有八九是卖了。巴克斯什统治一切。聪明人不同阔人翻脸。天下处处一样。人家说有的国度里穷富一样有公道；那全是鬼话！」
他环视一圈脸，看他的话起了什么效。随后他往身后一靠，卷起烟来。
先开口的那个年轻人炸了，对这般世道一顿痛骂。可他仍旧信自己的案子会是个大大的例外。他放肆而略带愚蠢地夸口，说判词若是不利，他必如何报仇。他连当权的都骂上了，听得众人嘨咕起来，眼里带惧。在这么个公众场所、一屋子生人面前，说这种话是要遭殃的。人人不安地你看看我、我看看你。赛义德尤其被挑出来受猜疑。他那件异邦样式的褐袍，尤其是那镶红绦，瞧着有几分官气。
后来一个胖子脸、贼溜溜眼的汉子开了腔，把财富天花乱坠地颂了一通，众人才松了口气——他坐的是最末的座，瞧着也是满屋最穷的。他站起来，学讼师或是当众演说家的派头，用一路高华的诗腔溜溜地胡说。胡说得相当出彩，把满座逗得不行。一个个捧着肚子笑得打滚。等这活宝重新坐下，他已是在场每个人的亲兄弟。作为公认的插科打诨之人，他就是坐上座，最傲的也不会见怪。
这以后谈话便散成了小股。人们接二连三打哈欠。挨着赛义德坐的几个凑上来攽交情。他们发问：从哪儿来，尊姓大名，有没有儿子，进城有何贵干，等等——这些问题常把赛义德问得张口结舌。为躲开这份盘问，他打着哈欠自称乏透了，请人引他去睡处。同座的已有一两位带了头。他朝满屋行了个总礼，出去了。

===Original===

The same bare-legged youth who had served him on his arrival led him now through the dim stable, among the sleeping beasts, to a place where a flight of stone steps was built against the wall. Ascending, he came into a long room like to that he had just left. The lantern his guide carried showed the floor bare save for four mattresses, on which as many men lay stretched, and a heap of dirty bedding in one corner. There was a lattice affording a glimpse of the stars above the uneven blackness of flat-topped roofs. The night air came freely into the chamber﻿—not the sweet breeze of the mountain or the seashore, but a breath of the sleeping city redolent of the day’s filth. The lad dragged a mattress and a covering from the heap and spread them close by the window. Then wishing the traveller a happy night, he departed.
Saïd lay awake a great while. Men came in by ones and twos, spread out their beds and lay down, until the floor was strewn with sleeping forms and the sound of loud snoring in every key floated out melodious into the night. He could not be rid of a feeling that he was still on horseback, riding at a foot’s pace over hill and dale, breezy mountain and burning plain. A fear was at his heart﻿—a fear that had been with him always of late, that he might fall in with a band of soldiers who would rob him of his horse even as he had robbed the rightful owner. He had indeed learned from a shepherd lad that there was no war but only a general movement of troops changing garrison. But as steeds were needed as much in the one case as in the other, the tidings in no way relieved his mind. By a cautious avoidance of towns and large villages, and choice of a bypath, even though it went a long way round, he had almost doubled the length of his journey, and had approached the city by the way of the hills, whereas the way of the plain was much shorter.
When at length he fell asleep it was to dream that the whole city had become solid, of a single stone, and that he was immured in a little cavity in the midst of it. The stone was populous, swarming with human beings who gave no heed to his cries. There were endless tunnels thronged with wayfarers, all bearing lanterns﻿—a nation which had never seen the sun. The weight of the whole stone was somehow upon him. He called to Allah for relief; but the thickness of that stone was inconceivable, and Allah very far away.

===Chinese===

还是那个迎他进店的光腿小厮，这会儿领他穿过昏暗的牲口房，在睡着的大小牲口中间，走到墙根一道石阶跟前。拾级而上，进到一间长屋，同方才那间相仿。向导提的灯笼照见光荡荡的地板上只有四张褥子，褥子上挺着四个人，还有墙角一堆脏铺盖。
一扇花窗透进来一角星空，缀在高高低低平顶屋的黑影之上。夜气在屋里畅行无阻——不是山间海滨的清风，是睡熟了的城呼出的一口气，饱含白日的秽浊。小厮从堆里拖出一张褥子一床盖的，铺在窗边。祝过旅人一夜安睡，他走了。
赛义德醒着躺了许久。人们三三两两进来，铺开铺盖躺下，直到地板上睡满人形，高低不一的鼾声大作，飘飘地融进夜里，居然成乐。他摆脱不掉一种感觉：自己还在马背上，一步一步走过山巅谷底、凉风的高山和灼人的平原。心里悬着一桩怕——近来无时不在的怕：怕撞上一队兵，夺走他的马，正像他从正主手里夺马一样。他倒是从一个牧童那儿探得实信：并没有战事，不过是军队换防的大调动。可换防同打仗一样要马，这消息丝毫没宽他的心。靠着小心翼翼避开城镇大村，专捾小路——哪怕绕远——他把路程几乎走成了两倍，从山那边进的城；其实平原那条道要近得多。
到末了睡着时，他梦见整座城凝成了一块整石，而他被封在石头正当中一个小小的空腔里。石头里挤满了人，闹闹攘攘，对他的呼喊一概不理。隧道无穷无尽，塞满行路人，个个提着灯笼——一族从未见过太阳的人。整块石头的分量不知怎么全压在他身上。他呼求安拉解救；可那石头的厚度不可思议，安拉又远在天外。

===Original===

However, the face of Muhammed the Prophet (peace be to him!)﻿—a fat sly face like Abdullah’s﻿—looked in upon him and sternly remarked, “It is Paradise.” Then arose a terrible cry for bakshìsh, and Saïd knew that the stone was no other than a court of law.

===Chinese===

幸而先知穆罕默德的脸（愿他平安！）——一张阿卜杜拉式的肥而狡的脸——探进来望了望他，严厉地说：「这是天园。」随后响起一片讨要巴克斯什的可怕呼号，赛义德于是明白：那块石头不是别的，正是一座法庭。

### XI / 十一
===Original===

Saïd awoke, as soon as it began to be light, to find the chamber already half empty of sleepers. His forehead was clouded as he went down the flight of stone steps into the stable, and threaded his way gingerly among the beasts and merchandise. His mind was busy laying plans for the day. There was much to be done. His horse must first be sold, and then he must look out for a lodging in keeping with his means. He must be on his guard every minute, for the dwellers in towns have ready wits and love to whet them on a stranger.
The ghost of daylight, looking in at the arched doorway, cast a pallor on the stumpy columns, on the humps and heads of camels, on the glossy flanks of horses and mules. He made his way to where his own steed was standing listless, awaiting the morning’s dole of chaff and barley. A soft neigh and a pricking of the ears welcomed him. He smoothed the horse’s mane lovingly, patted its neck and rubbed its nose, whispering all manner of endearment. It was a good beast, and he was sad to part with it.
In the guestroom he found the young man who had spoken so rashly overnight seated on the floor at a meal of bread, curds and olives. A handsome lad of sixteen or thereabouts, whom a strong likeness proclaimed his brother, sat with him, eating from the same tray. At a becoming distance their servant﻿—a swarthy, fierce-eyed fellow, whose weather-beaten tarbûsh had lost its tassel﻿—squatted on his bare heels awaiting their pleasure. Saïd greeted them politely before shouting for something to eat. While a servant who answered his cry was pouring water over his hands and helping to dry them on a dirty cloth, the voice of the young man rose in flowered eloquence.
He was rehearsing the speech he meant to make before the Qadi. It must have been written for him by some learned scribe skilled in all the bewilderment of tangled words; for no plain man could lay hold of its meaning. It was all of one piece from first syllable to last, and as it was recited, or rather intoned, there was no telling where one thought ended and the other began. Saïd’s mouth fell agape with admiration. He listened spellbound, forgetful even of his breakfast. Once or twice the orator, finding himself at a loss, drew a scroll from the bosom of his robe and passed his finger along and down it till he came to the passage. Then he replaced the scroll and went on with renewed fervour.

===Chinese===

天刚透亮萨义德就醒了，屋里睡觉的人已经走了一半。他下石阶进马厩、在牲口和货堆中间小心穿行时，额头上还挂着一片阴云。他心里忙着排这一天的计划。要办的事多着呢。先得把马卖了，再找一处跟身份相称的住处。他得分分钟提着神——城里人脑筋快，最爱拿外乡人磨刀。
白昼的幽魂从拱门里探进来，把惨淡的光投在粗短的柱子上、骆驼的驼峰和脑袋上、马骡油亮的胁腹上。他走到自个儿那匹牲口跟前——它正没精打采地站着，等早晨那份草料和大麦。一声轻轻的嘶鸣和一竖的耳朵欢迎他。他爱怜地抿平马鬃，拍拍它的脖子，揉揉它的鼻子，嘴里念着各种甜话。是匹好牲口，撒手他还真舍不得。
在客房里，他碰见昨夜口出狂言的年轻人，正坐在地上吃早饭——面包、凝乳和橄榄。跟他同吃一盘的是个十六岁上下、模样俊俏的小子，一脸的相像不用问是他兄弟。得体的距离外蹲着他家的仆人——一个黑黢黢、眼神凶悍的家伙，那顶风吹日晒的塔布什毡帽早就没了穗子——光着脚跟蹲着，候两位少爷的示下。萨义德客气地招呼了，才扯开嗓子喊吃的。应声来的仆人往他手上浇水，伺候他用一块脏布擦手，那边年轻人的嗓音已经拔起来，满口锦绣。
他是在排演预备在卡迪法官面前讲的那篇话。想必是哪位饱学的书记替他写的，深谙纠缠字句的一切迷魂阵；寻常人休想抓住它的意思。从头一个音节到末一个音节浑然一体，这么念着——与其说念，不如说唱着——谁也分不清这一层意思到哪儿完、那一层意思从哪儿起。萨义德佩服得张开了嘴。他听得入了定，连早饭都忘了。有一两回，演说家卡了壳，从怀里摸出一卷纸，手指顺着行往下捋，捋到那段，把纸卷收回怀里，重新以加倍的热忱接着来。

===Original===

“Capital!” cried the servant, when a complacent grin of his master announced the end. “In all my life I have heard nothing like it. It speaks with the mouth of the Quran, with the voice of an angel. It would melt the heart of the Chief of Mountains, by Allah! Rejoice, O my master, for our cause is won!”
“Good﻿—very good!” said the younger brother, his face eager with impatience. “Is it not the hour when we should repair to the Mehkemeh?”
Saïd also lent his voice to swell the chorus of praise. Such a speech, he protested, would grace the lips of princes. It was polished as a tray of gold, exquisite as a mosaic of divers kinds of precious stones, sweet as the voices of girls singing to the sound of the one-stringed lute. The ear of Allah would not disdain it. This high praise, which was perfectly sincere of its kind, flattered the orator and his boyish brother. Even the surly henchman looked at Saïd with grudging approval. The chief of the party informed him graciously that he had procured the speech of a scribe renowned in all the city for his learning, and that it had cost him a pretty sum of money, which he named. If his enemy could produce a better he would be surprised, and so forth. “Moreover,” he added, with a smile of such doltish cunning that Saïd envied his opponent﻿—“moreover, I have laid out much money already among the servants of authority, and I have here a great sum to be expended in the court itself. It is sure that I shall win.”
“There is no doubt!” his companions chimed in, the one eagerly, the other with a kind of sullen defiance.
“No doubt﻿—not a shred of doubt,” echoed Saïd, his bearing very respectful of a sudden as he heard the jingle of coins in the sack which the young man opened his robe to show him.
His fast fairly broken, he called for the reckoning. The lord of the khan appeared﻿—a very fat man wearing a robe of indigo blue, under which dirty white pantaloons showed to his ankles, the reddest of red slippers, and a girdle of many colours which, instead of restraining his bulk at all, bulged out frankly upon the most obvious part of him. His turban was richly embroidered, but old and dingy. His demeanour was important but polite, as became a substantial host requiring payment of a guest of unknown quality. The amount was twelve piastres, he informed the effendi.

===Chinese===

「绝了！」主人自得的一咧嘴宣告了收尾，仆人叫起来，「俺这辈子没听过这样的！这是拿古兰经的口讲话，拿天仙的嗓子说话。凭安拉说，山里的太岁听了心也得化！欢喜吧俺的主人，这官司赢了！」
「好——好得很！」小兄弟说，一脸跃跃欲试的急性。「这不正是该上迈赫凯迈（法庭）去的时辰么？」
萨义德也加入那片赞美的大合唱。这样的演说，他担保，配得上王公的嘴。它光润得像一只金盘，精巧得像各样宝石镶嵌的花砖，甜得像姑娘们和着单弦琴的歌声。安拉的耳朵也不会嫌弃它。这番高抬——那一门里倒全是真心——熨帖了演说家和他那孩子气的兄弟，连那横眉立目的仆人也老大不情愿地看了萨义德一眼，算是首肯。这一伙人的首领客气地告诉他，这篇话是他求全城有名的饱学书记写的，破费了数目可观的几个钱——数目也报了。他要是能拿出更好的来，那才怪哩，云云。「再者，」他添上一句，那一副憨态里的精明，叫萨义德都替他打官司的对手眼热——「再者，俺早在官老爷的仆役们身上铺了些钱，这里还有一大注，是预备在堂上花的。俺赢是赢定了。」
「没跑！」两个同伴齐声搭腔，一个热心，一个带着点阴沉沉的叫板。
「没跑——一丝一毫的跑也没有。」萨义德随声附和；听见年轻人敞开袍子给他看钱袋里叮当一响，他的做派忽然恭敬起来。
斋饭吃净了，他叫算账。客店主人现身——一个非常肥的男人，穿一身靓蓝的袍子，袍子底下露出脏白的长裤直到脚踝，一双红到顶的红拖鞋，一条五彩的腰带非但一点儿收不住他的块头，反倒在他顶扎眼的那一处坦然地鼓了出来。他的缠头巾绣得很阔，却又旧又暗。他的气派端得足，却也客气——正配一个殷实的店家，向一位来路不明的客人收账。他禀告埃芬迪：一共十二个皮阿斯特。

===Original===

After a little fruitless haggling, which only served to hurt the feelings of mine host and turn him to a boulder of dignity, Saïd discharged his debt and took leave of the hopeful litigant and his supporters.
Passing out into the stable he found the bare-legged lad of last night zealously brushing his nag’s mane and flanks. At a word he left work and fetched the saddle and bridle from a heap of trappings in a nook of the wall.
A group of camels were being laden from a heap of bales which stood piled round one of the pillars. The cursing of their drivers, three in number, was very lusty, as they made them kneel, then rise, and kneel again, to get them into position. The foremost of them, already accommodated with a load, stood across the doorway, blocking it. An oath from Saïd, ably seconded by the bare-legged stable-boy, called forth a perfect storm from the camel-drivers, one of whom ran forward and led the unwieldy beast to one side. The horse was taken out on to the causeway. Allah, who was being invoked within the archway to blast and utterly destroy the father, religion, and offspring of the half-dozen camels there lading, was humbly asked to increase Saïd’s wealth as that worthy rode off leaving a trifle in the brown palm of the hostler.
The long, roofed bazaar, from which others just like it branched to right and left, was already busy with people going to their day’s work. A coolness of the empty night still hung in its shadow, but that shadow was no longer grey and thin, but blue and deep, telling of a young sun reddening the roofs above. It was early yet to think of selling his horse; so Saïd rode forward at his ease, bent on viewing the city, taking this turning or that as fancy prompted.
Stalls were opening everywhere in the shady markets. Shutters were opened, bars removed, goods displayed. Merchants were settling themselves in dim nooks like caverns behind their wares. The ways were choked with a humming, gaily-coloured crowd. Cries of “*Oäh! Oäh!* Look out on your right﻿—on your left!” came in shrill tones or hoarse, as men with asses or mules forced a way through the press.

===Chinese===

白还了一阵价，毫无用处，只叫店家伤了感情、变成一块威严的顽石，萨义德清了账，辞了那位志在必得的讼师和他的啦啦队。
穿过马厩出来，他看见昨夜那个光腿小子正起劲地刷他那匹马的鬃毛和胁腹。一声招呼，小子摺下活计，从墙旮旯一堆鞍辔里取来鞍子和辔头。
一群骆驼正围着一根柱子码放的货包装货。三个赶驼人叫它们跪下、起来、再跪下、好就位，那顿咒骂骂得叫一个足。头一峰已经驮妥当的，横在门道口，堵了个严实。萨义德一句咒骂，光腿的马倌得力地帮腔，引出赶驼人一阵劈头盖脸的风暴，其中一个抢上来，把那笨重的东西牵到一旁。马牵上了石板道。拱门里头，正有人搬出安拉来，要他降雷殛诛那六峰骆驼主人的爹、教门和子嗣；一转脸，又恭恭敬敬求安拉增添萨义德的财富——那位好汉蹬鞍上路，往马夫棕色的手心里摺了点小费。
长长一带带顶的巴扎——左右又分出一条条一模一样的——已经热闹起来，人们各自奔一天的活计。空夜的凉意还挂在它的荫里；可那荫不再是灰而薄，而是蓝而深，透出上头一个初升的、正烧红屋顶的太阳。这会儿想着卖马还早，萨义德便信马由缰地往前逛，一心要看一看这座城，兴致往哪儿拐就往哪儿拐。
荫凉的市场里摊子处处开张。门板下了，闸条撤了，货色摆出来了。商人们安置在货后头暗沉沉的小窝里，像洞穴。道上塞满了嗡嗡的、花花绿绿的人潮。「哇赫！哇赫！右边看——左边看！」的喊声，尖的粗的此起彼伏——赶驴赶骡的人们从人堆里硬挤出一条路。

===Original===

Sweet, languorous odours, wafted from the shop of a vendor of perfumes, a whiff of musk from the shroud of some passing woman, the fragrance of tobacco, a dewy breath of the gardens from a mule’s panniers crammed with vegetables﻿—little puffs of sweetness were alternate in Saïd’s nostrils with the reek of dirty garments and ever-perspiring humanity, with vile stenches from dark entries, where all that is foulest of death and decay was flung to glut the scavenger dogs that slept, full-gorged, by dozens in every archway and along every wall. Saïd inhaled sweet and foul alike with a relish as part of the city’s enchantment.
He looked about him as he rode with wondering delight, shouting always “*Oäh! Oäh!*” as a warning to the multitude whose din drowned the clatter of hoofs. The greatness and the glory of it surpassed his dreams. Here was a whole bazaar wide, long and lofty, possessed exclusively by the workers in precious metals; another by the sweetmeat sellers; a third by those who inlay wood with mother-of-pearl; a fourth by those who sell rugs﻿—rich carpets of all the hues of the garden, of every make, from Bukhra and Khorassan, from Mecca and Baghdad and El Ajem. In one street he caught glimpses, through mean doorways, of precious stuffs, fine silks embossed and embroidered, the work of a lifetime. In the next there was nothing but the noise of grinding, chiselling and planing as the joiners squatted at their work, with the breath of the crowd in their faces.
He passed out of the shade of the covered bazaars and came at length to a place where the sun shone blinding on the ornate gateway of a mosque. Doves wheeled overhead about a tall and graceful minaret, which tapered dazzling white upon the dazzling blue, pointing to the heart of the great sapphire dome, to the throne of Allah himself. Through the archway he could see a flock of them strutting and pecking on the mosaic pavement of a cloistered court. Their cooing brought the inner stillness to him in spite of the noisy crowd, like a voice in a bubble of silence.
He rode on, rejoicing in the fierce sunlight and the peaceful shadows, in all the busy throng around him.
It began to be very hot, and he had been long riding.

===Chinese===

一缕一缕的甜香、懒香，从香水铺子那边飘来；过路女人寿衣里的一鼻子麝香；烟草的芬芳；驮骡驮筐里挤满蔬菜、透出园子里带露的一口清新——这些小阵的甜，跟脏衣裳和永远在出汗的人味轮着灌萨义德的鼻孔，跟黑洞洞的门洞里冒出的恶臭轮着来——那门洞里，死亡和腐烂最脏的一切都摞出来，喂那些清道夫野狗，它们吃得撑足，成打地睡在每个拱门下、每堵墙根。香的臭的，萨义德一概咂着滋味吸进去，当它是这座城魔力的一部分。
他一路骑，一路惊奇地东张西望，嘴里不停地喊「哇赫！哇赫！」，警告那一片人海——人声的嘈杂早淹了蹄声。这城的宏大和荣华超出了他的梦。这儿是一整条巴扎，宽、长、高，全叫打金银的匠人占了；另一条全归卖甜食的；第三条归拿珠母镶嵌木器的；第四条归卖毯子的——花园里一切色调的地毯，一切织工，布哈拉的和呼罗珊的，麦加的、巴格达的和埃勒阿杰姆的。这一条街上，他从寒磘的门洞里瞥见贵重的料子、压花刺绣的细绸，一辈子的活计。下一条街上只有一片磨、凿、刨的响动，木匠们蹲在活计前，人潮的呼吸喷在脸上。
他出了带顶巴扎的荫凉，末了走到一处地方：太阳晃眼地照着一座清真寺装潢华贵的门楼。鸽群绕着一座高挑秀美的宣礼塔盘旋，塔在炫目的蓝上削出炫目的白，直指那颗巨大的青玉穹顶的心——指安拉自己的宝座。透过拱门，他望见又一群鸽子在回廊院子镶嵌的地面上大摇大摆地啄食。它们的咕咕声穿过闹哄哄的人声，把里头那片静送到他耳边，像裹在一个静默的泡里的一声人语。
他接着骑，欢喜着烈日的光、平安的影，欢喜着身边整片忙碌的人潮。
天开始很热了，他也骑了很久。

===Original===

The cry of a certain vendor of iced drinks, who was elbowing his way through the crowd, clasping a huge bottle of greenish-yellow fluid and clinking two cups together as cymbals, was like the voice of an angel calling him.
“O snow of the mountain! How pure art thou, and how cold! O juice of the lemon! how refreshing when mingled cunningly with sugar as in my bottle! O drink of paradise, who could refuse thee? May Allah have pity on him who drinks not of this cup!”
Saïd drank of it and smacked his lips afterwards. In truth it was refreshing. He paid the smallest of coins﻿—it was all the ministering angel asked for his elixir﻿—into the dirtiest of hands, and received the parting blessing.
“May Allah have mercy on thy belly!”
Then he bethought him that it was time he took some steps toward selling his horse. He had been quite happy till then, drifting with the tide of inclination, having no aim beyond sightseeing. But the moment he came to harbour a definite purpose he felt crestfallen and ill at ease. The multitude, with which he had but now mingled lovingly as a brother, seemed to fall back from him of a sudden, becoming heartless and indifferent. He felt bewildered as his eyes strayed over numberless eager faces, seeking some person not too busy to answer a question. All at once, even as he drew rein irresolute, his hand was seized and kissed, and a man’s voice hailed him with cheerful deference.
“May thy day be happy, O my master!”
“May thy day be happy and blessed!” returned Saïd, graciously.
It was Selìm, the muleteer who had been his guide to the khan. The encounter was timely. Saïd straightway questioned him as to the best place for a man to go who was wishful to sell a horse to the best advantage. Selìm had the whole day on his hands. On his head, he was at Saïd’s service. He would lead him to a place which had not its like in all the world for horse-selling; it was the lord of all such places, by Allah! He would not conduct the effendi to a low place, of which there were many﻿—no, by his beard, but to the best of all. He had a great respect for the effendi, and, to be sure, the horse was a good horse, deserving to be sold in the best market.
