Saïd’s eyes caught fire from the speaker’s. Grasping the iron, he sprang to his feet. “Ready!” he cried; and with a bound like a wild beast’s they cleared the threshold together.
A live stream filled the alley﻿—a torrent of men and boys; all with the murder-light in their eyes, all flourishing weapons, all racing in one direction. The current caught them and swept them along.
“In case we be sundered in the tumult,” breathed Mustafa, “meet me in the place thou knowest﻿—in the secret place of our treasure among the gardens﻿—at the hour of sunset. Forget not!”
Saïd turned his head to answer; but the old man was torn away from him in a sudden eddy of the human tide to avoid the frantic kicking of a donkey which held the middle of the causeway. He found himself roughly shouldered between two Drûz of giant build, clad in the black-and-white cloak and white linen turban of their tribe. Each had a long-barrelled gun slung across his back and a knife in his hand. They ran steadily, with teeth clenched and eyes full of a grim purpose, hustling Saïd along with them unawares.
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!”﻿ ﻿… The mountaineers, though unbelievers, joined lustily in the cry of El Islâm. They had come fifty miles in pursuit of their quarry and now they had run him to earth. “Dìn ’hammed!”

===Chinese===

「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」……「安拉！安拉！」……「叫不信教的死！」……「叫拿撒勒教徒灭亡！」
萨义德醒来，一束日光裹着一场骇人的喧嚣从敞开的门里灌进来。全城泡在里头——像裹在一层雾里。为安拉和先知狂热的呐喊、魔鬼似的欢呼怪叫，混着外头街上大队人马的奔流，远处一通鼓，一阵零落的枪声。
店堂里黑沉沉，人已空了，只剩老叫花子立在他头顶，健全那只手里挥着一把镰刀似的弯刀，枯手指着萨义德手边地上的一截铁棍。他眼睛里探出一个凶神。
「起来，懒骨头！」他狂笑着喊。「这是睡觉偷懒的时候吗？来，出去！有血可放！——血——异教徒的血，像水一样冲刷街道！啊哈，城里的狗今儿晚上要喝上好酒了！」
萨义德的眼睛叫说话人的眼睛点着了。他抓起铁棍，一跃而起。「走着！」他喊；两人一齐一个兽跳，蹿过了门槛。
巷子里灌满一条活的洪流——男人和男孩的急流；人人眼里都是杀光，人人挥着家伙，人人朝一个方向狂奔。激流卷起他们，一并冲走。
「万一乱军里冲散了，」穆斯塔法贴着他耳朵说，「到你知道的那个地方碰头——园子里藏宝的秘密地方——日落时分。别忘了！」
萨义德回头要应；一阵人潮的漩涡猛地把老头从他身边卷开，去躲一头在道当中撒欢尥蹶子的驴。他自己也冷不丁叫人夹在两个大个子德鲁兹人中间，粗粗地挤着走。两人都披着本族黑白条纹的大氅，缠白布巾，背上一杆长筒枪，手里一把刀。他们咬着牙稳稳地跑，眼里一团森然的目的，不知不觉把萨义德也裹挟了去。
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」……山里人虽是异教徒，喊伊斯兰的口号喊得也起劲。他们追他们的猎物追了五十里，如今把它追到了窝。「丁·罕默德！」

===Original===

a child’s voice piped manfully; and Saïd beheld a little boy in a man’s arms, brandishing a toy knife as he was borne along, crowing for joy of the merry race and the shouting. There was a stoppage in front; but those behind still continued to push on, regardless of the protests of such as were tall enough to see the nature of the obstacle.
The giant on Saïd’s right proclaimed that certain persons of authority were sorting the crowd, sending some this way, others that, to join bands already at work. He licked his lips as he added that he himself had slain fifty Maronites between the first hour and the fourth, at the taking of Zahleh. By Allah, it was the business to whet a man’s appetite. He remembered to have eaten a whole sheep that day﻿—to have rent it limb from limb and devoured it yet warm and uncooked, he was so hungry. But his remarks were lost for the most part in the general uproar.
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!﻿ ﻿…” Saïd was past the obstacle, speeding over the rough pavement of a lane in shadow. The sky, a narrow streamer of living blue, seemed to flutter and wave overhead as he ran with throbbing brow and panting chest. With the two Drûz and a hundred others he was told off to join a part of the mob who were gone to raze the house of the Muscovite Consul, whose ill-timed meddling had fired the people. The two Drûz lost their eagerness.
“What have we to do with this Frank?” Saïd heard one say to the other. “Let us turn﻿—what sayest thou? Our enemies are yonder!”
“True,” breathed the other; and they slackened so as to drop behind.
The house of the Consul was already in flames when Saïd’s reinforcement came up. Little pillars and wreaths of brown smoke curled upward from it, to condense in a low cloud like a frown upon the tranquil sky. A seething, roaring throng, close-packed from wall to wall, choked every approach. By mounting on a high stone beside a doorway Saïd contrived to see what was doing.
Furniture and other goods, which the greed of the insurgents had dragged from the burning house, were being tossed back into the blaze by order of an aged man invested with some sort of authority. This person seemed some prophet or dervìsh﻿—a holy man in any case, for he was naked save for a loose shirt of sackcloth, and his legs and arms were almost black through long exposure.

===Chinese===

一个孩子的嗓音雄赳赳地尖出来；萨义德看见一个小男孩叫人抱在男人怀里，一路被扛着跑，挥一把玩具刀，为这场快活的赛跑和呐喊咯咯直乐。前头堵住了；后头的还照旧往前挤，全不管那些个头够高、看得清障碍是什么的人的抗议。
萨义德右边那个大个子说，有几位当权的正在分派人手，把人一批拨这头、一批拨那头，去并入已在动手的各伙。他舔着嘴唇补充：他自己在攻扎赫勒那天，头一个钟头到第四个钟头之间宰了五十个马龙派。凭安拉说，这营生最开人的胃口。他记得那天吃了一整只羊——生撕活掰，趁热血没凉生吞了，饿成那样。可他的高论大半叫全场的大喧嚣吞了。
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！……」萨义德过了障碍，在一条荫凉小巷的粗石路上飞奔。头顶那条窄窄的活蓝的天，仿佛随着他嗡嗡的额头、喘喘的胸口在飘在摆。他跟那两个德鲁兹人，还有一百来人，被拨去增援另一伙暴民——那伙去平莫斯科领事的宅子：他那个不合时宜的插手早把众人点着了。两个德鲁兹人蔫了。
「俺们跟这法兰克人有什么相干？」萨义德听见一个对另一个说。「回头吧——你说呢？俺们的仇人在那边！」
「说的是。」另一个呼出一口气；两人一放慢，落到了后头。
萨义德这支增援赶到时，领事的宅子已经着了。一缕缕棕烟盘着卷上去，凝成一朵低云，像晴天上蹙起的一道眉。翻滚咆哮的人团，从墙到墙挤得严实，堵死了一切进路。萨义德攀上一家门边的一块高石，才算看清了局面。
暴民的贪心从着火的宅子里拖出来的家具细软，正叫一个身负某种权柄的老人指挥着，一件一件抛回火里。这位像是哪路先知或德尔维希——反正是个圣人：他只披一件粗麻布的宽衫，胳膊腿叫日头晒得快成黑炭。

===Original===

He capered about in a solemn measure, screaming, praising Allah, and exhorting the faithful to fresh exertions.
There was a movement on the outskirts of the crowd. Where was the good in standing idle, looking on at the prowess of others, when there was work enough for every man that day?
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!”﻿ ﻿… Even to Saïd’s maddened brain it occurred that there was some rough order in the mob. A band of butchers were there in their slaughterhouse garb, with long knives dripping blood not of beasts. Men forced their way into homes, he among them, upsetting costly furniture, trampling rich carpets in their zeal to seize on the inmates. These they spat upon, spurned, insulted and dragged out into the street, where the aforesaid butchers waited to despatch them.
Girls were embraced brutally and borne shrieking away in the arms of men whose clothing was bespattered with the blood of a father or mother. Crones strained and knotted their wizened throats in supplication for the spark of life that yet warmed them. Dwellings were looted, then set on fire. Saïd, in his search of the house of a rich merchant, saw a foot peeping out from a heap of bedding. He laid hold of it and, pulling with a will, elicited an old, white-bearded man whose face was grey with terror. He shrieked to Miriam, Mother of God, to help him; but Saïd had him fast by the throat, thin and grisly as a hen’s, and soon pitched him headlong down a short flight of stone steps. He toppled senseless at the feet of one of the butchers, who, being idle for the moment, knifed him at once.
The thought of Ferideh, awaiting his further pleasure in the safe keeping of old Nûr, filled the fisherman with a kind of drunken joy. She had bitten his arm last night and the wound pained him yet. What matter! There would be plenty of leisure to punish and tame her by-and-by. She would learn to worship him in the beautiful house he would build for her out of her father’s hoard. His brain whirled. He had the strength of two men. He saw all things in the redness of eyelids closed against the sun; felt and cared for nothing save the lust of blood and the joy of killing.﻿ ﻿… “Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!”
A sound of firing came out of the distance﻿—a single volley followed by faint cries.

===Chinese===

他踩着一套庄严的步子蹦跳，尖声赞颂安拉，激励信士们再接再厉。
人群外圈起了一阵骚动。那天人人有活干，干站着看别人的勇力，算个什么？
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」……连萨义德烧昏的脑子里也闪过一念：这暴民里有一种粗粝的章法。有一伙屠户，穿着屠宰场的行头，长刀上滴的不是牲口的血。人们破门入宅，他也在内，掀翻贵重家具，踩着厚地毯，抢捉屋里的人。捉到的，啐、踹、辱，拖到街上，交那前述的屠户发落。
姑娘们叫人粗暴地搂住，尖叫着被抱走——抱她们的人衣裳上溅着她们爹娘的血。老太婆们拧着干瘪的喉咙哀求，为怀里那点还温着的命星。住宅抢净了，就放火烧。萨义德搜一座富商的宅子，看见一堆铺盖里露出一只脚。他揪住那只脚，狠命一拽，拽出一个白胡子老头，脸吓得灰白。老头尖声求天主之母米利暗救他；可萨义德已经掐住他的喉咙——细得像鸡脖子，咯咯响——随即一把把他掼下一截短石阶。他无知无觉地栽到一个屠户脚边；那屠户正闲着，一刀就结果了。
想到费丽黛——在老努尔稳稳的手里候他下一步摆布——渔夫心里灌满一种醉了的欢喜。她昨夜咬了他的胳膊，伤口还疼呢。疼就疼！往后有的是闲工夫调教她、驯她。等他拿她爹囤的财宝给她盖起漂亮宅子，她自会学着敬他如神。他的脑子打着旋。他一个人有两人的力气。他看见的一切都带上了对着太阳闭紧的眼皮的那层红；除了血的馋、杀的乐，他什么也觉不出、什么也不管。……「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」
远处传来一阵枪声——一排齐射，跟着是微弱的哭喊。

===Original===

One or two strained ears to listen; but the hoarse shouts of the slayers and piercing shrieks of their victims made it hard to ascertain noises more remote. Zeal continued unabated. Men, women and children were dragged out of the shadowy doorways to be hacked to death on the causeway beneath the ribbon of peaceful blue sky which the smoke of burning houses began to veil in part. The mob jeered and reviled their last agonies. Some were found to spit in the faces of the newly slain. And the name of Allah was in every man’s mouth.
Of a sudden a tremor ran through the multitude. The uproar dwindled to a murmur, above which terrified cries were heard, growing louder and nearer.
“The soldiers!”﻿—“The soldiers have scattered us!”﻿—“Allah destroy them!”﻿—“They have killed Ahmed, my brother!”﻿—“I am wounded even to death!”
The broken remnant of some other band poured headlong from the arched entry of a by-street and made haste to mingle and lose themselves in the stagnant crowd which choked their way. They came running, beards on shoulders, faces blanched with fright, and slipped in among the throng as a lizard slips under a stone for safety.
The butchers stayed their hands and wiped their knives on the skirts of their clothing. The feeders poured out of doorways to hear the news. Saïd struck a squealing Nazarene on the head with his iron bar and looked out from the lattice of the upper storey where he found himself. He glanced down upon the press of dark fezes and light turbans in fierce sunlight and plum-coloured shadow. The sea of heads rolled purposeless like beads unstrung from a chaplet. All at once a yell of rage uprose.
“The soldiers!﻿—Allah cut their lives!﻿—The soldiers!﻿—let us slay them!﻿—Let us fly!﻿—Let us stone them to death who favour the infidels!” At the street end, where there was a great pool of sunlight, Saïd caught the glint of gun-barrels and recognised the uniform of the irregular troops. He saw a sword flash as an officer of high rank flourished it; and through all the cursing of the mob he heard a word of command, short and gruff like the grunt of a pig. A howl of execration rent the air. The front rank of the troops were taking deliberate aim at the rioters.
Saïd beheld the surging sea of heads with the unconcerned pity of an angel or a sage. Packed close as they were down there, every shot must tell.

===Chinese===

有一两个竖起耳朵听；可杀人的嘶喊、遭杀的惨叫太吵，远些的动静辨不清。热忱一点儿不减。男人、女人、孩子从黑洞洞的门洞里拖出来，在石板道上剁碎——头顶那条安详的蓝天，正叫焚烧房屋的烟半掩起来。暴民拿他们的临终抽搐取笑咒骂。有人朝新杀的脸上啐唾沫。安拉的尊名挂在每个人嘴上。
忽然，一阵战栗传遍人海。喧嚣落成一片嗡嗡，嗡嗡之上腾起惊惶的叫喊，越来越响、越来越近。
「兵！」——「兵把俺们冲散了！」——「叫安拉灭他们！」——「他们杀了俺兄弟艾哈迈德！」——「俺伤得要死了！」
不知哪一伙的残兵败将，从一条小街的拱道里没头没脑涌出来，急急插进堵塞去路的一潭死水里，要把自己藏掉。他们跑着，胡子甩上肩膀，脸吓成白纸，像蜥蜴钻石头缝似的溜进人堆。
屠户们住了手，拿衣襟擦刀。正吃食的人们从门洞里涌出来打听。萨义德拿铁棍敲爆了一个尖叫的拿撒勒教徒的脑袋，这才发觉自己在一层楼的窗棂后，便从那里朝外望。他俯瞰烈日和梅子色阴影里那一片黑毡帽、白缠头的挤压。人头的海无目的地滚，像从念珠串上散落的珠子。骤然间一片怒吼腾起。
「兵！——叫安拉收他们的命！——兵！——宰了他们！——跑吧！——拿石头砸死这些帮异教徒的！」街口那一大片阳光里，萨义德瞥见枪管的寒光，认出非正规军的号衣。一位高阶军官挥剑一闪；隔着暴民的一片咒骂，他听见一声口令，短促粗嘎，像猪的哼哧。一阵诛绝的嚎叫撕开空气。军队的前排正朝暴民从容瞄准。
萨义德俯望那翻腾的人头之海，带着天使或圣哲那种不动心的怜悯。底下挤得那样密，一枪一个准。

===Original===

He gave warm praise to Allah Most High, who had placed His servant in that upper chamber, whence he could observe all that passed without peril.
Then he saw a strange sight. The rabble had shrunk back before the muzzles of the rifles covering them. Across the space of pavement thus deserted rushed the wild figure he had observed before the Consul’s house. The holy one ran up to the officer and confronted him with gestures of command and entreaty.
“Shall Muslim war with Muslim?” A shrill voice rang clear on the hush which ensued. “Will you then separate yourselves from the cause of Allah and His Apostles to side with pagans and idolaters? Will you shoot down the servants of the Highest like dogs? I heard a voice in the night saying, Go to the city, Es-Shâm, and tell the dwellers there: The word of Allah to such as are faithful. Slay me the unbelievers which aspire to sit in high places! Slay the whole race of them, the child with the strong man, the woman giving suck with the aged one whose eyes are dim! Let not a soul of them remain alive, for the welfare of Islâm is in it!﻿—Will you then anger the Praiseworthy? Will you then.﻿ ﻿…”
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!”
The words of the saint were drowned in a shout which thrilled Saïd to the marrow and made tears start in his eyes. The officer took a written paper embodying his orders and tore it to little pieces. The soldiers flung down their rifles with a great noise. With frantic exclamations the crowd surged towards them, enveloped them, embraced them and made them one with it. The Colonel waved his sword on high, shouting for Allah and the Prophet. It was who should kiss his hand, his scabbard, his clothing﻿—anything that was his.
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!﻿ ﻿…” The mob, thus reinforced, set to work once more. “To the French convent!” someone shouted. “Let the nuns be ravished and then slain!” The cry was taken up on all hands with laughter and coarse jibes. “The nuns! The nuns!” “Aha, the nuns are sweet!” “They have kept their flower for us, the darlings!” “Let us see how the nuns are fashioned!”
There was a breathless rush, of sheep following blindly the track of an unseen leader. Saïd was more than once crushed against a wall of the narrow ways they traversed; but he was stalwart and held his own. Then there was a standstill.

===Chinese===

他热烈赞颂至高的安拉，把他的仆人安顿在这间楼上，看尽一切又不担一丝风险。
随后他看见一场奇景。乌合之众在罩住他们的枪口前缩了回去。让出的那片石板地上，冲过一个人影——正是他在领事宅前见过的那个狂人。圣人直奔军官，以命令和恳求的手势迎面对住他。
「穆斯林要同穆斯林开战么？」一阵肃静里，一个尖嗓音字字清亮。「你们要这样脱离安拉和他使者的道，去帮异教徒、拜偶像的么？你们要像打狗一样打倒至高者的仆人么？俺夜里听见一个声音说：你往大马士革城去，告诉那里的居民：安拉对信士之言。替俺杀了那些想攀高位的异教徒！把他们全族杀净，孩子连同壮汉，奶孩子的连同眼花的老人！一个活口不留，伊斯兰的福祉在此！——你们要惹怒受赞颂的主么？你们要——」
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」
圣人的话淹在一声呐喊里，那呐喊震得萨义德骨髓发麻，眼泪都迸出来。军官掏出载着他命令的一纸文书，撕了个粉碎。士兵们哐当哐当把枪撂了。人群狂呼着涌向他们，裹住他们，抱住他们，同他们化成了一体。上校把剑举到头顶，高呼安拉和先知。人人抢着亲他的手、他的刀鞘、他的衣裳——他身上随便什么。
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！……」得了增援的暴民重新开工。「上法国修道院！」有人喊。「叫修女们先受用再杀！」这声喊四面八方应起来，夹着淫笑和脏话。「修女！修女！」「啊哈，修女可人！」「她们替俺们留着花呢，小宝贝们！」「瞧瞧修女长的是什么样子！」
一阵闭气的狂奔，羊群似的盲目跟着一个看不见的引领。在穿过的窄道里，萨义德不止一回叫人挤扁在墙上；可他身子骨硬，站得住。随后又是一顿。

===Original===

Those in front hammered at a strong door, while those behind stood on tiptoe and craned their necks to see what was doing.
All at once there was a backward movement. Another panic got hold of the crowd. A cry, “The soldiers!” was again raised; but was received with jeers by such of the mob as were of that calling. A small troop of armed men rode up to the door of the nunnery. They were seen plainly of all, towering as they did on horseback above the seething mass on foot. Most of them rode their chargers at the foremost, who drew back in alarm; while a few, among whom was the leader, dismounted and entered the convent, the door of which was promptly opened to them.
A mighty roar went up from the multitude.
“It is Abdul Cader!”﻿—“May Allah preserve his Grace!”﻿—“He goes to take vengeance upon his enemies!”﻿—“It was the French who wronged and imprisoned him, though he fought them brave as a lion!”﻿—“He is come to claim the French nuns for his harìm!”﻿—“Allah is just!”﻿—“May all the Franks perish, and their women be dishonoured!”﻿—“Long live the might of Islâm!”﻿—“May Allah preserve Abdul Cader, the glory of the Faith!”
But applause was turned to oaths and howls of rage when the hero and his officers reappeared, escorting with respect a train of black-robed nuns, each with the obnoxious cross shining on her bosom. The horsemen closed around them as a bodyguard; the leaders sprang to their saddles. Then the fury of the crowd broke all bounds. The coolness of the rescuers as they rode away had a point of contempt which stung the rout to madness.
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!”﻿ ﻿… “Death to the enemies of Allah!”﻿ ﻿… “Who dares protect those whose lives are forfeit to Islâm!”﻿ ﻿… “Perish Abdul Cader!”﻿ ﻿… “Death to the traitors of Eljizar.” Raging like a winter-torrent, the crowd surged forward in pursuit. The horsemen were constrained to a foot’s pace, having regard to the women in their midst. The mob was close upon them. Stones and other missiles began to whizz through the air. Of a sudden the whole mass swayed back, every man jostling his neighbour.
Abdul Cader had turned his horse about and was sitting motionless, his eyes ranging sternly over the sea of turbaned heads and swarthy, malignant faces. A last stone, flung at random from the heart of the throng, struck his arm and made him wince. He raised a hand to his tarbûsh, commanding silence.

===Chinese===

前头的砸一扇结实的大门，后头的踮脚伸脖子瞧动静。
忽然人潮往后一涌。又一阵惊惶攦住了人群。一声「兵！」又起；可暴民里当过兵的，报以嗤笑。一小队武装的人马驰到修道院门前。人人看得清楚——他们骑着马，高耸在沸腾的步人海之上。多数人把战马直逼前排，前排吓得直退；有几个，领头人在内，下马进了修道院——门随即为他们敞开。
人海里腾起一声巨吼。
「是阿卜杜勒·卡德尔！」——「叫安拉保他大人！」——「他去向他仇人讨债了！」——「委屈他、关他的就是法国人，他跟他们斗起来猛得像狮子！」——「他来收法国修女做他的内眷了！」——「安拉公道！」——「叫法兰克人全死绝，叫他们的女人受辱！」——「伊斯兰的威力万岁！」——「叫安拉保佑阿卜杜勒·卡德尔，信仰的光荣！」
可等那位英雄和他的军官重新现身，恭恭敬敬护着一列黑袍修女——每人胸口都亮着那可咒的十字——喝彩翻成了咒骂和狂怒的嚎叫。骑手们围拢成一个肉屏风；领头的翻身上鞍。人群的暴怒至此决了堤。救人们拨马而去时那一份镇定，带着一星轻蔑，把这一大帮刺得发了疯。
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」……「叫安拉的仇敌死！」……「谁敢护这些命该归伊斯兰的人！」……「叫阿卜杜勒·卡德尔完蛋！」……「叫埃尔吉扎的叛徒死！」人潮像冬汛一样暴怒地追压上去。骑队顾忌着夹在当中的女人，只得放步走。暴民已逼到近前。石头和各样的飞掷物嘕嘕掠空。骤然间整个人海往后一荡，人人挤撞着邻人。
阿卜杜勒·卡德尔拨转马头，一动不动地坐着，严峻的眼睛扫过缠头之海和黝黑恶毒的脸。从人堆当心胡乱飞来的最后一块石头打中他的胳膊，打得他一缩。他抬手按上毡帽，命令肃静。

===Original===

An awestricken hush spread like a breath over the crowd. This man was the established idol of the populace. He was the greatest living hero of Islâm, and at heart they gloried in his intrepidity.
“What is this, O my friends?” His voice rang out clear and measured. “Will you provoke the wrath of Allah against this city? Will you anger Him so that He turn away His face from us forever? It has been told you how I have fought for Islâm﻿—ay, and borne imprisonment and exile for our holy Faith. But I tell you I would rather be the meanest Christian slain this day in the sight of Allah than one of you whose hands are red with his blood. Shame on you, Muslimûn!﻿—Shame on you, I say! Would to Allah I had gone to my grave ere ever this day dawned for the Faith!”
He gazed for a moment, silent on the silent crowd; then, turning, set spurs to his horse and cantered away. But the foremost, among whom was Saïd, saw that his eyes glistened.
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!” It was the holy man who raised the shout once more, waving his gnarled brown arms above the crowd. “Who dares withstand the justice of Allah? Slay him also, who rescues the condemned of God! Onward! Dìn! Dìn!”
But the words of Abdul Cader had wrought a change in the temper of the multitude. Some there were who lagged behind. Saïd’s thirst for blood was somewhat slaked by this. There was time, he bethought him, to visit Ferideh and snatch a kiss from her before keeping his appointment with Mustafa. He slipped aside into an archway which gave access to a shady passage barely wide enough for two to walk abreast, and made his way by forsaken paths to the prison of his desire. And ever as he went the roar of the tumult was in his ears, now loud and near, now soft and melting in the distance, like the thunder of surf upon a rockbound coast:
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!”

===Chinese===

一片敬畏的肃静像一口气漫过人群。这个人是民众立定的偶像。他是伊斯兰在世最伟大的英雄，人人心底里都以他的无畏为荣。
「这是干什么，俺的朋友们？」他的嗓音清朗而有节。「你们要惹安拉向这座城发怒吗？要惹他从此永远扭过脸不看俺们吗？你们都听说过俺怎样为伊斯兰征战——是啊，还怎样为俺们神圣的信仰坐牢、流放。可俺告诉你们：俺宁可做今天在安拉眼前被杀的顶卑贱的基督徒，也不做你们中间一个双手染着他血的人。羞煞，穆斯林们！——俺说，羞煞！但愿安拉容俺早进坟墓，别叫俺看见信仰有这一天！」
他默默注视沉默的人群片刻；随后拨转马头，一磕马刺，小跑而去。可前排的人——萨义德在其中——看见他眼里有光闪动。
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」又是那位圣人扬起这一声，粗糙的棕胳膊在人海上挥舞。「谁敢挡安拉的公道？连这个搭救受神谴之人的，也一起杀了！前进！丁！丁！」
可阿卜杜勒·卡德尔的话在人海的性子里改出了一道岔。有些人落后了。萨义德杀的馋叫这个冲淡了几分。他想起，还来得及去看看费丽黛，趁赴穆斯塔法的约之前偷她一个吻。他闪身钻进一座拱门，门里一条荫凉过道，窄得只容两人并肩；他拣着无人的路径，一路奔他那欲望的牢笼。一路上，骚乱的轰响总在耳边，一时高而近，一时远而柔、化在远处，像大洋的浪在一个礁岸上打雷：
「丁！丁！丁·穆罕默德！」

### XXVI / 二十六
===Original===

For once Nûr was cross with Saïd. No sooner did she understand the reason of his coming than she lifted up her voice and chid him roundly. Upon his persisting, she threw herself in his way and forbade him to advance another step. The girl was ill enough already without the aggravation of his presence. If he so much as set foot in that upper room, she (Nûr) would cease to befriend him and would let the girl go free.
Cowed by her vehemence, Saïd grumbled that he had no instant wish to harm the maid; but was come just to see how she did; with much more, scarcely audible, about his own property, and kissing, and no sin. Whereupon the old woman became herself again, called him the light of her eyes, and detecting some telltale stains upon his raiment soaked a rag in a vessel of water and made haste to sponge it. The strong perfume of her unguents kept him quiet and submissive while she purified him. His eyes languished and his lips parted as he inhaled it.
Bending close to him over the task﻿—
“It is a kindness I do thee, O my soul!” she said. “Suppose soldiers or other slaves of authority met thee with the marks of blood on thy robe, by thy beard, I think it would fare ill with thee. As for that girl thou lovest, she has been all day like a madwoman. She is deaf to all my comfortable words, and cries ever to Allah that He should take her life. She boasts that she will beat herself to death against a stone of the wall sooner than endure thy embrace; that is why I stayed thee. Today is but the morrow of her disaster. Leave me alone to deal with her, and, after a few days, I warrant thou shalt find her tractable. When she is tame enough I shall send thee word. With thy share of the treasure of which Mustafa speaks, thou mayst well afford to hire a fine house for her. With a fine house and a gift in thy hand what girl could gainsay thee? For thou art handsome, my dear, straight as a palm-tree, strong as a lion. Does the work of slaughter flag that thou comest hither thus early?”
Saïd told her something of the day’s doings, while she, vowing that he must be famished, brought some bread and dried raisins from the inner room. He was in truth pretty hungry, though the fact had escaped his mind. His jaws worked as a busy mill to which grist came unfailingly by great handfuls.

===Chinese===

努尔这一回对赛义德动了气。她一明白他来意，就放声把他痛痛快快训了一顿。他再坚持，她就横身拦路，不许他再进一步。那姑娘已经病得不轻，犯不着再添他这一味。他只要踏进那间阁楼一步，她（努尔）就同他绝交，把姑娘放走。
叫她的气势镇住了，赛义德嘟嘟囔囔，说自己并不想立刻祸害那姑娘，不过是来看看她的情形；后头还有好些话，含含糊糊听不真，无非是他自己的产业呀、亲嘴呀、不算罪呀。这一来老太婆又做回了自己，唤他作她眼里的光，又瞅见他衣裳上有几处说嘴的血渍，忙在水罐里浸了块布，赶紧替他揩。她香膏的浓香熏着，他乖乖地任她拾擞，服服帖帖。吸着那香气，他眼睛发饧，嘴唇微张。
俯在他身上忙活着，她凑近他说——
「我这是疼你，我的魂儿！」她说。「倘若兵们或者官家的别的奴才撞见你袍子上带着血印，凭你的胡子说，我看你吃罪不起。至于你恋的那姑娘，她今天一整天像个疯婆子。我一句宽心话她也听不进，只朝安拉哭，求祂收她的命。她夸口说，宁可拿脑袋往墙石头上撞死，也不受你的抱——我拦你，就为这个。今天不过是她大难的第二天。交给我一个人对付她，不出几天，我保你见她服帖。等她驯得够了我给你捎信。凭穆斯塔法说的那份宝藏里你应得的，你满雇得起一座好宅子安置她。有好宅子，手里再提着礼，哪个姑娘驳得了你？你生得俊，亲爱的，挺得像椰枣树，壮得像狮子。杀伐的活计歇了，你怎么这个时辰就跑来了？」
赛义德对她讲了讲今天的事；她一口咬定他准饿坏了，从里间端来些饼和葡萄干。他确实相当饿，只是自己忘了。两片腮帮子像一盘忙碌的磨，大把大把的料源源不断地送进去。

===Original===

Nûr wished him two healths, and, squatting on her heels, kept her painted eyes fixed on him in a kind of dotage.
“I am sorry thou didst lose sight of Mustafa,” she said at length, speaking chiefly to herself. “He was ill yesterday in the evening﻿—very ill, so that I deemed him at the gate of death. Allah restore him to us in safety and good health!”
Saïd’s utterance was somewhat choked, his mouth being crammed with leathery bread.
“Hadst thou been with us in the tumult, O my eyes, thou wouldst not marvel that we were forced asunder,” he mumbled. “No man thought of his neighbour, but each ran alone for himself, taking care not to stumble lest the multitude behind should tread out his life. Praise be to Allah that He has granted me to see this day! Not a street of that quarter but has dark pools of blood on its pavement﻿—blood of the heathen, of the unbelievers, which to shed is a pious deed. At the hour of sunset I am bound to meet Mustafa in a place appointed among the gardens. O happy day!”
“In sh’Allah, thou wilt find him in the extremity of good health!” exclaimed Nûr, rising to prepare herself a narghileh. “As for the maiden, the daughter of Yuhanna, I have said that I will tame her for thee. Seek not to approach her until I send thee word. Prepare a fine house for her and bring a gift in thy hand. Force is one way to succeed, but there is a better, I do assure thee.”
The sun’s rays were red upon the upper roofs when Saïd left the cellar. He saw no man in the streets save such as were very old and feeble. Veiled women and girls, some with babies in their arms, stood chattering together in doorways or at the crossroads. They called to him for news.
In passing the tavern of Abu Khalìl, he beheld the fat host seated on a stool in the doorway, wide awake, his face expressive of the deepest disgust. He appeared to be afflicted with an itch in the calf of his leg, for he was scratching the place slowly and woefully with a shard.
“Peace on thee, O Abu Khalìl!” cried the fisherman as he sped by.
“Upon thee be the peace, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings!” retorted the taverner, with a dismal groan. “But say, why dost thou hasten? Stay a little and tell me, hast thou heard aught of my son?﻿—of Camr-ud-dìn? The villain escaped about the second hour. Doubtless, he is with the slayers﻿—curse his religion!

===Chinese===

努尔祝了他两遍康健，蹲坐在脚后跟上，一双画过的眼睛痴痴地盯着他。
「可惜你把穆斯塔法丢了，」她终于说，多半是自言自语。「他昨天傍晚就病了——病得重，我都当他到了死门关口。愿安拉叫他平平安安、健健康康回到我们跟前！」
赛义德的话音有点堵——嘴里塞满了牛皮似的干饼。
「你要是同我们一块儿在乱军里，我的眼珠儿，就不会奇怪我们怎么冲散了，」他咕哝着说。「谁也顾不上谁，个个只顾自己跑，还得留神别跌倒——后头的人潮能把人踩灭。赞美安拉，容我活着见到了这一天！那一区没有一条街的石面上不汪着黑血泊——异教徒的血、不信教者的血，流它正是敬虔的功课。日落时分，我约了穆斯塔法在园子里一个地方碰头。快活的日子！」
「托安拉的福，你见着他时，他好得没法再好！」努尔喊道，起身去给她自己备水烟。「至于那闺女，尤汉纳的女儿，我说过我替你驯她。我捎信之前，你休想去挨她。给她备一座好宅子，手里提上礼。硬来是一条路，可还有更好的一条，我真真告诉你。」
赛义德离开地窖时，日光红红地照在高处的屋顶上。街上一个男人也见不着，只除了一些老得走不动的。蒙着面的女人和姑娘，有的抱着奶娃，聚在门口或路口闲话。她们唤他问消息。
路过阿布·哈利勒的酒馆，他看见胖掌柜坐在门口小凳上，人醒得十足，一脸的深恶痛绝。他像是小腿肚发痒，正拿一片碎瓦，慢慢地、愁苦地挠那地方。
「愿你平安，阿布·哈利勒！」渔夫打门前飞过时喊。
「也愿你平安，并安拉的慈悯和祂的福！」掌柜回敬，惨惨地哼了一声。「可你说，你急什么？站一站，告诉我，听到我儿子的消息没有？——卡姆鲁丁那小子？约摸第二个钟头他溜了。多半同杀人的一伙去了——咒他的教门！

===Original===

and behold there is none left to serve in the house, his mother being sick this day. Wait a minute, I say﻿—may thy house be destroyed!”
But Saïd only cried “Allah comfort thee!” over his shoulder as he hurried on. The thought of Mustafa and the treasure lent wings to his feet. Besides, it seemed a small matter that Abu Khalìl should lack his son’s help that day, seeing it was a dull time of business, all likely customers keeping festival elsewhere. A surge-like roar was ever in his ears, loud or distant according to the trend of the streets he traversed.
Turning a sharp corner, he collided with a man in as great a hurry as himself. The shock was very great. Saïd rubbed himself ruefully, and so did the stranger. They were about to curse each other and pursue their several ways when recognition turned their gall to honey. The fisherman blessed Selìm, and Selìm blessed the fisherman. They embraced, and Saïd, having a view to his own profit, inquired with what eye his Excellency, the Wâly, deigned to regard the disturbance.
“Alas!” cried the other, lifting hands and eyes towards as much of the purpled heaven as was visible between the roof-lines, “my lord is distraught with grief. The Franks ply him ever with angry demands that he take instant measures to put down the tumult. Allah knows that he has done all that was in his power to do. The garrison was divided in two companies,4 and sent forth with orders to fire on the rebels without mercy. One division with its officers deserted to the people; the other, after firing one volley and wreaking great havoc, was withdrawn lest they too should make common cause with the insurgents. The Council was summoned, and Ahmed Basha signed with his own hand a paper declaring that the Government can do nothing. He sent an express for Abdul Cader, but was refused because Abdul Cader and all his followers were busy rescuing great numbers of the Nazarenes and conveying them by families to the castle. He invited the Basha to bring but fifty armed men and ride with him, saying that with so small a reinforcement as that he would undertake to quell the riot in a few hours.
“It was Selìm who was charged with the message, and I would to Allah it had been some other.

===Chinese===

「你瞧，家里一个跑腿的也不剩，他娘今天又病着。站一站，我说——叫你房子塌了！」
赛义德只回头喊了声「安拉慰你！」，照旧赶路。穆斯塔法和宝藏的念头给他的脚添了翅膀。再说，阿布·哈利勒那天缺个儿子帮衬，也算不得什么——生意正清淡，会光顾的主顾都在别处过节呢。
一片浪涛般的吼声一直在他耳边，随着所穿街巷的走向，或近或远。
拐过一个急弯，他同一个同样匆忙的人撞了个满怀。这一撞不轻。赛义德龇牙咧嘴地揉，那陌生人也揉。两人正要对骂一句、各奔各路，认出来了，胆汁化成了蜜。渔夫祝福塞利姆，塞利姆祝福渔夫。两人拥抱；赛义德打着自家的算盘，请教瓦利大人陛下垂顾这场乱子，是何观感。
「唉！」对方双手举眼，朝着屋檐之间望得见的那一线紫天，喊道，「我家老爷悲愤得没了主张。弗兰克人拿一道道怒函催他，要他即刻弹压。安拉知道，力所能及的他都做了。守军分作两队，派出去，奉令对乱民格杀勿论。一队连同军官倒戈到了民众一边；另一队放了一排枪、造了大杀孽之后，也撤了回来——怕他们也同乱党合流。议事会传齐了，艾哈迈德帕夏亲笔签署了一纸文书，声明官府无能为力。他派专差去请阿卜杜勒·卡迪尔，被回绝了——阿卜杜勒·卡迪尔和他全班人马正忙着救大批的拿撒勒人，一家一家往城堡里送。他请帕夏只带五十名武装随他去，说单添这一小支援兵，他保几个钟头平了这场乱。
「送这封口信的差使偏偏落在塞利姆头上。我巴不得安拉叫别人去。

===Original===

For my lord began to weep and wring his hands, being, as I guess, afraid for his life to ride forth, yet ashamed to play the coward in the sight of an old lion like Abdul Cader. Before I left his presence he took a leaf of paper and began to draw upon it what seemed a plan of the city, crying, ‘Thus and thus it should have been. So and so I should have acted.’ It was as though the squeak of the reed on the leaf brought comfort to him. Poor great man! I tell thee, my heart was sick for pity of him. All in the palace agree that the Franks will have him slain for this hesitation which is his infirmity.
“I go now to buy a little food for those who have taken refuge in the palace-yard. There is a great crowd, and who can tell how long the slaughter will last? Many must die of hunger, and that is not pleasant to see in the court of the house where one dwells. To slay a foe in anger, and his woman, and his sons and daughters, is natural for all the Franks say. It is natural that a man should seek to destroy his enemy once for all, and wash the land clean of his name. Vengeance of blood, from what they say, is a thing unknown among the Franks. The price of blood has no claim among their customs. Were it otherwise, they would better understand our manner of warfare. But what do I, loitering by the way? In thy grace, O my brother! Allah guard thee till we meet again!”
When Saïd at length passed out at the town-gate, twilight was rising from the ground. Shadows, which were half a light, floated among the tree trunks. He had yet a good way to go, and the sun was set; he hurried on, therefore, along a fair road almost roofed with leafage and bordered by hedges which smelt sweet. In a place where black trees of mournful seeming grew sparsely amid a wilderness of white stones, he beheld veiled figures flitting darkly among the tombs and knew them for women caring for their dead.
The zeal of the faithful must have waned with the sun, for he overtook and passed several groups of men, dusty and disordered; and, as he crossed a bridge, the twang of an oud and wailing chant of a singer reached him from some tavern down the stream. Nevertheless, he still heard the roar of the tumult through a tremulous veil, as it were, of nearer sounds﻿—the droning plaint of the singer, the bark of a dog, chirping of birds, croaking of frogs, the murmur of the stream and the rustle of leaves.

===Chinese===

「因为我家老爷竟哭起来，绞着双手——我猜是怕骑出去送命，又羞于在阿卜杜勒·卡迪尔那样一头老狮子面前当恋种。我告退之前，他取过一页纸，在上头画起来，画的像是城防图，一面喊着：『本该这样这样。我本该如此如此。』仿佛笔杆在纸上的吱吱声能给他安慰似的。可怜的大人物！告诉你，我的心都为他疼了。宫里人人断定：为了他这迟疑的老毛病，弗兰克人要他的命。
「我这会儿要去给避在宫院里的人买点吃食。人一大群，杀伐几时是个头谁说得清？总有人要饿死——那景象，在自家宅院的场子里，瞧着不痛快。怒起杀个仇家，连他的女人、他的儿女，天理人情都容——弗兰克人怎么说由他们。人要把仇敌一劳永逸除尽、把那名字从地上洗净，也是天性。听他们说，血仇在弗兰克人中间是闻所未闻的东西。血价在他们的风俗里没处讨。若不这样，他们倒更能懂我们这路战法。可我在这儿磨蹭什么？失陪了，我的兄弟！愿安拉护你，直到再会！」
赛义德终于出得城门时，暮色正从地上往起涨。一些半明半暗的影子在树干间浮动。他还有好一段路，日头又落尽了；于是他加紧赶，沿一条好路——枝叶几乎搭成了顶——两旁的树篱发着甜香。有一处地方，黑黢黢、愁惨惨的树稀稀落落长在一片白石头的荒场里，他望见蒙面的人影在坟茔间幽幽地穿行，知道那是照看亡人的女人。
信士们的热肠想必也随太阳落了：他赶过好几伙人，风尘满面、衣衫不整；过一座桥时，下游哪家酒馆里传来乌德琴的嘣响和歌者的哀唱。饶是这样，那乱子的吼声他仍听得见——隔着近处种种声响织成的一层颤巍巍的纱：歌人呜呜的怨调、犬吠、鸟啾、蛙鸣、溪流的絮语和叶子的沙沙。

===Original===

It was the same roar that he had heard on awaking, only fainter and with a note of satiety. He wondered what the drum was that had been beating all day, and was beating yet somewhere in the city. And even as he listened and wondered, the cry of the muezzin rose shrill above the din, followed by another﻿—by a host of others, until all the plain was filled with their message. The turmoil sank and died away. The drum was no more heard. The unbelievers enjoyed a respite while the faithful said their prayers.
Selecting a little patch of grass by the wayside, beneath a great mulberry-tree, Saïd fell on his face and gave praise and thanks to Allah. It pleased him to think on how few days of his life he had omitted to pray at each appointed hour. He asked Allah to forgive him the omissions, not to let them weigh against his virtues to destroy him. Then, shrugging his shoulders resignedly, he rose, inhaled a perfumed breath of the night, and murmured, “Allah is just!”
At the point where a garden-track branched from the main road, and blunting the angle, stood a building one would have taken for a large *wely* or saint’s tomb, flanked and dwarfed by twin cypress-trees. A pious foundation from of old, it served the double purpose of a fountain and a place of rest for wayfarers. It consisted of a centre arch, admitting to the spout and trough, and of a recess on either hand; and was surmounted by three domes in proportion to these divisions, that in the middle being much higher than the other two, which peeped over the square roof as a skullcap shows above a turban.
The fountain whitened in the half light amid the gloom of the surrounding foliage. The two cypress-trees stood up blackly, their tufts cutting the green sky, Saïd’s eagerness grew apace. He walked faster and faster, and was on the point of girding up his loins to run when a loud voice turned him to stone. It was the voice of Mustafa, but it had a new intonation which made his flesh creep. It came from within the building, very harsh upon the evening murmur and the twilight, which, between them, were soft as velvet.
“Allah will give to you!” There was something fierce and exultant in the cry, which assorted gruesomely with that prayer for alms. “Allah will give to you!﻿ ﻿… I slew him, I tell you.﻿ ﻿… See, I have a withered hand. O hand of my honour﻿—O blessed hand!﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

这吼声同他醒来时听到的是同一片，只是弱了，添了一层餍足的调子。他不明白那面鼓是什么鼓——敲了一整天，这会儿还在城里哪处敲着。他正听着纳闷，宣礼员的呼声尖尖地拔起，压过喧嚣；接着又一声——又是一大片，直到整个平原都灌满了它们传的话。乱局沉下去，歇了。鼓也不响了。信士们做礼拜的当儿，不信教的人得了片刻喘息。
赛义德在路旁一株大桑树底下捾了片草地，伏下脸去，赞美感谢安拉。他想起平生漏掉定时的礼拜的日子竟那样少，心里很得意。他求安拉饶恕那些疏漏，别叫它们压过他的功德、毁了他。随后他听天由命地一耸肩，起身吸进一口馥郁的夜气，喃喃道：「安拉是公道的！」
在一条园圃小径岔出大路的地方，切着那个岔角，立着一座建筑，乍看会当它是座大*圣墓祠*——两侧双柏夹侍，把它衬矮了。这是古时的一处敬虔善产，一身兼两用：泉眼，兼行路人的歇处。当中一个拱门通着泉口和水槽，左右各一间壁龛；顶上按着这三间起了三个圆顶，正中那个比两边高出许多，两个小的从方方的屋顶上探出来，像小帽从缠头巾上露出的那一截。
半明半暗里，泉水在四围浓叶的幽暗中泛白。两株柏树黑黑地立着，树梢剪开绿天。赛义德的急切一阵紧似一阵。他越走越快，正要束起腰布撒开跑，一个响亮的嗓音把他钉成了石头。是穆斯塔法的嗓音，却带了一种新的腔调，听得他皮肉发麻。它从建筑里面传出来，扎在黄昏的絮语和暮色上——那两样合起来，本软得像天鹅绒。
「安拉必赏报你们！」那呼喊里有种凶狠的得意，同这句讨钱的祷词拼在一起，狰狞已极。「安拉必赏报你们！……我杀了他，告诉你们。……瞧，我有一只枯手。我体面的手啊——有福的手！……主啊！……」

===Original===

Take pity, O my masters or I die.﻿ ﻿… Allah witness, I slew him. Aha, he was fat and lay on a bed of down, whereas I.﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿… Allah will give to you!﻿ ﻿… I am poor and lean while you are fat and dwell in palaces. See the stains on my hand.﻿ ﻿… O hand of my love﻿—O happy left hand! Take pity, hear you?﻿—or I will slay all the race of you, fat men that lie on soft cushions.﻿ ﻿… Aha, you look very funny, all you fat ones with your mouths open, lying on green couches and your eyes turned over in your heads. It is a merry sight.﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿… Have compassion or I die. Merciful Allah, is there none to pity me?﻿ ﻿… Behold my father’s house is washed clean of the reproach.﻿ ﻿… Blood!﻿ ﻿… I see blood!﻿—blood everywhere﻿—blood of pigs﻿—blood of unbelievers. Lo! the steam of it rises up to heaven, and it is counted to me for righteousness. Allah rejoices! The Prophet smiles at God’s right hand!﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿… Death to the unbelievers! Perish the Christians! Dìn! Dìn!﻿ ﻿…”
Daunted by the hideous outcry and the gathering night, Saïd stood still, shuddering, until the voice died away upon a frightful shriek. Then he ran forward.
“May his house be destroyed,” he breathed ruefully between his clenched teeth. “It is sure he is possessed with a devil. Why else should he cry aloud to summon all men to the secret place of our wealth!” The recess on either side of the fountain was very dark. Saïd stood by the trough of stone and whispered his friend’s name. He spoke it aloud, then shouted it, then made the vault ring with it on a despairing yell of terror. Dead silence and a darkness which the tinkle of a slender thread of water made hollow as a bell; more than all, the echo of his own voice almost killed him with fright. He was haunted, the sport of malicious fiends. They were mocking him somewhere in the gloom, pointing at him and laughing noiselessly. He was minded to run, but his feet were become of one piece with the uneven pavement. It was that hopeless, blind terror which knows no beyond﻿—the despair of a child alone in the dark. He shut his eyes; but fear lined their lids with eyes and wheels of flame, which rolled and dilated, scathing his very soul. Sure that dreadful shapes were drawing near him, he opened them from excess of fear; and, seeing nothing, was ten times more frightened than before. He breathed hard.

===Chinese===

「发发慈悲，老爷们，不然我死。……安拉作证，我杀了他。啊哈，他肥，睡鸭绒的垫，而我呢。……主啊！……安拉必赏报你们！……我又穷又瘦，你们肥，住宫殿。瞧我手上的血渍。……我心爱的手——快活的左手啊！发发慈悲，听见没有？——不然我把你们这一族全杀了，你们这些睡软垫的肥人。……啊哈，你们瞧着真滑稽，你们这些肥货，张着嘴，躺绿榻上，眼睛翻到脑后。好一出快活戏。……主啊！……行行好，不然我死。仁慈的安拉，没人疼我么？……看哪，我父家的耻辱洗净了。……血！……我看见血！——处处是血——猪的血——不信教者的血。看哪！血的蒸汽升上天去，算在我的义里。安拉欢喜！先知在真主的右手边微笑！……主啊！……不信教者死！基督徒灭亡！教门！教门！……」
那可怕的嚎叫同四合的夜色镇住了他，赛义德僵在原地发抖，直到那嗓音收在一声骇人的尖啸里咽了气。他才往前跑。
「叫他的房子塌了才好，」他咬着牙恨恨地喘。「他准是叫鬼附了。不然为什么高声叫唤，把满城人召到咱们藏财的秘地来！」泉水两侧的壁龛黑得很。赛义德立在石槽边，悄声唤他朋友的名字。又出声唤，又喊，末了一声绝望的恐怖长嚎，把拱顶都震得嗡嗡响。死寂，和一种黑暗——一线细水的叮咚把它敲得空落落像口钟；更要命的是他自己声音的回响，几乎把他吓死。他被缠上了，成了恶鬼们的玩物。它们在幽暗的哪一处学他，指着他，无声地笑。他想跑，两只脚却同高低不平的地面长成了一块。那是一种无路可想的、瞎眼的恐怖——小孩独自在黑暗里的那种绝望。他闭上眼；恐惧却在眼皮里衬上一层层的火眼和火轮，滚着，涨着，燎他的魂。他笃定有可怕的形体正朝他围拢，出于极度的怕又睁开眼；什么也没看见，反倒比先前十倍地怕。他喘得粗。

===Original===

However, as long seconds passed and nothing happened, little by little the panic left him, and his wits, faint and trembling, returned to him. The arch by which he had entered was full of dark forms of trees quivering upon a starry sky. He heard the howl and yelp of a jackal; no doubt there were vineyards near with green clusters of half-formed grapes such as foxes love. The well-known sound and the everyday thoughts it engendered calmed him somewhat. A jangle of bells approaching along the road wholly reassured him. For all that, it was with heart in mouth that he stepped into the recess whence the cry of Mustafa had seemed to proceed.
Straining his ears to retain the friendly sound of the camel-bells, he passed a hand along the wall. All at once he stumbled on something. He stooped down to feel what it might be.
“O Mustafa!” he whispered fiercely, “what is this?﻿ ﻿… Arise! Awake! Say, where is our treasure? Let us take each his share and return with speed to the city. Come, awake, I say! Make haste!”
No answer. The mass was inert as he shook it; an arm flopped and that was all. He had nothing wherewith to get a light, and it was very dark. Yet he felt brave and master of himself, for the clangour of bells was drawing near and he could hear the voice of a camel-driver chanting in praise of love.
He found the old man’s head and placed his hand over his mouth. There was no warmth of a breath; the lips were cold and sticky. Then Saïd knew for certain that he was handling a dead body.

===Chinese===

然而，长长的几秒捱过去，什么也没发生，那阵惊惶一点一点离开了他，他那晕晕颤抖的心窍一点一点回来了。他进来的那道拱门里满是树的黑影，在一星空上颤动。他听见豺的嚎和吠；近处必有葡萄园，挂着狐狸爱吃的那种青青的半成形的一串串。这熟惯的声音和它引出的家常念头叫他定了些神。一阵铃铛的乱响沿路而来，彻底安了他的心。饶是这样，他跨进那间壁龛——穆斯塔法的呼号像是打那儿出来的——心还是提到了嗓子眼。
他竖着耳朵留住驼铃那友好的声响，一只手贴着墙摸。冷不防绊着个什么。他弯腰去摸。
「穆斯塔法！」他狠狠地悄声喊，「这是什么？……起来！醒醒！说，咱们的宝藏呢？一人一份拿了，赶紧回城。来，醒醒，我说！快点！」
没有回答。他摇它，那一团死沉；一条胳膊软软地一甩，就此而已。他没有取火的家什，黑得又实。可他觉得自己有胆、拿得住自己——铃铛的铿锵近了，他听得见赶驼人唱歌的嗓音，唱的是颂爱情的调子。
他摸到老人的头，把手覆在他嘴上。没有一丝呼吸的暖；嘴唇又冷又黏。这时赛义德才确知，他手里摸的是一具尸首。

### XXVII / 二十七
===Original===

Saïd shuddered, not so much for the knowledge of his own uncleanness, nor for the fear of death, as for the loneness of this end by the roadside and for horror of the wild cry he had heard. Since last the sun rose he had been present at the killing of many men and women. But they all had perished in the open street in the sunlight, amid the shouting of a great multitude, with prayers and curses on their lips; whereas Mustafa had met death in the dark, in a lonely place with none to witness.
He thought of the treasure, that it was now all his own; and sorrow, like a spring of sweet water, welled up in his heart for the loss of his more than father. But the next minute he wished Mustafa no good for dying ere he had made him privy to the hiding-place. By Allah, a loose stone in the pavement was not so easy to find in the darkness, without lamp or direction, and with a corpse for company.
The clash of bells grew very near indeed. The chant ceased, and the singer shouted to a comrade at some distance. Then the bells lost their rhythmic chime and jangled confusedly. The train of camels had halted.
Soon an unwieldy, groaning bulk was led in to drink at the fountain. Saïd stood very still against the wall of his recess, watching the black shapes fearfully, quaking for his treasure, lest the drivers should strike a light or any movement of his should rouse suspicion. There were sounds of sucking, gurgling and groaning, the swinging tramp of great beasts, and a hairy smell. He heard the voices of the men debating whether to enter the city in its present disturbed state or to sleep at a khan they named without the gate. He grew fretful, burning to begin his search for the treasure. It must be taken away at once, lest the discovery of Mustafa’s body should lead to a thorough search of the place.
At length the last camel was watered and he could hear the men swear as they marshalled the train. The rhythmic clangour broke out afresh. With an oath of relief he began to crawl upon his hands and knees, feeling the pavement stone by stone as he went. He felt everywhere to within a hair’s-breadth of the corpse; but not a slab was loose, though he fancied one or two rang hollow as he rapped them. The camel-bells were but a tinkle in the distance. He was alone and fear breathed hot upon him.
In a kind of fury he gripped the dead man’s arm and dragged him into a corner.

===Chinese===

萨义德打了个寒噤——倒不全是知道自己一身的不洁，也不全是怕死，更是为这道旁这等孤零零的死法，为他听见的那声狂叫害怕。自打今儿太阳升起来，他眼看多少男男女女被杀。可那些人死在大天白日的街上，死在万众的呐喊里，嘴上带着祷告或咒骂；穆斯塔法却死在黑地里，死在没个人影做证的僻处。
他想到那笔财——如今全归他一人了；丧了这位胜似亲爹的人，悲哀像一眼甜泉从他心头涌上来。可下一分钟，他又怪穆斯塔法死得不是时候：还没把藏宝的地方告诉他！凭安拉说，黑灯瞎火、没灯没向导、拖着一具尸首做伴，要摸出地面上那块活石，谈何容易。
驼铃的脆响真的近了。歌声停了，唱歌人朝远处一个同伴喎话。随后铃声失了韵律，乱响起来。驼队停住了。
不多时，一大堆哼哼唧唧的笨重东西被牵进来，凑泉喝水。萨义德贴着壁龛的墙，一动不动，提心吊胆望着那些黑影，为他的财宝发抖，只怕赶驼人打火照个亮，或自己一动惹出疑心。一片吸水声、咕咚声、哼哐声，大牲口踏地一颠一颠，一股毛腥味。他听见伙计们商量：城这么乱，是这就进城，还是睡在城门外一家他们叫得出名字的客店里。他心焦起来，火烧火燎想动手寻宝。得赶紧把东西弄走，怕的是穆斯塔法的尸首一被人发现，这地方要遭一场大搜。
末了，最后一峰骆驼饮完，他听见人们吆喎着整队。有韵的铃铛声重新响起来。他松了口大气，骂一声，开始手脚并用地爬，一边爬一边一块一块摸地上的石板。他摸遍了，离尸身只差一根头发丝的地方也摸到了；可没有一块石板是活的——虽然有一两块他敲着听，仿佛空响。驼铃声只剩远处一点叮咚。他独自一个，恐惧朝他呼出热气。
一股火起，他攦住死人的胳膊，把他拖到一个角落里。

===Original===

With a shiver of that contact upon him he knelt down to examine the place where the body had lain. There was a stone cast up﻿—a wide hole. Oh, for a little light!
He let his forearm down into it; and his hand felt gold, both coinage and jewellery, which seemed to be contained in a strong coffer of iron or brass, of which the lid was open. Lying flat on his belly, with both arms in the hole, he long strove to lift that chest﻿—by the lid, by the side﻿—but it would not budge. Then he thought if he could only get his fingers under it he would have better purchase. He needed something thin yet strong to thrust beneath it as a prise.
“May Allah cut short his life!” he panted. “Who but a madman would have left our wealth thus exposed? By the Prophet, it is lucky that I alone was at hand to hear his last cry.﻿ ﻿… May his house be destroyed.”
“Peace to him,” he added as an afterthought, setting to work once more. He took a knife from his girdle, and managed so to force its stout blade under the treasure-box that his fingers could take hold. He tugged and strained, tendons cracking, sweat streaming from every pore. At last, after many failures, he raised it clear out of the hole and set it on the pavement. Praise to Allah!
Sitting back on his heels to recover breath he mopped his face with the lap of his robe. Mustafa was indeed a marvel of strength to have carried that burden with anything like secrecy from the house of Yuhanna hither. He turned the miracle over in his mind, seeking its human side. Of a sudden he recalled how the old man had spoken of the fountain as a place where he was used to hide trifles of price. The riddle was solved; there was no great wonder after all. The strong chest was the beggar’s own. He had brought the wealth of Yuhanna hither in a sack, or some vessel unlikely to raise suspicion. He had then uncovered the hole, opened the chest, and poured the treasure pell-mell upon its contents. This evening he had naturally wished to gaze upon his riches. And even as his eyes were glutted the angel of death had passed over him.
Saïd’s heart grew faint with rapture as he thought that here was more than all the treasure of the Christian. Allah alone knew what hoards Mustafa might have amassed during long years of begging and pilfering.
“Thanks be to Allah!” he murmured. “May Allah increase thy goods, O abu Mansûr!”

===Chinese===

带着那一场触碰的寒噤，他跪下来，细看尸身原躺的地方。有一块石头翻起着——好大一个洞。唉，要有一点光！
他把小臂探下去；手摸到了金子——钱币和首饰都有——盛在一只铁的或是黄铜的结实箱子里，箱盖敞着。他趴平了肚子，两条胳膊都伸进洞里，好一阵想抬起那只箱子——揪盖、扳帮——它纹丝不动。跟着他想，手指要是能伸到底下就有抓挠了。他需要一样又薄又硬的东西，插到箱子底下当撬棍。
「叫安拉收他的命！」他喘着。「不是疯子，谁会把俺们的财宝这么撂着？凭先知说，幸亏只俺一个人在跟前听见他最后那声喊。……叫他家宅败落。」
「愿他安息。」他想起礼数，补了一句，重新干活。他从腰里拔出刀，把那厚实的刀刃硬塞到宝箱底下，手指得着了抓处。他拽，他使狠劲，筋咯咯响，汗从每个毛孔里淌。末了，败了几回之后，他把它整个提离了洞，摺上地面。感赞安拉！
他坐回脚跟喘气，拿袍襟抹脸。穆斯塔法真是一尊神力：驮着这么个家伙，还能不叫人起疑，从尤汉纳家弄到这儿。他在心里把这件神迹翻来覆去，找它人间的这一面。忽然想起，老头子讲过这泉是他惯常藏贵重零碎的地方。谜底开了，原也没什么大不了。那口结实的箱子是叫花子自个儿的。他把尤汉纳的财宝装在一只口袋或什么不起疑的容器里弄到这儿，揭开洞，开箱，把财宝一股脑倒进他自己箱里的家当上。今晚他自然想饱看一眼他的财富——眼睛正看个饱，死亡的天使从他头顶过去了。
想到这比那基督徒的全部家当还多，萨义德的心欢喜得发虚。穆斯塔法长年讨饭扒窃，囤下了多少窖，只有安拉知道。
「感谢安拉！」他喃喃说。「叫安拉增添你的财物，阿布·曼苏尔！」

===Original===

But the question was urgent﻿—How to dispose of all this wealth for the time being? He dared not replace it, lest, when men came to remove the body of Mustafa, they should chance upon the loose slab and haply discover it. To bury it somewhere in the darkness and return with a sack in the early morning seemed a bright thought; but he could not regard it with perfect favour, knowing what mischievous devils lurk at night in lonesome places. A jinni might see him bury the chest and play some vile prank such as turning the gold to dross, or ashes, or salt, or freezing the ground above it to solid rock.
At last he resolved to take his fortune along with him in the pendant sack of his voluminous trousers. A weight down there would attract no notice, for it is the custom of all men to carry their marketings thus﻿—their implements or whatever is cumbrous in the hand. He stood and pulled up his overrobe. Holding up the placket of his pantaloons, he took money and jewels by handfuls and dropped them in. Passing his hand along the bottom of the coffer to be sure it was quite empty, he found a small coin which he left for an alms or gleaning. He took a step to and fro to see how it felt. The treasure swung as a solid whole, bumping his ankles, his shins, and the calves of his legs. There was no clink or jingle to betray its nature. It was clumsy, very uncomfortable, but (praise to Allah!) quite safe.
He squatted to replace the chest and close the hole. The posture was restful, for while it lasted the pavement bore his burden. Then he rose, and, with a faint glance towards the carcase of Mustafa, moved gingerly away. But no sooner had he turned his back upon the dead than a panic got hold of him. He stumbled through the archway out into the whispering night as fast as the weight of his treasure would allow.
Weary and bruised all over, he sank within the threshold of Nûr’s dwelling, bumping against a small donkey, saddled and hung about with gaudy tassels, which stood there patiently with swishing tail. A lamp was burning on the floor of the inner room, and Saïd could see the vast bulk of Abu Khalìl seated beside the mistress in a languorous attitude. Nûr rose full of reproach on beholding the fisherman.
“Thou art returned, O my soul? What is this? Did I not counsel thee not to come nigh her for a while? Moreover, it is not safe for thee to be here.

===Chinese===

可眼下火烧眉毛的问题是——这批财怎么处置？他不敢放回去：怕人们来抬穆斯塔法的尸首时，撞上那块活石板，兴许把它翻出来。黑地里找个地方埋了，一清早再拿口袋来起，听着是个妙主意；可他不能全然中意——夜里僻静地方潜伏着多少捣蛋的魔鬼，他知道。一个精尼兴许看见他埋箱，使个坏——把金子变成渣、灰、盐，或是把上头的土冻成铁石。
末了他打定主意：把他的财运装进他肥大灯笼裤下垂的裤筒里，随身带走。那底下坠个分量不惹眼——人人都是这么提携买东西的家什的：工具啦，凡是手上拿费劲的累赘啦。他站起来，撩起外袍，提着裤腰的活口，一把一把把钱和首饰丢进去。手顺着箱底捋一遍，确定空了，摸到一枚小钱——留下，当施舍，叫人拾个遗穗。他来回走了两步，试试滋味。那财宝沉成一整块，磕他的脚踝、胫骨、腿肚子。没有叮当声出卖它的底细。笨，硌得难受，可（感赞安拉！）稳当。
他蹲下去，把箱子放回、掩好洞口。这个姿势倒省力——蹲着的时候，地面替他驮着重负。随后他起身，朝穆斯塔法那把残骸虚虚一瞥，蹑手蹑脚退出去。可他背刚一转开死人，一阵惊惶就攦住了他。他跌跌撞撞抢出拱门，冲进窸窣作响的黑夜，快到两条腿上的财宝所许可的极限。
一身疲惫，浑身青紫，他一屁股跌进努尔住处的门槛，撞上一头小毛驴——鞍韝齐备，挂满花梢的穗子，正甩着尾巴有耐性地站在那儿。里间地上一盏灯点着，萨义德望得见阿布·哈利勒那一大堆肉，懒洋洋偎在女主人身边。一看见渔夫，努尔满腔责备地站起来。
「你回来了，俺的心肝？这是怎么说？俺不是嘱咐过你，这会儿别近她？再说，你待在这儿也不安稳。

===Original===

Search may perhaps be made; all wise men concerned in the riot sleep beyond the walls tonight. Our friend, Abu Khalìl, is come seeking news of his son, Camr-ud-dìn.﻿ ﻿…”
Peering into his face she broke off and cried﻿—
“How is Mustafa? Where is he? Speak!”
“O Nûr, Mustafa is dead!” murmured Saïd with a woeful shake of the head. And in truth his heart was near to breaking, for the treasure had barked the shins of both his legs, not to speak of ankles and the great weight to carry.
She screamed﻿—
“Just Allah! Hearest thou that, O Abu Khalìl?﻿ ﻿… O day of disaster! O evil day!﻿ ﻿… Where is he? Lead me to him! None but Nûr shall lay him out for burial!﻿ ﻿… Hearken, O Saïd﻿—O son of his soul and heir of all his wealth! I will hire a goodly company of women to bewail him with beating of breasts and tearing of hair. Thou wilt not grudge the money, for thou art a rich man through his death.﻿ ﻿… Where is he? Lead me to him!”
Very mournfully Saïd told her that the body lay a long way off, in the chamber of a certain fountain among the gardens. He recounted the cry he had heard, the sudden silence, and his finding Mustafa dead in the black recess.
“Allah is just!” he said. “It were well if some men set out at once to fetch him hither, for I heard the voice of a jackal near to that place, and I would not have my father’s corpse a prey to unclean beasts. For myself, I am weary and broken with grief, I may not return thither. I am now a rich man, as thou sayest, the wealth of Mustafa being greater than any man supposed. Let the burial be according to thy desire.”
During the narrative Abu Khalìl had risen slowly from the couch and dragged his vast bulk to the door to listen. Hearing talk of the wealth of Mustafa, he appeared dazed, and exclaimed, “Mashallah!” under his breath. He strove to treat Saïd as the heir, with a deference which old habits of patronage made to sit awkwardly upon him. Nûr was suddenly inspired. She laid her hands wheedlingly on the shoulders of the fat taverner and, darting love into his eyes﻿—
“O my beloved,” she pleaded, “thou wilt go to the fountain of which Saïd speaks. Thou canst find a neighbour or two to go with thee: and thou wilt bring hither the body of Mustafa! Saïd, as thou seest, is broken with fatigue, else he would bear thee company. I shall be very grateful to thee, O my soul, and I shall await thee here.﻿ ﻿… Say not ‘Nay’!”

===Chinese===

兴许要搜；有分寸的、沾着乱子的人，今夜都睡到城墙外头去了。俺们的朋友阿布·哈利勒，就是来找他儿子卡姆鲁丁的下落的。……」
她凑近端详他的脸，猛地断了话头，喊——
「穆斯塔法怎么样？他在哪儿？说！」
「努尔，穆斯塔法死了！」萨义德悲悲切切摇着头喃喃。说实在的，他的心也真快碎了——那袋财宝把他两条腿的胫骨都磕破了皮，更不说脚踝，还有那坠人的分量。
她尖叫一声——
「公道的安拉！你听见没有，阿布·哈利勒？……灾祸的日子！凶日啊！……他在哪儿？领俺去！收殓他，除了努尔谁也不配！……听着，萨义德——他魂的儿子、他全部家当的继承人！俺要雇一伙体面的女人哭他，捶胸的捶胸，扯头发的扯头发。这钱你不会舍不得：他这一死，你是富人了。……他在哪儿？领俺去！」
萨义德凄凄惶惶告诉她：尸身在很远的地方，在园子里一座喷泉的暗间里。他讲了他听见的那声喊、随后的骤然死寂、他在黑洞里发现穆斯塔法断了气。
「安拉公道！」他说，「顶好这就打发人去把他抬回来：俺听见那地方附近有豺叫，俺可不愿意俺爹的尸身叫不洁的畜生糟蹋。俺自个儿呢，乏透了，悲透了，回不去了。如今俺是富人，像你说的；穆斯塔法的家当比谁想的都大。丧事就照你的意思办。」
这一篇叙述里，阿布·哈利勒从榻上慢慢起来，拖着那一大堆肉挪到门口听。听到穆斯塔法的家当，他发懵了，压着嗓子叫了一声「马沙安拉」。他竭力把萨义德当继承人对待，那份恭敬叫他摆弄得别扭——他惯常是居高临下的人。努尔忽然得了灵感。她把两只手撒娇似的搭上肥店主的肩膀，朝他眼睛里射去爱意——
「俺的亲亲，」她央道，「你去萨义德说的那座泉。你寻得一两个街坊做伴，把穆斯塔法的尸身驮回来！萨义德你也瞧见了，乏散了架，不然他陪你走一趟。俺会顶顶承你的情，俺的心肝，俺就在这儿等你。……别说不！」

===Original===

she cried impetuously, discounting his scandalised stare by a pout and a girlish gesture. “I beseech thee, cross me not in this matter. He was a rich man, remember; and thou wilt not only oblige me, that am a woman and of no account, but also confer a favour upon Saïd Effendi, heir to all his wealth, who will henceforth rank with the great ones of Es-Shâm.﻿ ﻿… What sayest thou?”
Abu Khalìl, greatly perturbed, pushed his turban awry the better to scratch his head. He glanced furtively from Nûr to Saïd, and from Saïd back again to Nûr.
“Now, by Allah, this is no light thing you require of me. Nevertheless, since it is the case of an old friend﻿ ﻿… and to serve Saïd Effendi whom, I call Allah to witness, I have ever regarded as a favourite son﻿ ﻿… I say not that I will not go. For all that, it is a hard thing for an old man, the father of a family, to go out by night into the gardens where, as all men know, gipsies and other children of sin do abound; not to speak of those who are more than men﻿—jin, I mean, and *afaereet*; and the uncleanness I shall incur, and the tedious purification to follow.﻿ ﻿…”
Saïd broke in coaxingly﻿—
“Be assured, O Abu Khalìl, O lord of kindness, thou shalt have a large reward; may Allah increase thy property!”
“Good. I go!”
Abu Khalìl shuffled to the place where the ass stood swishing its tail, and bestrode it so earnestly that he nearly fell over on the other side. Then, remembering that his steed was tethered, he leaned over its head to untie the rope. Nûr led the staggering beast up the steps and out into the alley, which the beams of a rising moon were beginning to silver.
“I will seek out Zeid the carpenter and Abbâs the Nubian who sells sweet stuff!” said the taverner, bowing his head to avoid contact with the lintel as he rode out. “Both are young men, strong and fearless. Both have donkeys belonging to them, so that we shall seem a goodly company riding together. Moreover, Abbâs has a rare whip he showed me yesterday, being a strip of the hide of a crocodile or other monster common in Masr where he bought it. By Allah, it is a fine thong! Two strokes of it would flay a dog.﻿ ﻿… In your grace!”
“With my peace. Allah guard thee in safety!” cried Saïd and Nûr in one breath as the doughty taverner ambled away in moonlight and shadow, thwacking his steed bravely on the hindmost part.

===Chinese===

她急急地喊，拿一个撅嘴、一个女孩儿家的手势，提前堵死他那副受惊的瞪眼。「俺求你，这事上别驳俺。他是个富人，记着；你不光是给俺——一个女人、没斤两的人——面子，也是给萨义德·埃芬迪脸：他是全部家当的继承人，往后要跟沙姆的大人物平起平坐了。……你说呢？」
阿布·哈利勒心乱得很，把缠头巾推歪，好挠头。他从努尔瞟到萨义德，又从萨义德瞟回努尔。
「凭安拉说，你们要俺办的，可不是轻省事。不过，既是老朋友的一场……又是给萨义德·埃芬迪效劳——俺请安拉作证，俺一向拿他当亲儿子看……俺不说俺不去。饶是这么着，一个上了年纪、当爹的人，黑天半夜往园子里跑，谁都知道那地方吉卜赛人和别号罪人之子多得成堆；更别说那些不止是人的——俺是说精尼，还有阿夫里特魔精；还有俺要沾上的不洁，和随后那一套没完的净洗。……」
萨义德赶紧插上来哄——
「放心，阿布·哈利勒，善心的大人，你的报酬小不了；叫安拉增添你的产业！」
「好。俺去！」
阿布·哈利勒蹭到那头甩尾巴的驴跟前，跨上去使的劲太猛，差点从那一边翻过去。随后想起牲口还拴着，又俯过驴脖子解绳。努尔把这头晃晃悠悠的牲口牵上台阶、牵进小巷——初升的月亮正开始给小巷镀银。
「俺去寻木匠扎伊德，还有卖甜食的努比亚人阿巴斯！」店家骑出去时低头躲着门楣说。「都是年轻汉子，壮，胆大。两人都有自个儿的驴，俺们凑一伙人马上，瞧着也体面。再说，阿巴斯有一根稀罕的鞭子，昨天给俺瞧过：一条鳄鱼皮——反正是马斯尔那地方常见的怪物皮——削的，他就是打马斯尔买的。凭安拉说，好鞭梢！抽两下能剥一条狗。……叨光了！」
「送你平安。叫安拉保你周全！」萨义德和努尔一口气齐喎，那位骁勇的店家颠着步跑进月光和影子，勇敢地抽着他的坐骑的后半截。

===Original===

The clip-clap of the donkey’s hoofs and its thousand mocking echoes soon died away.
Nûr stood in the doorway looking after him. She stepped forth into the street and listened towards the Christian quarter.
“The tumult still continues,” she said, returning. “It is thin now and feeble﻿—the shadow of that I heard during the day. With the dawn it will revive; and so it will be for many days till every Nazarene is either slain or escaped far away. There is a redness of fire on the sky yonder, where all day long there was a cloud of smoke. They have slain Allah knows how many hundred Christians; and Mustafa is dead.
“My heart is very sad, O Saïd, light of my eyes! Hadst thou seen him as he was when first I knew him, thou wouldst grieve for the days of a man which are as steps hewn in the rock leading downward to a sepulchre. He was a fine man, I tell thee﻿—straight as a Bedawi’s lance, strong and healthy even as thou art. As the breath of winter tears leaves from a mulberry-tree, so does the length of years strip the beauty and the majesty from a man. At last the tree falls and only the bitter wind remains.﻿ ﻿… Allah is greatest!”
Saïd groaned aloud﻿—
“Allah is merciful! But, by my beard, it was a cruel word thou spakest, that I must go sleep without the city. Only let me abide here and I swear I will not go near the girl to trouble her.”
“It cannot be,” said Nûr, firmly. “My house is thy house, and thou art ever welcome to that which is mine. But Abu Khalìl has heard a rumour that search is made secretly for the leaders in rebellion. It is true, what I told thee, that no wise man sleeps within the city this night. Tomorrow, in the daytime, thou mayst show thyself without fear; the slaves of power will then be fast within doors for terror of their lives. I will care for the girl and order all things seemly for the burial of Mustafa. Go quickly, with my peace!”
Saïd, who, for all his freedom of address, stood greatly in awe of the old woman, rose grumbling from the floor, and, holding up the pouch of his trousers like a sack, stumbled up the steps into the moonshine. His nether limbs were very sore and stiff with bruises. In walking he was careful to keep his feet wide apart. He cut such a queer figure, seen from behind, that Nûr called after him to know what ailed him.
“I am happy﻿—in the extremity of good health!” he cried back with affected cheerfulness.

===Chinese===

驴蹄的嗒嗒声和它千百个嘲笑的回声，很快没了。
努尔立在门口望他的背影。她跨到街上，朝基督徒街区那头听了听。
「乱子还没停。」她回来说，「这会儿稀了，也弱了——白天那一场的影子。天一亮又要旺起来；要这样好多天，直到拿撒勒教徒杀的杀光、逃的逃远。那边天上红着火光，白天一整天那儿是一柱烟。他们杀了安拉才知道几百个基督徒；穆斯塔法也死了。
「俺心里苦啊，萨义德，俺眼里的光！你要见过俺头一回认识他那时候的他，你也要替一个汉子难过——人的日子，就像凿在岩石上的一级级台阶，一路往下通到坟墓。他当年是条好汉，俺告诉你——直得像贝都因人的长矛，结实康健，跟你如今一个样。冬天的风怎么从桑树上撕叶子，年岁就怎么从人身上剥俊拔和威严。末了树倒了，只剩下苦风。……安拉至大！」
萨义德大声叹——
「安拉是慈悲的！可凭俺的胡子，你说的话够狠：叫俺出城去睡。只消容俺待在这儿，俺起誓不近那丫头一步，不搅她。」
「不行。」努尔斩钉截铁。「俺的家就是你的家，俺的东西你随时候着使。可阿布·哈利勒听了风声，正暗中搜乱子的头头脑脑。俺跟你说的是实话：今夜有分寸的人没一个睡在城里。明天白天，你露面只管露，不碍事；权势的奴才们那会儿怕掉脑袋，个个紧闭大门。丫头俺照看，穆斯塔法的丧事俺样样办得体面。快走，送你平安！」
萨义德虽说举止随便，对这老太婆却存着大敬畏；他从地上嘟嘟囔囔爬起来，像提一口袋似的提着裤裆，跌跌撞撞上了台阶，进了月光。他两条腿又酸又硬，青一块紫一块。走路他小心把两只脚撇得开开的。从背后看去他那一副尊容实在古怪，努尔朝他背影喊，问他哪儿不对。
「俺好——好得不能再好！」他拿腔拿调地欢声回。

===Original===

“I did but trip over a stone as I ran hither. My knees are somewhat bruised from the fall.”
“Stay, O my eyes, and let me rub them with a salve!” she cried again with seduction; for, contrasting his gait with the tones of his voice, she knew that he lied.
“May thy wealth increase!﻿—there is no need,” he answered, striving to quicken his step.
From a rhythmic bellying of the skirt of his long robe, as well as from the manner of his going, Nûr made a shrewd guess at the nature of his embarrassment.
“He walks like a she-goat whose udders are overfull,” she thought, laughing to herself; “there is something heavy and cumbersome in the sack of his trousers.”
That he was loth to linger or speak of the matter afforded her more light.
“By the Quran, it is the treasure of Mustafa he carries thus for safety, lest one should rob him of it! He would not trust me so much as to let me know, and he bears his punishment along with him. Allah is just!”
And in the midst of her grief for the old beggar she chuckled most heartily out there in the moonlight, pointing the finger of scorn after him with keen and friendly relish of his avarice.

===Chinese===

「俺跑来的道上绊了块石头。膝盖磕得青了点。」
「站住，俺的眼珠儿，叫俺给你揉点药膏！」她又喊，声音带着勾；他那走相跟他的嗓音一对照，她知道他在撒谎。
「叫你的财富增长！——不必了。」他答，脚下加紧。
从他那长袍下摆一荡一荡的节奏，加上他那走法，努尔对他这份尴尬的底细，猜了个八九不离十。
「他走得像一头奶水胀满了的母羊，」她心里好笑，「他那裤裆里有样又沉又笨的东西。」
他既不肯耽搁、又不肯提这桩事，这又给她添了一分亮。
「凭古兰经说，他这么驮着的是穆斯塔法的财宝，防人抢！他连俺都不信到告诉俺的份儿上，就把刑罚随身驮着。安拉公道！」
于是，在那为老叫花子发的一腔悲里，她站在月光下痛痛快快地笑出声来，朝他的背影点着讥诮的手指，把他那点贪心咂摸得又毒又亲。

### XXVIII / 二十八
===Original===

That was a ghastly night for Saïd﻿—a night full of strange faces, of awful whisperings, and of the shadow of death. His first thought on leaving the city was to find some shelter where he might sleep within call of his fellow-men. To that end he sought the coffeehouse of Rashìd, thinking to find a welcome there now that he was again on cordial terms with Selìm. But as he went, in the tremulous shadow of the trees and the moonlight between, he grew more and more afraid, until the bump of the treasure against his shins and the patter of his own footsteps were separate terrors.
It was almost within hail of the tavern, in the gloom of some apricot-trees, that he blundered upon something soft, yet tight, like a body or a full waterskin. He drew back aghast. A shapeless mass rose before him with a horrid groan. Catching up the sack of his trousers he ran for dear life. Far from allaying his terrors the lowing of a cow at his back lashed him to fresh exertions. He knew it for the angry voice of a jinni cursing him.
For hours he fled on by shadowy ways, pursued by a host of devils. Foul shapes flitted and danced behind him; dread hands were stretched out to stay him and clutch his treasure; a flapping of huge wings filled the welkin. Pale faces he had seen in death that day grinned at him from the ground, from the sky, from the gloom of the trees. Even the dwellings of men﻿—a sleeping village half-seen between the trunks, flat-roofed hovels and pleasure-houses bosomed in foliage﻿—were sinister, the abode of unknown fears. Fiends rollicked over the whole earth. The vista of his life was packed with them﻿—a gruesome throng. From his youth up he had been their sport. In the hour of his prosperity, whenever wealth had seemed within his grasp, they had appeared to balk him. His flight from his native town, the loss of his donkey, the robbery which had deprived him of the price of his horse﻿—he saw plainly the cause of all his misfortunes. Then, as now, he had been the butt of evil spirits.
Of a sudden it occurred to him that the whole night was a procession of ghastly, pallid shapes, moving silently as one man. It seemed that he had a moment’s insight into the hidden mysteries of earth, that this gliding march of a vast, fiendish army, unsuspected of men, had been going on ever since the world began, and would continue unbroken till the Last Day.

===Chinese===

对赛义德，那是一个森森的夜——一夜的怪脸、可怕的低语和死亡的影子。出城后他的头一个念头，是寻个能睡的宿处，要睡在同类喊得应的地方。为这个他去投拉希德的咖啡馆——如今他同塞利姆又亲热了，想着那儿必有他的座。可一路走着，在树影的颤动和树影间的月光里，他越走越怕，到后来，宝藏磕碰小腿的动静、自己的脚步声，都各自成了一桩恐怖。
差不多快到酒馆喊得应的地方，在几株杏树的幽暗里，他一头撞上个软而绷的东西，像具尸身，或像一只灌满的水囊。他骇然倒退。一团不成形的东西带着一声可怕的呻吟从他面前立起。他一把提住裤裆里的钱袋，没命地跑。背后一声牛哞，非但没压下他的恐怖，反倒抽他跑得更急。他认得，那是精灵发怒咒他的嗓音。
他沿着影子里的路逃了几个钟头，一群魔鬼追着。污秽的形影在他背后掠过、跳舞；可怕的手伸出来拦他、抢他的宝藏；巨翅的扑扇灌满天穹。那天他见过的死人脸，从地上、从天上、从树影里朝他龇牙。连人的住处——树干间半隐半现的睡熟的村庄、平顶的窝棚和偎在绿荫里的乐馆——也透着凶，是未知恐惧的巢。妖魔在满地上撒欢。他一生的远景里挤满了它们——黑压压的一群。打从少年起他便是它们的玩物。每逢得意之时，眼见财富就要到手，它们就出来揽局。逃离家乡、丢驴、卖马的钱被人劫走——他一切灾殃的根由，他看得清清楚楚。彼时同此刻一样，他都是恶灵取笑的靶子。
蘶地他悟到：整个黑夜是一列鬼惨惨、白惨惨的形体的行进，无声地，如一人般移动。他仿佛有一瞬窥见了大地隐藏的奥秘：这支浩大妖军滑行的行军，人从不知觉，却自开天辟地行到如今，还要不断行到末日。

===Original===

The horror of it was not new to him. He had experienced it before many times, but could not remember when or under what circumstances.
Was not Abdullah himself an evil spirit? And the soldier who had lifted his donkey﻿—was he not an afreet in disguise. There was no doubt of it now as he recalled their faces.
In his despair he thought lovingly of Hasneh. Why﻿—oh, why had he cast her off? To his fevered brain she seemed desirable as on the day when he had first beheld her, a young girl, at play with other maidens on the seashore. He would have given the half of this treasure which was killing him for a touch of her hand, for the sound of her voice.
Once he stood still in an open place. He had a mind to lighten his trousers by flinging all his wealth upon the ground. It was for that the hordes of darkness were tormenting him. He cried aloud that all of them might know his purpose, and bade them swear a solemn oath that they would let him go in peace. But there came no answer; only a jackal’s cry out of the distance, ending in three short yaps. It rang derisive﻿—very like a laugh. At that Saïd grew dogged. Since not a jinni of them all would swear, it was their lookout and he would keep the treasure. For two seconds he felt courageous and knew that there were trees about him rustling peacefully in the moonlight.
Fear breathed hot on him again and he ran, a hideous whisper in his ears. The balm of the silky Eastern night had no sweetness for him. Shifting the sack of his trousers from aching hand to hand, striving to keep his mind intent upon the name of Allah, he fled on. The trees thinned about him; the gardens gave place to vineyards; the vineyards thinned in their turn with spaces of waste land between; the wide plain rolled out before him with soft undulations to some low hills on the horizon floating in pale haze. The boundless silence throbbed in his ears like the pulse of a living creature. The plain whitened in the moonshine. Here and there, as the ground waved, there were ribs of velvet gloom. A lonely tree, a peasant’s hovel, a dark patch of cultivated land, a square-built khan, a knoll, a jutting boulder﻿—the least object was distinct with a black shadow on the smooth-rolling expanse.
With a clear view all round him and no shades to irk his fancy, Saïd’s panic subsided to a holy awe and he slackened his pace.

===Chinese===

这份恐怖于他不新。他从前经历过许多回，只是记不起何时、在什么光景。
阿卜杜拉本人不就是恶灵么？还有牵走他驴的那个兵——不就是化形的恶魔么？这会儿回想他们的脸，再没有半点疑惑。
绝望里他深情地想起哈斯娜。为什么——哦，为什么丢下了她？在他发烧的脑子里，她同初见那天一样可心——一个年轻的姑娘，同别的少女在海边玩耍。这正害死他的宝藏，他肯让出一半，只换她一触手、一声嗓音。
有一回他在一片空地上站住了。他想把满裤裆的钱卸下来，统统摙在地上。黑暗的群魔缠的正是这个。他放声喊出来，叫它们个个知道他的用意，又叫它们郑重起誓放他安宁地走。可没有回答；只有远处一声豺嚎，收在三个短吠上。那声音听着是嘲弄——很像笑。赛义德反倒犟起来。它们一个也不肯起誓，那是它们自家的事，财他留定了。有那么两秒钟，他觉得自己有胆，也知道四围有树，在月光里安安静静地响着叶子。
恐惧又朝他呼热气，他跑起来，耳朵里一阵狞恶的私语。丝绒般的东方之夜的膏泽，于他没有一丝甜。他把裤裆的钱袋从一只酸痛的手倒到另一只，拼力把心钉在安拉的名上，一路奔逃。
树在他四围稀了；园圃让位给葡萄园；葡萄园又稀了，中间隔着一片片荒地；宽阔的平原在他面前舒展开，柔柔起伏，直到地平线上一线低山，浮在淡霭里。无边的岑寂在他耳中搏动，像活物的脉。月光里平原泛白。地波起伏处，这里那里卧着一道道天鹅绒的暗肋。一株孤树、一座庄稼人的窝棚、一方深色的熟地、一座四方方的客店、一个土墩、一块突出的圆石——顶小的物件也轮廓分明，在平滑起伏的旷野上拖着黑影。
四望无碍，没有阴影搅他的心，赛义德的惊惶退成一种神圣的悚惧，他放慢了步子。

===Original===

He was very weary, the weight of his wealth seeming more than he could bear. The howl of a wakeful dog was wafted to him from the distance. In the quarter whence it came black specks were discernible upon a rising ground. It was an encampment of Bedawin or gipsies, Saïd supposed, and instinctively turned his face thitherward. But care for his treasure and the fear of marauders prevented him, and he held straight on.
There was already a bite of dawn in the air when he came to a large khan, square-built and frowning like a fort, and caught the welcome tinkle and stamp of beasts in a stable. There was a well before the gate, watched by a great sycamore-tree. The door was open. Saïd stole among the beasts in the yard and found a snug nook amid a pile of bales. With a sigh of contentment he curled himself up and fell fast asleep.
He dreamed.
It was the last day, or he was newly dead; he knew not which. He was lying spellbound in a place of tombs. Mustafa lay not far from him with a great stone at his head. Veiled women flitted to and fro like phantoms. He knew without looking that Hasneh was among them, and his soul yearned after her. On either side the stone stood an angel, black and shadowy, with a mace in his hand. There was a balance between them, hanging in the air, and they were weighing the works of Mustafa. All that was good went into the one scale and all that was evil into the other. The faces of the examiners were set and moody, as those of men who watch a grave issue. Ever and anon they beat the old man’s head with their maces, so that he shrieked frightfully. Saïd sweated cold with fear lest Mustafa should lose Paradise, and also for his own turn, which was to come.
“This soul is lost, O brother,” said one, gravely. “Thy scale kicks the beam, though each deed placed there counts two of what is placed in mine. Allah is just!”
The other was thoughtful for a space. All at once his stern face brightened. A glory like moonlight emanated from it, flooding all the plain.
“See!” he cried, pointing towards the city. “There is blood﻿—blood of the heathen!﻿—blood of unbelievers!﻿—blood of the enemies of our Master! There is a great pool of it, and it is counted to him for righteousness!”
At that Saïd waxed faint with relief. Hasneh bent over him and peace dropped from her like a precious ointment. The vision faded.

===Chinese===

他乏透了，财富的分量像是扛不动了。一声醒犬的吠远远送来。来处的一方高地上辨得出一攝黑点。赛义德猜是贝都因人或吉普赛人的营地，本能地把脸转过去。可挂心宝藏、又怕劫路的，他终究没有去，照直前行。
走到一座大客店时，空气里已经咬着一点黎明的凉。店是四方形的，虎着脸像座堡；他听见厩里牲口亲切的叮咚和踏蹄，心头一暖。门前一口井，一棵大西克莫无花果树守着。门开着。赛义德溜进院里牲口中间，在一垛货包旁寻着个安妥的角落。他心满意足地叹口气，綣起身子睡死了。
他做了一个梦。
那是末日，或是他新死；他分不清。他被咒缚着躺在一片坟地里。穆斯塔法躺在不远处，头顶一块大石。蒙面的女人像幽灵般来来去去。他不用看就知道哈斯娜在她们中间，他的魂朝她渴慕。石头两侧各立着一位天使，黑而虚，手执一根权杖。两人之间悬着一架天平，吊在半空，正称量穆斯塔法的功过。一切善的进那一边的盘，一切恶的进这一边。查验者的脸绷着、沉着，像人盯着一桩生死攸关的事。他们时不时拿权杖敲老人的头，敲得他发出骇人的尖叫。赛义德冷汗直冒：怕穆斯塔法失去天园，也怕接下来轮到自己的那一场。
「这个魂完了，兄弟，」一位庄严地说。「你那边的秤翘了天，虽说放进去的每件功课抵我这边两件。安拉是公道的！」
另一位沉思了一阵。蘶地，他严厉的脸亮了。一轮月光似的荣光从他脸上放出来，淹了整个平原。
「看！」他喊，指着城的方向。「有血——异教徒的血！——不信教者的血！——我们主人的仇敌的血！好大一汪，都算在他的义里！」
这一下赛义德宽心得几乎晕去。哈斯娜俯下身来，平安从她身上滴下，像宝贵的香膏。幻象淡去了。

===Original===

There was sweet music of bells﻿—a caravan passing in the distance. With a deep sigh he awoke to a deafening clangour of real camel-bells and the pungent reek of a stable.
It was quite dark and a little chilly. But the khan was astir, and through the gate he could see a white eye of dawn opening over the edge of the desert. Men with lanterns moved sleepily among the beasts. A group of camels were being laden with black millstones, each of which it took four men to lift and hold in position, while a fifth lashed it fast with a strong rope. The task was enlivened by a chant panted in cadence, invoking the help of a holy dervìsh long since in Paradise.
Another and more numerous train of camels had just arrived. They were laden with sacks of corn and seemed to have been journeying all night, for the drivers were stiff and surly. With them was a woman of wretched appearance, who stood timidly in the gate, trying to dispose her tattered veil so as to conceal her face.
A bare-legged hostler threw a coarse jest at her in passing. An idler pinched her arm and tore aside her veil, vowing he was sick for love of her. But a sturdy old man, one of the camel-drivers with whom she had come, interfered. He pushed her insulter away roughly, saying that she was a good woman and none should vex her while he was by.
In the hope of a quarrel, Saïd stole forward among the beasts and merchandise, careful to lift the sack of his trousers above contact with any of the coils of rope, halters and saddles which cumbered the ground. The other camel-drivers stopped work and gathered about the disputants. But the aggressor was a coward, or he thought the woman not worth a fight, for he slunk off, muttering that he knew not she belonged to any man there. Her champion contented himself with nodding his head after him and explaining pithily, in a long growl, how he would have punished obstinacy. Their forms moved black in the gateway; beyond them was the grey dawn upon the plain.
“The woman is thine, O sheykh?” asked one who stood by with a lantern.
“No, by Allah!” answered the champion, with a shade of defiance; “but I hold her as a dear daughter. When I cut my foot upon a stone in the neighbourhood of Mazarìb and thought to die for loss of blood, she used me tenderly and rent her veil that my wound might be softly bandaged.

===Chinese===

一片铃的音乐，甜——一支商队远远地过。他深深一叹，醒在一片震耳的真驼铃和马厩刺鼻的气味里。
天还黑着，微微有点凉。客店却已骚动，透过大门，他望得见沙漠边上睁开一只黎明的白眼。提灯的人在牲口中间睡意惺忪地走动。一群骆驼正装载黑色的磨盘，一块磨盘要四个人抬着扶正，第五个拿粗绳捆牢。这活计有一支喘着气合拍的号子助兴，求的是一位早已在天园的圣洁德尔维希帮忙。
又一支更庞大的驼队刚到。驮着一袋袋粮食，像是一夜赶的路，赶驼的个个僵着身子、满脸乖戾。同他们一起的，有个形容憔悴的女人，怯生生立在门里，尽力扯那破面纱遮脸。
一个光腿的马夫打她跟前过，甩过去一句粗话。一个闲人掐她的胳膊，扯开她的面纱，赌咒说他为她害了相思。可一个结实的老人——同她一路的赶驼人之一——出来挡了。他把轻薄的一把搡开，说她是好女人，只要他在跟前，谁也不许欺负她。
指望看一场吵架，赛义德蹑手蹑脚凑到牲口和货堆跟前，留神把裤裆的钱袋提得高高的，不叫它碰着满地绊脚的绳圈、笼头和鞍子。其余的赶驼人停了活，围拢到争执的双方跟前。可那个轻薄的是个恋种，或者嫌那女人不值得打一架，溜了，嘴里咕哝着不知道她在这儿有主。护她的老人朝他背影点了点头也就知足，又拖长声咆哮着，言简意赅地讲了他本来要怎样惩治那不识好歹的。两条身影在门洞里黑黑地动；他们身后，是平原上的灰黎明。
「这女人是你的，谢赫？」一个提灯站在旁边的人问。
「不，凭安拉说！」护她的答，带一点抗声，「可我拿她当亲闺女。我在马扎里布地界叫石头划破了脚，血都快流干了、自以为要死的时候，是她温柔地照料我，撕了她的面纱，给我裹伤裹得软软的。

===Original===

No, she is not my woman, but was given into my care by the men of Beyt Ammeh beside Nablûs. There is a strange story belonging to her.”
At the name of Beyt Ammeh, Saïd pricked up his ears. Observing the form of the woman narrowly, his heart leapt so that it became a lump in his throat.
“The story, O sheykh! Deign to tell us the story!” urged the bystanders. Unnoticed, Saïd joined the press about the narrator.
“Know that this woman had a husband, a fisherman, whose name was Saïd. He set out on a journey to Damashc-ush-Shâm, the woman with him. In a lonely pass of the mountains between Beyt Ammeh and the sea he met a man called Farûn riding on a camel, asleep. Then Saïd, being a joker, picked up a stone from the path and flung it at Farûn so that he fell to the ground. And as he lay there, stunned and bleeding, Saïd took all the money that he had and beat him somewhat with a stick, and so left him.
“Saïd went on his way rejoicing until he came to the village of Beyt Ammeh. There, his woman being faint, he entered the house of a certain fellah, who took pity on her and let her lie on his own bed. After that, as they sat smoking and conversing, the lord of the house questioned Saïd, saying, ‘Didst meet in thy road hither one riding on a camel? Behold, my brother, Farûn by name, is gone this day to the coast with a load.’ Then Saïd﻿—a clever fellow, by Allah!﻿—answered thoughtfully, ‘Yes, it is true; I met such an one. I found him by the road in a sad plight. His blood was upon the stones of the path. He had been robbed and almost killed by wicked men. I stayed a little to bind his wounds, and gave him money﻿—all that I had. I caught his camel and set him upon it. Then I blessed him and came on hither.’
“At that the lord of the house praised and exalted Saïd above all the sons of Adam. He besought him to abide there several days. But Saïd, pretending that his brother was dead in Damashc-ush-Shâm, said that he must hasten to claim the inheritance. Nevertheless, since his woman was sick, he entreated that kind man to take care of her until she should recover her strength. The lord of the house agreed gladly, and when he had given Saïd to eat and drink, he blessed him and let him go. He paid great honour to the woman for the sake of the mercy shown by her husband to Farûn, his brother.

===Chinese===

「不，她不是我的女人，是纳布卢斯旁边拜特阿迈的人托付给我的。她身上有一段离奇的故事。」
一听「拜特阿迈」，赛义德竖起了耳朵。他把那女人的身形细细一打量，心一跳，跳成喉咙口的一团。
「什么故事，谢赫！给我们讲讲吧！」旁边的人催。赛义德不声不响挤进围着讲的人堆。
「你们可知道，这女人有个丈夫，是个渔夫，名叫赛义德。他动身去大马士革，女人随行。在拜特阿迈同海边之间一段荒僻的山隘里，他遇见一个叫法伦的，骑在骆驼上睡着。那赛义德是个促狭鬼，就从路上捾了块石头，朝法伦掷去，把他打了下来。他躺在那儿，晕着，涪着血，赛义德把他身上的钱搜罗个精光，又拿棍子敲了他几下，就这么走了。
「赛义德欢欢喜喜赶路，到了拜特阿迈村。那儿他女人发了虚，他进了一个庄稼人的家；那人怜她，让她睡了自己的床。随后两人坐着抽烟闲话，家主问赛义德：『你来时路上可遇见一个骑骆驼的？我兄弟，名叫法伦的，今天驮货上海边去了。』那赛义德——凭安拉说，真是个伶俐人！——若有所思地答：『是，不假；遇见了这么一位。我见他在路边，境况很惨。他的血溅在路石上。他遭了歹人抢劫，险些送命。我停下给他裹了伤口，又给了他钱——倾我所有。我牵住他的骆驼，扶他上去。然后我为他祝了福，接着赶路来了这儿。』
「家主听了，把赛义德夸上了天，抬到亚当众子之上。留他住上几天。可赛义德推说兄弟死在大马士革，须赶去承产业。不过他女人病着，便央那善人照看她，等她将养起来。家主欢然应允，请赛义德吃了喝了，为他祝福，放他去了。又看在她丈夫善待法伦——他兄弟——的分上，待那女人极尽礼敬。

===Original===

But after two days Farûn returned, and then, as you may guess, his mind was changed. All the men of Beyt Ammeh cursed that clever joker who, having first robbed and beaten Farûn, had then left his sick woman to the care of Farûn’s brother. They kept her for two months, making her the common drudge of all, supposing that Saïd would return or send to fetch her, when they would have slain him or his messengers as the case might be. But he was too clever for that. By Allah, he is a devil! He had no care for this woman, for it seems she is barren.
“So at last, weary of her sighs and weeping, they delivered her over to us as we passed through their village, telling us her story and giving us a little money to take her to Es-Shâm. They charged us, if ever we should meet with Saïd the Fisherman, to slay him without ado for the affront put upon their village. But I admire the rogue. He is a famous joker﻿—what say you?﻿ ﻿… By my beard, he is a devil!”
In the midst of the laughter at his cleverness, Saïd pushed through the group and confronted the woman. “Welcome, and thrice welcome, O Hasneh!” he cried. “Praise be to Allah, thou art alive and in health! My heart has been very sad for thee all this long time. I am rejoiced to find thee once again, O my soul!”
Throwing up her arms, with a shrill cry, she fell on his neck and wept.
“It is Saïd the Fisherman!”﻿—“Saïd the Joker!”﻿—“Saïd the Devil!” “How came he hither?” was whispered in tones of awe; as who should say, It is His Majesty the Sultàn﻿—His Excellency the Basha. Men pressed forward to touch but the hem of his robe, to get but a glimpse of his face; so that Saïd began to fear lest the fullness and weight of his trousers should be remarked. He saluted the company, and circling Hasneh with his arm, led her out into the brisk air of the dawning.
At the angle of the wall which looks towards the desert they sat down on their heels side by side. He told of the awful night he had just passed, and she listened, with patient eyes devouring him.
“I am rich, O my beloved!” he cried, plucking at a dew-drenched thistle. “I will buy a fine house where we shall dwell together. Thou shalt rule over a numerous harìm. I have a sweet girl﻿—a beauty!﻿—the daughter of a Christian pig who is slain. She shall be thy handmaid to do thy bidding.

===Chinese===

「可过了两天法伦回来了，随后——你们猜得到——他就换了一副心肠。拜特阿迈全村的男人都咒那个伶俐的促狭鬼：先抢了法伦、打了法伦，回头又把病中的女人丢给法伦的兄弟照管。他们扣了她两个月，叫她当全村的公用苦役，指望着赛义德回来、或打发人来接——到那时他也好、来人也好，看情形宰了。可他精得不回来。凭安拉说，他是个魔鬼！这个女人他也不心疼——她像是不生养。
「所以到末了，他们听腻了她的叹与哭，趁我们打村里过，把她交托给我们，讲了她的来历，又给了几个钱，叫我们带她去沙姆。他们还嘱咐：若撞见渔夫赛义德，不必废话，一刀杀了——为的是他给全村的那场羞辱。可我倒佩服这无赖。他是出了名的促狭鬼——你们说呢？……凭我的胡子，他是个魔鬼！」
一片笑赞他伶俐的声浪里，赛义德挤出人群，同那女人打了照面。「欢迎，三倍的欢迎，哈斯娜！」他喊。「赞美安拉，你活着，还康健！这长长的一段日子，我的心为你难过了又难过。又寻着了你，我欢喜，我的魂！」
她张开双臂，尖叫一声，扑上他的脖子哭了。
「渔夫赛义德！」「促狭鬼赛义德！」「魔鬼赛义德！」「他怎么到这儿来的？」人们敬畏地低声传——那口气像在说：苏丹陛下——帕夏大人。人们挤上来，摸一摸他的袍角也好，看一眼他的脸也好；赛义德倒害怕起来，怕人留意他裤裆的鼓与沉。他向众人行礼，一条胳膊圈住哈斯娜，领她出到黎明清冽的空气里。
在朝沙漠那一面的墙角，两人并排蹲坐。他讲起刚熬过的那可怕的一夜；她听着，一双耐心的眼睛把他吞吃。
「我发财了，我的心肝！」他拔着一株浸透露水的蓟，喊道。「我要买一座好宅子，咱们一起住。你管着一院人数众多的闺眷。我有个甜姑娘——美人儿！——一个死掉的基督徒猪猡的女儿。她做你的丫头，听你差遣。

===Original===

Let us abide here today, for while the tumult continues there is neither buying nor selling in the city.﻿ ﻿…”
He paused, thoughtful, remembering the burial of Mustafa and his duty to be present. But reflecting that men would suppose him with the slayers, and excuse him for the cause of the Faith, his brow cleared directly and he continued﻿—
“Tomorrow, or the next day, we will return thither, when thou shalt help me to choose a grand house, and shalt see the girl Ferideh of whom I spake. She is sweet, I tell thee﻿—a perfect pearl. But thou art mistress of my fancy﻿—that is understood. Now, in the name of Allah relieve me of some part of this treasure which bruises my legs and impedes my going.”
The prospect seemed very bright to Hasneh. She ceased to grieve that her veil was torn. Gladly she opened the bosom of her robe and bestowed the half of their riches in the pouch she wore there. The transfer made, Saïd rose and took a turn to enjoy his novel lightness. The well and the sycamore-tree grew rosy, casting long blue shadows. The wide plain was barred and flecked with pink.
“O Saïd, dost thou remember the fig-tree and our house among the sandhills by the sea?” murmured Hasneh; and then, with a blissful sigh, her eyelids closed against the sun’s first ray, “Allah is Merciful!”

===Chinese===

「今天咱们就歇在这儿，城里乱着，买也买不成，卖也卖不成。……」
他顿住，寻思——想起穆斯塔法要下葬，他本该到场。可转念一想，人家只当他同杀人的在一处，为教门的缘故必能见谅——眉头当即展了，接着说——
「明天，或是后天，咱们回城去，你帮我挑一座气派的宅子，再见见我说的那个姑娘菲丽黛。她甜，我告诉你——十全的珍珠。可你是我心尖上的主母——这个不用讲。这会儿，看安拉的分上，替我分担些这财吧——它硌我的腿，绊我的脚。」
这前景在哈斯娜看去亮堂堂。她不再为破面纱伤心了。她欢欢喜喜敞开怀，把两人家当的一半收进她贴身的口袋。交割完毕，赛义德起身来回走了两趟，受用这份新得的轻快。井和那棵西克莫无花果树泛出玫瑰色，投下长长的蓝影。宽阔的平原，一道一道、一点一点，抹着粉红。
「赛义德，你还记得那棵无花果树，记得咱们海边沙丘里那个家么？」哈斯娜喃喃道；随后，幸福地一叹，她阖上眼皮挡开太阳的头一线光：「安拉是慈悲的！」

## Part / 第二部 他的命运之书
===Original===

II The Book of His Fate
