“Then be kind to her, as thou regardest thy salvation. Remember that, in the last day, the weak shall take their vengeance upon the strong, the unarmed upon the armed, the unhorned cattle upon the horned cattle. For Allah is just, and in the end He will make the balance level.”
And again﻿—
“Thou that art a fisherman, and knowest the ways of the sea, tell me, What does a mariner when shipwrecked on the coast of his own country?”
Saïd reflected a minute, supposing it had been a riddle.
“By my beard, I suppose that he will praise Allah, and then he will return with speed to his own place.”
“Good,” replied the great man; “the case is thine. A while ago thou didst set out in the hope to gain honour; but now behold thou art shipwrecked. Out of thy mouth I counsel thee, Take thy woman with thee and go home, return to thy native place and to thy fishing, and perchance we shall find thee money wherewith to buy nets and a house.”
This advice did not please Saïd. He dreaded the triumph of Abdullah, who must by this time be among the greatest of his native town. However, he said nothing openly to his benefactor, but feigned to fall in gladly with the plan.
At the house of Ismaìl Abbâs there was much company, for the host was renowned for hospitality, and many loved him. All present used Saïd friendly, wishing him a blessed feast, and not scorning to sit at meat with him. Throughout the night there was good cheer and the wisest discourse; for above all things save piety, Ismaìl Abbâs prized wisdom and learning, and his friends were chosen for their qualities rather than wealth or rank.

===Chinese===

到得清静处，赛义德放声哭出来，就要伏下去吻救主的脚，被那位好人拦了。
「不，安拉不容你拜倒在我面前！」伊斯玛仪·阿拔斯有点嗔怪地说。「你若有可谢的，把赞颂归给该得的。我为你做的，不比为一个落难的狗做的多多少；狗也自有活魂，有人这么说来着。」
他领着赛义德捾僻静的路走，一路问他些出格的古怪问题；比如——
「大难临头的日子，身边还剩下个老婆么？」
「还给我留着我的老伴，埃米尔——从头就跟着我的那个，我头一个有的。」
「那就要善待她，如果你还看重你的得救。记住：到末日，弱的必向强的讨还，无兵刃的必向有兵刃的讨还，无角的牛必向有角的牛讨还。安拉是公道的，末了祂必把秤摆平。」
又问——
「你这打鱼的，识得海路，告诉我：水手在自己的国境海岸上失了船，怎么办？」
赛义德寻思了一分钟，当它是谜语。
「凭我的胡子说，我想他先赞美安拉，然后赶紧回自己老家去。」
「好，」大人物答；「这案子就是你的。前些时候你出海求名；如今瞧，你失了船。我拿你自己嘴里的主意劝你：带上你的女人，回家去，回你生身的地方、回你的渔本行；兴许我们还能替你凑出买网买房的钱。」
这主意不中赛义德的意。他怕看阿卜杜拉的神气——那人此刻必已是他家乡数一数二的大人物。可他没对恩人流露半句，装作欢欢喜喜领了这番筹划。
伊斯玛仪·阿拔斯府上宾客盈门——主人好客出了名，爱他的人也多。满座待赛义德都和气，祝他节日蒙福，不以同席为耻。一整夜有吃有喝、有最智慧的谈吐；因为除虔诚之外，伊斯玛仪·阿拔斯最珍重智慧与学问，他的朋友是按品性挑的，不问财势。

===Original===

Towards morning, when men rose to go, the Sherìf took Saïd apart to speak with him alone. He advised him strongly to go back to his first trade of a fisherman. Es-Shâm was full of his enemies, an evil story being current there concerning him. He (Ismaìl) had judged it false from the first; and yet many were found to put faith in it. It behoved Saïd to leave the city as soon as the sacred month should expire.
This last counsel fell in timely with the fisherman’s own wishes, and he promised humbly to follow it. Then, having received his host’s blessing, and a handsome present of money wherewith to buy nets and a house, Saïd took his leave, kissing his patron’s hand repeatedly, and calling upon Allah to reward his kindness.
It wanted but four hours of daybreak and the sounds of revelry were growing faint and rare. Many of the candles had guttered and gone out, and those which remained burned dimly and awry. The stars resumed their sway and a slumbrous calm wrapped the city. There would be peace now until an hour before sunrise, when most men would rise and eat again, to fortify themselves against the long day’s fast. Saïd met several parties wending homeward from carousals. He himself went not home, but to the dwelling of Selìm, where there were lights burning. The mother of Mûsa opened to his knocking. She peered hard at him. “Praise be to Allah!” she cried, flinging up her hands. “Deign to enter, O my lord! It is indeed the master! Come, O Selìm! Behold, his Eminence is restored to us in safety. Know, O Effendi, that Selìm has been greatly troubled this night on thy account, because thou camest not to the tavern of Rashìd though he sat there long awaiting thee. He feared some evil had befallen thee; but now we behold thee safe, thanks to Allah!”
Selìm rushed forward with the like expressions of joy and gratitude. It was some time before Saïd could make himself heard, for the stir of his entrance had awakened the children, who screamed and roared in chorus. But at last, by the exertions of Mûsa and his mother, the din subsided, and he said﻿—
“After five days I leave Es-Shâm forever, and Hasneh with me. By the grace of Allah, I have now a little money with which we shall journey to the seacoast, and there take ship, I care not whither, so that it be far from this city of falsehood.”
Selìm received the news with a cheerful face.

===Chinese===

近早晨，人们起身告辞，谢里夫把赛义德拉到一边单独说话。他力劝他重操渔夫的旧业。沙姆满城是他的仇家，一桩恶毒的故事正传得凶。他（伊斯玛仪）从头就断它是假的；可肯信的人还是不少。圣月一满，赛义德就该离城，迟疑不得。
末了这条忠告恰合渔夫自家的心意，他谦卑地应承照办。随后领了主人的祝福，和一份买网买房的厚赠，赛义德告辞出来，一而再地吻恩主的手，呼求安拉报答他的仁慈。
离天亮还差四个钟头，欢宴的声息渐稀渐杳。许多蜡烛淌尽熄了，剩下的烧得昏昏斜斜。星星重掌了权柄，一阵欲睡的静笼住全城。此后太平，直到日出前一个钟头，大多数人还要起身再吃一顿，好支撑漫长的白日之斋。
赛义德遇上几伙从宴饮归家的人。他没回家，去了塞利姆的住处——那里还亮着灯。穆萨的母亲应声开门。她使劲端详他。「赞美安拉！」她扬起双手喊。「请进，老爷！真是东家回来了！来呀，塞利姆！瞧，大人平安回到咱们跟前了。您可知道，埃芬迪，塞利姆今夜为您急坏了——您没去拉希德的酒馆，他坐等了好久。他怕您遭了什么不测；这会儿托安拉的福，见您好好的！」
塞利姆抢上前来，说着一番同样的欢喜感激。赛义德好一阵子插不上嘴——他进门这一惊动把孩子们吵醒了，齐声哭喊震天。末了亏得穆萨和他娘一阵整治，聒噪平息，他才说——
「五天之后，我永离沙姆，哈斯娜随我。托安拉的恩，我如今有点钱了，够我们上路到海边，上船，去哪儿都行——只要离这座谎城远远的。」
塞利姆得了这消息，一脸的欢喜。

===Original===

“It is but a minute since I spoke to the same purpose,” he said; “is it not so, O mother of Mûsa? Of a truth, since thy ruin this city displeases me and, thanks to thee under Allah, I am well provided with money, which can serve us both. I thought to go into Masr﻿—what sayest thou? I have a brother who migrated thither in the time of Ibrahìm Basha, when Masr was as one country with Es-Shâm. He is well established in the city of Iskendería, and from time to time he sends a word to me by travelling merchants. He declares it to be a pleasant land, favourable for every kind of trade. We will journey together, by thy leave; Allah grant us a safe voyage and prosperity in the end!”
At that Saïd seized both hands of his friend and kissed them, blessing Selìm for a good man and a faithful﻿—none like him in all the world!
So it came to pass, one early morning, that Saïd and Hasneh left the great city, in the company of Selìm and all his family, by the same road which Saïd had followed at his coming, nearly twelve years before. At the brow of the hill, beside the shrine which is there, they turned to look their last upon that place of gardens. Saïd’s eyes brooded long and lovingly over it, as though it had been indeed the early paradise he was leaving; and it was with a choking voice that at last he bade Selìm lead on.

===Chinese===

「前一分钟我还正说这个来着，」他说；「是不是，穆萨的娘？说真的，自你败落，这城就叫我生厌；托安拉的福、也托你的福，我手头很有些钱，够咱们两个花用。我本打算去马斯尔——你说呢？我有个兄弟，易卜拉欣帕夏那年迁去的，那时马斯尔同沙姆算一国。他在亚历山大城站得很稳，时不时托来往的商贾给我捎句话。他说那是个好地方，样样买卖都相宜。咱们一道上路，你若容我；愿安拉赐我们一路平安，末了兴旺！」
赛义德一听，抓住朋友的双手就吻，为塞利姆祝祷——好人，忠仆——普天下没有第二个！
于是，一个清早，赛义德和哈斯娜离开了那座大城，塞利姆一家全体同行——走的正是将近十二年前赛义德来时的那条路。在山脊上，在那座神龛旁边，他们回身，最后一望那片园囿之地。赛义德的眼睛久久地、恋恋地抚过它，仿佛那真是我将辞别的早年的天园；末了，他哑着嗓子，叫塞利姆头前引路。

### X / 十
===Original===

The little company journeyed but slowly, for the sake of the women and children. The weather was hot and breathless, as it often is at the extreme end of summer, when the air begins to grow heavy with the first storm. Selìm had provided two donkeys to carry the baggage, and also to give a spell of rest to anyone who grew weary. One bore the weight of his household treasures, and his wife with her young baby rode upon it when she chose. Saïd generally bestrode the other, which was laden with his goods, while Hasneh walked meekly beside; though sometimes, feeling the need to stretch his legs, he would alight and bid her take his place for a time. Often he would take up one of Selìm’s children to ride with him; and Selìm himself, with Mûsa, made shift to carry the others when they tired.
At first their way lay through mountains, barren and treeless, except for certain favoured nooks, where there was water and deep shade of fruit-trees. Through the heat of the day the landscape seemed to bronze, so massive it was and sullen under the burning sky. A rare terebinth, growing high up among the cliffs, was rusty black, and cast a shadow uncouth as the rocks themselves. But in the early morning, what with the young sunlight and the dewy shade, every boulder had a charm and freshness of its own, so that the little band sang blithely at setting out. And towards sundown, when the peaks were all purple and gold, and the level spaces coloured like flowerbeds, they drank in the coolness of the evening with sighs of relief.
They crossed the plain called El Bica’a, with its scattered villages, and all through one afternoon they moved along in the growing shadow of Lebanon. Ere noon of the next day they paused on the crest of the mountain and beheld the coast-plain far below them languishing in a haze of heat. The sea beyond was like a burnished sheet of silver. Saïd’s heart leapt at the familiar sheen of it, but the sight brought no enduring pleasure. His native land was very dear to his soul now that the time drew near when he must quit it. They were now on the Sultàn’s highway﻿—a great white coach-road, the work of a Frankish company, whose zigzag windings could be traced as a wan and crumpled ribbon down all the mountainside.

===Chinese===

这一小队人马走得慢，为着女人孩子。天气又热又闷，像夏末常有那样——空气开始叫头一场雷雨压得沉了。塞利姆备了两头驴驮行李，也给走乏的人歇脚。一头驮着他全家的宝贝，他老婆抱着奶娃娃，愿意的时候就骑上。萨义德多半骑另一头，驴上驮着他的行李，哈斯娜乖乖走在旁边；有时他觉得该活泛活泛腿脚，便下来，叫她顶替一阵。他还常常抱起塞利姆的一个孩子同骑；塞利姆自己带着穆萨，孩子们乏了就换着背。
起先一路是山，荒赤无树，只有几处得宠的洼坳有水、有果树浓荫。白日最热时，整片山水像蒙了一层青铜——那么厚重、那么阴沉地压在烧着的天底下。难得一株笃耨香长在崖高处，锈黑色，投下的影子同岩石本身一样笨拙。可一清早，趁着年轻的阳光和带露的荫凉，每块圆石都有各自的妩媚和清新，小小一队出发时唱得欢。到日头将落，山尖一片紫金，平地染得像花床，他们把傍晚的凉一口口吸进去，一声声舒坦地叹。
他们过了叫贝卡的平原，一路散布着村庄；一个整下午，他们走在黎巴嫩渐渐铺长的影子里。第二天晌午前，他们在山脊上停住，望见脚底远远的海岸平原，热得恹恹地卧在一片热雾里。海在更远处，像一整幅擦亮的银。萨义德的心为那熟识的光一跳，可这景象带不来持久的欢喜。故乡在他的魂里此刻格外亲——眼看他就要抛下它了。他们此刻在素丹的官道上——一条白色的大马车路，法兰克公司修的——它一路之字盘旋，顺着整面山坡，像一条惨白的、揉皱了的缎带。

===Original===

Carriages dashed past them, filled for the most part with Christians in semi-Frankish dress, forcing the group of wayfarers to the roadside, blinding and choking them with a cloud of dust.
The sun was near his setting when they reached the level of the plain. On all sides there were gardens plumed with date-palms, and fine stone dwellings bosomed in leafage. Seaward, across the plantations, loomed a dark belt of pines. A flight of bee-eaters wheeling in the flush of sunset seemed like dead leaves the sport of a wind. The road lay straight before them, stained with sunset light. There was much people in carriages and on horseback﻿—townsfolk of Beyrût﻿—come forth to taste the sweets of evening. Shadows were long and grey-blue to eastward.
The sight of the palm-trees and the diffused fragrance moved Saïd deeply. He knew that the sea was at hand﻿—the sea which he had known from babyhood, whose voice was a home voice to him. Yet at that time he loathed the thought of it, his heart yearning to the sweet gardens and the peaceful life of a husbandman.
Weary and footsore they entered the city of Beyrût, and it seemed to Saïd that he was already in a strange land. The Frankish garb was almost as common in the streets as the dress of the country, and four men out of every five he saw were Christians. He had been there once before on an errand of commerce, but the foreign character of the town had not struck him then as now. Nearly all the houses had red-tiled roofs, and the shops were of a pattern unfamiliar to him. The streets were wide and ablaze with lights. Wheeled carriages, each drawn by a pair of horses and driven by one who sat aloft with frenzied shouting and cracking of a whip, were frequent here though in the capital they were still esteemed a fine rarity. He began to be afraid for the future. If he felt thus lonely in a seaport town of his own country, how could he bear to dwell in a foreign land? He made his uneasiness known to Selìm, who bade him be of good cheer, for that Beyrût stood alone, the lord of all the world for iniquity and unbelief. In Masr he would find it quite otherwise; there the faithful outnumbered the infidels as ten to one.
Selìm was well acquainted with the city, having often visited it in the days when he was a muleteer.

===Chinese===

马车一辆辆从他们身边飞驰而过，多半载着半法兰克装束的基督徒，把这一伙行路人挤到路边，一蓬尘土呛得人睁不开眼。
他们下到平地时，太阳快落了。四面是插着枣椰羽冠的园子和藏进树丛的漂亮石屋。朝海那边，隔着种植园，浮起一带黑沉沉的松林。一群蜂虎在晚霞里盘旋，像叫风耍弄的枯叶。路笔直铺在前头，染着落日的颜色。路上人多得很，坐车的、骑马的——贝鲁特的城里人，出来尝傍晚的甜头。影子长长的，蓝灰色，铺向东边。
枣椰的景象和漫开的香气深深打动了萨义德。他知道海就在近旁——他从襁褓里就认得的海，它的声音在他耳朵里是乡音。可这一刻他厌恶想它，他的心恋着甜园子和庄稼人的太平日子。
一身乏、两脚疼，他们进了贝鲁特城；萨义德觉得自己已经置身异邦了。法兰克装束在街上几乎同本乡装束一样多，他看见的人，五个里有四个是基督徒。他从前为买卖来过一趟，可这座城的外国气派从没像今天这样扎他。几乎家家红瓦顶，铺子的样式他不认得。街宽，灯火通明。带轮子的马车——两匹马拉着，赶车的高高坐着，发疯似的吆喎甩鞭——在这儿比比皆是，虽说在都城它们还算稀罕物。他开始为将来发怵。在本国的一座海口城里他都这样孤单，到了外国怎么住得下去？他把这份不安说给塞利姆，塞利姆叫他宽心：贝鲁特是独一份，天下头一号的邪恶不信之城；到了马斯尔就全然两样，那儿信士对异教徒是十对一。
塞利姆同这座城熟得很——赶骡那些年常来。

===Original===

He led his company by quiet and tortuous ways to the Muslim quarter, where there was less of a foreign appearance to trouble Saïd. They took their lodging at a khan which overlooked an ancient burying-ground tufted with black cypresses. Hard by was a mosque whose squat, ungainly minaret stood up against the last green of evening. An owl hooted in some bush of the graveyard. The place had a wistful sadness in the gathering night.
After they had washed and prayed, Saïd and Selìm took Mûsa with them to the guest-chamber, where they ate apart, the women being entertained elsewhere in the house by their own kind. The room was filled with men of all conditions, from the rich merchant with his saddlebags beside him to the servant who sat or rose at his master’s nod, and the muleteer squatting shamefaced by the door. A portly man of middle age sat with his back against the wall, sucking luxuriously at a narghileh. His bright, shifty eyes were keenly observant of all that went on. He looked earnestly at Saïd and watched him all the while he was eating. At length, when the coffee was brought, he coiled the tube and mouthpiece about the vessel of his pipe and crossed the room.
“Peace be upon thee, O Saïd, O my dear!” he said heartily. “Allah be praised that I behold thy face once more! How is thy health? If Allah will, it is the best possible!”
Surprised by the warmth of this greeting in a place where he was a stranger, Saïd eyed the man narrowly as he rose in acknowledgment. Surely it could not be!﻿—And yet, who else?﻿ ﻿… In dismay and amazement he recognised his sometime friend and partner, Abdullah the fisherman. He stepped aside with him.
“How goes thy business all this long time, O father of Azìz?” he asked, when the perfunctory compliments had given him time to recover from the shock of the encounter.
“Praise be to Allah, not ill; I cannot complain, for I am now high in honour in our city. It is a small city﻿—that is true﻿—but what eminence may be attained therein I have attained. There is talk of recommending me to the *Mutesarrif* to be *Caimmacàm*, when the time comes to make a change. Of a truth, if they choose me not I know not of whom they will make choice, for there is none in all those parts to vie with me in wealth and consequence.”
He bragged with assurance, but his dress belied his words, for he was meanly clad.

===Chinese===

他领着他的队伍，捾僻静的弯路进了穆斯林街区，那里外国气派少些，不搅萨义德的心。他们落脚在一家客店，窗子对着一片古墓地，黑柏树一丛丛。近旁一座清真寺，那矮墩墩、笨拙的宣礼塔衬着傍晚最后一线绿。一只猫头鹰在墓地什么树丛里叫。渐浓的夜给这地方罩上一层怅惘的哀。
洗过、礼过，萨义德和塞利姆带上穆萨去客房，他们单独吃，女人们由她们同类在屋里另一处招待。房里坐满各色人等：从身边搁着鞍袋的富商，到主人一点头或坐或起的仆人，再到臊眉耷眼蹲在门边的骡夫。一个中年胖子背靠着墙坐，美滋滋咂着一袋水烟。一双亮而溜的眼睛把一切动静盯得紧紧的。他恳切地望萨义德，萨义德吃饭的当口一直盯。末了，咖啡上来，他把烟管和烟嘴往烟壶上一盘，穿过了屋子。
「愿你平安，萨义德，俺的亲亲！」他热热地说。「安拉可赞，又见着你的脸了！你身子可好？安拉愿意，顶好吧！」
在一个人地两生的地方受这样滚热的招呼，萨义德起身还礼，把来人细相。不能吧！——可不然，又能是谁？……他又惊又懵，认出了他从前的朋友兼伙伴——渔夫阿卜杜拉。他同他退到一边。
「这一向买卖如何，阿齐兹他爹？」等那通例行的客套给了他从震动里缓过来的工夫，他问。
「感赞安拉，不赖；俺没什么可抱怨的，俺如今在俺们城里体面得很。城是小城——不假——可城里够得着的体面，俺都够着了。正有人念叨，等换人的时候，保举俺见穆特萨里夫，做凯马卡姆。说实话，他们要不挑俺，俺不知道还能挑谁：那一带论家业论身份，没人跟俺比得了。」
他吹得笃定，衣裳却拆他的台：穿得寒磘。

===Original===

“As for thee, O my soul, how fares it with thee?” he inquired in his turn.
“By the grace of Allah, I thrive,” said Saïd, casting up his eyes fervently. “By the Quran, I am happiest of men. All that belongs to wealth and honour and prosperity is mine, and I am risen to the supreme height of my desire. And behold all this is come to me because of that foul trick thou didst play me years ago, O sly robber that thou art!”
“Whoever robbed thee it was not I﻿—Allah be my witness! No, by my beard, it was some other, and that a devil in all likelihood,” murmured Abdullah, blandly, as if disclaiming an honour one would thrust on him. “But say, where dwellest thou, O my eyes?”
“In Es-Shâm﻿—in the great city, O my dear, where I own a fine house such as a prince might envy. By Allah, I am become a great one in that city, which is the first of all cities in the world. All the notables are my friends, and the Wâly himself disdains not to seek my advice in the affairs of state. Allah is bountiful!”
“Allah is bountiful indeed,” said Abdullah, regarding Saïd with a new interest. “But tell me, art thou that Saïd the Merchant whose name is in all men’s mouths?”
“I am in truth that great one,” was the reply; “but I know not what thing thou hast heard, for many lies are spoken concerning me.”
“Listen, and thou shalt hear all I know. It is but a few hours since I met one who was just returned from the country of Rûm. And in that country he heard the story of Saïd, a merchant of Damashc-ush-Shâm, who was robbed by the woman whom most he favoured. She caused him to drink a potion wherein was a strong drug, pretending that it was a sherbet of figs. Her lover, a young Nazarene of the same city, is cunning in pharmacy, having studied here in Beyrût and also among the Franks to become a chemist. It is he who gave her the drug and taught her how to administer it. Her lord trusted her in all things, and she was in the secret of his wealth, so she robbed him easily of all that he had, and took her little son and fled away with that Nazarene while he slept. The cunning of the Christian﻿—may Allah destroy him!﻿—had caused him to make himself a French subject long ago, in the year of the great slaughter when all was confusion. He had a passport and Frankish clothes in waiting.

===Chinese===

「你呢，俺的心肝，你混得怎么样？」轮到他问。
「托安拉的恩，兴旺。」萨义德说着，热切地翻起眼睛。「凭古兰经说，俺是天下头一个快活人。财富、体面、顺遂，样样是俺的；俺升到了俺愿望的顶顶上。瞧啊，这一切全拜你多年前提弄俺的那场脏把戏所赐，你这条滑贼！」
「抢你的不管是谁，反正不是俺——安拉作证！不，凭俺的胡子，是别人，八成是个魔鬼。」阿卜杜拉甜甜地咕哝，像推让一份硬塞给他的荣誉。「可你说，你住哪儿，俺的眼珠？」
「住沙姆——住那座大城，俺的亲亲，俺在城里置了一座好宅，王公见了眼热。凭安拉说，俺成了那城的大人物了；那城是天底下头一座城。名流个个是俺的朋友，瓦利本人议国事也不嫌弃问俺。安拉慷慨！」
「安拉确实慷慨。」阿卜杜拉说，对萨义德添了新的兴味。「可你告诉俺：人人嘴上那个商人萨义德，就是你？」
「俺正是那位大人物，」是回答，「可不知道你听了些什么，说俺的谎话多着呢。」
「听着，俺知道的全告诉你。就在几个钟头前，俺碰见一个刚从鲁姆国回来的人。他在那边听说了萨义德的故事——大马士革的商人，叫他顶宠的女人抢了。那女人哄他喝了一剂下了烈药的饮料，只说是无花果香露。她的相好，同城一个年轻的拿撒勒教徒，精于药道——在贝鲁特读过书，又上法兰克人那儿学的药剂——药是他给她的，怎么下也是他教的。她的主子事事信她，她攦着全部家当的底细，轻而易举把他刮了个净，带上她的小儿子，趁他睡着，同那拿撒勒教徒跑了。那基督徒——叫安拉毁了他！——刀笔精得很：早在大屠杀那年、满城乱糟糟的时候，就给自个儿入了法国籍。护照、法兰克衣裳，早备好了等着。

===Original===

To make more sure, the dragoman of the consulate﻿—who was the son of his aunt on the mother’s side﻿—journeyed with them in the public coach to this city, where the people of the customhouse, supposing them to be Franks, let them pass unquestioned, the child with them. They tell me this Nazarene hates the child, which is natural, being the work of another than himself. He would fain be rid of the burden, but the woman will not part with it. So they took ship and came at last to the country of Rûm, where they now dwell in the largest city, in the best manner, with all luxury. Their story is known to all men, and the laugh is ever against Saïd the Merchant of Damashc-ush-Shâm.﻿ ﻿… The Christians are all wild beasts, by Allah﻿—foul and wicked things, unclean and accurst. But surely thou art not the man they tell of? Allah forbid! It is impossible!”
All this was bitter as death to Saïd. His teeth and hands clenched. For a moment he thought of nothing but to pursue those two who had wronged him over sea and land, to slay them, if it might be, in each other’s arms. He saw his son attired as a Christian, despised and ill-treated by the pig, his enemy. He gnashed his teeth with the knowledge that men made mock of him, that his name was become a byword of scoffing to unbelievers in distant lands. But he swallowed the gall of his anguish as best he could. When he spoke it was with a scornful countenance.
“O my eyes, a part of thy tale is true, but not all. That son of a pig, that Christian of whom thou tellest did certainly carry off a woman of mine, but what is that?﻿—I can afford to replace her. As for the child, I have been concerned for him, but now that I know whither they are gone I will inform the Government, and it shall go ill with me but I will recover him. The woman did in truth rob me of a sum of money; but she was not fully in my confidence. There were two hoards, thou understandest, hidden in two separate places. She mistook the lesser for the greater, and so, far from being ruined, as she fondly supposed, I am now, by the blessing of Allah, even more prosperous and higher in honour than I was before. Allah is just!”
“Praise be to Allah!” said Abdullah, feelingly. “I rejoice with thee”; and upon that he wished Saïd a happy night and withdrew, saying that he must hie to bed, as he was to start betimes on the morrow on his journey home.

===Chinese===

为稳妥起见，领馆的通译——他姨表兄——还同他们一道坐班车到了这座城。海关的人当他们是法兰克人，连孩子带人，没查就放了。听说那拿撒勒教徒嫌那孩子碍眼——也是人情，毕竟不是他自己的种。他想甩掉这个累赘，可那女人不肯撒手。随后他们上了船，末了到了鲁姆国，如今住在那边顶大的城里，日子过得顶讲究，样样奢侈。他们的故事人人皆知，笑话永远落在商人萨义德头上——大马士革的商人萨义德。……基督徒全是野兽，凭安拉说——又脏又坏的东西，不洁又可咒。可你总不是他们说的那个人吧？安拉不容！不能够！」
这一切，对萨义德苦得要命。他的牙咬紧，手攦紧。有一刻他什么也不想，只想追那两个负了他的人，跨海越陆，能把他们宰在那彼此的怀里才好。他看见他的儿子穿一身基督徒的衣裳，受那头猪——他的仇人——的白眼和作践。他咬碎牙：人人拿他当笑柄，他的名字成了远国异教徒嘴里的笑谚。可他把这口苦胆能咽地咽了。他开口时，脸上挂着讥诮。
「俺的眼珠，你的话有一半是真的，不全真。那猪儿子、你说的那个基督徒，确实拐了俺的一个女人，可那算什么？——俺再置一个就是。孩子，俺一直挂心；如今知道他们去了哪儿，俺就报官，拼了命也要把他追回来。那女人确确实实抢了俺一笔钱；可她没摸透俺的底。你要知道，藏的是两窖，两处分开藏。她把小的当成大的拿了。所以她当自己毁了我，其实呢，托安拉的福，俺如今比先前更兴旺、更体面。安拉公道！」
「感赞安拉！」阿卜杜拉由衷地说。「俺跟你一块儿欢喜。」说罢他祝萨义德一夜安好，退了出去，说他得赶紧睡——明天一早要动身回家。

===Original===

So these two, so long asunder, met once more on friendly terms and lied freely one to the other, neither doubting his fellow’s words.
Saïd slept ill that night. Divers projects turned in his brain, distracting him. Every forward course seemed grievous, fraught with danger. There was but one bright point in all his weary musings as he tossed to and fro upon his pallet﻿—the face of a girl he had seen once in a garden﻿—an English girl and mistress to the son of a pig, a dragoman. He recalled all that he had heard of the land of the English, and ever he swore, with Allah’s leave, he would contrive to go there ere he died.
Selìm was abroad early in the morning, for there was much to be done, and in his loving care for his former master he took all charge of it upon himself. First, he visited sundry taverns and places of resort, publishing the news that he had two fine donkeys for sale. By the third hour there was a small crowd gathered at the stable, and the sale, when it took place, was in the nature of an auction, one man bidding above another. When that was done and the beasts had been led away by their purchasers, Selìm betook himself to the Seraï to get permission to leave the country, and have the passports put in order. He was so long absent on this business that Saïd, who waited him at the khan, began to be uneasy. When at last he did return, the expression of his face was woebegone in the extreme. Saïd cried out in alarm to know what was amiss. Whereupon the faithful fellow wrung his hands, and tears rolled down his cheeks.
“O Saïd! O my brother! Allah be my witness, I have striven long with prayer and argument to turn their hearts; but in vain. Ah, woe is me, to be the bearer of such ill tidings! Know, O my beloved, that the men of the Government gave me free leave to depart with my family; as thou knowest, I have a letter which Ismaìl Abbâs﻿—may Allah requite his honour!﻿—procured for me from the Wâly. But thee they will by no means suffer to quit the land, both because thou hast no such letter, and for some other cause which is hid from me. All my entreaties, all my reasons were unavailing; thou art forbidden to travel further by order of the Government.”
Fear came into Saïd’s eyes as he heard. Heretofore the Government had seemed to him remote as the sky is, something impassive, neither friend nor foe.

===Chinese===

就这样，这两位久别的人又亲亲热热地见了一面，痛痛快快地互相撒谎，谁也不疑谁的话。
那夜萨义德睡得坏。各样的打算在脑子里打转，搅他。每一条向前的路都苦，都带着险。他在铺上翻来覆去，一身疲乏的盘算里只有一个亮点——一张姑娘的脸，他有一回在一座园子里见过——一个英国姑娘，一头猪的儿子、一个通译的相好。他把听来的关于英国地方的一切又过了一遍，一遍遍起誓：凭安拉容许，死以前一定要设法去一趟。
清早塞利姆就出去了：事多，而他疼他的旧主人，一概揽在自己身上。先，他跑了各家酒馆和聚处，放话有两头好驴出卖。到第三个钟头，马厩边聚了一小伙人；买卖做成时活像一场竞买，一个比一个往上抬。办完、驴叫买主牵走，塞利姆去塞拉伊官署办出境许可、理护照。这趟差他去了那么久，在客店等他的萨义德发起慌来。末了他总算回来了，脸上那副苦相苦到了头。萨义德惊叫着问出了什么事。忠心的家伙绞着手，眼泪顺着腮帮子滚。
「萨义德！俺的兄弟！安拉作证，俺又祷告又说理，磨了他们半天，想转他们的心；白磨。唉，俺苦啊，做这个报凶信的人！你要知道，俺的亲亲：官府的人准俺携眷出境；你知道，俺有伊斯梅尔·阿巴斯——叫安拉报答他！——替俺从瓦利那儿求得的一封信。可你，他们说死也不放你出境：一来你没这么一封信，二来还有一层缘故，俺摸不着。俺怎么求、怎么讲理都白搭；官府有令，禁你再往前走。」
萨义德听着，眼里进了惧。直到如今，官府在他觉来远得像天，一个不动声色的东西，不是友也不是敌。

===Original===

He had stood in the same vague awe of it that a simple man has of some mighty engine whose working is a mystery to him. Now that he suddenly found it his enemy, the shock was like an earthquake destroying old landmarks. He remembered the dark net of which the Chief of Police had spoken, and felt himself already caught in its meshes.
“I must leave the country, and that at once!” he muttered fearfully. “In the old days I was known for a strong swimmer. Say, O Selìm, is there no ship far out in the bay, beyond call of the Custom House, to which I can swim by night?”
“There is an English ship, O my brother﻿—a steamer which comes hither at times with merchandise. She will depart, they tell me, tomorrow after sunrise. She lies tonight in the bay, but far out; thou couldst hardly swim so far. If thou trustest indeed to escape by swimming, wait two days, I pray thee, until our steamer arrives, so we may yet journey together.”
Saïd caught at the words “an English ship.” In a flash he had a vision of fair forms, and faces full of love, in a light subdued and gentle﻿—the light, as he conceived it, of cloudy Lûndra. The next moment he was reminded of the woman who was a clog upon him, and he broke out fretfully﻿—
“There is Hasneh,﻿ ﻿… O Lord!﻿ ﻿… How may I be rid of Hasneh? I must escape at once; this very night I must swim out to the English steamer, and she alone hinders me.”
Selìm heard him with mild surprise.
“She will go with me to Masr, as was at first arranged,” he said soothingly. “Let thy mind have rest concerning her. My passport is so worded that she may journey with us unquestioned. The mother of Mûsa will be glad to have her company in a strange land, for they love one another, and Hasneh is very skilful in all housework. Be assured, O my brother! By Allah’s leave, thou shalt find her safe when thou rejoinest us yonder. But alas! how can I part from thee, O my soul! As long as I live I am thy servant, for the sake of the kindness thou hast ever shown me, from the day thou didst give me that rich garment, the root of my honour, to this hour. Couldst thou not swim as well to one ship as to another? and what are two days that they should have power to ruin thee? I will find out some private place where thou mayst be snugly hid. Allah forbid that ever I should part from thee!”

===Chinese===

他对它怀着的那种懵懂的敬畏，同乡下人对一部大机器的敬畏一样——机器怎么转，他是摸不透的。此刻它猛然成了他的仇家，这一震像一场地震，把旧的界石全震翻了。他想起警察总长说过的那张黑网，觉得自己已经缠在网眼里了。
「俺得离境，立刻！」他惶惶地喃喃。「早年间俺是出了名的水性好。你说，塞利姆，湾里远处有没有一条船——海关喎不应的地方——俺夜里泅过去？」
「有一条英国船，俺的兄弟——一条轮船，有时载货来。他们告诉俺，明天日头一出它就开。今夜它泊在湾里，可泊得远；你泅不了那么远。你当真要泅，俺求你等两天，等俺们的轮船到，俺们好还做一路。」
萨义德一把抓住「英国船」这三个字。一眨眼，他看见了一群姣好的身影、一张张盛满爱意的脸，浸在一片温柔收敛的光里——他心想象中的、云雾笼罩的伦德拉（Lûndra）的光。下一刻他想起了那个拖着他腿的女人，烦躁地炸了——
「还有哈斯娜，……主啊！……俺怎么甩掉哈斯娜？俺得这就跑；今儿夜里就得泅到那条英国轮船上去，就她一个人碍俺的事。」
塞利姆听着，温和地一奇。
「她随俺们去马斯尔，原先就是这么定的。」他宽慰地说。「你这份心放下。俺的护照写法上，她可以同俺们同行，没人盘问。穆萨的娘在生地方也乐意有她做伴：她们俩投缘，哈斯娜持家又是全把式。你放心，俺的兄弟！凭安拉容许，你同俺们会合的时候，保管她安安稳稳。可唉！俺怎么同你分手，俺的魂儿！俺活一天就是你一天的仆人，为你一向待俺的好——打你赏俺那件阔袍、俺体面的根那天起，直到这个时辰。哪条船你不一样泅？两天算什么，还能有本事毁了你？俺寻个稳妥地方，把你藏得舒舒服服。安拉不容俺同你分手！」

===Original===

But a great unreasoning fear possessed Saïd, and nothing which Selìm could say might change his purpose. The father of Mûsa blubbered like a baby. Saïd himself was deeply moved, but otherwise, the dread of this instant peril swaying him. Moreover, a thought of the fair ones awaiting him in that distant land of the English helped somewhat to soften the parting on his side. He spent the rest of daylight in preparing for his venture. By the agency of Selìm he procured a stout leathern bag of handy size, wherein he stowed all such of his belongings as seemed indispensable. Of the things which remained over he gave some to Hasneh and some to Selìm, according to their nature and use. Towards evening Selìm went forth to make inquiries, whilst Saïd did somewhat to comfort Hasneh. After a very little while he came back in a hurry, and with a face full of concern.
“It may not be, O my brother,” he said, “thou canst by no means swim to the steamer. Know that there has lately been much emigration﻿—of Christians for the most part, and Drûz out of the mountain. It is their custom to do even as thou purposedst; and to check the tide of them, a watch is set upon the beach at night with orders to fire on all who take the water. Allah have pity! I know not what is to be done.”
Saïd paced the paved yard of the khan, raging like a hunted beast at bay, while Hasneh, in hopes that she might not lose him after all, sobbed with relief. At length he stopped short in his prowl, and, lifting hands and eyes to heaven, “Allah succour me!” he muttered fiercely. “I will take the risk of it.”

===Chinese===

可一股没来由的大恐惧攦住了萨义德，塞利姆说什么也扳不动他的主意。穆萨他爹哭得像个娃娃。萨义德自己也深深动了情，可那眼下大险的惊惶压倒了一切。再说，想到那遥远英国地方等着他的仙女们，倒也多少软了他这头分手的滋味。他把白日剩下的工夫都花在打点这场冒险上。托塞利姆的手，他弄到一只结实称手的皮袋，把一切少不得的家当塞了进去。余下的东西，按各自的性子和用处，一些给了哈斯娜，一些给了塞利姆。傍晚，塞利姆出去打探，萨义德则多少安慰了哈斯娜一阵。去了不大工夫，他匆匆回来了，满脸的焦灼。
「不成，俺的兄弟，」他说，「你万万泅不到那条轮船。要知道，近来出境的人多得很——多半是基督徒，还有山里下来的德鲁兹人。他们的老法子正同你想的一样；为堵这股潮，海滩上夜里设了岗，奉令朝一切下水的人开枪。安拉可怜！俺不知道还有什么法子。」
萨义德在客店石铺的院子里来回踱，像一头被围的困兽；哈斯娜想着兴许到底还丢不了他，抽噎着松了口气。末了，他踱着踱着猛地站住，双手双眼朝天：「安拉助俺！」他狠狠地喃喃。「这个险，俺冒定了。」

### XI / 十一
===Original===

About an hour after sundown Saïd took a sad farewell of his friends, and, all alone, went forth to the shore. He wore an ample cloak of haircloth to conceal the leathern sack he carried. As he made his way through the concourse of the streets his heart thumped so loudly against his ribs that he thought all men not deaf to hear it. On the sea-beach, where the din of the city mingled as a distant murmur with the sigh of the ripples, the clamour of it filled his brain.
The wide bay lay smooth and glassy, fringed along the shore with points of yellow light shining among dark forms of trees and bushes. The mountains rose in outline beyond, ending seaward in a bluff promontory, the lights of many villages plainly seen upon the nearer slopes. A dusky gloom was on all the land﻿—the velvet of a moth’s wings. The lamps of the shipping had dancing pendants in the water.
Saïd tried to seem careless, as if he strolled for pleasure. It was dark and he met no one after he had won clear of the town; but his fancy peopled every wall and garden, every shrub of tamarisk to landward, with soldiers on the lookout; and in spite of all his endeavours the manner of his going betrayed uneasiness. The cry of a mariner wafted across the still water was startling, as if one had called him by name.
He could see the English steamer, a dark mass, with a funnel and three masts, lying motionless a good way out. A red light in the bows shed a sparkle of rubies in the near water. He strove to judge of the distance, seeking that part of the shore which would most favour his project.
A ruined wall ran out a little way on to the sand. On the side remote from the town he sat down and strove to think. A great pulse throbbed in his brain, so that his whole frame was shaken with it. The sea and the lights and the mountains swam before his eyes; the very wall seemed to rock as he leaned against it. The sharp yelp of a dog among the gardens rang bewilderingly in his ears.
At length, his mind growing clearer, he lighted a cigarette and smoked it to the end. Then he got up and took off his garments one by one, throwing some away, and binding others with a sash to the well-filled leather-bag. When he was naked he sat down again, and, holding the bundle pressed on his cap and turban, set to work to lash it to his head with strips torn from his cast-off raiment.

===Chinese===

日落约一个钟头后，赛义德同朋友们凄然道了别，独自一人走向海岸。他披一件宽大的粗毛布斗篷，遮住随身那只皮囊。他穿过街市的人流，心跳撞得肋骨咚咚响，他疑心只要不是聋子，人人都听得见。到了海滩上——城里的喧嚣到这里淡成一片远营，同涟漪的叹息混在一处——它仍灌满他的脑子。
宽阔的海湾平得像玻璃，沿岸一线缀着点点黄光，在树丛灌影的黑团里亮着。山在远处起起伏伏显出轮廓，朝海收作一道陡岬；近坡上，许多村落的灯火望得分明。整片陆地上罩着一层烟色的暝——蛾翅上那种天鹅绒。泊船的灯火在水里曳着跳舞的垂饰。
赛义德竭力装出闲散，仿佛散步取乐。天黑了，出城之后一个人也没遇上；可他的想象给每一堵墙、每一座园子、内陆一侧每一丛柽柳，都安上了望的兵；任他怎样努力，那走相还是漏出了慌张。一声水手的呼号飘过静水，惊得他一跳，像有人点名喊他。
他望得见那艘英国轮船：一团黑影，一根烟囱三根桅，一动不动泊在老远的洋面上。船头一盏红灯，在近处水里撒下一把红宝石的光屑。他努力估量距离，物色岸上哪一段于他的谋划最有利。
一截残墙伸到沙地上。他捾了背城的一面坐下，强迫自己想。一根大脉在脑子里搏动，全副骨架都跟着颤。海、灯火、群山在他眼前打晃；靠着墙，连墙都像在摇。园子里一声狗的尖吠，在他耳朵里撞得发懵。
末了，脑子清了些，他点上一支烟，抽到了头。随后起身，一件一件褪下衣裳：有的扔了，有的拿带子捆在那只塞得饱满的皮囊上。脱到赤条条，他又坐下，把包袱按在帽子头巾上，撕下弃衣的布条，动手往头上捆。

===Original===

By vigorous shaking he made sure it was quite firm, then he stole to the end of the wall and peered cautiously forth.
Two men were approaching﻿—soldiers with rifles on their shoulders. The wall alone had prevented him from hearing their voices. The place he had chosen was sheltered and convenient for keeping watch upon the shore to northward. It was most likely that they were making for it. There was not a second to be lost.
With a bound he ran swiftly across the sand and splashed in the water, dropping at once on his hands and knees. He heard a shout, followed in the same minute by the report of a gun. A shot whizzed past him; it played duck and drake along the surface, striking up little plumes of spray. A second followed, but it was wider of the mark, and by that time Saïd was out of his depth, swimming strongly. He ducked frequently to baffle the marksmen. A bullet, the last which was fired, hit the bundle and remained bedded in it.
At first he struck out blindly, thinking only of his life; but afterwards, when the bullets ceased to whirr, he made boldly for the steamer, which might then have been three-quarters of a mile distant in a straight line. He could hear the soldiers yelling and hallooing on the beach, but had little fear that a boat would put out to intercept him, for the harbour was a long way off on the left and he had passed few craft in his walk along the sands. Even supposing that those in the guardhouse on the quay heard the cries of their comrades and understood them, it would take them some time to get afloat; and a man’s head, though with a bundle lashed to it, was no easy thing to mark on all the wide expanse of darkling water.
With the joy of his narrow escape yet full upon him he revelled in the freedom of the cool water. The little waves smote him friendly and the stars twinkled at him out of the pale sky. As a boy, it had been his delight to swim out, wherever a ship came to anchor off his native town, and perform all kinds of antics in the sea, diving for the coins that voyagers threw to him and catching them in his mouth as they sank. In those days people had marvelled at his prowess in the water, accounting him half a fish; and it pleased him, now that he was middle-aged and bulky, to know that he had still the trick of it. He frolicked, swimming now frogwise, now on this side, now on that.

===Chinese===

他狠狠摇晃了一阵，确认它纹丝不动，才溜到墙头，小心地探头。
两个人影正走近——兵，肩上扛着枪。全亏这堵墙，他才没听见他们的说话。他捾的这地方又隐蔽、又便于监视北面的海滩。多半他们正是奔这儿来的。一秒也耽误不得。
他一纵身，飞快地掠过沙地，噗地扎进水里，立刻四肢着水伏低。他听见一声喊，同一分钟里跟上一声枪响。一发子弹嗖地擦过，贴着水面打水漂，激起一朵朵小小的水花。第二发跟上，偏得更远；那时赛义德已过了脚不沾底的深处，正有力地游。他频频扎猛子，搅乱射手的准头。最后一发子弹打中了包袱，嵌在了里面。
起初他只顾逃命，瞎划一气；后来枪弹不啸了，他放胆直取轮船——直线算，那时约有四分之三英里。他听得见兵们在滩上叫喊呼号，却不大怕有小船出来拦截：海港远在左手，他沿沙岸走来也没见几条船。就算码头卫所里的人听得见同伴的呼喊、也听得懂，他们下水也要耽搁一阵；何况一颗人头——哪怕捆着个包袱——在整片渐黑的大水上，也不是容易瞄准的。
死里逃生的欢喜还满胀在心头，他纵情受用凉水的自在。小浪友善地拍他，星星从淡天里朝他眨眼。少年时，凡是他的镇上有船下锚，他就爱游出去，在海上耍尽百般把戏：潜下去追旅客抛来的钱，趁钱下沉一口叼住。那时候人人惊叹他的水功，说他是半条鱼；如今人到中年、发了体，知道自己那手还在，他心里受用。他嬉起水来，一会儿蛙泳，一会儿侧这边，一会儿侧那边。

===Original===

He turned over on his back and paddled along for a few strokes in that position. Then, righting himself, he splashed forward, hand over hand, like a dog. But ere long he grew weary of such fancies and settled down to a steady and enduring stroke which should carry him to his goal.
The steamer was yet a pretty long way off when he began to doubt if he would ever reach it. The smart of the brine blurred his eyes. The surface of the sea seemed now all starlight, anon black as pitch. He was sadly out of condition and had spent the flower of his energy in wantoning. Wishing to husband what strength remained to him, he slackened speed somewhat. He grew numb. His eyes were blind to everything except the steamer; and that seemed very big, ten times its natural size, filling all the horizon. His limbs lost feeling; stern resolve alone upheld him and kept him moving. The ship loomed nearer all of a sudden. He plunged forward, floundering rather than swimming, his mouth and nose full of salt water at every stroke. It towered above him very near indeed; but all his life was gone. He knew in his heart that he could never reach it. The veins of his forehead were bursting, his eyes were very dim. All kinds of incongruous memories thronged his brain. “Allah is just,” he thought, “and this is the end of me.” But, a second later, he had caught hold of a rope which fell from the steamer’s prow, and hung by it, clinging for dear life.
“Praise be to Allah!” he murmured, quaking from head to foot. Presently he raised a feeble shout. A face looked down at him, then more faces﻿—a crowd of them. Questions were shouted, but he could make nothing of the jargon spoken. “There is much money with me!” he cried in Arabic. “I would go to the great city, Lûndra of the English!”
At that there was a great shout of laughter, and another rope was flung to him, which he caught, and with which he was hauled on board. Queer Frankish faces grinned at him, grotesque as masks, all red and many quite devoid of hair. The light of a fixed lantern sufficed to show them to him. Rough hands smote his dripping shoulders hard in applause, their owners roaring with laughter. In truth, he cut an odd figure as he stood there stark naked and streaming wet, a great bundle bound to his head with strips of calico. But to Saïd it was no laughing matter.

===Chinese===

他翻个身，仰面划了几把。随后正过身，像条狗似的交替着前爪扑水。可不多时他玩腻了，定下一副匀净持久的划法，好送他到目的地。
轮船还隔着好一程，他已开始疑心自己到不了了。咸水的刺得他视线发花。海面忽而满是星光，忽而黑得像漆。他的底子亏透了，精力的精华又在方才的嬉水里耗尽。想省着剩下的力气，他略略放慢。他麻了。眼睛里除轮船外什么也看不见；而它显得很大，大过本来十倍，塞满了整个海平线。四肢失了知觉；单靠一股铁打的心思撑着他、推着他动。船影蘶地近了。他扑上前去——与其说游，不如说扑腾——每一划都灌一嘴一鼻子咸水。船巍巍地压在他头顶，真的近了；可他的力气全尽了。他心里明白：到不了了。额上的血管要迸裂，眼前一片昏花。种种不相干的记忆挤满了脑子。「安拉是公道的，」他想，「这是我到头了。」可一秒之后，他抓住了一条绳——从轮船船头垂下来的——吊在上头，死命攋着。
「赞美安拉！」他喃喃着，从头到脚地抖。过了一刻他扬起一声微弱的喊。一张脸朝下望他，接着更多的脸——一大片。人们朝他喊话，可那行话他一个字不懂。「我带着许多钱！」他用阿拉伯语喊。「我要去那座大城，英吉利的伦敦！」
这一下喍堂大笑，又一条绳抛给他；他抓住，人被拽上了船。一张张古怪的弗兰克脸朝他咧嘴，丑怪如面具，通红，不少还寸发无存。一盏固定灯笼的光够把它们照亮给他看。粗糙的手掌拍他湿淋淋的肩膀，拍得山响，算作喝彩，拍的人喍笑不止。说真的，他那样子够滑稽的：赤条条站着，水顺着淌，头顶拿印花布条捆着个大包袱。可对赛义德，这没什么可笑。

===Original===

He sprang to anger under their blows, glaring round on them with curses, and showing his teeth. But they laughed all the more at his resentment, slapping their knees and hugging themselves for glee.
The press about him gave way suddenly. A man came forward, clad in some sort of a uniform, with a gold badge on his cap. He spoke in a stern voice to the sailors and they fell back sheepishly. It seemed they made excuses, pointing to Saïd where he stood naked and shivering, his feet very conscious of the smooth planks. This man, whom Saïd took to be the lord of the ship, then addressed him in a childish sort of Arabic, asking to know what he wanted; whereupon Saïd told a grievous tale of tyranny and wrong, such as might justify any man in flight from his native land. He repeated his statement that he had plenty of money, adding that he would gladly pay the price of his passage to Lûndra. The officer eyed him doubtfully for a minute. Then, with a face of compassion, he gave a gruff order to one who stood near, and Saïd was led away to a small chamber, dim with the savoury fumes of cooking, where was a fire burning.

===Chinese===

挨着拍打，他勃然大怒，瞪着四围咒骂，龇出牙来。可他们见他动气愈发笑得凶，拍膝盖、搜自己，乐不可支。
围着他的人堆骤然闪开。一个人上前，穿着某种制服，帽上一枚金徽。他朝水手们厉声说了几句，他们讪讪退下。像是在辩解，指指赛义德——他赤身站着发抖，两只脚在光滑的船板上说不出的别扭。
此人——赛义德当他是船的主子——随即拿一口孩子腔的阿拉伯语问他要什么；赛义德便讲了一段暴虐与冤屈的惨史，足以叫任何人逃离故土都情有可原。他重申自己钱很充裕，又说情愿付去伦敦的船钱。军官狐疑地打量了他一分钟。随后，一脸恻然，朝旁边一人粗声吩咐了一句，赛义德便被领去一间小舱——舱里满是饭菜的香雾，烧着一炉火。

### XII / 十二
===Original===

Next morning there was a great bustle on board the steamer. Saïd awoke in his narrow bunk to a noise of splashing and scrubbing overhead. The door of the sort of cupboard where he lay stood open; now and then a man’s shadow darkened it in passing.
It did not take long to remember where he was. The adventure of the previous night recurred vividly to his mind, seeming a madman’s to the sanity of early morning. He marvelled at the daring of it, and then, looking forward, his heart grew sick with forebodings. What future awaited him in the land of the English? It was a country favourable for all manner of trade, but he carried no merchandise with him. He had money, it was true, but when the price of his journey had been deducted from it only a small sum would be left. The fair women and girls, so easy to conquer, the chief attraction of that distant shore, seemed not so very desirable after all.
The great red face of a mariner looked in upon him with the roar of some savage beast. Its grin was friendly and its appearance cheered Saïd somewhat, so that, when it was withdrawn, he shook off his listlessness and got up. As he did so, his clothes and the leathern bag which held his treasure fell on the floor, covering it almost completely, so little space was there. Being naked, he had been hurried to bed overnight and had quite forgotten his bundle. Someone must have brought the things and laid them upon him while he slept. The garments had the crispness of linen dried at the fire.
An agony of fear seized him lest the sack should have been rifled and his money taken out. Naked save for his skullcap and turban, he knelt down in the narrow space between wall and bunk, and with trembling hands loosened the mouth of the bag; but a little groping reassured him. He smiled, drawing forth a small but heavy pouch with a string attached, which he made haste to hang as an amulet about his neck; first shutting the door so that no one passing by could observe him. “Allah is bountiful!” he murmured.
By the time he reached the deck the engines were panting like some huge beast held in leash that frets to go free. A crowd of little boats clung to the steamer’s side, waiting to see the last of her. Already the sun stood high above the ridge of Lebanon, and his beams made a dazzle on the dancing blue sea.

===Chinese===

第二天早晨，轮船上下一片大忙乱。萨义德在他那窄铺里醒来，头顶上一片泼水刷洗的响动。他躺着的那口柜子似的屋门开着，不时一条人影经过，把它遮暗一下。
没多大工夫他就想起身在何处。昨夜那场冒险活生生地回到脑子里，在清晨的清明里瞧，活像疯子干的事。他为自己的胆大啎舌，随即朝前一想，一颗心叫预感泡得发苦。英国地方等他的是什么前程？那是个做各样买卖都便当的国度，可他没带货。钱是有的，扣去船钱，剩不下几个。那边的仙女和姑娘们——一攻就破、那远岸的头号招牌——这会儿瞧着也不怎么馋人了。
一张水手的大红脸伴着某种猛兽的吼声探进来。那咧嘴是友好的，那模样也叫萨义德壮了些胆；脸一缩回去，他就抖掉懒劲，起了床。起身时，他的衣裳和装财宝的皮袋摔落在地——铺位那么窄，衣裳几乎盖满了地面。昨夜他是光着身子被塞进铺的，早忘了他的包袱。必是有人把东西送来、趁他睡着搁在他身上。衣裳带着炉火烘干麻布的那种脆。
一阵怕的苦楚攦住他：万一袋子叫人掏了、钱没了呢？他光着身子——只剩顶帽和缠头巾——跪在墙与铺之间那一窄条里，抖着手松开袋口；摸了一小把，心就放下了。他含笑捾出一只小小而沉重的、拴着绳的钱袋，赶紧关上门——免得过路人看见——把它当护符挂上脖子。「安拉慷慨！」他喃喃。
他上到甲板时，机器已经喘得像一头拴着的大兽，急得要挣脱。一小群小船贴着轮船的船帮，来送它的最后一程。太阳已经高过黎巴嫩的山脊，光在跳动的蓝海上打出一派晃眼。

===Original===

The whiteness of the town, relieved by high red roofs, drew the eye to the southern horn of the bay, where the waves lapped its walls. Suburbs half hidden in foliage stretched all along the shore at the foot of the hills. Palm-trees rose conspicuous, singly and by clumps of two and three. The huge mountains, as yet in shadow, filled all the background, seeming very near indeed. Snow gleamed on the high, long crest of Jebel Sunnìn. The balm of the land and its murmur were wafted on the breeze.
Saïd’s heart went out to his native country. The singsong shouting of the sailors, the clank of a chain, the creaking swing of a windlass﻿—all the noise attendant on weighing anchor sounded cruel and callous in his ears. It jeered him as the voice of fate made audible. His past was slipping from him irrevocably with every pant of the mighty engines, with every puff of the funnel, which began to belch forth dense clouds of whitish smoke that tossed seaward before it like the blown mane of a horse.
The hiss and roar of the safety-valve ceased of a sudden. In place of panting there was a dull, strong throb which was felt in every plank and plate of the ship. The smoke from the funnel wavered a moment, as if doubtful which direction to take, then streamed out steadily over the stern, casting a ribbon of shadow on the churned-up waters in the wake. The little boats fell away from the side with men standing up in them, waving goodbye. They dwindled, were left far behind, and ever the throbbing grew to fuller purpose, as though the ship had a soul, an imprisoned jinni toiling with bitter sobs.
Saïd was shortly led below to a breakfast of weird bread in which was no sustenance, of butter whose exceeding yellowness and bitter, saltish flavour filled him with distrust, of coffee such as he had never tasted and hoped to Allah he might never taste again. There was meat also, but that he would not touch, believing it to be pig’s flesh or something unclean. He did not dwell long upon the meal, but when he returned on deck the city and the shoreline had already sunk out of sight; only the crests of Lebanon stood up sheer out of the sea with white streaks of snow among them, the wake of the ship stretching, an ever-widening path, to their feet.

===Chinese===

白城衬着高高的红屋顶，把人的目光牵到海湾南边的岬角，波浪正舐着那儿的城墙。半掩在树丛里的郊野，沿岸铺满了山脚。枣椰树一株一株、两三株一丛，拔得很显眼。大山还罩在影子里，填满了整个背景，显得真近。松宁山高而长的山脊上，雪泛着光。大地的香和低语乘风飘来。
萨义德的心扑向他的故国。水手们拉腔拉调的号子、铁链一声锒铛、绞盘吱呀一荡——起锚的一切响动，在他耳朵里都冷酷无情。它嘲弄他，像命运的声音化成了听得见的。他的过去，随着大机器每一喘、烟囱每一喷，从他身上不可挽回地滑走——烟囱已经朝海那边吐出大团发白的浓烟，像一匹马被风吹起的鬃。
安全阀的嘶吼忽地停了。喘息换成一记沉而猛的搏动，船身每一块板、每一片铁都感得到。烟囱的烟晃了一晃，像拿不定往哪边走，随后稳稳地朝船尾泻出去，在搅起的尾流上投下一条影带。小船从船帮旁落下，船上的人站起来挥手道别。它们缩小了，远远落在后头；那搏动越来越足、越来越有主意，仿佛这船有一颗魂，一个被囚的精尼，一边苦叹一边做工。
不久萨义德被领下去吃早饭：一种邪门的面包，里头没有一点养分；一种黄油，黄得吓人、又苦又咸的味道叫他不信任到底；一种咖啡，他生平没尝过、求安拉也别叫他再尝。也有肉，他碰都不碰，认定是猪肉或什么不洁之物。他在饭桌上没坐多久；再回甲板，城和海岸线已经沉出了视野，只剩黎巴嫩的峰顶光光的立出海面，夹着雪的白道子，船的尾流一路铺展，越铺越宽，直铺到山脚下。

===Original===

For hours Saïd sat cross-legged in the lee of a cabin, watching those summits dwindle and grow dreamy in the distance, till at last they were no more than a thin cloud on the horizon. The sailors smiled and spoke friendly to him as they went about their work. He sat in the shade, with hot sunshine all about him, and the eternal lapping of a sea, dead blue as lapis lazuli, sounded pleasant in his ears. “O Allah! O Lord, have mercy!” was his soul’s bitter cry as the coasts of Es-Shâm sank beneath the sea-line. And yet he felt not half so wretched as he had expected.
That night a heavy thunderstorm burst, and all the next day the sky was overcast with rain driving in torrents before a cold wind. It was the beginning of winter, and Saïd shunned the bleakness of the upper deck. Having paid an instalment of his passage-money in advance, he was looked upon with unmixed liking by the crew as an honest fellow and a queer customer. Yet Saïd resented the rough kindness of the sailors, as touching his dignity. When they smote him, as their manner was, in all goodwill, he would sometimes round upon them with a snarl, making them laugh as if their hearts would break, and seeming only to increase their kindness for him. They used his word, “Lûndra,” against him as a nickname; and at first he would nod and grin when they uttered it, repeating it after them until they roared. But afterwards, hearing it everywhere and at all hours of the day, he grew sick of the sound of it.
There were two other passengers on board﻿—men of consequence, with whom he had nothing to do. But one of them, a young man, with flaxen hair and moustache, and the bloom of a ripe peach on either cheek, had a smattering of Arabic and was fain to air it a little. After the storm was passed and the fine weather had resumed its sway, he often joined Saïd as he sat upon the deck and struggled to converse with him. It was a little hard sometimes to understand what he said, for all his verbs were in the imperative mood.
One morning when the steamer rode at anchor off a seaport of the kingdom of Rûm, Saïd ventured to ask this person how long it would be before they reached that great city, Lûndra of the English.

===Chinese===

好几个钟头，萨义德盘腿坐在一间舱房的背风处，望着那些山顶缩小、在远处变虚，末了只剩地平线上一痕薄云。水手们干活来去，朝他微笑，同他友好搭话。他坐在荫里，四围是热烘烘的太阳，一片死蓝——蓝得像青金石——的海永无休止地舐着，声音在他耳朵里受听。沙姆的海岸沉下海平线时，他魂里的苦喊是：「安拉啊！主啊，怜悯！」可他竟没有自己料的一半苦。
那夜一场大雷雨炸开；第二天一整天，天阴着，冷风赶着瓢泼的雨。入冬了，萨义德躲着上层甲板的荒寒。船钱他预付了一期，船上人拿他当好汉加怪物，喜欢得不掺一点假。可水手们粗手粗脚的好意，萨义德觉得伤体面。他们照他们的规矩，一团好心地拍他，他有时回身一声低吼，反叫他们笑破肚子，还好像越发疼他。他们拿他那个词「伦德拉」当他的绰号使；起初他们一喊他就点头咧嘴，跟着念，惹得他们狂吼。可后来一天到晚处处听，他听腻了那个音。
船上还有两位旅客——有身份的人，他同他们没来往。其中一位年轻人，亚麻色的头发和唇髭，两腮熟桃的红，懂几句阿拉伯话，想露一露。风暴过去、好天重新当家之后，他常凑到甲板上萨义德身边，费劲地同他攀谈。他说的话有时不好懂：他的动词全在命令式里。
一天早晨，轮船泊在鲁姆国一座海港外，萨义德壮着胆子问此人：到那座大城——英国人的伦德拉——还要多久。

===Original===

Looking out over the crisp, blue waves to a white town at the foot of violet mountains, with cypresses rising gaunt among its buildings and olives silvering all the slope behind, it seemed to him that they were yet a long way distant from that sunless land of which the dragoman had spoken.
“Two weeks and more,” was the answer, “but know, O effendi, that this ship goes not to Lûndra but to Liverpool, which is distant from it a day’s journey on the iron road.”
“Merciful Allah!” Saïd exclaimed. “Hear now my story, O *khawaja*, and judge between these men and me. When I asked them they told me that the steamer went to Lûndra, and I gave them much money on that understanding. Of a truth the people of this ship are all liars; there is no vestige of truth found in them. May their house be destroyed and the fire quenched on their father’s hearth!”
“Nay, O effendi, they meant not to deceive thee. The country of the English is a small country, and the iron road brings distant places close together. Liverpool is reckoned the haven of Lûndra almost as Beyrût is the port of Damascus, and the journey takes not so long. It was no lie they told thee.”
“Without doubt the right is with thee, O *khawaja*,” said Saïd with a semblance of conviction; but in his heart he felt bitterly that he had been beguiled. Lûndra was the city of his dreams, the abode of wealth and luxury, the paradise of fair women partial to strangers. “Lifferbûl” was quite a different place. He had heard the name of it before, but baldly, as of a town like another, without splendour or charm. Thenceforth, aware of a plot to inveigle him thither, he saw something sinister in the jovial comradeship of the sailors, though cunning made him seem their friend. At length, when one morning he awoke to find the steamer at anchor in a fair bay whose shores were clothed with a city and its suburbs, his airy scheme became an instant purpose. The name of the place, he knew, was Nabuli. To southward rose a lonely peak which smoked at the top like a heap of ashes smouldering. Ships were there of every sort and size, a great multitude of them, dotting the sparkling waters. Surely, among them all, there must be one that was bound for the greatest city of the earth. When he had prayed and broken his fast he took his leathern sack privily under his robe and went on deck.

===Chinese===

朝外望着细碎的蓝浪，望见紫山脚下的一座白城——柏树瘦瘦地立在屋宇之间，橄榄树把背后整面坡镀了银——他觉着，离通译说过的那个不见天日的地方还远着呢。
「两个礼拜还多。」是回答。「可你要知道，埃芬迪，这条船不去伦德拉，去利物浦；利物浦离伦德拉，铁轨路一天的路。」
「慈悲的安拉！」萨义德叫起来。「你听俺的故事，霍瓦贾，你评评这些人和俺的理。俺问他们，他们告诉俺轮船到伦德拉；俺照这个理给了他们好些钱。说实话，这条船上的人全是谎精，一丝真的都寻不出。叫他们家宅败落，叫他们爹灶上的火灭掉！」
「别这样，埃芬迪，他们不是要骗你。英国是个小国，铁轨路把远处拉近。利物浦算作伦德拉的港口，差不多就像贝鲁特算大马士革的港口，路上花不了多久。他们没骗你。」
「无疑是你有理，霍瓦贾。」萨义德拿出一副被说服的样子说；心里却苦得明白：他叫人耍了。伦德拉是他梦里的城，财富与奢华的居所，偏爱外乡人的仙女们的乐园。「利弗布尔」可完全是另一个地方。这名字他从前听过，干巴巴的，像随便哪座镇，无光无彩。从这一刻起，他知道有一场把他诱到那儿去的图谋，水手们那伙热络里便透出阴险来——尽管他的心计叫他装他们的朋友。末了，一天早晨他醒来，发现轮船泊在一个晴美的海湾里，两岸叫一座城和它的郊野铺满，他那空中的盘算登时成了实心的主意。那地方的名字，他知道，叫纳布利。朝南耸起一座孤峰，顶上冒烟，像一堆焖着的灰。船各式各样、各头各等，好大一群，撒在闪光的水面上。这么多里头，保准有一条，是开往天下第一大城的。他祷告了、开了斋，把皮袋悄悄掖进袍子，上了甲板。

===Original===

A boat manned by certain of the crew was just putting off for land. Saïd shouted to the men in it, explaining by eloquent signs and grimaces that he had a mind to view the town. They laughed up at him, roaring and beckoning to him to make haste; so without more ado he climbed down among them and was rowed ashore.
In the confusion of landing, amid the busy throng upon the quays, he contrived to escape from his fellowship. For some time he dodged hither and thither, taking advantage of every turning to put more walls between himself and those he supposed in pursuit. His outlandish garb and the hurry he was in turned many heads of the passersby to look after him. At last, finding himself again by the seaside, but at a point remote from his landing-place, he fell to scanning the faces of all he met, seeking someone to question.
Seeing a man of peaceful demeanour stand alone by a pile of bales he inquired of him in Arabic how he might best get to Lûndra. “Lûndra?” repeated the other after him with a vacant look and a shake of the head. He smiled, however, showing white teeth, and, motioning Saïd to stay, called to a knot of men who lounged hard by. They turned their faces at the call, and, seeing one so strangely clad, drew near out of curiosity. One of them, who at first sight appeared a Frankish sailor, shouted a salutation in pure Arabic spoken with the accent of Masr.
Saïd ran to him eagerly, his question on his lips. He told a fine story, how he was a great merchant bound for Lûndra whither his wares were gone before, how an unforeseen accident, which he was at pains to specify, had forced him to leave his ship, and how he would be deeply obliged to anyone who would direct him to another. His hearer, taken with the narrative, made ready offer of his service.
From this new friend Saïd learnt that there were at least two vessels in the harbour on the eve of departing for Lûndra. The Egyptian pointed out a huge steamer in the offing, and, upon Saïd shaking his head at that, showed him a sailing-ship moored to the quay close by. The great merchant stroked his beard and thought a minute. Then he nodded with deliberation, and begged the sailor to bear him company and support him at the bargain.
At first the lord of the ship looked askance at them and spoke roughly to the interpreter.

===Chinese===

一条由几名水手划的小船正要靠岸。萨义德朝船上人喊，拿生动的手势和鬼脸说明他想进城看看。他们仰头大笑，吼着、招手叫他快下来；他不再啰唆，爬下去落进他们中间，被划上了岸。
登陆的乱糟糟里、码头上忙忙的人潮中，他设法脱开了他的同伙。有一阵他东躲西闪，逢弯就拐，把一堵堵墙垫在自己和他想象中的追兵中间。他那异邦装束、那副急相，引得多少过路人回头。末了，他发觉自己又到了海边，却远离登陆的地方，便开始相所有迎面人的脸，找一个能问话的。
见一个神色和气的人独自站在一堆货包旁，他用阿拉伯语问去伦德拉顶好的走法。「伦德拉？」对方学了一遍，一脸茫然，摇摇头。可他随即一笑，露出白牙，示意萨义德稍候，朝不远处闲晃的一小伙人喊。那一伙应声转脸，见一个装束这般古怪的，好奇地围拢来。其中一个，乍看是个法兰克水手，用纯利的阿拉伯语喊了一声招呼，带着马斯尔的口音。
萨义德热切地奔过去，问题顶着嘴唇。他讲了一个漂亮故事：他是个大商家，往伦德拉去，货已先行；一场意外——他细细编明——迫使他离了原船；谁肯指点他另寻一条，他感恩不尽。听的人入了迷，满口应承效劳。
从这位新朋友这里，萨义德得知港里至少有两条船临着开往伦德拉。埃及人指给他远处一条大轮船；萨义德朝它摇头，他便给他看近旁码头旁泊着的一条帆船。大商家捋着胡子想了一分钟，随后郑重一点头，请水手陪同前往、在讲价时替他撑腰。
起初船主斜眼打量他们，对通译说话粗声粗气。

===Original===

But by dint of long parley and a little earnest-money he at last changed his tone and agreed to take a passenger. Saïd thought him an evil man to look at, for he had only one eye and his face was red, inflamed with boils and spots. His voice was harsh and rasping, and he spoke to men as one speaks to a dog. Saïd confided his feelings to his new friend, who only shrugged his shoulders, declaring that the Franks were all like that, unmannerly, possessed with the foulest of devils. As for the man’s appearance, it was from the hand of Allah, and so no blame attached to him.
The ship was not to sail till the evening, so Saïd had some time on his hands. The Egyptian led him to a tavern in a narrow street, where high houses all but shut out the sky. The place was kept by the son of an Arab, and most of the customers were Orientals. Saïd, on his friend’s introduction, was treated with much honour; and he sat there, drinking cup after cup of the coffee he loved, enjoying a narghileh, until the afternoon was far spent, when the Egyptian led him back to the ship. Before he slept that night he could hear the waves lapping against the vessel’s side, and knew that he was speeding on his way to Lûndra. His dreams were all of fair women languishing in a chastened gloom.

===Chinese===

可磨了半天的嘴皮，加上一小笔定钱，他末了换了口气，答应载一名客人。萨义德瞧着他不像好人：他独眼，脸红，长满疖子和斑。嗓音又哑又锉，跟人说话像跟狗说话。萨义德把他的观感悄悄告诉新朋友；朋友只耸耸肩，说法兰克人全这样：没规矩，一身最脏的鬼附体。至于那副相貌，那是安拉的手笔，怪不得他本人。
船要到傍晚才开，萨义德手头就有了些闲工夫。埃及人领他去一条窄街上的酒馆，两边高楼把天几乎关死。店是一个阿拉伯人的儿子开的，客人多半是东方人。经朋友引见，萨义德受了顶大的礼遇；他坐在那儿，一杯接一杯喝他心爱的咖啡，抽一袋水烟，一直坐到下午将尽，埃及人才领他回船。那夜睡前，他听得见波浪舐着船帮，知道自己正一路奔往伦德拉。他的梦全是仙女，在一片收敛的幽暗里慵懒。

### XIII / 十三
===Original===

It was not long ere Saïd regretted the step he had so blindly taken and wished himself back on board the steamer, let it bear him to Lifferbûl or to the world’s end. Skipper and crew of his new transport were altogether of a coarser type. Though the men grinned as they passed him in their work, the laugh was at him, not to him, and it filled him with distrust.
Day by day the ship leapt or glided with full sails on an endless waste of waters. To Saïd, as he squatted on the deck smoking cigarettes bought from the captain at what seemed to him a ruinous price, it occurred sometimes that the vessel was not moving at all, but was still with the waves racing past her. The fancy amused him and he would indulge it for minutes at a time until he was almost persuaded that it was so; it needed a glance at the strained canvas overhead, and another at the passing water, to dispel the illusion. He thought if Allah would grant a man wings like the birds he saw, how pleasant it would be to make long voyages, swooping down when weary to close wings and rest, letting the sea rock him for a little space. He considered the fishes of the deep, how they swim ever under water, yet, by the great mercy of Allah, are not drowned. “Allah is great!” was the outcome of all his musings.
But, as the days wore on, he grew very tired of sitting alone. He would keep near the sailors and try to ingratiate himself with them; even their unfailing rudeness and the horse-tricks they played him seemed better than sheer loneliness. The shifts he was forced to make in order to say his prayers undisturbed were a heavy burden on his conscience. Very earnestly he besought Allah to pardon any omissions in a place where clean water was hard to come by, where there was no sand and but little dust to serve for a substitute. Allah was merciful, he reflected, and would forgive his shortcomings, taking the circumstances into account.
Day by day the world grew sadder and less familiar. Skies lost their lustre, the sea darkened and waxed fierce, the very sun shone pale. Coasts, when sighted, were black and low-lying on the edge of leaden waters heaving in eternal unrest. It turned cold﻿—more bleak than any winter. Saïd rubbed his eyes, supposing that there was a film on them which made the world seem dim.

===Chinese===

不久，赛义德就悔了这一步盲棋，一心想回到那艘轮船上去——随它载他去利物浦还是天涯海角。他这条新船的船长同水手，全是一路粗坯。干活打他身边过，那些人也咧嘴笑，可那笑是笑他，不是对他笑，笑得他满心提防。
日复一日，帆吃饱了风，船在无边的一片荒水上，时而跃，时而滑。赛义德蹲在甲板上抽烟——烟是向船长买的，价钱在他看来毁家灭业——有时他忽然觉得，船根本没有动，是停着的，浪在它身边赛跑。这怪念头有趣，他由着它辖制自己，一回几分钟，几乎信以为真；得抬头望一眼绷紧的帆，再望一眼流淌的水，幻觉才散。他想，安拉若肯给人安上翅膀，像他望见的那些鸟，长途旅行该多美：倦了就敛翅落下来歇，叫海摇他一小阵。他又琢磗深水里的鱼：终年潜在水底游，却蒙安拉的大慈，淹不死。这一场玄想的总结是：「安拉至大！」
可日子一长，他独坐得腻透了。他挨着水手们待着，试着讨他们的好；就连他们那份从不缺席的粗鲁、那些加在他身上的恶作剧，也比纯粹孤单强。为了不受搅扰地做礼拜，他不得不变出的种种法子，成了良心上一副重担。他恳切地求安拉宽宥这里的疏漏：在这地方净水难得，又没有沙、土也稀少，顶替不上。安拉是慈悯的，他盘算，体察了处境，必饶他的欠缺。
一天一天，天地愈发愁惨、愈发生疏。天失了光泽，海黑了、凶了，连太阳都照得发白。望见海岸时，海岸黑黑地、低低地伏在铅色水沿上——那水永恒不安地掀动。天冷了——比什么冬天都荒寒。赛义德揉眼睛，疑心眼上蒙了层翳，才叫世界显得这样昏沉。

===Original===

He realised that the land of the English was near, the land of cloud of which the dragoman had spoken; but the knowledge brought no gladness. He grew homesick, longing for a known face, for the sight of a palm-tree, for a train of camels passing in the blinding sunshine with sweet jangle of bells, for a word in his native tongue.
The very welkin lowered unfriendly, like a menace. The wind howled as a hungry beast of prey; the waves ravened as they leapt against the ship. All things, animate and inanimate, were hostile, and he saw their fury personal to himself. To make matters worse, a gale arose, and he became helpless through sickness. Utter despair got hold of him; he prayed ever that Allah might take his life ere he should retch again. He could take no food, but a little drink. The sailors came and mocked his wretchedness; but he was too prostrate to care for their jeers, only begging them to kill him where he lay.
After the illness he was feeble and shaky for a day or two, and felt the cold more keenly than before, though every garment he possessed was upon him, and a tarpaulin, which a sailor in savage pity flung to him, wrapped over all like a great shawl. The queer figure he cut as he tottered about shivering was the butt and derision of the whole crew.
The wind abated and the sea calmed. The sun, a mere ghost, looked down through worn places of the cloud-rack, like a pale face pressed to a rain-smeared pane. A long, wavy line of cliffs, dirty-white, blurred and indistinct in a perpetual mist, was pointed out to him as the land of the English. He saw it vaguely as one sees whose sight is dim with tears. All his hope centred in the little moneybag at his chest; there was comfort in thinking that he had enough to pay the price of a return voyage to the land of sunlight. Not for a day would he sojourn in this region of eternal gloaming, but would seek out a ship at once and take passage in her. There was sure to be some good Muslim at the landing-place who would direct him for the love of Allah and the Faith that saves.
The cliffs were gone and the ship moved along by a low, marshy coast. Here and there a group of dwellings, a lighthouse, a lonely hut broke the sullen monotony of the shoreline blackly. There was land on both sides now﻿—flat and dreary, shadowed, grim and inhuman as Jehennum itself.

===Chinese===

他明白英吉利的国土近了——通译说过的云之乡；可这知道并没带来欢喜。他害起乡愁，想一张熟脸，想看一眼椰枣树，想一队骆驼在晃眼的烈日下走过、铃声叮咚，想听一句乡音。
天本身阴沉得不怀好意，像一句恫吓。风嚎如饿兽；浪扑打船身，如饥似渴。万物，有生的无生的，一概怀敌，他还认定这份怒火专冲他一人。更糟的是起了大风，他又晕船晕得没了办法。彻底的绝望攋住了他；他一味祷告，求安拉趁他还没再干呕先收了他的命。饭粒不能进，只饮得少许。水手们来笑他的狼狈；他瘫得顾不上他们的嘲弄，只求他们就地处决了他。
病后他虚弱发抖了一两天，比先前更禁不得冷，尽管他把所有的衣裳都上了身，外头还罩着一条油布——一个水手发了蛮性的怜悯，甩给他的——裹得像条大披巾。他哆哆嗦嗦、摇摇晃晃走动的那副怪样，成了全船的笑柄。
风衰了，海平了。太阳只剩一缕幽魂，从云堆的破处朝下望，像一张苍白的脸贴在雨渍斑驳的窗玻璃上。一道长长的、波状起伏的悬崖，脏白脏白，在经年不散的雾里虚虚花花——人家指给他，说那就是英吉利的国土。他望得恍惚，像含着一包泪的人望东西。他全部的希望都聚在胸口那只小钱袋上；想到袋里的钱足够付一回程的船资、回那片阳光之地，就有了点安慰。这永恒暮色的地界，他一天也不多待：一上岸就寻船订舱。码头上准有个好穆斯林，看安拉和救人的信仰的分上，肯给他指路。
悬崖过去了，船沿着一片低湿的沼岸行。这里那里，一片屋舍、一座灯塔、一间孤零零的小屋，黑黑地打断海岸线阴沉的单调。如今两边都是陆地——平坦、荒凉、罩着影，狰狞无人气，同哲罕南地狱一样。

===Original===

Saïd wondered what kind of men could dwell in that wilderness meant for the damned. The waterway was dotted with ships great and small. The sun was shining, but so faintly that he hardly knew it. A few wan snakes at play upon the ripples were all the brightness it gave.
Anon the gloom deepened in spite of the feeble sun and became of a dull, yellowish brown. The shore drew nearer on either hand. They entered a great river, populous with all manner of craft﻿—by far the greatest Saïd had ever seen. After noon, as they still glided on, the face of the sun took on a reddish hue, and the water glinted cold and coppery to its lifeless rays. The world seemed dead, and the stir of human life upon it loathsome as the foul brood of corruption. The river wound between two banks of fog, on which strange shapes of roof and chimney, tower and steeple, and the masts of ships appeared carven or painted by a tremulous hand. From all sides clouds of smoke arose, feeding the gloom and blending with it perpetually. It was as if the whole land smouldered. Ships were moored along the wharves, at the foot of huge buildings frowning like precipices. Here and there a large steamer, lying out towards midstream, had a swarm of small craft﻿—lighters, wherries and row boats﻿—about her, clinging to her, trailing from her like driftwood: a floating island, long and black upon the burnished water.
A mighty clamour filled all the gloom and seemed a part of it. The beat of hammers rang out so thunderous that Saïd trembled to guess what made it. There was a constant hiss of escaping steam, the throbbing of huge engines, the creak and rattle of cranes culminating now and then in a long roar, the whistle and hoot of steamers, sounds of puffing and the swish of paddle-wheels, shouts and cries of human kind. Smells found their way out on to the river and dwelt there, in spite of a light breeze blowing up from the sea﻿—smells of the furnace and the tan-yard, of pitch and resin, and the prevailing pungent smoke. The taste in Saïd’s mouth was a mixture of smoke and brine. He was choked, deafened, wholly bewildered.
One of the sailors, the most villainous-looking of all, who had of late made friendly overtures to him in the shape of devilish grins and murderous digs in the ribs, drew near and smote the tarpaulin.
“Lûndra!” he said, leering into Saïd’s face.
“Lûndra!”

===Chinese===

赛义德纳闷，什么样的人才住得下这片为罪人备下的荒野。水道上泊满大大小小的船。太阳照着，淡得他几乎不认得。它给的亮，全在涟漪上嬉戏的几条惨白小蛇。
不久，饶有这轮弱日，暝色反而深了，深成一种沉滞的黄褐。两岸渐渐逼近。他们驶进一条大河，河里挤满各色船只——是赛义德平生仅见的大河。午后，船还在滑行，太阳的脸泛出暗红，河水映着它死气的光，闪着冷冷的铜色。世界像死了，上面人间活动的搅动，可憎得如腐物上孪生的一窝蛆。河在两道雾堤之间蜿蜒；堤上映出屋顶烟囱、高塔尖顶和船桅的怪形，像一只颤抖的手刻上去、画上去的。四面八方腾起烟云，喂养着暝色，同它融作一体。仿佛整片大地都在闷烧。
船沿着码头泊成一列，码头尽头是巍巍的楼宇，虎着脸像峭壁。这儿那儿一艘大轮船泊向中流，周身围着一群小船——驳船、渡艇、划子——贴着它、曳着它，像漂木：一座浮岛，在擦亮的水面上，又长又黑。
一片浩大的喧声灌满整个暝色，仿佛就是暝色的一部分。铁锤的敲击响得如雷，赛义德猜不出是什么打的，猜得发抖。还有蒸汽泄出的长嘶、巨机的搏动、吊机的吱嘎哐啷——时时拔成一声长吼——轮船的鸣笛和嘶叫、喘息声、明轮的搅水声，以及人类的呼喊。种种气味也窜到河面上住了下来，任海上吹来的轻风也赶不走——炉子的气味、鞣皮场的气味、沥青和松脂的气味，还有无处不在的呛烟。赛义德嘴里的滋味，是烟和咸水的混合。他呛着，聋着，全然懵了。
一个水手凑过来，拍了拍他的油布——那是全船相貌最凶的一个，近来频频向他示好，示好的方式是狞笑和往死里杵他的肋骨。
「伦敦！」他说，斜眼觑着赛义德的脸。
「伦敦！」

===Original===

echoed the passenger with a series of nods and a bright display of teeth, explaining that he understood. At that the mariner laughed hoarsely and began a lively pantomime, twitching Saïd’s robe, pointing to the shore, slapping his own chest, and then making as if he would embrace the fisherman. Saïd was slow to see the drift of all this; the whole show had to be repeated a second time. But at last he gathered that this sailor of the evil countenance was his sincere well-wisher and would take charge of him when the time came to disembark.
The sun, swathed in smoke-wreaths, was already setting in crimson when, amid hoarse shouts of greeting and command, the frenzied blowing of a whistle and much flinging about of ropes and chains, the ship drew up to a wharf-side. The river flowed as turbid blood, parting a dark wilderness of masts and rigging, of endless, shapeless buildings. Here and there a pane of glass or other polished surface caught a beam and sprang to lurid flame. Westward, over against the sun, a great black dome brooded over the misty roofs. The din of the city had a note of weariness, like the sighing of a great multitude.
He shrank from landing. At least the ship was known to him, familiar in its every part; whereas this boundless, black city, whose sweat was filthy smoke, frightened him as a living monster lying in wait to devour. Surely it was the realm of Eblis, the abode of evil spirits and of souls in torment. For a long while he watched the business of the wharf, his brain ahum with doubt and bewilderment, so that he could not read or unravel his thoughts.
The skipper came and spoke gruffly to him, pointing to the gangway. He dragged the tarpaulin from Saïd’s shoulders and flung it aside upon a heap of cordage. The Arab saw plainly that there was no choice left for him. Trembling and shrinking, in his flowing Eastern dress of many colours, he hurried across the plank, looking back to the ship, the scene of so much anguish for him, with longing as to a well-loved home.
The quay on which he found himself was a narrow one, oppressed and shadowed by a great warehouse. It reminded him faintly of a strip of beach at the foot of a steep cliff. He could see no way from it except through the great doors which yawned like caverns, showing bales of merchandise piled within.

===Chinese===

乘客一叠声点头，灿然一喣牙，回了一句「伦敦」，表示他懂了。水手听了哑声一笑，做起一出活泼的哑剧：扯扯赛义德的袍子，指指岸，拍拍自己的胸，又作势要拥抱渔夫。赛义德半晌没看懂路数；整套戏又从头演了一遍。末了他总算明白：这个恶脸水手是他诚挚的好心人，下船时会照应他。
太阳裹在烟缭里，正沉向一片猩红；一片沙哑的招呼与号令、一阵狂吹的汽笛、一番缆绳铁链的甩舞之中，船靠上了码头。河水淌得像浑浊的血，分开一片黑压压的荒野——桅杆索具的荒野，无边无形楼宇的荒野。这里那里，一块窗玻璃或什么抛光的面接住一束光，窜起幽幽的火苗。西边，同太阳相对，一座黑色大穹顶蹲在雾蒙蒙的屋海上。城市的喧嚣里带一个疲惫的调子，像一大群人的叹息。
他怯于登岸。船至少是他识得的，一钉一板都熟；这座无边的黑城，出的汗都是腕臜的烟，骇着他，像一头趴伏待噬的活怪。这必是易卜劣斯的国，恶灵与受苦之魂的巢。他久久看着码头上的营生，脑子里嗡嗡全是疑与懵，理不出、解不开一个念头。
船长走过来朝他咕哝了一句粗话，指指跳板。他把油布从赛义德肩上扯下来，甩到一堆缆索上。阿拉伯人看得分明：没有第二条路了。他抖着、缩着，一身五彩飘飘的东方衣裳，匆匆抢过跳板，一步一回头望那船——他在船上受尽苦楚的地方——像望着一个亲爱的家。
他落脚的码头窄窄一条，叫一座大货仓压着、罩着影。他恍惚觉得它像陡崖脚下的一线沙滩。望得见的出路只有那几扇大门——门张着大口如洞穴，看得见里面堆到顶的货包。

===Original===

He felt quite helpless, imprisoned, cut off from everywhere yet within sound of a multitude. Yellow light streamed from every aperture of the building before him, making shapes of men fiendish as they moved in black outline across it. The lapping of the ripples against the piles, which is the same song all the world over, sounded more friendly than the voices of his kind speaking sternly and abruptly in a foreign tongue. Worst of all, no one heeded him. A chance look, a grin, a shrug of the shoulders, and he was passed by, dismissed from the minds of those busy workers. There was something very sinister in such absorption. Feeling dazed, he stood still, not knowing which way to look, the voice of the city in his ears﻿—the sullen roar of a vast, unfriendly throng.
A mighty stroke on the back roused him from torpor. The sailor, who some two hours before had accosted him on the deck, stood at his side, speaking rapidly in a scolding tone. Then he laughed, and smote him once more between the shoulders. Linking arms, he led him away by a little passage Saïd had not perceived at the extreme end of the quay.
The streets were broad and open to the sky; they were lighted by lanterns set on high poles. The houses were tiny compared with the big warehouses of the riverbank, and were separated by spaces of blank wall, over which the masts and spars of ships rose ghostly. The sailor led Saïd to a house which stood, a blaze of light, at a place where three roads met. Pushing open a swing-door, he dragged him into a room full of men.
The brightness almost blinded Saïd, coming, as he did, out of the dark, and the noise deafened him. A number of red-faced Franks, seated on benches at wooden tables, were laughing and talking at the top of their voices. In his dazed condition he saw them vaguely as a multitude of strangers hostile to him. The atmosphere of the room, charged with the fumes of tobacco and strong drink, was hard to breathe; only the warmth and the light pleased him. Full of distrust of that noisy company, he would fain have drawn back, but his friend restrained him, forcing him to a seat at one of the tables.
He was aware of a crowd of faces close to his, of hands tweaking his raiment, of a buzz of curiosity ending in a mighty burst of laughter. Then a glass was set before him, full of some amber fluid. It had an evil smell and he loathed it.

===Chinese===

他觉得自己全然无措：被囚了，同处处断了路，却又在千万人的声浪之内。面前那座建筑的每个窟窿眼里淌出黄光，人影以黑的剪影在光前走过，一个个狰狞如鬼。涟漪拍打桩柱的声——普天下同调的一首歌——听着竟比同类的话语亲：他们说的是外国话，又硬又急。最糟的是，没有一个人理会他。偶然一瞥、一龇牙、一耸肩，他就被跨过去了，从那些忙人心里注销了。这份全神贯注里有一种说不出的阴森。他晕晕地站着，不知朝哪边看，城的声音灌在耳朵里——一大群不怀好意的人的、阴沉的吼。
背上重重一掌，把他从麻木里打醒。就是约摸两个钟头前在甲板上同他搭话的那个水手，站在他身边，连珠炮地说，口气像训人。说完他一笑，又在他两肩之间来了一掌。两人挽起胳膊，领他从码头极头一条赛义德没留意过的小夹道走了出去。
街道宽阔，露天见天；照明靠高杆上挂的灯笼。同河岸的大货仓一比，屋子小得很，彼此隔着一段段白墙，墙头上探出船的桅桁，鬼影似的。水手领赛义德到一座宅子——灯火通明的一座——立在三岔路口。推开一扇弹簧门，把他拽进一间满是人的屋子。
从黑暗里来，那份亮几乎晃瞎赛义德，那份吵震聋了他。一群红脸的弗兰克人，坐在木桌边的长凳上，放开嗓门又笑又谈。他晕晕地看着，恍惚只当是一大群怀敌的生人。屋里的空气，饱浸着烟草和烈酒的雾，难以下咽；只有那暖、那亮中他的意。他对这一屋子闹哄哄的人满肚子提防，直想退出去，可他的朋友拦着，把他按到一张桌边的座上。
他觉出一圈脸凑在他脸边，一只只手捻他的衣裳，一阵好奇的嗡嗡，收在一声轰然大笑里。随后一只杯子摆到他面前，盛着琥珀色的什么。气味不善，他厌恶它。

===Original===

Remembering the potion given him by Ferideh, he had no doubt but that this was in the same nature. At best it was wine, a forbidden thing. They made instant signs to him to drink, but he pushed it from him, shaking his head vehemently and calling out that it was a sin. At that they laughed the more, and he began to fear, reading mischief in their eyes. A man of giant build caught hold of him and kept his hands, while another flung his head back and forced open his mouth. Saïd kicked with all his might, but his feet were powerless between the legs of the table. While he was yet struggling, the liquor was poured down his throat, and one held his mouth shut until he had swallowed every drop, although he came nigh to choking. Then he was released amid a roar of merriment.
A second glass was presently set before him and, sooner than submit to further violence, he made shift to empty it with a wry face. The stuff, though nasty in the mouth, had a pleasant effect, diffusing unhoped-for warmth through all his body. Soon he was joining in the general laugh against himself. Just as he finished one glass there was another full to his hand.
Instead of enemies he found himself among friends. He could have wept for the joy he had in beholding them. In a broken voice he told them all his troubles, about Ferideh and his love for her, about her elopement and the evil days he had known in Damashc-ush-Shâm, where he had been a great merchant, none like him in all that city﻿—no, by Allah, nor in any city of the earth! It was the bald truth he was telling them﻿—by the beard of the Prophet, he was an honest man, a man of consequence, and no liar! Whatever he said, they laughed madly; he thought it so kind of them to laugh. His eyes filled with tears as he thought on all their kindness.
His head swam queerly, and his eyes grew somewhat dim. He fancied he saw a woman somewhere in the room and, with a hazy remembrance of his purpose in coming to Lûndra, held out his arms to her enticingly. The laughter grew ever more boisterous. It was very rude of them to laugh, he considered. The Franks were fools, every one of them﻿—accursed unbelievers having no knowledge of Allah or of Muhammed His apostle. He stood up, balancing himself with difficulty, and rated them soundly, cursing them for a lot of pigs and adjuring Allah Most High to destroy their houses and slay their parents.

===Chinese===

想起菲丽黛给他吃的那服药，他毫不疑心这是同一路货。往好里说也是酒，犯禁的东西。他们立刻比划叫他喝；他推开杯子，拼命摇头，喊这是罪。他们越发笑；他从他们眼里读出祸心，开始怕了。一条大汉捉住他、摁住他双手；另一个扳他的头往后仰，撬开他的嘴。赛义德拼命蹬腿，可两只脚夹在桌腿之间，使不上劲。他还在挣，那东西已经灌进喉咙，又有一只手捂住他的嘴，直捂到他咽净最后一滴——险些呛死。随后他在一片欢腾的喍笑里被松开。
第二杯跟着摆上来；与其再受一遭强暴，他皱着眉认了，一饮而尽。那东西嘴里虽恶，效用却好，一股料想不到的暖散遍全身。不多时，他已加入众人，一起笑起自己来。一杯刚尽，又一杯满满递到手边。
原来不是仇敌，竟是朋友。他望着他们，欢喜得要哭。他颠三倒四地把苦楚全讲了：菲丽黛，他对她的爱，她的私奔，他在大马士革过的那些苦日子——他在那儿曾是大商贾，全城无双——不，凭安拉说，普天之下的城也没有一个比得上的！他讲给他们的是光秃秃的实话——凭先知的胡子，他是老实人，是有体面的人，不是谎精！他说什么他们疯笑什么；他觉得他们肯笑，真是厚道。想到他们种种的好，他眼眶热了。
他的头古怪地打转，眼也蒙了。他恍惚看见屋子里哪个地方有个女人，记起自己来伦敦的目的（也是一团模糊），便朝她张开胳膊勾引。笑声愈发掀翻屋顶。他觉得他们这样笑很失礼。弗兰克人个个是蠢货——遭咒的不信教者，不识安拉、不识祂的使者穆罕默德。他站起来，摇摇晃晃勉强立定，痛痛快快训了他们一顿，咒他们是一窝猪，并恳请至高的安拉毁了他们的房、杀了他们的爹娘。

===Original===

The next minute, he knew not how, he was sprawling face downwards on the floor, and his hands and clothing were coated with sawdust. They crowded about him, slapping their thighs and hallooing with glee. He cursed them again, declaring that they were bad men full of strong drink, and thereupon endeavoured to recite to them a passage of the Quran. But one caught hold of his leg and proceeded to drag him round the room, while another sat on him, using him as a sort of carriage. He had no breath to resent the horseplay, but could only pant beneath the weight of the man on his back, emitting from time to time a feeble chuckle.
By-and-by they lifted him to a sitting posture and gave him more of the burning fluid to drink. He sat for a little while swaying to and fro, an insane grin on his swarthy face. Seeing his cap and turban lie at some distance upon the floor, he conceived an indistinct notion of trying to reach them upon his hands and knees; but they were so far off he fell asleep on the way.

===Chinese===

下一分钟，不知怎么的，他已面朝下摊在地板上，两手两衣沾满锯末。众人围拢来，拍着大腿欢呼。他又咒了他们一遍，宣称他们是一帮灌满了烈酒的坏人，随即还要给他们诵一段古兰经。可一个抓住他的腿，拖着他满屋转；另一个坐上他的背，拿他当一回车驾。他连恶作剧都无力反抗了，只能驮着背上那人喘，时不时漏出一声微弱的嘿嘿。
过了一阵，他们把他扶坐起来，又灌了他几口那烧人的东西。他坐了一会儿，前仰后合，黑黄的脸上一副痴笑。望见帽子和头巾躺在不远的地板上，他起了一个影影绰绰的念头：手脚并用爬过去拿；可它们太远了，他爬到半路睡着了。

### XIV / 十四
===Original===

Saïd awoke to a headache and violent sickness. Supposing himself on the sea in a tempest, he marvelled at the quiet all about him. Presently he sat up and essayed to rub his eyes, but sudden dizziness caused him to fall back again with a groan. His couch was hard and wooden, like the planked deck of a ship, strewn, however, with something soft and powdery, like sand or sawdust. The place where he lay was dark and had a nauseous smell. He was distressed with thirst. “Water!﻿—Water!” he moaned. “In the name of Allah, bring me a little water!﻿—”
But the tones of his voice rang lonely in an empty room.
Events of the previous night loomed on his mind, as forms seen gigantic through mist. Sore shame and anguish fell upon him, illumined in a moment by a sudden terror. His money, his last ray of hope﻿—where was it? He felt in the bosom of his robe, fingering his hairy chest frantically. The pouch and the string which held it were gone﻿—stolen! He fumbled in every part of his clothing and scoured the floor with his hands; but in vain. “O Allah, All-merciful!﻿—” He beat his breast with hoarse cries of rage and despair.
From a trance of grief, embittered by feverish thirst, he was roused by the noise of footsteps in an adjoining room. A light shone yellow through a glass hatch in the wall of partition, throwing long shadows of bottles upon the pane. He could hear a swishing noise, as of someone sweeping diligently with a broom. His eyes, sharpened by the habit of darkness, saw every part of the chamber in which he lay. It was the same to which the sailor had brought him. At sight of the tables and benches his shame redoubled so that he wept aloud. He picked up his tarbûsh and turban, which had been kicked under a trestle, and made haste to put them on. It degraded him to know that he had played the buffoon, bareheaded, in the sight of unbelievers. The sound of his lamentation filled the room.
A door opened and a woman looked in upon him. She held a candle aloft in one hand, while with the other she screened her eyes from the flame. The light reddened between her fingers and shed a warm glow on her dirty face. She yawned as one not yet wide awake, and spoke crossly to him.

===Chinese===

萨义德醒来，头疼欲裂，呕吐连连。他只当还在海上、还在风暴里，满耳的静反倒叫他纳帘。过了一会儿他坐起来想揉眼，一阵晕眩又把他掀倒，哼了一声。他的铺又硬又木，像船上的板铺，上面撒了一层软粉粉的东西，像沙，像锯末。他躺的地方黑，一股恶心味。渴折磨他。「水！——水！」他呻吟。「看在安拉份上，给俺一点水！——」
可他的嗓音在空屋里荡出一片孤零。
昨夜的事在他脑子里浮起，像透过雾看见的巨影。一阵剧痛的羞耻压下来，又被一记突然的恐怖照亮。他的钱——他最后一星指望——哪儿去了？他往袍子怀里摸，手指在毛茸茸的胸口狂扒。钱袋、拴袋的绳，都没了——偷了！他把浑身上下摸了个遍，两只手把地面刮了一遍；白费。「安拉啊，大慈的！——」他捶着胸口，发出嘶哑的怒嚎和绝望。
他从悲恸的出神里——又被热渴腌着——叫隔壁屋的脚步声惊起。一星黄光从隔墙的玻璃小窗透进来，把瓶子的长影投在窗上。他听得见嘕嘕的响，像谁在起劲挥扫帚。他的眼睛叫黑暗惯出了尖，把躺的这间屋看得一清二楚。还是水手带他来的那间。一看见桌子和条凳，他的羞耻翻了一倍，放声哭出来。他拾起叫人踢到条凳底下的毡帽和缠头巾，赶紧戴上。他记起自己曾光着头、在异教徒面前耍过活宝，这一知，又矮了一截。他哀哭的声音灌满屋子。
一扇门开了，一个女人朝他张望。她一手高举蜡烛，另一手遮着眼挡火。光从她指缝里透出红，把她那张脏脸照得暖暖的。她打着哈欠，像没全醒，凶巴巴地对他说话。

===Original===

He stretched out his hands, beseeching her by gestures to give him to drink; but she only grew angry, and setting down the candlestick upon a bench, shook her fist in his face and nodded significantly towards the door. Saïd strove to reason with her, craving only a little water to quench the thirst ravaging him; but she cried out and pushed him from her. The noise of approaching footsteps and a man’s voice came to second her endeavours. Hearing those sounds and dreading fresh violence at the hands of the lord of the house, Saïd suffered the dirty woman to unbar the door for him, and fled out precipitately into the sharp air of the morning.
Having made a few paces, he turned with a shiver to look back at the place he was leaving. It was a two-storeyed house, flanked with two chimneys. A board upon the face of it seemed to be painted with characters or symbols, but he could not see much in the dark with only a distant lamp to help him.
