It stood in a region of blind walls and scattered dwellings of dilapidated appearance. There was a flagstaff on the roof, which made Saïd think it was a consulate. Beyond, the masts and rigging of great ships seemed drawn with a pencil upon the first pale mist of dawn. In the gloom of the door by which he had come forth he descried the form of a big man in act to watch him; and he shuffled hurriedly away, his face pinched with the cold.
He walked aimlessly forward, not knowing which way to take, desirous only to escape from that wicked quarter to some part of the city where men of honour dwelt, where he might happen on a Muslim in the streets. More than once he found his way blocked by a dingy wall and had to retrace his steps. Many men passed him, clad in soiled garments and carrying tools or sacks. They stared, turning their faces after him; but, being sleepy for the most part, they did not hinder or molest him. Day broke at his back, suffusing the dun mist wanly. It showed a thin dust like salt whitening the ground, the housetops, and along the coping of the walls. The air was biting; it stung his nostrils so that he smelt blood. To get a little warmth, he tucked his hands beneath his robe and stamped his slippered feet hard upon the pavement.
In the shelter of an entry he found a little dry dust, with which he rubbed his face, hands and feet preparatory to saying his prayers.

===Chinese===

他伸出两手，打手势哀求她给口水喝；她只管发火，把烛台往条凳上一放，朝他脸前挥拳头，又朝门口意味深长地点头。萨义德想同她讲理，只讨一点水，压一压那啃他的渴；她喊起来，把他往外推。渐近的脚步和一个男人的嗓音来给她助阵。听见这动静，怕屋主再动粗，萨义德由那脏女人替他拔了门闩，一头抢出门，扎进清晨刺骨的空气里。
走出去几步，他打着寒噤回头，看一眼正离开的地方。一座两层楼，两个烟囱分列两翼。楼面上挂一块板，像写画着字或符，可天黑，只有远处一盏灯，看不大清。四下是一带盲墙和东倒西歪的零星住屋。屋顶上一根旗杆，叫萨义德当成了领事馆。再远处，大船的桅樯索具像拿铅笔画在破晓第一片淡雾上。他出来的那扇门的暗影里，他瞥见一条大汉的身影正要盯他；他赶紧蹭开，脸叫冷风夹得生疼。
他漫无目的地往前走，不知该挑哪一头，一心只想逃离那片恶人窝，到城里体面人住的地方去，好歹街上撞见一个穆斯林。不止一回，一堵灰墙堵死他的路，只得原路折回。多少男人打他身边过，衣裳油污，扛着家伙或麻袋。他们瞪着他，脸跟着他转；可多半没睡醒，也没拦他、没撩他。天在他背后破晓，把灰黄的雾淡淡染开。天光显出一层细尘，像盐，白了地面、屋顶，和沿墙的压顶。空气咬人；呛他鼻孔，呛得他闻见血。为讨一点暖，他把手拢进袍底，趿着拖鞋的脚在石板上狠狠跺。
一处门道的荫蔽里，他寻到一小撮干土，拿它搓脸、搓手、搓脚，预备礼拜。

===Original===

In the midst of his devotions, however, heavy footfalls sounded in the street, and a tall man, darkly-clad, with a strange form of hat and a cudgel stuck in his belt, spoke roughly and hit him on the back. He rose to his feet, expostulating, but the man made urgent signs to him to move on, and his mien was so full of authority that Saïd dared not disregard the bidding of his outstretched hand. “Allah pardon!” he muttered as he went his way, feeling that the day had begun badly.
Presently he came into a spacious street, so long that he could not see the end of it. The sun, just risen, looking sickly through the wreathing vapours, shed a milky stain on the roadway and parts of the buildings, casting the faintest of grey shadows. But for gilt signs on some of the houses, Saïd would scarcely have known that it shone at all. He strode on with his back to the light, wrapped close in his long robe, trembling with cold, very conscious of the inquisitive gaze of other wayfarers. The road was thronged with carriages, great and small, of shapes unknown to him. Some were like wheeled houses, crowded with people inside and upon the roof. These queer conveyances pleased him by their gay colours, which he admired, as he did also certain hoardings decked with painted paper﻿—as much as a hopeless and utterly destitute man can admire anything.
Suddenly hoots and yells of derision struck his ears, and he became aware of a horde of ragged urchins following him, capering, grimacing, and howling with all the strength of their lungs. They picked things out of the gutter to throw at him, bespattering his raiment with filthy refuse. He rounded upon them with a snarl, showing white eyes and teeth; whereat they fled helter-skelter, only to return again and pester him the moment his back was turned. He looked appealingly at the passersby for help; but they laughed for the most part, though some of the women had eyes of pity, and a man who seemed to rank superior to the multitude stopped and spoke sternly to the pursuers. Saïd was beginning to despair of ever getting rid of them, when the rabble suddenly dispersed of its own accord, flying this way and that like small fry at the approach of some big fish of prey.

===Chinese===

礼拜正做到一半，街上一阵沉重的脚步，一条黑衣大汉——帽子样式古怪，腰里别一根短棍——粗声粗气说话，一巴掌拍在他背上。他站起来分辩，那人只管一个劲儿挥手叫他走；那副威风满得叫萨义德不敢不依他伸出的手。「安拉饶恕！」他边走边咕哝，只觉这一天开张不吉。
不多时他进了一条大街，长得望不见头。太阳刚起，隔着缭绕的雾气一脸病容，在路面和一带房屋上涂了一片奶渍似的亮，投下淡到没有的灰影。要不是有些宅子上挂着描金的招牌，萨义德简直不知它亮没亮。他背着光大步走，长袍裹得紧紧的，冷得发抖，一路觉出行路人打量的眼。路上挤满了马车，大的小的，样式他一概不识。有几辆像装了轮子的房子，车里、车顶全是人。这些怪车叫他欢喜，欢喜那份花花绿绿——他欣赏着，如同欣赏几面糊了彩纸的围板——一个没了指望、光到底的人，还能欣赏的，也就这么多了。
忽然一阵嘲骂的尖啸怪叫扎进他耳朵：一伙破衣烂衫的小瘪三跟上他了，又蹦又做鬼脸，扯开嗓子嚎。他们从阴沟里抓东西砸他，把他的袍子溅满脏水烂渣。他回身一声低吼，翻出白眼白牙；他们抱头鼠窜，可他背刚一转，又围上来缠。他向过路人投去求告的眼；多半人笑，虽然有几个女人眼里有怜悯，一个瞧着高出众人一头的男人停下来，朝那伙追赶者厉声说了几句。萨义德正绝望于甩不掉他们，那群乌合忽然自行散了，东一头西一头，像小鱼群见了大鱼逼近。

===Original===

Looking in astonishment for the cause of his deliverance, he beheld a man in a tall, dome-shaped hat and dark clothing, having a bludgeon in his belt, so like the party who had cut short his orisons, that Saïd believed it was the same. He saw in this individual, drawing near with deliberate tread and solemn bearing, a high officer of the irregular troops charged with the maintenance of peace and order. He bowed low to the personage and invoked blessings on him in passing.
In the relief of being unmolested for a while, his spirits rose, and he felt almost happy. The streets grew ever more crowded as he advanced. The road was filled with two streams of wheeled vehicles, going in opposite directions. The throng on the footway jostled and elbowed him roughly, giving no more heed than the sea gives to a piece of driftwood. It surprised him to see no horsemen nor pack-animals, not so much as a train of mules. All was busy, yet orderly. Though the press of the traffic was so great that the wheels of one vehicle grated those of another, and the nose of a carriage-horse was in the back of a cart in front, there was no frenzied shouting, such as might have been expected, no gesticulation on the part of drivers, but only a dull rumble and roar akin to thunder.
A display of familiar dainties in a vast window caught his eyes and held them for a while. He flattened his nose against the pane, gloating on oranges and lemons, bananas and pomegranates, dried figs and dates and raisins, with grins of delightful recognition. He stood a long time gazing at them, shouldered impatiently by wayfarers. It was with a sigh that at last he turned away and pursued his endless walk.
Many women and girls passed him, clad in the immodest fashion of the Franks, which excites a man by its cunning suggestion of the form beneath. They wore strange headgear, such as never man saw. Some were young and beautiful, so that Saïd leered at them meaningly. One fair girl of provoking charm, who was walking with an elder woman, laughed at him and touched her companion’s arm. At that Saïd tingled in every vein, believing that she wished for him. All that the dragoman had told concerning the beauties of Lûndra surged gladly in his brain. His pulse quickened; he forgot that it was cold.

===Chinese===

他惊愕地寻找解围的缘由，看见一个戴高圆顶帽、穿黑衣、腰里别短棍的人——同打断他礼拜的那位像透了，萨义德认作同一个。他看这个人迈着沉稳的步子、端着庄严的架子走近，认作非正规军里一位维持治安的高官。他朝这位人物深深一躬，路过时为他祝了福。
得了一阵清净，他的精神头起来了，几乎算得上快活。越往前，街越挤。路面上两股车流，方向相反。人行道上的人潮粗粗地挤他撞他，理都不理，像海理一块浮木。叫他奇怪的是，见不着骑马的，见不着驮货的，连一队骡子都没有。处处忙，处处有秩序。车挤得轮子磨轮子、辕马的鼻子顶着前头货车的后帮，却没有预想中那种狂喊，赶车的也不手舞足蹈，只有一片沉雷似的隆隆。
一面大橱窗里陈列的熟识吃食勾住他的眼，扣了好一会儿。他把鼻子贴上玻璃，馋眯眯地看橙子柠檬、香蕉石榴、干无花果、椰枣葡萄干，咧出一张张欢喜的认亲脸。他站着看了老大半天，被行人一次次不耐烦地挤。末了他叹口气，转身接着走他那走不完的路。
多少女人姑娘打他身边过，一身法兰克人那种挑逗的装束——那装束狡猾地暗示底下的形，勾人的火。她们头上戴的物件古怪，人间没见过。有几个年轻貌美，萨义德朝她们意味深长地瞟。一个俊俏得恼人的姑娘，同一位年长女人走着，朝他一笑，碰了碰同伴的胳膊。这一下萨义德浑身血脉发麻，只当她有意思。通译讲过的伦德拉的美人，一齐欢欢喜喜涌上他的脑子。他脉快了；忘了冷。

===Original===

Turning, he overtook the two women and walked at the young one’s side, grinning into her face, and speaking words of love in Arabic. She shrank from him, pale with fright, and clung to the older woman’s arm; but he kept close to her, wooing her hotly with every term of endearment. They hastened their steps, so that he had to run to keep up with them. All at once they stopped short, and the old woman, who wore a fine cloak of fur and a headdress of many colours, spoke earnestly with a tall man clad in the sombre uniform already known to Saïd, having a high, dome-shaped hat and a leather truncheon in his belt. He stepped forward and seized the fisherman by the shoulders, shaking him and speaking sternly to him in a tone there was no gainsaying. Then, as the women made their escape, he pointed imperiously up the street and gave Saïd a push in that direction. The Muslim, completely taken aback, obeyed mechanically, the policeman following him a little way to mark his behaviour.
All day long he strayed on purposeless, growing more and more weary, a prey to thirst, and hunger, and intense cold. After noon the gloom deepened, the puny sun becoming quite obscured in cloud. He found a large piece of Frankish bread in a gutter, which he ate ravenously; and a little later, by good luck came to a drinking-fountain with a cup fixed to a chain for the service of poor wayfarers. Feeling refreshed, he prepared to face the night, and looked about for some sheltered place where he might sleep undisturbed. In a square court surrounded by high houses there was a sort of garden planted with sorry trees and shrubs, black with the prevailing soot, having seats and paved walks, and in the midst a great idol upon a pedestal. He stretched himself on one of the benches and composed his limbs to rest. But the cold was so great that he dared not fall asleep, but was fain to get up and walk again lest he should stiffen and die.
The streets by night were even more bewildering than in the daytime. The long vistas of yellow lamps, branching endlessly one out of another, confused his brain. Every wheeled vehicle had monstrous bright eyes to frighten him. The mist of light was blinding﻿—the eternal mist of cloud by day, of fire by night, from which the dull roar of traffic seemed inseparable.

===Chinese===

他一转身，追上那两个女人，挨着年轻的走，朝她脸咧嘴，用阿拉伯语讲情话。她吓得脸白，直缩，死抓着年长女人的胳膊；他贴住不放，把各种甜话热热地泼过去。两人加快脚步，他只得小跑才跟得上。忽然两人猛一站住：老女人——穿一件讲究的毛皮斗篷，头上多层彩——热切地同一个高个男人讲，那人穿的正是萨义德已认得的暗色号衣：高圆顶帽，腰里一支皮短棍。他上前一把抓渔夫的双肩，摇他，狠狠说话，那口气不容分说。两个女人脱身走脱，他威严地朝街那头一指，朝那个方向推了萨义德一把。穆斯林全无防备，机械地照办，警察跟了他一小段，看他的行止。
一整天他漫无目的地流浪，越走越乏，被渴、饿和透骨的冷轮班啃。晌午过后，昏暗更深，那点可怜的太阳整个叫云吞了。他在阴沟里捾到一大块法兰西面包，狼吞虎咽；过不多时，运气好，撞上一座饮泉——泉上拴着链子挂一只杯，专供穷路人。缓过点劲儿，他预备对付这一夜，四下寻一处背风、睡得安稳的地方。一个四方院子，四面高楼，当中一片小园子，栽着可怜巴巴的树木，叫遍城的煤烟熏得漆黑；有座椅，有石铺的小径，正中立一尊基座上的大神像。他往一张长椅上一伸，把四肢安顿下歇。可冷得太狠，他不敢睡实，只得起来再走，怕一僵就死。
夜里的街比白天更惑人。黄灯的长巷一望无尽，一巷叉出一巷，没有头，搅昏他的脑子。每一辆带轮子的车都长着怪亮的眼睛吓他。光的雾晃瞎人——白昼是云的雾，黑夜是火的雾，那沉闷的交通轰响，像跟雾长在一起，拆不开。

===Original===

The crowd where no man saluted other, no one looked friendly at his neighbour, but every face was grim with a set purpose, seemed awful to him. He feared it with the fear of evil spirits. The cries which assailed his ears were mournful as a wailing for the dead.
At length, after hours of wandering, he found an archway giving access to a quiet court and flung himself down in its gloom, too weary to know or care that the stones were icy cold. But it seemed that he had scarcely fallen asleep ere he was awakened by the flash of a lantern in his face. A gruff voice made a humming in his ears, and the form of a policeman loomed tremendous in his heavy eyes﻿—a dark form holding the light which dazed him. He struggled to his feet, and seeing the enemy in the act to step forward and seize him, made off through the archway and down the sounding street as fast as his stiff limbs would carry him.
After that he dared not lie down again, but wandered on, sometimes resting on a doorstep, sometimes leaning against a wall or some railings, until a pallor of dawn appeared in the east. He found a quiet place where he said his prayers undisturbed, and soon after, by the grace of Allah, lighted on another crust of bread﻿—a huge chunk on which he broke his fast. Then, when the day was fully come, he entered a public garden enclosed with palings and lay down upon the first seat he came to.
How long he slept he could not tell, for when he awoke the sky was completely overcast, and the brown fog had no point of brightness to indicate the sun’s whereabouts. But the place where he lay was noisy with the play of ragged children, some of whom fled pell-mell as his eyes opened on them. His limbs were numbed so that, setting foot to the ground, he had to support himself by the back of the seat; and it was long ere he could walk safely.
As he issued from the garden he espied a well-known object amid the hurrying crowd on the footway of a great thoroughfare﻿—a scarlet tarbûsh. With the strength of hope renewed, he ran as fast as he could to overtake its wearer. He came up with him, panting a salutation. But the face turned to him was not the face of the son of an Arab, but darker and of an olive tint not far removed from mouse-colour, the eyes set closer together.

===Chinese===

那人群，无人招呼人，无人朝邻人投一记友好的眼，张张脸都绷着一份狠目的——在他瞧来可畏。他怕它，像怕恶灵。灌进他耳朵的种种叫卖，哀得像哭丧。
流浪了几个钟头，末了他寻到一座拱门，通一个静院；他扑进那片幽暗里，乏得顾不上——也认不出——石头冰冷。可像才合眼，一盏灯笼的光就刷在他脸上。一个粗嗓门在他耳朵里嗡嗡，警察的身影在他沉重的眼皮前涨成一片巨物——一条黑影，举着晃他眼的光。他挣扎着爬起来，见对头正要上前来抓，夺路穿过拱门，顺着咚咚响的街，两条僵腿能带多快跑多快。
打那以后他不敢再躺，接着晃荡，一时坐人家门槛，一时靠墙、靠栏杆，直到东天泛出黎明的苍白。他寻了处清静地方，不受搅扰地做了礼拜；随后不久，托安拉的恩，又撞上一块面包皮——好大一块，他开了斋。天大亮之后，他进了一座围木木栅的公园，头一张椅子就躺下了。
睡了多久他说不上：醒来时天阴得严实，棕色的雾没有一个亮点能透出太阳在哪儿。可他躺的地方叫一群破衣孩子闹翻了，他眼睛一睁，几个撒腿就跑。他的肢体麻透了，脚落地时不得不扶着椅背；好半天他才敢放开步子。
出园的时候，他在一条大街的人行道上、匆匆的人潮里，望见一样认得的东西——一顶猩红毡帽。指望还了魂，他撒腿追那戴帽的，能跑多快跑多快。追上了，喘着招呼。可转过来的那张脸，不是阿拉伯人的子孙的脸：更黑，一层橄榄色，离耗子色不远，两眼挤得近。

===Original===

The reply to his salutation was in an unknown language; it was the speech of an unbeliever, in which the name of Allah did not occur. With a gesture of apology, expressive also of the deepest despair, Saïd fell back from him.
He got little heartbreaking reminders of the East from the form of a building here and there, and from homely objects in the shop windows. The sullen roar of the city was terrible in his ears, seeming now the voice of a cruel monster, now the growl of thunder﻿—always hostile and inhuman. His eyes, unused to the subdued light, unable to appreciate its half tints, met a grey-brown horror everywhere. The women, too, dressed to provoke desire, had a share in his loathing of the scene. He would have liked to kill them for the involuntary thrill they gave.
Men and women with great baskets crouched by the edge of the roadway, selling flowers. Some of the foot-passengers stopped to buy them. Saïd met people with nosegays in their hands, and it surprised him that they did not smell at them as folks used to in the East; but on reflection it seemed likely that in this land of gloom and disappointment the blossoms had no smell or, if any, a foul one. He saw the sign of the cross often in all sorts of places, and spat on the ground for hatred of it, cursing the religion of the country secretly under his breath.
His brain grew confused. He was hunting for the sunlight which was lost. Little patches of colour drew his eyes and caused him a moment’s rejoicing as for a treasure found at last. But each disillusion left him more despairing. Of a sudden, at the turning of a street, a blare of trumpets smote his ears, together with the rhythmic beat of a drum. In the heart of an eager, hurrying crowd, of like hue with the houses, the fog and the mud of the roadway, marched a company of soldiers clad in gorgeous scarlet﻿—a hundred of them moving as one man. Their brightness and the marvel of their going attracted Saïd. He followed them spellbound, yet with a kind of horror such as one has of jin in the night-season. He knew nothing of the crowd’s roughness. The moving streak of red glowed like a flowerbed in that sombre street﻿—like a bed of wild anemones amid the dull rocks of his native land. He battled to get near to them, but could not.

===Chinese===

对他招呼的回话，是一种不识的语言；异教徒的话，里头没有安拉的尊名。萨义德拿一个道歉的手势——也道尽了最深的绝望——从他身边退开。
这里一座楼的样式、那里橱窗里一件家常物什，都给他送来一阵阵剜心的东方。这城阴沉的轰响灌在他耳朵里可怕极了，一时像一头凶兽的声音，一时像雷的滚——总是敌意的，总不是人的。他的眼睛不惯这收敛的光，辨不出它的半调，处处撞上一片灰棕的恐怖。那些女人也一样，一身惹火的装束，在他对这景象的憎恶里占了一份。她们那不由他来的战栗，叫他恨不得杀了她们。
卖花的人，男男女女，挎着大篮子蹲在路边。有些行人停下买。萨义德遇见手里擎着花束的人；叫他奇怪的是，他们不凑上去闻，东方人个个要闻的；转念一想，在这片昏暗和扫兴的土地上，花兴许没有香——有，也是臭的。他处处见十字的记号，各式地方都有，他朝地上啐，压着嗓子暗咒这国的教门。
他的脑子乱了。他在找那丢失的阳光。一小片一小片的颜色勾他的眼，叫他一时欢喜，像终于寻着了宝。可每一场破灭都把他丢进更深的绝望。忽然，在一条街的拐角，一阵喇叭劈头灌进他耳朵，还有一通有板的鼓。在一群同房屋、同雾、同街泥一个颜色的、急匆匆的人潮正中，开过来一队兵，一身华贵的猩红——一百个人，行动如一人。那份亮、那种走法，把萨义德迷住了。他跟着他们，入了定，又带着一种怕——像人对夜里精尼的怕。人潮的推搡他一概不知。那一条移动的红，在阴沉的街上烧得像一片花床——像他故乡灰石间的一片野银莲。他拚了命要挨近，挨不近。

===Original===

To his mind, unhinged by fatigue and exposure, it was clear that, if only he could win to walk with them he would be saved. They were his life, his destiny, and they were slipping from him.
At length he lost sight of them altogether and the blackest despair took hold of him. He wandered into a region of quiet streets. The air had grown perceptibly warmer since the morning, and now a fine rain began to fall. Of a sudden, as it seemed to him, lamps were lighted; it was night. The sky lowered as a vast cloud; it was like a close lid oppressing him. Here was a maze in a box, shut out from sun, moon and stars, and he was doomed to roam in it forever. All at once he felt deadly cold; the next minute he was burning from head to foot. It occurred to him to pray to Allah; but where was the use of prayer when he was already condemned and in torment? He ceased to fight against his lot.
A host of evil spirits beset him, gibbering, snapping their fingers, grinning, and mocking his wretched plight. Things faded and grew dim. He knew the horror of a great army coloured like blood, thousands moving in silence as one man. Shrieking, he clung to some railings for protection, vaguely aware that a crowd was gathering about him in a place which, a minute before, had been quite deserted. Then he was back again in his native land.

===Chinese===

在他那叫疲乏和风霜卸了轴的脑子里，事情清楚得很：只要能赢得同他们并排走，他就得救了。他们是他的命，他的运数，正从他手边溜走。
末了他彻底望不见他们了，最黑的绝望攦住了他。他晃进一带安静的街。打早上起，空气已暖了些；这会儿下起细雨。像是忽然之间，灯全亮了；到夜里了。天压下来，像一整块云；像一口贴身的盖，闷着他。这是一只匣子里的迷阵，日月星辰全关在外头，他判了在这里头永世转悠。骤然他冷得要死；下一分钟他又从头到脚烧起来。他想起该求告安拉；可他已经判了、已经在受刑，祷告还有什么用？他不再同他的命争了。
一群恶鬼围上他，咭咭笑，捻着响指，龇着牙，嘲他这副惨状。东西淡了，暗了。他看见一支大军，血一样颜色，几千人没一点声息、行动如一人的恐怖。他尖叫着抱住一道栏杆求庇护，迷迷糊糊觉出人正围拢来——在一分钟前还空无一人的地方。随后，他回到了故乡。

### XV / 十五
===Original===

Saïd raved of palm-trees and gardens, the great sunshine and the inky shadows. He saw again the little house among the sandhills beside a calm blue sea. There were his nets spread to dry upon the beach. There was his fig-tree with the gnarled boughs and trunk, and the big leaves wide apart. There was the fringe of tamarisks along the shore, and the little city with its dome and minaret, clear-cut upon the vivid sky. He heard the distant music of bells, as some train of camels or mules passed slowly among the landward gardens.﻿ ﻿…
Suddenly there was a dun fog, effacing the vision and wrapping him in its gloom. Lamps without number shone blurred through the darkness. There was a sullen roar. He cried aloud in fear, but the sound of his voice was strange to him﻿—a new terror. He grew aware of a bright and silent army, streaming ever out of darkness into darkness across the narrow range of his sight; tens of thousands moving as one man. Their colour entranced Saïd, but the order of their going chilled him with an eerie dread. He was awestricken, in the presence of a force beyond man’s control. He felt that, if he could only draw near and walk with them, he would be informed of all things concerning his lot; but his limbs were frozen where he stood. He cried out upon the name of Allah.﻿ ﻿…
The fog melted away, the throng with it.
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn Muhammed!”﻿ ﻿… He was in the streets of Damashc-ush-Shâm, frenzied with the sunlight and the shouting. He slew and slew, until he waded in the blood of unbelievers. All at once he was confronted with an old man whose name was known to him. Unthinking, he flew at his throat and strangled him, flinging the body aside into an entry. Then he fell a prey to the bitterest anguish, perceiving that he had killed Mustafa, his adopted father. His wail tore the blue sky, as it had been a curtain, and dun fog poured in through the rent. Again he was beset with darkness, and the shiver of the silent host was upon him. He saw well-known faces in the ranks:﻿—Abdullah, Selìm, Hasneh, Ibrahìm the doorkeeper, Ferideh, Ismaìl Abbâs, Mustafa, Nûr, Mahmud Effendi. All the people he had ever known passed in endless review before him. They were changed to the likeness of devils, and moved in silence all together, as though one will actuated them.﻿ ﻿…
Presently he was sitting alone on the deck of a ship. Anon, he was drowning in the sea.

===Chinese===

赛义德谵语着椰枣树和园圃，大太阳和墨黑的影。他又看见了沙丘间那座小屋，傍着一片平静的蓝海。他的网摊在滩上晾。他的无花果树，枝干虬曲，大叶张开。沿岸一线柽柳，小城连同穹顶和宣礼塔，在天青上刻得分明。他听得见铃声远远的音乐——哪一队骆驼或骡马正缓缓走过内陆的园子。……
忽然一片褐雾，抹掉幻象，把他裹进它的昏暝。数不清的灯，隔着黑暗晕晕地亮。一片阴沉的吼。他害怕地大叫，可自己的嗓音听着陌生——又一重新恐怖。他觉察到一支明亮而无声的大军，川流不息，从黑暗涌进黑暗，横过他视野窄窄一线；千万之众，行进如一人。他们的颜色使赛义德出神，他们行进的秩序却叫他寒毛倒竖。他敬畏了——面对一股人力管束不了的力量。他觉得，只要能凑近、能同行，他命数的一切底细都会被告知；可他的肢体在原地冻住了。他高呼安拉的名。……
雾散了，人潮随它散了。
「教门！教门！穆罕默德的教门！」……他在大马士革的街上，日光和呐喊烧得他发狂。他杀啊杀啊，杀到在异教徒的血里蹚。蘶地，一个老人同他打了照面，名字是他识得的。他想也不想，扑上去掐住喉咙勒死了他，把尸身甩进一条门洞。随即他坠入最苦的痛楚——认出自己杀的是穆斯塔法，他的义父。他一声哀号撕开青天，像撕开一幅帷幕，褐雾从裂口灌进来。他又教黑暗围住，那支无声大军的一阵颤栗落在他身上。他看见队行里一张张熟脸：——阿卜杜拉、塞利姆、哈斯娜、看门的易卜拉欣、菲丽黛、伊斯玛仪·阿拔斯、穆斯塔法、努尔、马哈穆德埃芬迪。他生平识得的人，无穷无尽地在他眼前受阅。他们都变了魔鬼的模样，一齐无声地行进，仿佛一个意志在驱策他们。……
一会儿，他独自坐在一条船的甲板上。转眼，他淹在海里。

===Original===

Then he led a bride to his house on the sands, but ere he could reach it the fog came upon him. Once more there was brown twilight and that nameless horror.﻿ ﻿…
---
It was late afternoon. Wintry sunlight, enfeebled by the smoke-clouds, made lurid ripples on the bare white walls of a spacious room lined with sickbeds. At one end there was a comfortable fire burning in a recess of the wall, before which three women in white caps and aprons sat at a table, conversing in low tones. The ward was full of tossings, groans and sobs of pain, relieved by the subdued laughter of the nurses at their table; the roar of the city coming as a murmur from without.
Saïd opened his eyes upon the scene, but there was no light of understanding in them. He strove to raise himself on his elbows, but fell back upon the pillows with a moan. When next he looked there was a woman at his bedside watching him. She held a steaming bowl whose contents she kept stirring with a spoon. Her face showed neither pity nor sympathy, but all her movements were deft and gentle.
While she was busy feeding him, propping his back upon a heap of pillows, two men entered the room together and came straight to where he lay. One of them, who was dressed all in black, his face smooth save for a great tuft of hair on either jaw, hailed Saïd courteously in Arabic, inquiring after his health and commending him warmly to the mercy of Allah. Sitting down on a chair by the bed he informed the invalid that he had been for many years a missionary among the Arabs, and wished to know if he could serve him in any way. The sound of his native language seemed to gladden the sick man, for he listened intently, a dreamy smile on his face; but he answered nothing to the purpose, though his lips formed words. After many fruitless efforts to chain his attention, the visitor sighed and departed. He returned on the following days to meet with the same disappointment. Saïd always listened eagerly, sometimes his face wore a puzzled look, sometimes he smiled; but he never answered a word articulate. His silence was the more surprising that the nurses declared him to be very talkative when left alone, often muttering and exclaiming to himself for minutes together.
As the days wore on his strength came slowly back to him. He was able to sit up, then to walk a little way with the arm of a nurse.

===Chinese===

随后他迎着新娘走向沙上的家；还没到，雾追上了他。又是褐色的暝色，又是那无名的恐怖。……
---
时近傍晚。冬日的日光叫烟云削弱了，在一间宽绰病房的光白墙上荡起幽幽的涟漪——病房两侧排满病床。一头，墙龛里烧着一炉舒坦的火；火前一张桌，三个戴白帽系白围裙的女人坐着低语。病房里满是辗转、呻吟和痛楚的呜咽，护士们桌边压低的笑声为它解着闷；城市的吼声从外头传来，只像一片营营。
赛义德睁眼看着这一切，眼里却没有一点悟解的光。他撑着肘要起身，一声呻吟又跌回枕上。再一回看时，床边立着一个女人守着他。她端着一只冒气的碗，拿匙不住地揽。她脸上没有怜惜也没有同情，可一举一动都轻巧温柔。
她正忙着喂他——背底下垫起一堆枕头——两个男人一同进屋，径直走到他躺的地方。其中一个一身黑衣，脸刮得精光，只在两腮各留一大绺毛，用阿拉伯语客气地向赛义德致意，问他的贵恙，并恳切地将他托付给安拉的慈悯。他在床边椅上坐下，告诉病人：他在阿拉伯人中间做过多年传教士，愿知道能否为他效劳。乡音入耳，病人像是欢喜：他听得出神，脸上挂一个梦似的微笑；可答非所问——只嘴唇动，不成话。访客想把他的注意力拴住，枉费了好多回，叹口气走了。随后几天他又来，回回一样失望。赛义德总是热心地听，有时一脸困惑，有时微笑；可从没吐出一个囫囵字。他的沉默越发可怪：护士们说，独个儿搜下他时他话多得很，常常自言自语、自叹自哎，一说就是几分钟。
日子一天天过去，力气慢慢回他了。他坐得起来了，随后又能挽着护士的胳膊走一小截。

===Original===

But he took no delight in anything, seeming bewildered, as if stunned from a blow. His eyes dwelt long and puzzled on every object, as though they would fathom its meaning and could not. The doctor, going his round one morning, took him by the shoulder and gazed searchingly into his eyes. He made as if he would strike Saïd’s face, watching the patient carefully.
“An idiot,” he pronounced. “The man’s mind is gone.”
When next the person in black came to the hospital, he sat not with Saïd, but with the doctor. The Arab was gaining strength with every day. He could not remain much longer in a place devoted to sick people. It seemed desirable that the poor fellow should be sent back to the East, where there was just a chance that he might recover his wits. The missionary undertook to lay the case before the society whose minister he was. He had little doubt but that the matter could be easily arranged. At shaking hands, the doctor begged that he might be informed if the sea-voyage and return of familiar scenes wrought any noteworthy change in his patient. The case was a rare one, and its peculiar circumstances interested him.
Ten days later, Saïd left the hospital, supported by the man in black and another man, and was driven in a close carriage to the docks. There was a film on his eyes so that he could see nothing clearly. His companions talked much by the way, but a dull roar in his ears made their speech seem remote. He muttered often to himself; but whenever the missionary addressed him, he became intent at once, listening with strained attention, a faint smile on his face.
His brain was still full of visions, of scenes slowly changing. But from being an actor in them he was become a peaceful spectator, regarding them with the interest one has in a pageant. They were pleasant for the most part, succeeding one another with a dream’s inconsequence. Sometimes they were even funny, making him laugh aloud. But there were times when a cloud shadowed him suddenly and he shuddered, conscious of a vast army moving evenly and in silence, held together as one man by some mysterious force beyond his ken.

===Chinese===

可他对什么都不起劲，一脸的迷惘，像挨了一记闷棍还没醒。他的眼睛在每样物件上久久地、困惑地停着，像要参透它的意思，又参不透。一天早晨，医生查房，扳住他的肩，探究地凝视他的眼睛。他作势要打赛义德的脸，留神察看病人的反应。
「白痴，」他断言。「这人的神智没了。」
下一回那位黑衣人来医院，坐的不在赛义德床边，而在医生那里。阿拉伯人的体力一天天见长，在专收病人的地方不能再久留了。这可怜人似乎该送回东方去——那边兴许还有一线指望，叫他找回神智。传教士应承把案子提交给他供职的那个会。他毫不怀疑，这事容易办妥。握手作别时，医生托他：倘若海上之行和旧地重临叫病人起了什么值得注意的变化，务请见告。这病例罕见，它特殊的情形勾着他的兴味。
十天之后，赛义德出了医院，一边一个，由黑衣人和另一个人架着，塞进一辆密闭的马车，驶往船坞。他眼上蒙着一层翳，看什么都不清楚。同行的两人一路话说得多，可耳朵里一片钝钝的轰鸣，他们的话听着遥远。他时时喃喃自语；可传教士一开口，他立刻专注起来，凝神细听，脸上挂一抹淡淡的笑。
他脑子里仍满是幻象，一场一场缓缓地换。只是他已从戏中人变成了安闲的看客，拿看盛典的那份兴味看着它们。它们多半是愉快的，像梦一样没来由地衔接。有时还逗趣，惹他出声地笑。可也有时候，一片云蘶地遮了他，他一颤——他觉出浩浩一支大军，均匀地、无声地行进，靠一股他参不透的神秘之力，凝成如一的一个人。

### XVI / 十六
===Original===

Day by day the air grew warmer. Sky and sea put off their gloom, shining ever bluer and more lustrous as the sun gained in strength. Day by day, as he sat on the deck of a great steamer, looking out over the restless waves, Saïd had glimpses of remembered things, at first dimly, growing clearer as time went on. Once more he knew the difference of day and night, could tell when it was morning, or high noon, or evening; and he observed the hours appointed for prayer and thanksgiving to Allah. Scales seemed to fall from his eyes so that he saw distinctly, and sought the meaning of what he saw. The roaring in his ears dwindled to stray murmurs, letting him hear the voices and sounds about him as something more than mere senseless jabber.
Much of his past life came back, as a tale heard long ago; but it had no significance for him. Knowing that it concerned him nearly, it distressed him that he could not guess its import. He had the same trouble with regard to all that passed on board the steamer. Everything was very hard to understand. He would puzzle for hours over some trivial detail of the scene, knowing it familiar, yet powerless to grasp its meaning. The outer shell of form and colour held his mind and prevented it from penetrating any deeper. Worst of all, he was conscious of this flaw in his vision, though he strove in vain to better it.
Yet, in spite of drawbacks, his heart was glad because of the great sunlight and its dazzle on the sea. He would smile and laugh for no reason, and would croon old songs to himself where he sat apart in the lee of a cabin. Words came to his lips, which somehow suited his frame of mind; and he was pleased, recognising their fitness, but the words, like everything else, had no meaning for him.
Sometimes, glancing down at his clothing, he was almost convinced that it was not himself at all, but someone else whom he had never known. The close-fitting trousers which strained at the knees when he sat cross-legged, the loose-hanging black coat with needless buttons upon the sleeves, the Frankish boots so tiresome to put off and on, the hat of plaited straw, bound about the crown with a black ribbon﻿—all were strange, and vexed him with misty doubts of his identity.

===Chinese===

一天一天，空气暖了。天和海卸下它们的阴沉，随着太阳日盛，越来越蓝、越来越亮。一天一天，萨义德坐在一条大轮船的甲板上，望着不宁的浪，恍惚瞥见一些记得的东西——起初朦朦胧胧，随着时日渐渐清楚。他重新分得出昼和夜，认得出早晨、正午、傍晚；也守着给安拉礼拜谢恩的时辰。鳞片仿佛从他眼睛上脱落，他看得清楚了，也去寻他看见的东西的意思。耳中的轰响缩成零星低语，放他听见身边的嗓音和声响，不再是一片无意义的聒噪。
他过去生活的一大部分回来了，像很久以前听过的一篇故事；可它于他没有意义。他知道它同他切身相关，却猜不出它的要旨，这叫他苦恼。轮船上经过的一切，他也一样拿不准。什么都难懂。他能为一个毫不足道的细节琢磿几个钟头，明知它熟识，却抓不住它的意思。形和色的外壳扣住他的心智，不许它往深处去。最糟的是，他明知自己眼力有这一处破洞，怎么使劲也补不好。
饶是有这些短处，他的心是欢喜的——为那大片阳光，为它在海上的晃眼。他会无缘无故地笑，独自坐在舱房背风处哼老歌。词句来到他嘴边，不知怎么正合他的心境；他为它们的合辙而欢喜，可那些词，同旁的一切一样，于他没有意思。
有时他低头看看身上衣裳，几乎相信那根本不是他，是一个他从来不认得的人。那条盘腿一坐膝盖就绷紧的紧身裤，那件袖子上钉着多余扣子的黑褂子，那双脱穿都费劲的法兰克靴子，那顶辫草的帽、帽箍一道黑缎带——样样陌生，样样拿一团雾似的疑心搅他：他到底是谁。

===Original===

He would turn from the contemplation of them with a sigh, content simply to bask in the warmth and the brightness, leaving the riddle of his existence unsolved for the present.
The people of the ship were very kind to him. On all sides he saw smiling, friendly faces. One man in particular came often to sit with him; who always wore black clothes and dwelt in a part of the steamer whither Saïd was not allowed to go. He spoke in a familiar tongue, and the fisherman returned his greetings naturally, as an echo answers; but when he talked at any length his speech became mere words, having form and even colour, but no sense. One early morning this person came to the place where Saïd slept, and awoke him. He led him up on to the deck and showed a city resting on the dimpled bosom of the sea, with minarets and domes and a lighthouse, and great buildings dark beside the rising sun. And Saïd laughed for joy, he knew not why.
The vessel entered a fine harbour, where there was much shipping. As the sun got higher, the sea grew vivid blue and the sands of the coast had the colour of a ripe orange. There was green of foliage beyond the houses, the sky towards the horizon was soft and pearly. Hundreds of little boats plied upon the dancing water between large vessels which lay inert and supine, like sleeping monsters. The men and boys in them were gaily clad, with red caps, light turbans and clothing of divers colours. Homely shouts were in the air.
Saïd’s heart went out to the brightness of that merry scene. He hated his companion all at once with a fierce and unreasoning hatred. He would gladly have slain him where he stood smiling indulgently at the idiot’s glee. He loathed the steamer and all on board. He longed to be free of them, to escape on shore and mix with those men in bright apparel, who were his own people.
The noise of the engines ceased with the pulse of the screw; and almost directly there was a swarm of rowing-boats to the steamer’s side. In one of these, Saïd discerned a Frank sitting, dressed all in black on the pattern of the man at his side, of the man he hated. He scowled at this new blot in the sunlight; and his eyes chose that boat out of all others, following it closely. He saw the Frank step out and mount the ladder to the deck. A minute later he shrank back with a snarl.

===Chinese===

他会叹口气从这番端详里转过脸，甘心只晒着这暖、这亮，把他存在的谜暂且挂着不猜。
船上的人待他极好。四下他望见的都是微笑友好的脸。有一个尤其常来陪他坐：那人永远穿黑衣，住在轮船上一处不许萨义德去的地方。他说的是一种熟识的话，渔夫回他的招呼回得自然，像回声答人；可那人一长篇说下去，那些话就只是词，有形，甚至有色，没有意。一天清早，这个人来到萨义德睡的地方，唤醒他，领他上甲板，指给他看一座卧在海的酒窝上的城：宣礼塔、穹顶、一座灯塔，一轮初日旁边一片深色的大建筑。萨义德欢喜得笑了，不知为什么。
船进了一座好港，港里泊满了船。太阳升高，海蓝得发艳，岸边的沙是熟橙子的颜色。房屋后头一片叶子的绿，天边那一头柔和如珠。几百条小船在跳动的浪上穿梭来往，大船僵僵地仰躺着不动，像睡熟的巨怪。小船里的男人和男孩穿得花花绿绿，红帽、浅色缠头、各色衣裳。空气里是家常的呼喝。
萨义德的心扑向那片欢腾的光彩。他忽然恨透了他身边的同伴——一股凶的、不讲理的恨。那人正笑眯眯看着白痴的欢腾，他恨不得就地处了他。他厌恶这条轮船和船上的一切。他巴不得脱开他们，逃上岸，混进那些亮衣裳的人——他自己的同胞——中间去。
机器的响声随螺旋桨的搏动停了；几乎立刻，一群划子围上轮船的船帮。其中一条里，萨义德辨出一个法兰克人坐着，一身黑，同他身边这人——他恨的这人——一个样式。他朝阳光里这个新污点一皱眉；他的眼睛从所有船里单挑出那条，紧紧跟定。他见那法兰克人跨出来，攀上舷梯上了甲板。一分钟后，他嘶了一声，缩了回来。

===Original===

The evil one had come near, and was staring at him, grasping the hand of the other man in black and speaking with him as an old friend. Presently he essayed to take Saïd’s arm to lead him, but the latter sprang aside and, scrambling hotfoot down the ladder, was first in the boat.
During the brief passage to the shore, his new enemy strove to engage him in conversation; but Saïd, absorbed in watching the boatmen and listening greedily to their talk, had a deaf ear for him. Arrived at the landing-place, however, he submitted to be led through the lively crowd. He was as one demented, laughing for no apparent reason and shouting salutations to all he met. His excitement made no distinction between true believers and infidels, but beamed alike upon all who wore bright clothing. People turned in astonishment to look after one, who, though clad in all respects like a poor Frank, and walking with a well-known missionary, yet swore by the Quran and accosted everyone in Arabic with a marked Syrian intonation.
Feasting his eyes on the warm hues of the crowd and its animation, Saïd felt that he was at home again. Great joy engrossed him to the exclusion of all else in the world. He forgot the existence of the man in black, ignored even his own existence; content to wander on through the merry, noisy streets, no matter who his guide. But at a point where several ways met, the missionary tried to draw him out of the sunshine, and the colours, and the shouting, into a shadowed, silent street, where the houses were large and of Frankish build, with big glass windows. He pulled Saïd’s sleeve and spoke earnestly to him. The fisherman stared at him without comprehension, a fool’s laugh dying in his throat. His glance followed the guide’s stretched-out hand. Something in the aspect of the houses made him shiver. In a flash he had the vision of a vast dun cloud and a devilish blood-coloured throng moving silently through its heart. That road led somehow to it, and the man in black, the false guide, was suborned to drag him thither. With the cry of a wild beast, he sprang upon the astonished missionary and gripped his throat, forcing him to the ground. It was in his mind to strangle him there and then, and so make an end of the gloom, the silent horror and all the hideous nightmare he personified.

===Chinese===

那个恶人走近了，正盯着他，攦着另一个黑衣人的手，像老朋友一样攀谈。过了一会儿，那人伸手要挽萨义德的胳膊引他，萨义德一侧身闪开，手脚并用地溜下舷梯，头一个跳进小船。
去岸的短短一程，他的新仇人设法同他攀谈；萨义德只顾看船夫、贪听他们说话，对他充耳不闻。可一到登岸处，他由人领着穿过了热闹的人堆。他像个失了心的人，无缘无故地笑，逢人就打招呼。他的兴奋不认信士异教徒，凡穿亮衣裳的，一律沐着他的笑。人们惊愕地回头望这个人：他分明一身穷法兰克打扮，走在一个出了名的传教士身边，却指古兰经起誓，逢人便是叙利亚口音重重的阿拉伯语。
饱看那人堆的暖色和生气，萨义德觉得到家了。大喜吞没了他，吞掉了世上其余的一切。他忘了黑衣人的存在，连自己的存在也忘了；只管往那片欢腾喧闹的街里走下去，管他是谁引路。可走到一处几条路的岔口，传教士要拉他出日光、出颜色、出呼喝，进一条背阴的、无声的街——街两边房子高大，法兰克式样，大玻璃窗。他扯萨义德的袖子，恳切地同他讲。渔夫瞪着他，不懂，一声傻笑死在喉咙里。他的目光顺着向导伸出的手望过去。那些房子的什么神气叫他一抖。一眨眼，他看见一片大得没边的土黄云，一团血色的、悄然移动的人群在云心走。这条路不知怎么通到那儿；那黑衣人，那假向导，是受了收买要拖他去的。一声野兽的嘶吼，他扑上那惊呆的传教士，扼住喉咙，把他掼倒在地。他心里就要当场掐死他，好叫那昏暗、那无声的恐怖、他化身的一切噩梦，一并了结。

===Original===

But a concourse of people clothed in bright colours diverting his eyes, he quitted his hold and stood up.
“Dìn Muhammed!” he said, and burst out laughing.
At that the faces of the crowd changed their looks of menace for those of concern.
“Run, O my uncle!”﻿ ﻿… “Make haste!”﻿ ﻿… “By this way!”﻿ ﻿… “Save thyself!”﻿ ﻿…
Friendly cries came from all hands. And Saïd, without knowing why, leapt forward with a shout of exultation, and ran he cared not whither.
His Frankish hat had fallen and was forgotten. His head, which had not known the razor for many weeks, bristled with a shock of white hair. His beard, white also, was long and unkempt. Women in shrouds of indigo, with queer cylinders between their eyes, ran from him with screams of terror. Brown-limbed children tumbled headlong into doorways, yelling for their lives. Men in flowing robes flattened themselves against the wall as he passed, and stood to stare after him, exclaiming together. Soldiers, set to keep order in the streets, retired trembling to their hutches, and asked a blessing on that awful runner. An old man with white hair and beard bounding forward like a boy, shouting and laughing as he ran.﻿ ﻿… The apparition was new to the men of Iskendería, and they wondered what it might portend. Surely, thought they, it is a madman, or some true prophet sent from Allah! Did ever man see the like? Verily the end of all things draws nigh!
Saïd sped on, laughing in pure joy of the sunshine and the shadows, the bright hues and merry sounds of a life familiar to him. Swarthy faces looked out at him from dark thresholds of taverns and shops. There were donkeys, mules, camels, laden with sacks and bales and panniers. There was nothing sad, nothing to recall the cloud and its fear, save only a few Franks here and there; and even they failed to anger him, being clad not in dull raiment but in white. The sunshine on the multicoloured crowd, the chattering and gesticulation, the blue sky, the air, the very smells were friendly, redolent of home.
In a place where there was less traffic he slackened his pace, panting, and found himself bathed in sweat. For the first time he grew aware of the sun’s beams scorching his uncovered head, and instinctively he sought the shade of a wall, near the shop of a petty trader.
His own cries and laughter rang yet in his ears, but hollow and senseless.

===Chinese===

可一片亮色衣裳的人潮岔开他的眼，他松了手，站起来。
「丁·穆罕默德！」他说，放声大笑。
这一下，人群的脸从威胁换成了关切。
「快跑，俺的叔！」……「快着点！」……「打这条道！」……「逃命吧！」……
友好的喊声四面八方起。萨义德不知为什么，欢呼一声朝前就蹿，跑到哪儿算哪儿。
他的法兰克帽子掉了，忘了。他那许多星期没见过剃刀的头，炸着一头白发。胡子也白，又长又乱。裹靓蓝袍子的女人们——两眼之间支着古怪的筒子——尖叫着躲他。棕色胳膊腿的孩子们头前脚后跌进门洞，喊爹叫娘。长袍的男人贴墙扁着让他过去，站着望他背影，齐声惊叹。街上司秩序的兵，哆嗦着缩回岗亭，为那个可怕的奔人求了句福。一个白头发白胡子的老人，像小伙子一样朝前弹跳，一边跑一边喊、一边笑。……这副景象，伊斯肯德里亚人没见过，不知道主什么兆头。他们想：准是个疯子，要么就是安拉打发来的真先知！谁见过这样的？实在的，万物的尽头近了！
萨义德一路飞奔，为阳光和影子、为他熟识的生活的亮色和欢声，笑个不停。黝黑的脸从酒馆和铺子黑洞洞的门槛里朝外望他。有驴，有骡，有骆驼，驮着麻袋、货包、驮筐。没有一样叫人伤心，没有一样叫他想起那片云和云里的怕——只有这里那里几个法兰克人；连他们也没惹他的气：他们没穿那暗色的衣裳，穿的一身白。日光洒在五色人堆上，那谈笑、那比划，蓝天、空气、连气味都是友好的，是家的气味。
走到一处人车稀少的地方，他放慢了步，喘着，发觉自己汗透了。他头一回觉出太阳直烧他那没遮没盖的头，本能地寻到一面墙的荫凉，捾着一家小铺子。
他自己方才的喊和笑还在耳朵里响，只是空了，没了意思。

===Original===

In the plum-coloured shade he sat down to rest, his eyes dwelling on the sunlit buildings opposite. Their tint against the sapphire sky made him think of barren, stony hills﻿—the sunburnt hills of Es-Shâm. Of a sudden, there was a swimming in his head. Sickness seized him, forcing him to vomit. He groaned aloud, calling heartbroken on the name of Allah and bewailing his evil day. The merchant reclining at ease in the coolness of his shop hard by, hearing the sound of lamentation, came forth to see who made it. He was a tall, bearded man of middle life, wearing a high fez and embroidered turban; and his robe of mixed silk and cotton was green and crimson striped. Seeing an old man sit there bareheaded, he reproved him gravely for his folly, vowing by Allah that if he got a sunstroke he could blame no one but himself.
Saïd raised despairing eyes to the speaker﻿—eyes which saw nothing but his own immediate wretchedness. He heard the voice of Selìm cry﻿—
“Merciful Allah!﻿ ﻿… O my master!﻿ ﻿… O my eyes! O my dear! Is it indeed thyself, and in this shameful plight?﻿ ﻿… O mother of Mûsa, get food and drink! Let Hasneh make ready a pleasant bed! Behold Saïd, my beloved, is returned to us at the point of death, having white hair and the clothes of a Frank. Praise be to Allah that he is returned to us! May Allah spare him to us, and grant him peace and good health once more!”
Saïd heard Selìm’s voice and was glad to hear it. It sounded familiar, and he knew it friendly. “Praise be to Allah!” he murmured naturally. But his mind had no real knowledge of Selìm, and the words were but empty sound.

===Chinese===

他在梅子色的荫凉里坐下歇，眼睛落在对过日头晒着的房子上。它们衬在宝蓝的天前的那种颜色，叫他想起荒赤多石的山——沙姆那些晒焦的山。忽然，他头脑里一晕。一阵恶心攦住他，逼他呕吐。他大声呻吟，肝肠寸断地唤安拉的名字，哭自己命苦。近旁铺子里那位商人正偎在凉快里受用，听见哭声，出来看是谁在哭。他是个高个、有胡子、年纪居中的男人，戴一顶高毡帽、绣花缠头；袍子丝棉混纺，绿红条纹。见一个老头光着头坐在那儿，他庄重地训他糊涂，指安拉起誓：中了日射，怨不得旁人，只能怨他自个儿。
萨义德抬起绝望的眼睛望说话的人——那双眼睛只看得见眼面前的苦。他听见塞利姆的嗓音在喊——
「慈悲的安拉！……俺的主人！……俺的眼珠！俺的亲亲！真是你？落到这步田地？……穆萨的娘，拿吃喝来！叫哈斯娜铺一张舒坦的床！瞧，俺的心肝萨义德回来了，回来的路上只剩一口气了，一头白发，一身法兰克衣裳。感赞安拉，他回来了！叫安拉把他留给俺们，再给他平安，给他康健！」
萨义德听见塞利姆的嗓音，欢喜听。它听着耳熟，他知道它友好。「感赞安拉！」他自然而然地喃喃。可他的心里并不真认得塞利姆；那句话，不过是空空的一声。

### XVII / 十七
===Original===

When Saïd recovered of his sunstroke, he was the honoured guest of the little household. Selìm’s love for him, born years before of gratitude for the gift of a stolen garment, was now doubled with the respect for one of unsound mind. The whole house was Saïd’s, the shop also and all it contained. Selìm or his wife would have waited on him all day long had Hasneh not forestalled them. Mûsa was told off to shadow him when he walked abroad, lest any evil should befall him. His head and the hair of his body were shorn duly according to the law, and he was arrayed in good clothes, which the master of the house bought for him at no small cost.
At the hour of the evening meal, when men are sociable in the relief of the day’s task done, Selìm would often tell his children and any chance guest the story of his acquaintance with Saïd. He would lift the brown dressing-gown with the red braiding out of the chest where it was kept, and tears would stand in his eyes as he showed it to the little circle, handling it reverently as a priceless relic. He would glance ruefully at the fisherman where he sat cross-legged, muttering often to himself and making strange play with his hands.
The young ones loved better to hear of the great slaughter and how bravely Ahmed Pasha met his death. They would clamour for their father to act the scene for them, showing where the Sultàn’s envoy stood, where the Wâly, where the file of soldiers who shot him down. Mûsa clenched teeth and hands at the point where the soldiers shirked their work, and for a time doggedly refused to fire. He vowed that he would rather be killed himself than slay an old man and a pious Muslim to pleasure infidels. They loved that story best for the fighting and bloodshed that were in it; but Selìm liked most to tell of Saïd the Fisherman and his great goodness.
Every morning, having broken his fast, Saïd roamed forth out of the city to a place he had discovered, where there were palm-trees beside a sandy road, and whence, through the dusty leaves of a garden, he got a glimpse of yellow sands and the dark blue sea.

===Chinese===

赛义德的中暑痊愈之后，成了这个小户人家尊贵的客人。塞利姆对他的爱——多年前由一件偷来的衣裳的恩情生出的——如今又加上一层对神智失全之人的敬重，翻了一倍。整座宅子任赛义德住，铺子和铺中一切也是。若不是哈斯娜捷足先登，塞利姆两口子会整天伺候他。他出门时，派穆萨暗暗跟着，怕他遭什么不测。他的头和身上的毛发，依法如期剃了；又给他置了一身体面衣裳——是家主花了不小价钱买的。
晚饭时分——一天的活计卸下，人正爱说话——塞利姆常对孩子们和座中偶然的客人，讲起他同赛义德结识的始末。他会从收藏的箱子里，捧出那件镶红绦的褐色晨衣，捧给这一小圈人看，像捧一件无价圣物，眼里噙着泪。他又愁苦地望一眼渔夫——那人盘腿坐着，时常自言自语，两只手比划着古怪的戏。
孩子们更爱听大屠杀，爱听艾哈迈德帕夏死得多么壮烈。他们闹着要父亲把那一场演给他们看：苏丹的使者站在哪里，瓦利站在哪里，开枪的一列兵站在哪里。演到士兵们撂挑子、硬是不肯开枪那一段，穆萨咬紧牙、攋紧拳。他赌咒说，宁可自己被杀，也不肯杀一位老人、一个虔敬的穆斯林，去讨异教徒的好。那个故事里有厮杀有流血，孩子们最爱；塞利姆却最爱讲渔夫赛义德，讲他的大仁厚。
每天早晨，开了斋，赛义德便溜达到城外一处他自己发现的地方：沙路旁立着椰枣树，从一座园子蒙尘的叶子间，望得见一线黄沙和深蓝的海。

===Original===

There, sitting cross-legged in the shade, he was happy all day long, laughing and crooning to himself, receiving homage from the poorer class of wayfarers﻿—camel-drivers and muleteers, beggars and gipsies, snake-charmers and itinerant merchants﻿—who respected the fine robe and the embroidered turban with which Selìm had invested him.
He loved to watch the long trains of camels winding with the road, and would strain his ears to hold the music of their bells when it grew faint and died in the distance. It pleased him to see big men and fat go jogging by upon small donkeys, their legs distended because of full saddlebags, their feet not far from the ground. The blue-robed peasant women made eyes at him as they walked with swaying bodies, sleek brown arms raised like twin handles of a vase to steady the burdens on their heads. Sometimes rich men on prancing horses, sometimes a carriage dashed past him, heralded by an outrunner with girt-up loins. He took a childish pleasure in saluting these great ones, prizing a chance smile from one of them more than the effusion of humbler passengers. All was passionate, highly-coloured of the East. Every wayfarer was merry or furious, laughing or cursing, sullen or smiling, in the depth of despair or the height of glee, hot and heady as the sunlight itself. But sometimes, in a minute, a deep gloom would fall on him, isolating him so that he seemed to sit alone, aware of the silent march of a great bright army. At such moments he knew that the mystery was eternal, that it had been going on unguessed through all the time he had forgotten, and must go on irrevocably until the last day. He shuddered when the fit left him, and it was long ere he could shake off the horror of it.
Sometimes Hasneh would accompany him to his favourite spot and sit near him in the shade, delighting in his childlike gladness. But the wife of Selìm could seldom spare her from the house; more often it was Mûsa who dogged Saïd’s footsteps and lay hid in the garden close to where he sat. The lad got amusement out of his allotted task by imagining great perils for his father’s guest, seeing himself as rescuer dashing like a young hurricane to save him, scattering a hundred well-armed men like chaff.

===Chinese===

在那儿，他盘腿坐在荫凉里，一整天都快活，自己笑、自己哼，受着穷苦路人的敬礼——赶驼的、赶骡的、叫花子、吉普赛人、弄蛇的、跑货郎——他们敬的是塞利姆给他披挂的那身好袍子和绣花头巾。
他爱看长长一列列骆驼随路蜿蜒；驼铃的音乐淡下去、死在远处时，他还要竖起耳朵多留它一阵。大块头、胖肚子的人骑着小毛驴颠颠地过，两边鞍袋塞得鼓出来，腿也撑得开开的，脚离地不远——这景象他看了就乐。蓝袍的农家女人扭着腰肢走过，油亮的棕色胳膊高高举起——像花瓶的一对耳——扶住头顶的重负，一边朝他抛眼风。有时是烈马上面的阔人，有时是一辆马车，前头一个束紧腰布的狂奔开道，从他面前风驰而过。他孩子气地乐于向这些大人物行礼，他们偶然的一笑，在他比低下路人的千言万语还贵重。
一切都是东方的那份炽烈、那份浓彩。每个行人或喜或怒，或笑或咒，或阴沉或含笑，或在绝望的底、或在狂欢的顶，同日光本身一样滚烫上头。可有时候，一分钟的工夫，一片深暗会罩下他来，把他隔成孤身独坐，觉出那支明晃晃的大军无声的行进。这样的时刻他知道：那奥秘是永恒的，在他忘却的全部岁月里它一直悄悄行进、无人猜到，还必得不可挽回地行进到末日。一阵过去他发着抖，好久抖不掉那份惊怖。
有时哈斯娜陪他到心爱的地界，坐在近旁的荫凉里，分享他孩子似的欢喜。可塞利姆的老婆轻易放不下她；更多的时候，是穆萨缀着赛义德的脚步，藏在他坐处近旁的园子里。少年从这份差事里自找乐趣：替父亲的客人想象出种种大险，把自己想成救主，一阵小飓风似的扑过去搭救，把百十个持械的大汉扫得像糠皮。

===Original===

When the sun was set and the smoke from hidden dwellings curled blue upon the delicate flush of evening or yellowish on the dove-grey which followed, Saïd would rise and turn his face homeward; he loved to spend the livelong day in the open, detesting the imprisonment of four walls.
For months, for years, he led this peaceful kind of life, without care or thought, conscious only of the appearance of things, their outward shape and colour, troubled only at long intervals by the ghost of a memory. But there came a time of disturbance, when the crowd in the streets wore anxious looks, and men formed knots together, speaking excitedly with fierce eyes. Selìm, fearing a tumult, thought it wise to confine his guest within doors lest he should come to harm. His loving care would not trust the fisherman out of his sight. This imprisonment fretted Saïd, to whom the sunshine and the fresh air of the gardens were become as daily food. He grew very cross and irritable, and Hasneh, into whose charge he was given, had to bear the brunt of all ill-humour which could hear no reason.
Once when a great uproar arose in the city Saïd’s eyes flamed suddenly and he sprang to his feet. For a moment there was understanding in his face; but the fire died as suddenly as it leapt up, and he fell back into the old, listless bad temper. For more than a month he was constrained by Selìm’s order, going out only occasionally, when the master of the house had leisure to accompany him. He was kept in the house in deep shadow, with nothing bright to look at, and time hung very heavy on his hands.
One day Selìm closed his shop and came to sit in the room with his family. He spoke seldom, and was very grave. A neighbour with a scared face looked in on them from time to time, bringing tidings or feeling the need of company. Through long hours there was booming of cannon, followed by explosions near at hand, the crash and roar of falling masonry. Saïd strained ears to hearken, and his face wore a puzzled expression, such as is often seen on faces of the blind. The firing ceased towards evening, and Selìm, praising Allah, went out to gather tidings, but refused to take Saïd with him.
The next day there was no more booming, but towards noon the city was filled with shouting and tumult. The whole household running out to learn the cause of the din, Saïd was left unguarded for a few minutes.

===Chinese===

日落了，深藏的住家升起烟来，在傍晚娇红的天光上卷成蓝的，或在随后鸽灰的天上卷成黄的；这时赛义德便起身往家转——他爱整日待在露天，恨四壁的囚禁。
几个月，几年，他过着这样太平的日子，无忧无虑，只有事物的皮相入他的心——外形与颜色——隔上好久好久，才有一缕记忆的幽灵来搅他一搅。
可乱子终于起了：街上的人群面带忧色，男人们一撮一撮聚着，眼冒凶光，激动地交谈。塞利姆怕出暴动，觉得把客人锁在家里稳妥，免得他吃亏。他那份疼爱，不容渔夫离开自己的视线。这囚禁磨得赛义德难受——阳光和园子的新鲜空气早成了他每日的口粮。他变得暴躁易怒；看管他的差事落在哈斯娜身上，一切不讲道理的坏脾气，都由她兜头受着。
有一回，城里一阵大乱，赛义德的眼蘶地烧起来，他跳起身。有那么一刻，他脸上有悟解；可那火来得快去得快，他又跌回原先那副倦怠的坏脾气里。一个多月，他奉塞利姆之命拘着，只偶或出门——家主得空陪他才行。他闷在深影的屋里，没有一样亮东西可看，时光在他手上沉得坠人。
一天，塞利姆关了铺子，来同家眷坐在一间屋里。他话少，脸色极重。一个吓白了脸的邻人时不时探进头来，或送个信，或只是要个伴。长长的几个钟头里，炮声隆隆，跟着是近处的炸响、砖石倾塌的轰然。赛义德竖着耳朵听，脸上挂着困惑的神情——盲人脸上常见的那一种。炮声傍晚停了；塞利姆赞着安拉出门探信，却不肯带赛义德同去。
第二天炮声没有了；可近中午，满城灌满了呐喊与骚乱。全家人都跑出去看动静，赛义德独个儿搜了几分钟，没人看管。

===Original===

They had hidden away his outer garments, thinking that his love of finery would prevent him from going abroad without it. But he was a match for them. He knew where to find a robe﻿—an old garment of outlandish fashion, prettily bound with soiled red braid, which had often been spread out before his eyes of evening, when there were guests present. He opened the chest and took it out, smoothing it lovingly with a furtive glance to make sure that no one saw. Then he put it on, chuckling.
Thus attired, he stole to the door and peeped out. Hasneh and the mother of Mûsa were talking with some other women a good way off. Selìm himself was nowhere to be seen. Girding up his loins, Saïd took to his heels, laughing as he ran. Clouds of smoke blurred the sky before him above the roofs; his eyes dwelt on them curiously as they did always on a new thing. There was a noise of shouting in the air.
Suddenly on turning a corner he found himself in a yelling, furious mob, all rushing in one direction. Fierce eyes, brandished weapons, curses and a roar of shouting. It was as though a door swung open in Saïd’s brain, admitting light into a chamber long shut up. Understanding flashed in his eyes.
“Dìn Muhammed!” he cried, and rushed forward with the rest, only more fiercely, with more of frenzy. Even in that turmoil men looked at him and, looking, made way for him to pass. There was something awful in his face, a light of madness or inspiration beyond their ken. He was a prophet and would bring them good fortune. They pressed on behind him, shouting louder than before. On he ran, tearing a way through the crowd. At length he led them, was at their head, still rushing on.
All at once cries of warning and terror arose. The crowd surged backward, forsaking him. A sudden fear came upon him, a shudder﻿ ﻿… the noiseless horror!﻿ ﻿… A bright host, moving together as one man, appeared out of a side street, and formed a wall before him. He pressed both hands to his temples, staring wildly. There was a word of command, short and incisive as a pistol-shot. All the sunlight was filled with yells of rage and fright. Again the word of command, followed by a line of flashes and a loud report which burst his head.
“Dìn! Dìn! Dìn!﻿ ﻿…”
He flung up his arms. His eyes seemed to turn over in their sockets, as he fell backwards on the ground.

===Chinese===

他们把他的外袍藏了，想着他爱体面，没有袍子总不肯出门。可他们斗不过他。他知道哪儿有一件袍子——一件旧衣，异邦的样式，镶着一圈脏了的红绦，怪好看的——有客的夜晚，它常在他眼前摊开展示。他打开箱子取出来，恋恋地抚平，斜眼四顾，确信没人看见。然后穿上它，嘿嘿地笑。
这样装扮停当，他溜到门口张了张。哈斯娜和穆萨的娘在好远的地方同几个女人说话。塞利姆本人不见影。赛义德束紧腰布，撒腿就跑，一路笑。一球球的烟糊在他前方屋顶上的天；他好奇地盯着它们——他见着新鲜物事从来这样。空气里一片呐喊。
猛地一拐角，他扎进了一片呐喊狂怒的人潮，全都朝一个方向涌。凶眼，挥舞的兵器，咒骂，喊声的大浪。仿佛赛义德脑子里有一扇门豁然洞开，光涌进一间尘封已久的屋。悟解在他眼里一闪。
「穆罕默德的教门！」他喊，随着众人冲上前——冲得比众人更凶、更狂。那样的乱军之中，人们也看他；一看，就让路。他脸上有一种骇人的东西，一炬疯癫或灵感的火，出于他们的见识之外。他是先知，会带他们好运。他们紧紧跟在他背后，喊得比先前更响。他一路跑，从人堆里撕开一条路。到末了，他领着他们，冲在他们最前头，还在冲。
蘶地，一片示警与恐怖的呼号。人潮向后倒涌，抛下了他。一阵骤然的怕落在他身上，一阵颤……那无声的恐怖！……一支明亮的队伍，行动如一人，从一条横街里出来，在他面前排成一堵墙。他双手按住太阳穴，狂乱地瞪。一声口令，短促干脆，如一响手枪。满天的阳光灌满愤怒与惊惶的呐喊。又是一声口令，跟着一线火光齐闪、一声巨响——炸开了他的头。
「教门！教门！教门！……」
他双臂朝天一扬。眼睛像在眼窝里翻了个个儿，他仰面倒在地上。

===Original===

So the garment of the Christian missionary became the death-robe of a martyr for El Islâm, and the sunlight swam bloodred at the last.

===Chinese===

就这样，基督传教士的那件衣裳，成了一位伊斯兰殉道者的寿衣；阳光涌起，到末了，红得像血。

## Endnotes / 尾注
===Original===

1. It has been told me for a fact that when the exiled Khedive Ismaìl Pasha (known to London street-boys of the period as old Ishmel Parker) was at Naples, one of the officers in attendance on him challenged an Italian in a café for having dared to insult a Prophet of his (the Egyptian’s) religion. The man had been blaspheming, it appeared, as only a Neapolitan or a Tuscan knows how to blaspheme, heaping foul epithets on the name of his Saviour and the Blessed Virgin. A duel, my informant assures me, actually took place on these grounds. ↩︎
2. All the territory successively annexed to the rising of the Ottoman Empire was classed either as forming part of the *dar ul Islâm*, the house of Islâm, or as belonging to the *dar ul harb*, or the house of war, according as it was inhabited by Mohammedans or by Christians. In the latter case the new subjects of the Sultàn were called *rayahs*, and they were personally assessed to ransom their lives, which were forfeited by defeat, and as an equivalent for military service from which they were exempted, or rather, which they did not enjoy the privilege of rendering. This capitation-tax received the name of *haratsh*, and its payment entitled each Christian to keep his head on his shoulders for the space of one year. (Skene: *Anadol, or the Last Home of the Faithful*.) ↩︎
3. It was predicted in the beginning of the present century by a much-revered sheikh that when the first of the sevens falls the ruin of Islâm will commence, and when the second falls it will have been completed. We are now in the year of the Hegira 1277; the year about to open will invert the first of the two Arabic sevens read from right to left﻿—V becoming Ʌ; that is, 7 becoming 8, and in the year 1280 of the Hegira the second 7 will also be inverted. This prophecy, supported as it is by the reality of the troubles now arising in various quarters, has naturally exercised a great influence on the fatalist tendencies of the Mussulmans and increased their ill-will towards other sects. (Skene: *Rambles in Syrian Deserts*.) ↩︎
4. Ahmed Pasha sent some troops under the command of two colonels into the streets. They soon applied to him for instructions, under the impossibility of keeping the peace without resorting to violence. He ordered them in writing to fire upon the people.

===Chinese===

1. 有人确切地告诉过我：当年被放逐的赫迪夫伊斯梅尔·帕夏（Khedive Ismaìl Pasha，那时伦敦街头的顽童们管他叫「老伊什梅尔·帕克」〔old Ishmel Parker〕）侨居那不勒斯时，他随行的一名军官曾在一家咖啡馆里向一个意大利人提出决斗，因为那人竟敢侮辱他（这位埃及人）所奉宗教的一位先知。据说那人当时正以一种唯有那不勒斯人或托斯卡纳人才精通的方式亵渎神明，将种种污言秽语加在他救主和圣母的名号上。我的消息提供者向我保证，一场决斗确曾因此事而发生。 ↩︎
2. 奥斯曼帝国崛起过程中陆续兼并的一切疆土，或被划入 *dar ul Islâm*（伊斯兰之家），或被归为 *dar ul harb*（战争之家），视其居民为穆斯林或基督徒而定。在后一种情形下，苏丹（Sultàn）的新臣民被称为 *rayahs*（赖亚，非穆斯林顺民），他们须以人头纳税来赎回自己的性命——这些性命本已因战败而被剥夺——并以此代替兵役；更确切地说，代替他们无福享受的那份服役「特权」。这种人头税名为 *haratsh*（哈拉吉），完税之后，每一名基督徒便有权让脑袋在自己的肩膀上再多留一年。（斯基恩：《阿纳多尔，或信士最后的家园》〔Anadol, or the Last Home of the Faithful〕。） ↩︎
3. 本世纪初，一位备受尊崇的谢赫曾预言：当第一个七倾倒时，伊斯兰的毁灭将随之开始；当第二个七倾倒时，毁灭便已完结。我们如今正处在希吉来历 1277 年；即将开启的一年，将把两个阿拉伯数字的七当中从右往左数的第一个倒转过来——V 变成 Ʌ，亦即 7 变成 8；到希吉来历 1280 年，第二个 7 也将随之倒转。这一预言，又有当今各地乱象的现实为佐证，自然对穆斯林的宿命论倾向产生了巨大影响，并加深了他们对其他教派的恶感。（斯基恩：《叙利亚沙漠漫游记》〔Rambles in Syrian Deserts〕。） ↩︎
4. 艾哈迈德·帕夏（Ahmed Pasha）派两名上校率部上街。由于不动用武力便无法维持治安，他们很快向他请示机宜。他以书面命令他们向民众开枪。

===Original===

One of the colonels in command of the *regulars* obeyed his order and dispersed the mob, proving thus that the evil might have been checked. The other colonel, who had charge of the *irregulars*, was won over by a Mussulman sheikh, who adjured him in the name of the Prophet and their common religion to join them and clear the holy city of Damascus of infidels. He went over to the insurgents with his troops. (Skene, as above.) For further particulars of the massacre, see Skene, already quoted, Churchill: *Druzes and Maronites*, and *Ten Years in Mount Lebanon*, and the newspapers of the latter half of 1860. ↩︎

===Chinese===

指挥*正规军*的那名上校执行了命令，驱散了暴徒，由此证明这场祸乱本是可以遏止的。另一名统率*非正规军*的上校则被一位穆斯林谢赫争取了过去；那谢赫以先知及他们共同宗教的名义恳求他加入他们一方，把圣城大马士革的异教徒清除干净。于是他率领部队投向了叛乱者。（斯基恩，出处同上。）关于这场大屠杀的更多详情，可参看上引斯基恩的著作、丘吉尔的《德鲁兹人与马龙派》〔Druzes and Maronites〕与《黎巴嫩山十年记》〔Ten Years in Mount Lebanon〕，以及 1860 年下半年的各家报纸。 ↩︎

## Glossary of Arab Expressions and Names of Places / 阿拉伯语词汇与地名释义
===Original===

A servant, a slave, much used with an epithet of the Deity in the formation of proper names, as Abdullah, the servant of God; Abdul Cader, the servant of the Powerful, and so forth.
Father of. A man assumes his son’s name with this prefix as an honourable title, letting his own name be almost forgotten.
A devil, a jinni (pl. *afaereet*).
Job.
Para. The Arabs have no letter *P* and cannot pronounce it.
Pasha.
Tax in lieu of military service, levied on unbelievers.
Cain.
A local governor, inferior to the provincial governor (*Wâly* or *Mutesarrif*) and appointed by him.
Damascus. Shâm in this name is generally taken to mean “Left” in contrast with “Yemen” meaning “Right.” But it has more likely to do with Shem (Ar. Shâm); Syria is called Es-Shâm or Birr-ush-Shâm.
David.
Antichrist.
Religion, faith﻿—e.g., *dìn Muhammed* = *El Islâm*.
A Druze (pl. *Drûz*).
Son﻿—e.g., *ebn Ali* = the son of Ali.
A title of respect given generally to Muhammadans.
Persia.
Algiers or Algeria (often confused with Eljezireh = Mesopotamia).
An epithet of the patriarch Abraham appropriate to his city of Hebron.
Prince, an hereditary and purely Arab title of nobility, having nothing to do with the Turkish gamut of dignities which, like the Russian, are purely official. It is given, for instance, to all the kindred of the Prophet, in addition to the epithet Sherìf ( = honourable, holy).
A husbandman, a peasant (pl. fellahìn).
An imaginary person (cp. Span. Don Fulano) as we say Mr. So-and-so.
Abel.
Aleppo, surnamed the White (Esh-Shahbah).
(Lit., if God will) I hope.
Jesus (Muhammadan).
Alexandria.
Constantinople.
Gibraltar.
A geni, a fallen angel dwelling on earth and sharing with man the chance of salvation (pl. jin or jân).
Infidel, heathen.
A title of respect given exclusively to unbelievers.
London.
A Maronite (pl. Mowarni).
Egypt.
(What does God wish!) the commonest exclamation of surprise.
A court of law presided over by the *Qadi*.
Mary.
A religious judge in every city.
Moses.
A Muhammadan (pl. *Muslimûn*).
A governor of a province, less than a *Wâly* in dignity, but, like a *Wâly*, dependent directly on the Sultàn.
Naples.
Prophet.
Noah.
A cry equivalent to “Look out!”
The point towards which the face is turned at prayers (for Jews, Jerusalem, for Muhammadans, Mecca).
Greece.

===Chinese===

仆人、奴隶，常与真主的尊称连用构成人名，如阿卜杜拉（Abdullah），意为「真主的仆人」；阿卜杜勒·卡迪尔（Abdul Cader），意为「全能者的仆人」，等等。
意为「……之父」。男子将儿子之名冠以此词作为荣誉称号，自己的名字反而几乎被人遗忘。
魔鬼、精尼（复数 *afaereet*）。
约伯。
帕拉（Para）。阿拉伯语中没有字母 *P*，发不出这个音。
帕夏。
代替兵役之税，向非信徒征收。
该隐。
地方长官，职位低于省督（*Wâly*，瓦利，或 *Mutesarrif*，穆台萨里夫），由省督任命。
大马士革。此名中的 Shâm 一般解作「左」，与意为「右」的 Yemen（也门）相对；但它更可能与闪（Shem，阿拉伯语作 Shâm）有关；叙利亚即称 Es-Shâm 或 Birr-ush-Shâm。
大卫。
敌基督。
宗教、信仰——例如 *dìn Muhammed*（穆罕默德的宗教）即 *El Islâm*（伊斯兰正教）。
德鲁兹人（复数 *Drûz*）。
意为「……之子」——例如 *ebn Ali* 即「阿里的儿子」。
一种尊称，通常加于穆斯林之名。
波斯。
阿尔及尔或阿尔及利亚（常与 Eljezireh，即美索不达米亚相混淆）。
族长亚伯拉罕的一个别号，与他的城邑希伯仑正相称。
埃米尔，意为亲王，世袭的纯阿拉伯贵族头衔，与土耳其那一整套品级毫无关系——后者如同俄国的品级，纯属官职。例如，先知的全体亲族除谢里夫（Sherìf，意为尊贵、圣洁）这一尊号外，皆得此衔。
农夫、庄稼人（复数 fellahìn）。
虚构的人物（参照西班牙语 Don Fulano），即我们所说的「某先生」。
亚伯。
阿勒颇，别号「白色的」（Esh-Shahbah）。
（字面义：如若真主意欲）我希望。
耶稣（穆斯林所称）。
亚历山大港。
君士坦丁堡。
直布罗陀。
精尼，堕落的天使，居于人间，与人共享得救的机会（复数 jin 或 jân）。
异教徒、不信者。
一种尊称，专用于非信徒。
伦敦。
马龙派教徒（复数 Mowarni）。
埃及。
（真主想要什么啊！）最常用的惊叹语。
由卡迪（*Qadi*）主持的法庭。
马利亚。
宗教法官，每座城市皆有设置。
摩西。
穆斯林（复数 *Muslimûn*）。
省区长官，品级低于瓦利（*Wâly*），但与瓦利一样直接听命于苏丹。
那不勒斯。
先知。
挪亚。
一声呼喊，相当于「当心！」
礼拜时面朝的方向（犹太教徒朝耶路撒冷，穆斯林朝麦加）。
希腊。

===Original===

An old man; hence (age implying precedence) a chief, the headman of a tribe, a village, or indeed of any community.
Solomon.
Tripoli (Tarabulus-Esh-Shâm, Tripoli of Syria; not Tarabulus el Gharb, Tripoli in Barbary).
Mount Hermon.
Baghdad.
Lebanon.
The north coast of Africa west or Egypt: The Barbary States.
The governor-general of a province, appointed directly by the Sultàn (or at least from Constantinople) and for a period of five years.
The province governed by a *Wâly*.
Japheth.
(O God) the commonest of all exclamations, meaning whatever you please, oftenest with a sense of “Make haste!” or “Forward!”
Jesus (Christian).

===Chinese===

老人；因年长即居先，引申为首领、头人——部落、村庄乃至任何群体的长官。
所罗门。
的黎波里（Tarabulus-Esh-Shâm，叙利亚的黎波里；而非 Tarabulus el Gharb，巴巴里的黎波里）。
黑门山。
巴格达。
黎巴嫩。
非洲北海岸、埃及以西之地：即巴巴里诸邦。
省区总督，由苏丹直接任命（或至少出自君士坦丁堡），任期五年。
由瓦利（*Wâly*）治理的省区。
雅弗。
（主啊）最常用的感叹语，含义随语境而定，最常带有「快点！」或「前进！」之意。
耶稣（基督徒所称）。
